### [1] [PragyaDoc: A Universal Document Intelligence Framework for Multilingual Medical Document Understanding in Low-Resource Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07478)
*Jagpal Singh Jhala*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PragyaDoc is a Universal Document Intelligence Framework designed to bridge India's medical language accessibility gap by transforming English-only medical documents into locally accessible formats through a four-layer pipeline: parallel ensemble OCR, geometric-lexical fusion, deterministic domain structuring, and dual-LLM medical reasoning/localization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: India's 22 official languages create a critical barrier where most medical documentation is in English, excluding rural populations, ASHA workers, and patient families who urgently need this healthcare information but cannot understand it.

Method: A four-layer pipeline: (1) parallel ensemble OCR extraction layer, (2) geometric-lexical fusion layer, (3) deterministic domain structuring layer, and (4) dual-LLM medical reasoning and localization layer.

Result: The abstract does not yet specify quantitative results or evaluation metrics, as it focuses on presenting the framework architecture and addressing the accessibility gap.

Conclusion: PragyaDoc aims to address India's medical language accessibility crisis by providing a universal document intelligence framework that bridges English medical documentation and India's multilingual patient population.

Abstract: India's 22 official languages create a critical accessibility barrier: the majority of medical documentation exists exclusively in English, yet the patients who most urgently require this information - rural populations, ASHA workers, and patient families - are functionally excluded from understanding it. This paper presents PragyaDoc, a Universal Document Intelligence Framework that addresses this gap through a four-layer pipeline: a parallel ensemble OCR extraction layer, a geometric-lexical fusion layer, a deterministic domain structuring layer, and a dual-LLM medical reasoning and localization layer

</details>


### [2] [NeuroPilot: An Agent-Driven Smart Pipeline for Processing, Quality Control, and Managing Neuroimages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07541)
*Yiyao Chen,Yucheng Li,Jungong Tong,Shaoqi Wang,Kunhao Zhou,Ziquan Wei,Monica Murea,Marissa DiPiero,Tingting Dan,Guorong Wu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: NeuroPilot is a multi-agent LLM-driven system that automates neuroimage processing workflows, including data standardization (dcm2bids-skill), modality-specific preprocessing (neuroimage-pre-skill), and quality control (qc-agent-skill). It was deployed across 17 cohorts (>123,000 subjects) covering diverse populations and MRI modalities, achieving 100% completion on QC-validated infant data, validated QC flags against FreeSurfer metrics on 558 subjects, and compressed the traditional 2-3 month processing timeline into one week.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neuroimage processing pipelines are fragmented into three brittle stages (standardization, preprocessing, QC) that require project-specific scripts, environment-adaptive tuning, and labor-intensive manual QC, making orchestration difficult and unscalable across diverse cohorts and infrastructure.

Method: NeuroPilot digitalizes neuroimage processing expertise into three LLM-invocable skills (dcm2bids-skill, neuroimage-pre-skill, qc-agent-skill) within a multi-agent LLM-driven architecture. The agent autonomously orchestrates workflows, generalizes infrastructure into a single configuration, dynamically routes datasets to optimal preprocessing pipelines based on modality and cohort traits, and performs evidence-based semi-automated QC via a 3-D browser dashboard with a multi-tiered verification system.

Result: Deployed across 17 cohorts (>123,000 subjects) spanning infant to aging populations and multiple MRI modalities. The QC agent screened 558 production subjects with validated automated flags against FreeSurfer topology-defect metrics. The infant pipeline achieved 100% (201/201) completion rate on QC-validated inputs. NeuroPilot reduced the traditional 2-3 month training and processing timeline to a single week.

Conclusion: NeuroPilot demonstrates that LLM-driven multi-agent systems can effectively orchestrate complex neuroimage processing workflows with high scalability and generalizability across diverse cohorts, significantly reducing manual effort and processing time while maintaining quality through automated evidence-based QC.

Abstract: Transforming raw neuroimage archives into analysis-ready derivatives relies on three brittle stages: data standardization, modality-specific preprocessing, and quality control (QC). While individual neuroimaging tools are well developed, their orchestration requires project-specific scripts, environment-adaptive tuning, and labor-intensive manual QC. To address this, we introduce NeuroPilot, a multi-agent system that digitalizes the expertise of neuroimage processing, QC, and data management into three LLM-invocable skills: dcm2bids-skill, neuroimage-pre-skill, and qc-agent-skill. The LLM-driven agent autonomously orchestrates workflows, generalizing various infrastructure settings into a single configuration to achieve the highest scalability. Demonstrating the system's generalizability, we deployed NeuroPilot across 17 cohorts (>123,000 subjects) spanning infant to aging populations and multiple MRI modalities (structural, diffusion, functional). In practice, after standardizing data via the dcm2bids-skill, the agent dynamically routes datasets to the optimal neuroimage-pre-skill based on available modalities and cohort traits (e.g., dispatching T1w and fMRI data to fMRIPrep, or selecting specialized pipelines for infant cohorts). The qc-agent-skill then drives an evidence-based, semi-automated QC via a 3-D browser dashboard, utilizing a multi-tiered verification system to optimize failed cases and escalate complex issues for supervisor inspection. Quantitatively, our QC agent screened 558 production subjects, validating its automated flags against FreeSurfer's topology-defect metrics. The infant processing pipeline achieved a 100% (201/201) completion rate on QC-validated inputs. Importantly, NeuroPilot compresses the traditional 2--3 month timeline for training staff and processing complete datasets into a single week. NeuroPilot is deployed in https://wanda-cyberbench.com/.

</details>


### [3] [Performance of large language models in the optical diagnosis of colorectal polyps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07543)
*Joshua C. Vences,William T. Tran,Nikko Gimpaya,Catharine M. Walsh,Rishad J. Khan,Robert Bechara,Asher C. Wiggins,Celine N. Rousan,Kaitlyn V. G. L. Morgado,Angie Ibrahim,Kevin H. M. Kuo,Daniel von Renteln,Alexander Hann,Dennis L. Shung,Michael A. Scaffidi,Charles Ménard,Joshua Landy,Samir C. Grover*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This study evaluates the diagnostic accuracy of five multimodal large language models (Claude Opus 4, Gemini 2.5 Pro, GPT-o3, GPT-4o, GPT-5) for classifying colorectal polyps using the PRIME dataset. While all models achieved F1 scores >0.9 for neoplastic vs. non-neoplastic classification, diagnostic accuracy for more nuanced classifications (invasive vs. non-invasive, low- vs. high-grade adenoma) was much lower, and none met ESGE standards for clinical deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate optical diagnosis of colorectal polyps is critical for guiding resection strategy and surveillance decisions. With multimodal large language models (MLLMs) showing potential for image-based diagnosis, there is a need to rigorously evaluate their diagnostic accuracy before clinical adoption.

Method: A retrospective diagnostic performance study using the PRIME dataset (white light and NBI images, 132 cases). Five MLLMs (Claude Opus 4, Gemini 2.5 Pro, GPT-o3, GPT-4o, GPT-5) were evaluated for Paris classification, NICE classification, and predicted histology. F1 scores, percent correct scores, and accuracy were calculated against expert responses, with Cochran's Q and McNemar's tests for statistical comparison.

Result: All MLLMs achieved F1 scores >0.9 for neoplastic vs. non-neoplastic polyps. Gemini 2.5 Pro had the highest F1 scores for invasive vs. non-invasive (0.560) and low- vs. high-grade adenoma (0.492). Claude Opus 4 and GPT-5 achieved statistically significantly higher percent correct scores (41.7%) for Paris classification than other models. However, sensitivity and specificity did not meet ESGE standards.

Conclusion: Claude Opus 4 and Gemini 2.5 Pro performed closest to expert consensus in differentiating polyp subtypes. However, current MLLM performance falls short of clinical standards, necessitating prospective multicenter trials and human-in-the-loop workflow design before clinical deployment.

Abstract: Background and Study Aims: Accurate optical diagnosis of colorectal polyps guides resection strategy and surveillance, with multimodal large language models (MLLMs) showing potential for image-based diagnosis. We aimed to evaluate the diagnostic accuracy of MLLMs in classifying colorectal polyps and predicting histology. Methods: We conducted a retrospective diagnostic performance study using the PRIME dataset, a curated set of white light and narrow-band imaging (NBI) images. We evaluated Claude Opus 4, Google Gemini 2.5 Pro, GPT-o3, GPT-4o, and GPT-5. For Paris, Narrow-band Imaging Colorectal Endoscopic (NICE), and predicted histology, we calculated F1 scores, percent correct scores, and accuracy of each MLLM compared to expert responses for 132 cases. Cochran's Q and McNemar's Test were used to determine differences between predicted values of each MLLM. Results: The F1 scores among MLLMs were >0.9 for all models for neoplastic vs. non-neoplastic polyps. Gemini 2.5 Pro demonstrated the highest F1 scores for invasive vs. non-invasive polyps and low- vs. high-grade adenoma, at 0.560 and 0.492 respectively. Claude Opus 4 and GPT-5 had statistically significantly higher percent correct scores than other MLLMs at 41.7%, using Paris classification. Conclusions: Claude Opus 4 and Gemini 2.5 Pro showed the highest accuracy in differentiating polyp subtypes, performing closest to expert consensus. Sensitivity and specificity, however, did not meet ESGE standards, highlighting the need for prospective multicenter trials and the design of human-in-the-loop workflows before clinical deployment.

</details>


### [4] [Learning an Interior Layout Policy in a Domain Specific Language Action Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07547)
*Yuhao Lu,Weichen Zhang,Wenyi Xiao,Haohui Chen,Yiyun Fei*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LayoutDSL is an LLM-based framework for indoor scene layout generation that uses a domain-specific language (DSL) as a structured action space for layout reasoning, trained via supervised fine-tuning on a new 3D-FrontDSL dataset and optimized with reinforcement learning using design-principle-based rewards, achieving improved spatial plausibility and design logicality over existing baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing indoor layout generation methods oversimplify room conditions (reducing them to coarse 3D bounding boxes, ignoring doors/windows) and formulate layout design as direct coordinate regression, which prevents models from learning the underlying reasoning logic of intelligent layout design.

Method: LayoutDSL proposes an LLM-based framework that learns an interior layout policy in a DSL action space. The DSL provides an explicit symbolic representation of layout information where each action is an interpretable design decision. The method constructs the 3D-FrontDSL dataset with room-structure annotations and synthetic DSL action sequences for supervised fine-tuning, then designs rewards based on interior design principles and physical plausibility, optimizing the policy via reinforcement learning.

Result: Extensive experiments show that LayoutDSL substantially improves spatial plausibility and design logicality over strong baselines and existing methods.

Conclusion: The DSL-based policy learning paradigm with verifiable feedback enables more generalizable and scalable indoor layout generation, demonstrating that symbolic action spaces are superior to raw coordinate regression for teaching layout reasoning to models.

Abstract: Indoor scene layout generation is a challenging task in interior design. Existing methods often oversimplify the task by reducing room conditions to coarse 3D bounding boxes and neglecting structural elements such as doors and windows. More fundamentally, many prior approaches formulate spatial reasoning as direct coordinate prediction, thereby casting interior layout design as continuous regression over raw geometric parameters, which hinders the model from learning the underlying reasoning logic of intelligent layout design. We propose \textbf{LayoutDSL}, a novel LLM-based framework for learning an interior layout policy in a domain-specific language (DSL) action space. The DSL provides an explicit symbolic representation of layout information and serves as a structured action space for layout reasoning, where each action corresponds to an interpretable design decision. Under this DSL-based policy learning paradigm, we construct 3D-FrontDSL, a dataset of room-structure annotations paired with synthetic DSL action sequences for supervised fine-tuning. To promote a more generalizable and scalable policy with verifiable feedback, we design rewards grounded in interior design principles and physical plausibility, and optimize the policy via reinforcement learning. Extensive experiments demonstrate that LayoutDSL substantially improves spatial plausibility and design logicality over strong baselines and existing methods.

</details>


### [5] [P2Voxel: Pyramid Pivot Voxelization for 3D Mesh Tokenization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07549)
*Zhenhong Sun,Haozhe Liu,Yifu Wang,Xibin Song,Senbo Wang,Huadong Mo,Daoyi Dong,Hongdong Li,Pan Ji*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: P2Voxel is a pyramid pivot voxelization framework that converts triangle meshes into compact, structured, and learnable tokens by sampling minimal local surface evidence within active voxels, enabling efficient mesh reconstruction for downstream 3D tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Triangle meshes have irregular topological connectivity, making 3D mesh tokenization a challenging geometric sampling problem. Existing approaches like field-centric volumetric sampling and edge-intersection surface sampling are suboptimal; the goal is to identify minimal geometric evidence within each active voxel that is sufficient for deterministic surface recovery.

Method: P2Voxel introduces three key innovations: (1) Pivot Voxelization under Local Planarity assumption, representing each active voxel with a surface pivot and orientation sign for deterministic reconstruction; (2) Pyramid Pivot Voxelization under Spatial Complexity assumption, allocating finer pivot tokens to geometrically complex regions while keeping smooth regions coarse; (3) Pyramid VAE under Block Reconstructability assumption, learning compact multi-resolution latent codes over locally reconstructable pivot blocks.

Result: The framework converts meshes into compact, structured, and learnable pyramid pivot tokens, enabling efficient mesh reconstruction for downstream 3D tasks.

Conclusion: By retargeting mesh tokenization as local surface evidence sampling and leveraging spatial non-uniformity, P2Voxel achieves compact mesh representation that avoids modeling the entire high-resolution voxelized shape as a dense global field.

Abstract: Triangle meshes provide explicit and accurate surface geometry, yet their irregular topology connectivity makes 3D mesh tokenization a geometric sampling problem: how to sample and organize geometric evidence into compact, structured and learnable tokens. Beyond field-centric volumetric sampling and edge-intersection surface sampling, we retarget mesh tokenization as \textit{local surface evidence sampling}: identifying the minimal geometric evidence inside each active voxel that is sufficient for deterministic surface recovery. To this end, we introduce \textbf{P2Voxel}, a pyramid pivot voxelization framework for compact and reconstruction-aware mesh tokenization. P2Voxel is built on three key innovations. Under the \textit{Local Planarity} assumption, Pivot Voxelization represents each active voxel with a surface pivot and an orientation sign, providing minimal local evidence that can induce the corner values required for deterministic reconstruction. Under the \textit{Spatial Complexity} assumption, Pyramid Pivot Voxelization exploits the spatial non-uniformity of real surfaces by allocating finer pivot tokens to geometrically complex regions while keeping smooth regions coarse and compact. Under the \textit{Block Reconstructability} assumption, a Pyramid VAE learns compact multi-resolution latent codes over locally reconstructable pivot blocks, avoiding the need to model the entire high-resolution voxelized shape as a dense global field. Together, these designs convert meshes into compact, structured, and learnable pyramid pivot tokens, enabling efficient mesh reconstruction for downstream 3D tasks.

</details>


### [6] [Auditing Medical Vision-Language Models on Chest Radiographs: Estimating Reference Agreement Across Institutions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07550)
*Pengyang Yu,Yiou Wang,Zhongping Dong,Sahraoui Dhelim,Chun-Mei Feng,M. Tahar Kechadi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper evaluates three generative vision-language models on over 345,000 chest-radiograph finding-level predictions across three institutional corpora and six findings under two elicitation protocols, testing whether agreement with institutional reference standards transfers across sites, findings, prediction directions, and question formats. The study finds that adaptive estimator selection does not improve over simple fixed alternatives, and reference agreement must be re-evaluated per site and per interface.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language models return chest-radiograph findings without confidence scores, so receiving institutions cannot gauge how much to trust individual judgments. Whether agreement with institutional reference standards transfers across sites, findings, prediction directions, and question formats is largely unmeasured, motivating this investigation.

Method: Three generative vision-language models were evaluated on three institutional chest-radiograph corpora and six findings under two elicitation protocols, generating more than 345,000 finding-level predictions. Reference agreement at a receiving institution was estimated from a small budget of local labels, and seven estimation strategies were stress-tested under repeated strict institution-held-out evaluation, excluding the receiving institution from development entirely.

Result: Adaptive selection among seven estimators did not improve on simple fixed alternatives: it achieved a mean Brier score of 0.1083, versus 0.0853 for always using a Beta-Binomial empirical-Bayes estimator and 0.0855 for a target-only logistic model (difference of 0.0003, within the family's sensitivity to solver version changes). Each leads in about half the settings, so no default can be recommended. The advantage over cross-institution pooling was concentrated at one site and non-confirmatory when clustered by institution. A plug-in empirical-Bayes posterior-predictive count interval at nominal 95% coverage covered only 87.0%, less at the hardest institution.

Conclusion: Reference agreement must be re-evaluated per site and per interface, as agreement does not reliably transfer across institutions. The results concern agreement with institutional labels, not clinical correctness, and no universally preferred estimator default can be recommended.

Abstract: Vision-language models return structured chest-radiograph findings through interfaces exposing no confidence score, so a receiving institution cannot read off how far to trust an individual judgment. Whether agreement with an institution's reference standard transfers across sites, findings, prediction directions and question formats is largely unmeasured. We evaluated three generative vision-language models on three institutional chest-radiograph corpora and six findings under two elicitation protocols, comprising more than 345,000 finding-level predictions, and estimated finding-by-direction reference agreement at a receiving institution from a small budget of local labels. Estimation strategies were then stress-tested under repeated strict institution-held-out evaluation. Under evaluation excluding the receiving institution from development entirely, adaptive selection among the seven estimators that design admits did not improve on simple fixed alternatives: it achieved a mean Brier score of 0.1083, against 0.0853 for always using a Beta-Binomial empirical-Bayes estimator and 0.0855 for a target-only logistic model. Those two differ by 0.0003, less than this family's own sensitivity to a change of solver version, and each leads in about half the settings, so no default can be recommended. Their advantage over estimators pooling across institutions was concentrated at one site and not confirmatory once clustered by institution, and a plug-in empirical-Bayes posterior-predictive count interval at a nominal 95% level covered 87.0%, less at the hardest institution. Reference agreement therefore has to be re-evaluated per site and per interface; these results concern agreement with institutional labels, not clinical correctness.

</details>


### [7] [Impact of Dataset Composition on Embedded Real-Time UAV Wildfire Detection Using Compact YOLO Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07554)
*Eduardo de los Santos,Andre S. Kelbouscas,Ricardo B. Grando,Bruna V. Guterres*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper evaluates how dataset composition affects embedded real-time UAV wildfire detection using compact YOLO models. Four training configurations (real non-augmented, real augmented, hybrid non-augmented, hybrid augmented) were compared, where hybrid sets mix real wildfire images with AI-generated samples. Results show the real non-augmented dataset achieved the best balance of recall and mean average precision, and neither synthetic data hybridization nor augmentation improved final deployment performance, suggesting dataset realism and domain alignment matter more than synthetic expansion for embedded UAV wildfire detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-based wildfire detection systems for UAVs are constrained by the limited availability of diverse real-world training images, motivating an investigation into whether synthetic data mixing and augmentation can overcome this data scarcity under resource-constrained deployment conditions.

Method: Four training configurations were evaluated using compact YOLO models as a controlled validation family: real non-augmented, real augmented, hybrid non-augmented, and hybrid augmented. The hybrid sets combine real wildfire images with AI-generated samples to test whether synthetic data improves detection performance.

Result: The best overall operating point was achieved with the real non-augmented dataset, which delivered the strongest balance between recall and mean average precision for UAV-based wildfire detection. Neither hybridization with synthetic data nor augmentation produced a better final deployment choice.

Conclusion: For embedded UAV wildfire detection, dataset realism and domain alignment are more valuable than increasing training set size through synthetic expansion, indicating that data quality and relevance should be prioritized over data quantity.

Abstract: The development of vision-based wildfire detection systems for unmanned aerial vehicles is constrained by the limited availability of diverse real-world training images. This paper investigates the impact of dataset composition on embedded real-time UAV wildfire detection using compact YOLO models as a controlled validation family. Four training configurations were evaluated: real non-augmented, real augmented, hybrid non-augmented, and hybrid augmented, where the hybrid sets combine real wildfire images with AI-generated samples. The objective is to determine whether synthetic data mixing and image augmentation improve practical detection performance under resource-constrained deployment conditions. Experimental results show that the best overall operating point was obtained with the real non-augmented dataset, which achieved the strongest balance between recall and mean average precision for UAV-based wildfire detection. The results also show that neither hybridization with synthetic data nor augmentation produced a better final deployment choice. These findings suggest that, for embedded UAV wildfire detection, dataset realism and domain alignment are more valuable than increasing training set size through synthetic expansion.

</details>


### [8] [MVMD: A Multi-View Approach for Enhanced Mirror Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07559)
*Yidan Shen,Yu Wen,Chen Zhang,Xin Fu,Renjie Hu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MVMD is a multi-view mirror detection method with a new multi-view mirror database, using cross- and self-attention mechanisms across three blocks (Inter-Views, Intra-View, Refinement) to improve mirror detection accuracy by up to 2.6% and IoU by up to 11.1% over single-image methods, enhancing 3D reconstruction in mirror-dense environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mirrors create distorted and fragmented spaces in 3D reconstruction, leading to inaccurate 3D models. Existing mirror detection methods only work on single images, ignoring the rich multi-view information available in typical 3D reconstruction pipelines. Detecting mirrors across multi-view images before reconstruction can effectively solve this problem.

Method: MVMD leverages inherent associations between objects seen from different views and those reflected inside/outside mirrors, learned via cross- and self-attention mechanisms. It consists of three blocks: (1) Inter-Views Block tracks object shifts within mirrors due to viewpoint changes; (2) Intra-View Block detects object reflections inside mirrors; (3) Refinement Block sharpens mirror boundaries and enhances detection details. A first multi-view mirror detection database is also introduced.

Result: MVMD improves accuracy by up to 2.6% and IoU by up to 11.1% compared to single-image mirror detection methods, demonstrating substantial gains particularly relevant for 3D reconstruction in mirror-dense environments.

Conclusion: Multi-view information significantly boosts mirror detection performance compared to single-image approaches. The proposed MVMD method and the new multi-view mirror database provide an effective solution for improving 3D reconstruction accuracy in environments containing mirrors.

Abstract: In 3D reconstruction, mirrors introduce significant challenges by creating distorted and fragmented spaces, resulting in inaccurate and unreliable 3D models. As 3D reconstruction typically relies on multi-view images to capture different perspectives of a scene, detecting and labeling mirrors in multi-view images before reconstruction can effectively address this issue. However, existing methods focus solely on single-image detection, overlooking the rich information provided by multi-view setups. To overcome this limitation, we propose MVMD, a novel Multi-View Mirror Detection method, along with the first database specifically designed for mirror detection in multi-view scenes.
  The design of MVMD is grounded in the inherent associations between objects seen from different views and those reflected inside and outside of mirrors. These relationships are learned through cross- and self-attention mechanisms. MVMD consists of three key blocks: the Inter-Views Block tracks the shifts of objects within mirrors caused by changes in viewpoint; the Intra-View Block detects object reflections inside mirrors; and the Refinement Block sharpens mirror boundaries and enhances detected details.
  Experimental results show that our method improves accuracy by up to 2.6% and IoU by up to 11.1%, compared to single-image mirror detection techniques. This substantial improvement makes MVMD particularly effective for computer vision tasks, especially in enhancing the accuracy of 3D reconstruction in mirror-dense environments.

</details>


### [9] [XEns-CKD: An Explainable Ensemble-Based Approach for Chronic Kidney Disease Stage Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07561)
*Rehan Ahmad,Gousia Habib,Muhammad Shaban,Ishfaq Ahmad Malik*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes XEns-CKD, an ensemble vision transformer-based framework for classifying chronic kidney disease (CKD) stages from ultrasound images, achieving 86.36% accuracy with a 4% improvement over existing methods, and uses explainable AI (LIME, LRP, Attention-Min/Max) to interpret affected kidney regions for clinical trust.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CKD is a silent disease whose progression may not significantly affect daily life, yet early detection of the CKD stage is critical for slowing progression. Existing methods lack sufficient accuracy and interpretability for clinical deployment.

Method: Three vision transformers (ViTs) are trained on a private ultrasound image dataset with different training parameters, then ensembled. Explainable AI techniques (LIME, LRP, Attention-Min, Attention-Max) are applied, with a combined attention map used to identify and interpret kidney regions affected during CKD progression.

Result: The ensemble model achieves 86.36% overall classification accuracy, a 4% improvement over existing methods. The attention map effectively identifies and highlights kidney regions affected by CKD progression across stages.

Conclusion: XEns-CKD provides a more accurate and clinically interpretable solution for CKD stage classification from ultrasound images, improving trust and aiding early detection.

Abstract: Chronic kidney disease (CKD) is a silent disease. Its progression may not significantly hamper a person's daily routine. Human kidney function can be classified as normal or as one of the five stages of CKD. Early detection of the CKD stage can help patients understand the functional status of their kidneys and follow medical advice to slow CKD progression. In this paper, we propose XEns-CKD, a novel ensemble vision transformer-based scheme for CKD stage classification using ultrasound images. Three ViTs were trained on a private ultrasound image dataset using different training parameters. The performance of each ViT was evaluated using macro sensitivity, macro specificity, macro precision, macro F1-score, macro Youden index, the Matthews correlation coefficient (MCC), and macro balanced accuracy. The ensemble model achieved an overall classification accuracy of 86.36%. This work also emphasizes identifying and interpreting kidney regions affected by CKD progression. Explainable artificial intelligence techniques, including LIME, LRP, Attention-Min, and Attention-Max, were used to improve model transparency and clinical trust. An attention map combining the Attention-Min and Attention-Max results effectively identified and interpreted kidney regions affected during CKD progression from one stage to another. The attention map also highlighted the effects of CKD progression in these regions. Compared with existing methods, the proposed method classified the five CKD stages and normal kidney status with a 4% improvement in accuracy.

</details>


### [10] [Mechanistic Interpretability-Guided Selective Fine-Tuning of Vision-Language Models for Centimeter-Level Flood Depth Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07562)
*Nafis Fuad,Xiaodong Qian,Dongxiao Zhu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper introduces FloodLlama, three vision-language models for real-time street-level flood-depth estimation from imagery. A fully fine-tuned dense model (FloodLlama-Dense) achieves 0.40 cm MAE, while two interpretability-guided sparse variants (FloodLlama-MI5/MI6) fine-tune only 5-6 causally relevant cross-attention layers identified via mechanistic interpretability analysis, reducing trainable parameters by 86-88% with minimal accuracy loss and outperforming the STURM-FloodDepth baseline on real-world data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Urban flooding threatens transportation infrastructure, yet no operational system provides real-time, street-level flood-depth estimates at centimeter resolution. The authors aim to address this gap while also demonstrating that mechanistic interpretability can guide parameter-efficient fine-tuning.

Method: Three vision-language models are fine-tuned for continuous flood-depth estimation: FloodLlama-Dense (full QLoRA fine-tuning), FloodLlama-MI5 and FloodLlama-MI6 (sparse variants fine-tuning only top 5 or 6 causally relevant cross-attention layers). Training uses an ~610K-image subset of a 2.81M-image synthetic Unreal Engine 5 corpus with flood depths 0-40 cm. Mechanistic interpretability (linear probing, logit lens, CKA, cross-attention entropy) identifies relevant layers and a two-stage adaptation pattern (L13-L22 restructure visual reps; depth decodable from L23).

Result: FloodLlama-Dense achieves 0.40 cm MAE, 1.97 cm RMSE, and 97.59% Acc@5cm. Sparse variants cut trainable parameters from 54.4M to 6.55-7.86M (86-88% reduction) with minimal accuracy loss. On a real-world benchmark, FloodLlama-MI6 achieves 98.62% accuracy vs. 86.61% for the STURM-FloodDepth baseline.

Conclusion: Mechanistic interpretability can effectively guide sparse fine-tuning of vision-language models for flood-depth estimation, achieving near-parity with dense fine-tuning while dramatically reducing computational cost, and substantially outperforming existing baselines on real-world data.

Abstract: Urban flooding poses an escalating threat to transportation infrastructure, yet no operational system provides real-time, street-level flood-depth estimates at centimeter resolution. This paper presents three vision-language models fine-tuned for continuous flood-depth estimation from street-level imagery: FloodLlama-Dense, a fully fine-tuned QLoRA baseline, and FloodLlama-MI5 and FloodLlama-MI6, interpretability-guided sparse variants that fine-tune only the top five and six causally relevant cross-attention layers identified through mechanistic interpretability analysis, respectively. Training uses an approximately 610,000-image subset of a 2.81-million-image synthetic corpus generated in Unreal Engine 5. The dataset combines single-vehicle subsets with 5 cm depth increments and mixed-vehicle subsets with 1 cm depth increments, spanning seven vehicle types, four weather conditions, and flood depths from 0 to 40 cm. FloodLlama-Dense achieves an MAE of 0.40 cm, an RMSE of 1.97 cm, and an Acc@5cm of 97.59%. Mechanistic interpretability analysis combining linear probing, logit lens, centered kernel alignment (CKA), and cross-attention entropy reveals a two-stage adaptation pattern: layers L13-L22 restructure visual representations, while depth first becomes linearly decodable at layer L23. FloodLlama-MI5 and FloodLlama-MI6 leverage this insight by fine-tuning only five or six of the eight cross-attention layers, achieving an 86-88% reduction in trainable parameters (6.55-7.86 million versus 54.4 million) with minimal accuracy loss. On a real-world benchmark, FloodLlama-MI6 achieves 98.62% accuracy, compared with 86.61% for the published STURM-FloodDepth baseline.

</details>


### [11] [What to Edit Next: Visually Aligned Image-Editing Follow-Up Suggestions in Conversational Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07565)
*Zhijing Zhang,Jinpeng Yu,Xin Song,Bingnan Li,Chuyue Li,Changhui Du,Xiaolin Fang,Jiaming Liu,Ruihua Huang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents a three-stage framework for multimodal follow-up edit recommendations in image-creation conversations, trained on 100,000 real samples from Qwen App. It uses SFT with a human-reviewed intent table, multi-objective RL with user click feedback, and a visual verifier to reduce inconsistencies. Live A/B tests show visual inconsistency dropped from 3.7% to 0.9%, CTR improved 32.70%, and conversation turns increased 39.90%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing conversational assistants primarily handle text-only follow-up recommendations, leaving image-creation conversations underexplored. In image-creation tasks, recommendations must reflect user preferences, be diverse, and remain executable on the current image—requirements unmet by text-only systems.

Method: A three-stage framework: (1) Build a human-reviewed table of follow-up editing intents from 100,000 real multi-turn conversations, create SFT targets, and fine-tune a multimodal policy; (2) Optimize the policy through multi-objective reinforcement learning using user click feedback to align with actual choices; (3) Introduce a visual verifier as additional training supervision to reduce visual inconsistencies between suggested edits and the current image.

Result: The framework outperforms baselines in automatic and human evaluations. In a live A/B test with millions of users, visual inconsistency dropped from 3.7% to 0.9%. Recommendation CTR improved by 32.70%, image take-away rate by 16.32%, and average conversation turns per user by 39.90% (all p<0.05).

Conclusion: Multimodal follow-up edit recommendations significantly enhance user engagement in image-creation conversations. The combination of SFT, multi-objective RL, and visual verification effectively addresses the unique challenges of this setting and yields substantial improvements in real-world deployment.

Abstract: Conversational assistants increasingly recommend follow-up edits to help users continue a task. Existing systems primarily target text-only interactions, leaving image-creation conversations underexplored. In image-creation tasks, useful follow-up edit suggestions must reflect user preferences, offer diverse directions, and remain executable on the current image. We collected 100,000 real multi-turn image-creation conversation samples from Qwen App and found that 80.1% are image-dependent, underscoring the need for multimodal recommendation. We address this setting with a three-stage framework. In Stage 1, we use real online data to build a human-reviewed table of appropriate follow-up editing intents, then create SFT targets and fine-tune a multimodal policy. In Stage 2, to align rule-guided SFT suggestions with actual user choices, we use user click feedback to optimize the policy through multi-objective reinforcement learning. In Stage 3, to reduce visual inconsistencies between suggested edits and the current image, we introduce a visual verifier as additional training supervision. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our framework significantly outperforms baselines on both automatic and human evaluations. In a live user-randomized A/B test with millions of users, our final framework reduces visual inconsistency from 3.7% to 0.9%. Furthermore, it significantly improves recommendation CTR by 32.70%, image take-away rate by 16.32%, and average conversation turns per user by 39.90% (all p<0.05).

</details>


### [12] [Temporal Generalization in fNIRS-Based Autism Classification: A Cross-Time-Window Transfer Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07567)
*Marios Petrov,Sahana Vinayak,Targol Bakhtiarvand,Moses Smith Guddah,Adham Atyabi,Frederick Shic,Kevin A. Pelphrey*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper formalizes the cross-time-window transfer problem in fNIRS-based ASD classification, where optimal observation windows vary across subjects due to hemodynamic delays. Using topographic map representations, the authors benchmark three vision architectures under zero-shot and eight adaptation strategies across 124 subjects, finding that zero-shot performance is near chance (54-69%), subject-specific fine-tuning with ~5% data recovers 90-96%, and domain-adversarial/self-supervised methods achieve 78-90% without target data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing fNIRS-based ASD classification assumes temporally aligned evaluation, but in practice optimal observation windows vary across subjects due to differences in hemodynamic delay and neurovascular coupling, creating a temporal distribution shift that degrades performance. This cross-time-window variability has not been formally addressed.

Method: The paper introduces a cross-time-window transfer protocol that varies window length (2.5-10s) and offset within biological motion trials. fNIRS recordings are represented as topographic maps, and three vision architectures are benchmarked under two zero-shot baselines and eight adaptation strategies using leave-one-subject-out cross-validation with N=124 subjects.

Result: Key results: (1) zero-shot cross-window accuracy is near chance (54-69%); (2) ~5% subject-specific fine-tuning recovers 90-96%, while a subject-specific upper bound reaches 97-100%, showing inter-subject variability is the dominant barrier; (3) domain-adversarial and self-supervised strategies achieve 78-90% without target-subject data; (4) discriminative information is recoverable from windows as short as 2.5s.

Conclusion: The findings provide a practical roadmap for deploying fNIRS-based ASD classifiers under realistic temporal variability, demonstrating that while zero-shot transfer across time windows is challenging, subject-specific adaptation and domain adaptation strategies can substantially recover performance.

Abstract: Functional near-infrared spectroscopy (fNIRS) is a promising modality for autism spectrum disorder (ASD) classification, yet existing approaches assume temporally aligned evaluation. In practice, the optimal observation window varies across subjects due to differences in hemodynamic delay and neurovascular coupling, creating a temporal distribution shift that degrades performance. We formalize this as a \textit{cross-time-window transfer problem}, introducing a protocol that varies window length (2.5--10\,s) and offset within biological motion trials. Using topographic map representations of fNIRS recordings, we benchmark three vision architectures under two zero-shot baselines and eight adaptation strategies under leave-one-subject-out cross-validation ($N{=}124$). Key findings: (1) zero-shot cross-window accuracy is near chance (54--69\%); (2) ${\approx}5\%$ subject-specific fine-tuning recovers 90--96\%, while a subject-specific upper bound reaches 97--100\%, identifying inter-subject variability as the dominant barrier; (3) domain-adversarial and self-supervised strategies achieve 78--90\% without target-subject data; and (4) discriminative information is recoverable from windows as short as 2.5\,s. These findings provide a practical roadmap for deploying fNIRS-based ASD classifiers under realistic temporal variability.

</details>


### [13] [Latent-Frequency Validity: Fast Spectral Editing with Screened Video-VAE Transfer Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07569)
*Bowen Xue,Jiafeng Xiong,Xin Quan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces latent-frequency validity (LFV), a method for controlling noise, flicker, smoothness, and frequency content directly in video-VAE latents without decode-filter-reencode passes. LFV learns a compact VAE-specific spectral response and uses validation selection to choose between a cheap diagonal per-frequency calibrator (C1) and full channel mixing (CM), making cross-channel capacity a controllable resource. Across 544 VAE-edit cells, 423 operators are emitted with high fidelity, and frozen operators generalize across generated-domain cells with ~3x speedup over pixel filtering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Video VAEs redistribute pixel-space frequency bands across latent channels, making latent-space edits unreliable. Direct latent editing risks disrupting VAE round-trip dynamics, so a method is needed to know when latent spectral edits are valid and when they require more complex cross-channel treatment.

Method: LFV learns a compact VAE-specific spectral response and deploys it only when it improves decoded-target fidelity without worsening round-trip drift. It follows a validation-selected path from a diagonal per-frequency calibrator (C1) to full channel mixing (CM), making cross-channel capacity a controllable per-edit resource.

Result: Across 544 VAE-edit cells spanning six spectral families, LFV emits 423 cheap operators (277 via C1, 146 requiring CM). On the primary 120-cell radial sweep, 99/100 emitted operators pass held-out evaluation, and all 323 operators across five additional filter families pass. Fully frozen operators pass all 20 tested CogVideoX and HunyuanVideo generated-domain cells. LFV matches direct latent-filter latency and is ~3x faster than pixel filter-reencode.

Conclusion: The resulting spectral response maps reveal distinct VAE regimes, including strongly channel-coupled CogVideoX responses and a sharp Open-Sora high-band stability frontier, demonstrating that LFV provides a practical and generalizable approach to direct spectral editing in video-VAE latents.

Abstract: Direct spectral editing in video-VAE latents can control noise, flicker, smoothness, and frequency content without a decode--filter--reencode pass. However, video VAEs may redistribute pixel-space frequency bands across latent channels, and latent edits can disrupt VAE round-trip dynamics. We introduce \emph{latent-frequency validity} (LFV), which learns a compact VAE-specific spectral response and deploys it only when it improves decoded-target fidelity without worsening round-trip drift. LFV follows a validation-selected path from a diagonal per-frequency calibrator (C1) to full channel mixing (CM), making cross-channel capacity a controllable per-edit resource. Across 544 VAE--edit cells spanning six spectral families, LFV emits 423 cheap operators: 277 are handled by C1, while 146 (34.5\% of emitted operators) require channel mixing. On the primary 120-cell radial sweep, 99/100 emitted operators pass source-video-grouped held-out evaluation. Across five additional filter families, all 323 emitted operators pass held-out evaluation. Fully frozen OpenVid-fitted operators, including the validation-selected path coefficient, pass all 20 tested CogVideoX and HunyuanVideo generated-domain cells without adaptation. The selected response matches direct latent-filter latency and is about $3\times$ faster than pixel filter--reencode. The resulting maps reveal distinct VAE regimes, including strongly channel-coupled CogVideoX responses and a sharp Open-Sora high-band stability frontier.

</details>


### [14] [COMEX: A Composition-Grounded Benchmark and Learning Framework for Explainable Aesthetic Image Cropping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07570)
*Rui Yang,Wei Zhou,Dingyong Gou,Xiaohui Cui,Cong Li,Yinyin Gong,Yipo Huang,Jiliang Zhao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper reformulates explainable aesthetic image cropping as a structured crop-composition-explanation problem, introducing the COMEX benchmark with 33,161 quadruples and a two-stage SFT+GRPO framework for joint cropping, composition understanding, and explanation generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing crop-and-explain methods treat explanation as post-hoc text generation and overlook composition, which is a key aesthetic factor linking crop decisions with interpretable reasoning.

Method: The authors introduce COMEX, a benchmark built via image expansion and an IO-reversal pipeline containing 33,161 quadruples (expanded image, crop box, composition category, and composition-grounded explanation). They propose a two-stage SFT+GRPO framework where supervised fine-tuning establishes the structured output protocol and basic cropping ability, and GRPO improves crop quality, composition prediction, and explanation faithfulness.

Result: The framework is benchmarked against 15 large vision-language models and existing cropping methods on COMEX, demonstrating strong performance across evaluation metrics and transferability on prior benchmarks.

Conclusion: The proposed structured approach and framework effectively enable composition-grounded explainable aesthetic image cropping, providing a comprehensive testbed for future research.

Abstract: Explainable aesthetic image cropping requires not only localizing a visually pleasing crop but also explaining why it is preferred. Existing crop-and-explain methods largely treat explanation as post-hoc text generation and overlook composition, a key aesthetic factor that links crop decisions with interpretable reasoning. In this paper, we reformulate explainable aesthetic image cropping as a structured crop-composition-explanation problem. To support this setting, we introduce COMEX, a new benchmark built through image expansion and an IO-reversal pipeline. COMEX contains 33,161 quadruples, each consisting of an expanded image, a crop box, a composition category, and a composition-grounded explanation, enabling joint learning of crop localization, composition understanding, and explanation generation. We further propose a two-stage SFT+GRPO framework, where supervised fine-tuning establishes the structured output protocol and basic cropping ability, and GRPO further improves crop quality, composition prediction, and explanation faithfulness. We benchmark 15 large vision-language models and existing cropping methods on COMEX, establishing a comprehensive testbed for composition-grounded explainable aesthetic cropping. Experiments on both COMEX and prior benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness and transferability of our framework, with strong performance across evaluation metrics.

</details>


### [15] [A Review of Vision-Based Vehicle Detection for UAV-Based Traffic Monitoring: Experimental Insights and Future Directions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07571)
*Jianlin Ye,Christos Kyrkou*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This survey reviews recent advancements in UAV-based traffic monitoring for Intelligent Transportation Systems, focusing on deep neural network models for traffic analytics. It identifies three main challenges: compatibility with traffic control systems, real-time processing, and robust detection across environmental conditions, and suggests future research directions in optimal detection models, edge processing, and adaptive control integration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: UAV-based surveillance offers wide-coverage, real-time traffic monitoring compared to fixed ground infrastructure, but faces challenges in vehicle detection at varying altitudes, motion-induced image variation, and high-resolution image processing. A critical assessment of accuracy, latency, and harmonization with existing transportation systems is needed for practical deployment.

Method: The paper is a survey that reviews recent advancements in deep neural network models for UAV-based traffic analytics in various urban settings, analyzing existing research to identify key challenges and gaps in the literature.

Result: Three main challenges are identified: compatibility with traffic control systems, achieving real-time processing to optimize traffic flow, and maintaining robust detection in different environmental conditions. Existing solutions often lack comprehensive frameworks for utilizing UAV data to respond to incidents and manage traffic effectively.

Conclusion: Future research should focus on optimal detection models, edge processing, and adaptive control integration to improve the responsiveness of urban traffic management.

Abstract: In Intelligent Transportation System (ITS), unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV)-based surveillance offers an innovative solution to traffic surveillance with wide coverage and real-time data collection capabilities. In comparison to fixed ground-based infrastructure, UAVs are able to respond to dynamic traffic but present challenges such as vehicle detection at varying altitudes, compensation for motion-induced image variations and efficient processing of high-resolution images. Deep learning has been largely beneficial on improving the detection accuracy; however, for practical deployment, a critical assessment of the accuracy, latency, and harmonization with current transportation systems needs to be carefully considered. This survey reviews recent advancements in the UAV-based traffic monitoring, with a primary focus being deep neural network models for traffic analytics in various urban settings. Three main challenges identified in the literature are ensuring compatibility with traffic control systems, achieving real-time processing to optimize traffic flow, and maintaining robust detection in different environmental conditions. Existing solutions often lack comprehensive frameworks for utilizing UAV captured data to respond to incidents and manage traffic effectively. Future research should focus on optimal detection models, edge processing, and adaptive control integration to improve the responsiveness of urban traffic management.

</details>


### [16] [BRACE: Taming Sharp Irregularities via Barycentric Rational Forecasting for Fast Diffusion Transformers Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07572)
*Jinlong Yang,Jinke Wu,Lizilin,Yao Zhou*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: BRACE introduces barycentric rational forecasting with Chebyshev enhancement to improve temporal feature caching in Diffusion Transformers, replacing unstable derivative-based polynomial extrapolation with numerically stable feature-driven rational aggregation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing cache-then-forecast methods for DiTs use derivative-based polynomial extrapolation, which causes severe quality degradation under high acceleration due to unstable long-step predictions when features exhibit sharp irregularities and local non-smoothness.

Method: BRACE maintains a local sliding window to cache sparse historical features and uses adapted Chebyshev weights to formulate a barycentric rational function, directly aggregating raw features for numerical stability, shifting from derivative-driven to feature-driven forecasting.

Result: BRACE achieves state-of-the-art quality-efficiency trade-offs across various DiT architectures with negligible computational overhead.

Conclusion: Feature-driven rational forecasting via barycentric rational functions with Chebyshev enhancement provides a more stable and effective solution for accelerating DiT inference than derivative-based polynomial methods.

Abstract: Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) have demonstrated exceptional performance in high-fidelity image and video generation. To alleviate their massive computational overhead, temporal feature caching has been proposed to bypass redundant computations. However, existing cache-then-forecast methods driven by derivative-based polynomials often cause severe quality degradation under high acceleration due to unstable long-step predictions. To address this bottleneck, we propose Barycentric Rational Forecasting with Chebyshev Enhancement (BRACE). Motivated by the observation that DiT feature trajectories are globally smooth yet frequently exhibit sharp irregularities and local non-smoothness, BRACE shifts the paradigm from derivative-driven polynomial extrapolation to feature-driven rational forecasting. Specifically, it maintains a local sliding window to cache sparse historical features and leverages adapted Chebyshev weights to formulate a barycentric rational function, directly aggregating these raw features to ensure numerical stability. Extensive experiments demonstrate that BRACE achieves state-of-the-art quality-efficiency trade-offs across various DiT architectures with negligible computational overhead.

</details>


### [17] [Multimodal Skin Lesion Classification with Swin Transformer and Clinical Metadata Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07574)
*Nethmi Pathirana,Isuru Munasinghe,Dileeka Alwis*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a multimodal skin lesion classification framework combining Swin Transformer image features with clinical metadata, achieving 92.55% accuracy and 91.33% macro F1-score. The model incorporates temperature scaling for calibration, uncertainty estimation, and explainability analysis to provide a trustworthy diagnostic tool.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Skin lesion classification is critical for early skin cancer diagnosis, but automated analysis faces challenges from class imbalance, inter-class similarity, and intra-class variability in dermoscopic images, necessitating a more robust approach.

Method: A multimodal classification framework that fuses Swin Transformer-based image features with structured clinical metadata, enabling integrated visual-context learning. Temperature scaling is applied post-hoc for calibration, and uncertainty estimation is incorporated to assess prediction confidence.

Result: The model achieves 92.55% test accuracy and 91.33% macro F1-score, with strong performance on minority classes. Temperature scaling reduces expected calibration error, improves prediction reliability, and qualitative analysis shows the model focuses on lesion regions during inference.

Conclusion: Multimodal fusion combined with calibration and interpretability analysis provides an effective and trustworthy approach for automated skin lesion classification.

Abstract: Skin lesion classification plays an important role in supporting the early diagnosis of skin cancer. However, automated analysis remains challenging due to class imbalance, inter-class similarity, and intra-class variability in dermoscopic images. This paper proposes a multimodal classification framework that combines Swin Transformer-based image features with structured clinical metadata to improve diagnostic performance through integrated visual-context learning. Experiments on a publicly available dataset show that the proposed model achieves a test accuracy of 92.55% and a macro F1-score of 91.33%, with strong performance across minority classes. Temperature scaling is applied as a post-hoc calibration method, resulting in a reduction in expected calibration error and improving prediction reliability, while uncertainty estimation is incorporated to further assess the confidence of model predictions. Qualitative explainability analysis further shows that the model focuses on lesion regions during inference. Therefore, the results demonstrate that multimodal fusion, combined with calibration and interpretability analysis, provides an effective and trustworthy approach for automated skin lesion classification.

</details>


### [18] [Beyond Isotropic Assumptions: Continuity-Constrained Segmentation and GPU Morphometry for Nanoscale GBM Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07575)
*Arash Fatehi,Robin Ebbestad,Linus Butt,Hans Blom,Sigrid Lundberg,Hannes Olauson,Hjalmar Brismar,David Unnersjö-Jess,Thomas Benzing,Katarzyna Bozek*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A GPU-accelerated, end-to-end framework for segmenting anisotropic 3D confocal microscopy volumes without requiring dense annotations in upsampled isotropic space. It trains on native acquisition volumes using random lateral-plane rotations and a z-axis continuity loss, adapting both 3D U-Net and SwinUNETR backbones, and enables automated 3D morphometry (e.g., GBM thickness measurement) matching inter-expert agreement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Confocal microscopy of cleared/swelled tissue yields highly anisotropic volumes where the axial direction is under-sampled, causing discontinuous structures that hamper reconstruction and quantitative analysis. Standard remedies upsample axially before training segmentation models, which demands dense annotations in the upsampled space—a prohibitive labeling burden.

Method: Trains segmentation models directly on native acquisition volumes without upsampling. Random rotation of training patches exploits the well-resolved lateral plane to supply missing axial information; a z-axis continuity loss maintains neighboring slice consistency. Adapts both a convolutional backbone (3D U-Net) and a transformer backbone (SwinUNETR), aggregates overlapping patches via Gaussian consensus, and computes point-spread-function-corrected membrane thickness using GPU ray-surface intersection.

Result: Applied to the glomerular basement membrane (GBM) in kidney tissue, the segmentation accuracy matches inter-expert agreement. Continuity-aware training improves reconstruction smoothness and suppresses periodic terracing artifacts at minimal accuracy cost. The method captures disease-related GBM thickening, enabling fully automated anisotropic 3D morphometry.

Conclusion: The framework enables fully automated anisotropic 3D morphometry of biological structures without dense volumetric labels or image restoration, offering a practical solution for analyzing thin, convoluted membrane structures in pathological conditions.

Abstract: Confocal microscopy of optically cleared and swelled tissue resolves complex biological structures in 3D, but such acquisitions are highly anisotropic: along the under-sampled axial direction the structure can appear discontinuous, hampering reconstruction and automated quantitative analysis. The usual remedy upsamples the axial dimension to an isotropic volume before training a segmentation model, which requires dense annotations in the upsampled space, a prohibitive labeling burden.
  We present an end-to-end, GPU-accelerated framework that overcomes this without additional annotations. The model is trained on the native acquisition volume; random rotation of training patches leverages the well-resolved lateral plane to supply the missing axial information, and a z-axis continuity loss keeps neighboring slices consistent. We adapt both a convolutional (3D U-Net) and a transformer (SwinUNETR) backbone, aggregate overlapping patches by Gaussian consensus, and compute point-spread-function-corrected membrane thickness by ray-surface intersection on the GPU.
  We apply the method to the glomerular basement membrane (GBM), a thin, highly convoluted part of the kidney's filtration barrier that grows more irregular in disease. Segmentation accuracy matches inter-expert agreement. Continuity-aware training improves reconstruction smoothness and suppresses a periodic terracing artifact at minimal accuracy cost. We quantify GBM thickness across the reconstructed 3D surface and capture disease-related thickening, enabling fully automated anisotropic 3D morphometry of biological structures without dense volumetric labels or image restoration.

</details>


### [19] [Open-World Hierarchical Perception: Taxonomic Abstraction over Class-Agnostic Proposals for the Safe Handling of Out-of-Vocabulary Road Objects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07577)
*Felix Schaller*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper extends hierarchical taxonomic abstraction from closed-set detectors to class-agnostic region proposals, enabling open-world object handling. The approach never emits a confident wrong specific label on out-of-vocabulary objects—achieving zero confident categorical mistakes on held-out classes—though it recovers correct super-categories only 26% of the time, with the rest conservatively flagged as UNKNOWN OBSTACLE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Closed-set detectors are forced to either mislabel or drop objects outside their fixed label set. Prior work addressed this with hierarchical taxonomies but only evaluated on boxes already produced by closed detectors, leaving objects the detector never boxes unhandled. This paper tackles the true open-world setting where objects fall entirely outside the detector's detection and classification capacity.

Method: The authors place taxonomic abstraction on top of class-agnostic region proposals so objects the closed detector never boxes can still be classified or flagged. They conduct a feasibility study of three open-world signals—class-agnostic segmentation, appearance-based out-of-distribution scoring, and monocular depth—demonstrating why no single 2D cue suffices and how they compose. They evaluate on a ground-truth leave-classes-out benchmark using real annotated objects.

Result: On seven held-out COCO classes with 235 ground-truth crops, a flat closed head makes a confident wrong specific label 100% of the time (37% in the wrong super-category), while the hierarchical layer produces zero confident wrong specific labels and safely handles 94% of objects. Correct super-category is recovered only 26% of the time; the remaining 69% are conservatively flagged unknown.

Conclusion: The contribution is an open-world perception layer that never makes a confident categorical mistake on out-of-vocabulary objects, explicitly positioned as a safety result rather than a specificity one, with an honest account of the cost in terms of reduced specificity.

Abstract: A closed-set detector for autonomous driving must assign every object one of a fixed set of labels. On an object outside that set (a horse-drawn carriage, road debris, livestock on a rural road) it can only force a confident but wrong specific label or drop the object. Prior work in this series replaced the flat label set with a hierarchical taxonomy and a runtime abstraction rule, but evaluated it only on the boxes a closed detector already produces. This paper takes the layer open-world: we place taxonomic abstraction on top of class-agnostic region proposals so objects the closed detector never boxes can still be classified or flagged; we report a feasibility study of three open-world signals (class-agnostic segmentation, appearance-based out-of-distribution scoring, monocular depth) that shows why no single 2D cue suffices and how they compose; and we run the evaluation the earlier papers could not, a ground-truth leave-classes-out benchmark on real annotated objects. Holding out seven COCO classes and classifying their 235 ground-truth crops, a flat closed head emits a confident wrong specific label 100% of the time (37% of them in the wrong super-category, e.g. an animal named as a vehicle), whereas the hierarchical layer emits zero confident wrong specific labels and safely handles 94% of the objects (a correct super-category, or an explicit UNKNOWN OBSTACLE). We are explicit that this is a safety result, not a specificity one: the correct super-category is recovered only 26% of the time and the remaining 69% are conservatively flagged unknown. The contribution is an open-world perception layer that never makes a confident categorical mistake on an out-of-vocabulary object, together with an honest account of its cost.

</details>


### [20] [Geometry Beats Estimated Depth: RGB-Only Multi-Camera 3D Tracking under Sim2Real](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07579)
*Abdullah Naeem,Anav Katwal,Ayon Dey,Noman Khan,Md Tamjidul Hoque*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents a controlled comparison of two RGB-only approaches for multi-camera 3D perception in warehouse settings under Sim2Real conditions. A geometry-first pipeline (homography-based lifting with 3D priors) achieves 13.0 3D HOTA, while a pseudo-LiDAR approach using monocular depth collapses to 0.12, demonstrating that cross-view geometric consistency—not monocular depth accuracy—is the critical factor for Sim2Real performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether cross-view geometric consistency or monocular depth accuracy governs multi-camera 3D perception performance under synthetic-to-real transfer, by isolating these two factors through controlled RGB-only pipeline comparison.

Method: Two RGB-only routes are compared: (1) a geometry-first pipeline using YOLO11x detection, homography lifting, class-level 3D size priors, multi-camera fusion, world-coordinate tracking, and offline tracklet stitching; (2) a pseudo-LiDAR pipeline using monocular depth (D4RT, Metric3D v2) back-projected into fused point clouds fed to a 3D detector (V-DETR). Ablations test SAHI detection, appearance Re-ID, learned lifting, RT-DETR ensembling, TTA, and domain randomization.

Result: Geometry-first achieves 13.0 3D HOTA (51.6 LocA), while pseudo-LiDAR collapses to 0.12 (9.2 LocA). The collapse is traced to cross-view inconsistency of monocular depth; scale correction is necessary but insufficient, and domain-adaptation fine-tuning fails within budget. Only offline stitching helps the geometry pipeline; all other interventions fail to beat the baseline. Bottlenecks are complementary: detection quality (DetA) bounds the geometry route, localization consistency (LocA) bounds pseudo-LiDAR.

Conclusion: Cross-view geometric consistency, not monocular depth accuracy, is the governing factor for Sim2Real performance in multi-camera 3D perception. The authors release a complete, reproducible RGB-only pipeline and ablations.

Abstract: The AI City Challenge 2026 Track 1 evaluates multi-camera 3D perception in large indoor warehouses under a synthetic-to-real (Sim2Real) setting; depth is available only for training and validation, so inference is RGB-only. We use two RGB-only routes as a controlled test of one hypothesis: that cross-view geometric consistency, not monocular depth accuracy, governs performance under Sim2Real. The first is a geometry-first pipeline: YOLO11x detection, homography lifting to the world frame, class-level 3D size priors, multi-camera fusion, world-coordinate tracking, and offline tracklet stitching. The second is estimated-depth pseudo-LiDAR: monocular depth (D4RT, Metric3D~v2) back-projected into a fused point cloud and passed to a 3D detector (V-DETR), mirroring prior point-cloud winners that used depth. The gap is decisive: geometry-first reaches 13.0 3D HOTA (51.6 LocA), whereas pseudo-LiDAR collapses to 0.12 (9.2 LocA). We trace the collapse to cross-view inconsistency of monocular depth---scale correction is necessary but not sufficient---which domain-adaptation fine-tuning does not repair within budget. Within the geometry pipeline, offline stitching is the only intervention that helps; SAHI detection, appearance Re-ID, learned lifting, RT-DETR ensembling, test-time augmentation, and domain randomization all fail to beat the baseline detector. The bottlenecks are complementary: detection quality bounds the geometry route (DetA), localization consistency bounds pseudo-LiDAR (LocA). We release a complete, reproducible RGB-only pipeline and ablation.

</details>


### [21] [Real-time physics inversion for retrieval of sub-pixel wildfire temperatures from VSWIR imaging spectroscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07580)
*William R. Keely,Philip G. Brodrick,Katherine Mistick,Adam Chlus,Robert O. Green,Philip E. Dennison*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents a full-physics wildfire temperature retrieval framework for VSWIR imaging spectroscopy, applied to NASA's AVIRIS-3 data. The method uses a forward model with nonlinear least squares optimization on GPU for fast convergence, achieving 41.8 K RMSE on simulated spectra. It was applied across 168 overflights in the 2025 FireSense campaign with residual radiance fits ≤10% in SWIR, and validated for generalization to space-borne spectrometers like EMIT with ~27-30 K errors at coarsened resolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a physically-based, computationally efficient method for estimating effective wildfire fire temperature from airborne VSWIR imaging spectroscopy data, capable of running within flight cadence and generalizing to space-borne instruments.

Method: A full-physics forward model simultaneously resolves solar and emitted radiance from a temperature distribution, using the full spectral range in the residual fit. Nonlinear least squares optimization is implemented on GPU for fast convergence, and validation includes simulated spectra with injected thermal signatures, application to the 2025 FireSense AVIRIS-3 campaign, and coarsened spatial resolution tests for space-borne generalization.

Result: RMSE of 41.8 K on simulated spectra; residual radiance fit ≤10% across SWIR bands for 168 overflights; absolute error of 30 K across quantiles and mean absolute error of 27.16 K between spatial resolutions when generalizing to EMIT-like coarsened data.

Conclusion: The retrieval framework provides accurate and rapid wildfire temperature estimation from AVIRIS-3 data, with validated performance on simulated and real data, and demonstrates potential applicability to space-borne imaging spectrometers such as EMIT.

Abstract: In this work, we present a wildfire temperature retrieval framework for VSWIR imaging spectroscopy data, employed on data from NASA's Airborne Visible Infrared Imaging Spectrometer (AVIRIS-3). The retrieval framework utilizes a full-physics approach in which a forward model is employed to resolve both solar and emitted radiance derived from a temperature distribution and utilizes the full spectral range in the residual fit. To optimize the forward model retrieval, we use state-of-the-art nonlinear least squares methods implemented for fast convergence on the on-board GPU, allowing for estimation of effective fire temperature within flight cadence. We verify the forward model assumptions on simulated spectra with an injected thermal signature and find good agreement with an RMSE of $41.8$ Kelvin (K). We apply the retrieval over the full 2025 FireSense AVIRIS-3 campaign, totaling 168 overflights with probable active fire spectra, and demonstrate a residual radiance fit of $\leq 10\%$ across bands in the short-wave infrared (SWIR). Lastly, we verify the applicability of the retrieved posterior fire temperature parameters to generalize to space-borne imaging spectrometers such as EMIT, by retrieving at coarsened spatial resolution. We find that the posterior distribution exhibits good coverage of the underlying sub-pixel temperature range with an absolute error of $30$ K across quantiles and a mean absolute error of $27.16$ K between spatial resolutions.

</details>


### [22] [Multi-Branch Policy Optimization for Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07581)
*Shuai Lyu,Yuning Gong,Ruiling Gao,Xiaoran Shang,Zhonghong Ou,Ping Zong,Yifan Zhu,Yuan Sun,Yang Qin,Peng Hu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MBPO is a tree-based reinforcement learning framework for multimodal large language models that addresses the inadequacy of uniform trajectory-level credit assignment by building reasoning trees at vision-language decision boundaries, assigning segment-level credit via branch-relative advantages, and using a temporal replay buffer for efficient reuse of informative segments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal reasoning involves higher perceptual uncertainty than text-only settings, requiring repeated visual re-examination and diverse visual hypotheses. Trajectory-level credit assignment with a single advantage for all tokens is inadequate because different visual groundings can lead to divergent reasoning paths, and relative advantages degenerate toward zero.

Method: MBPO constructs reasoning trees at vision-language decision boundaries, allowing sibling branches to explore diverse visual hypotheses. It assigns segment-level credit through branch-relative advantages and introduces a temporal replay buffer to reuse informative segments while controlling policy staleness.

Result: Experiments on multimodal reasoning benchmarks show MBPO outperforms representative baselines, improving both learning signal quality and optimization efficiency.

Conclusion: The tree-based framework with segment-level credit assignment and temporal replay buffer provides a more effective approach to RL training for multimodal LLMs compared to group-based methods using uniform trajectory-level credit.

Abstract: Group-based reinforcement learning methods for multimodal large language models typically rely on trajectory-level credit assignment that applies a single advantage to all tokens in a response. However, multimodal reasoning involves substantially higher perceptual uncertainty than text-only settings, where the model must repeatedly re-examine visual information to verify intermediate interpretations, and different visual groundings can lead to divergent reasoning paths, making such uniform credit assignment particularly inadequate and causing relative advantages to progressively degenerate toward zero. To address these challenges, we propose Multi-Branch Policy Optimization (MBPO), a tree-based framework that constructs reasoning trees at vision-language decision boundaries, enabling sibling branches to explore diverse visual hypotheses and assigning segment-level credit through branch-relative advantages. We further introduce a temporal replay buffer to reuse informative segments while controlling policy staleness. Experiments on several multimodal reasoning benchmarks show that MBPO outperforms representative baselines, improving both learning signal quality and optimization efficiency. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/ShuaiLyu0110/MBPO.

</details>


### [23] [Predictive Failure Detection in Network Hardware Using Thermal Imaging and Deep Learning with Sensor Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07582)
*Ashly Joseph*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents a deep learning-based predictive maintenance framework for network hardware (routers, switches, servers) using thermal imaging and power sensor data. A CNN-LSTM multi-modal fusion model achieved 94% accuracy in classifying equipment states (Normal, Warning, Critical), outperforming single-modality CNN models, with ROI pre-processing significantly boosting performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unplanned network hardware failures cause service interruptions and expensive data center downtime, motivating the need for proactive maintenance strategies that can detect early signs of equipment degradation before failures occur.

Method: The approach combines thermal imaging and power sensor data to classify equipment into three operating states (Normal, Warning, Critical). The study evaluates three ImageNet-pretrained CNNs (ResNet-50, InceptionV3, VGG16) alongside a multi-modal CNN-LSTM fusion model integrating visual and time-series sensor information, with and without ROI extraction and normalization pre-processing.

Result: Without pre-processing, CNNs achieved moderate accuracy (e.g., ResNet-50 at 52%), but ROI-based pre-processing improved ResNet-50 to 91%. The CNN-LSTM fusion model achieved the highest accuracy at 94%, with precision and recall approaching 95%.

Conclusion: Domain-specific pre-processing (ROI extraction) and multi-modal sensor fusion substantially improve early failure prediction in network hardware, offering a viable foundation for proactive, non-intrusive maintenance in data centers.

Abstract: Unplanned network hardware malfunctions can interrupt services and result in expensive downtime in data centers.
