A deep learning-based predictive maintenance strategy is presented that utilizes thermal imaging and power sensor data to detect early indicators of equipment breakdown in routers, switches, and servers. A simulated dataset was generated comprising annotated thermal pictures and power readings indicative of three operating states: Normal, Warning, and Critical. Three ImageNet-pretrained convolutional neural network (CNN) models ResNet-50, InceptionV3, and VGG16 were assessed together with a multi-modal CNN-LSTM fusion model that integrates visual and sensor time-series information. Experiments were performed with and without pre-processing procedures, including region-of-interest (ROI) extraction and normalization. In the absence of pre-processing, CNNs attained moderate accuracy (e.g., ResNet-50 at 52%), but ROI-based pre-processing significantly enhanced performance (ResNet-50 accuracy reaching 91%). The CNN-LSTM model attained the greatest accuracy of 94%, with precision and recall approaching 95%, illustrating the effectiveness of multi-modal fusion. The results validate that domain-specific pre-processing and sensor fusion substantially improve early failure prediction, providing a potential foundation for proactive maintenance of network hardware through non-intrusive monitoring.

</details>


### [24] [ComplexityWorld: Benchmarking Vision-Language Models on Verifiable Visual Decision Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07584)
*Ningxin Pan,Hanyu Li,Yehui Tang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces COMPLEXITYWORLD, a benchmark of 390 tasks across 39 visual worlds and 29 decision categories, designed to evaluate vision-language models on decision-making tasks that require using visual evidence to satisfy global constraints. Results show models perform poorly (below 40% acceptance rate) except GPT-5.6-Sol (75.6%), with performance improving when decision information is given explicitly but still varying across equivalent visual presentations, revealing a persistent visual-to-decision bottleneck.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current VLM benchmarks focus on recognition of image content, but many real-world tasks require models to use visual evidence to make complete decisions that jointly satisfy global constraints. Existing evaluations do not capture this gap between visual perception and complex decision-making.

Method: The authors construct COMPLEXITYWORLD, a benchmark with 390 tasks generated from hidden structured specifications, rendered as visual scenes, and scored by executable verifiers that accept any feasible solution. They evaluate models under direct inference, with explicit structured decision information, and with agent scaffolds across models including GPT-5.6-Sol.

Result: All evaluated models except GPT-5.6-Sol remain below 40% verifier acceptance rate (VAR) under direct inference, while GPT-5.6-Sol reaches 75.6%. Performance substantially improves when decision information is given explicitly in structured form, but varies sharply across equivalent visual presentations. Agent scaffolds provide smaller, model-dependent gains.

Conclusion: There is a persistent visual-to-decision bottleneck in current VLMs that additional inference alone does not remove, indicating a fundamental gap between visual understanding and constraint-satisfying decision-making that remains unresolved.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) have made rapid progress in visual perception and increasingly support real-world tasks that depend on images. Many such tasks, however, require more than rec- ognizing what an image contains: a model must use visual evidence to make a complete decision whose parts jointly satisfy global constraints. We introduce COMPLEXITYWORLD, a benchmark of 390 tasks across 39 domain-inspired visual worlds and 29 decision categories. Each task is generated from a hidden structured specification, rendered as a visual scene, and scored by an exe- cutable verifier that accepts any feasible solution. Under direct inference, all evaluated models ex- cept GPT-5.6-Sol remain below 40% verifier ac- ceptance rate (VAR), while GPT-5.6-Sol reaches 75.6%. Performance improves substantially when the same decision information is made explicit in structured form, yet varies sharply across equiva- lent visual presentations. Agent scaffolds provide smaller, model-dependent gains. Together, these results reveal a persistent visual-to-decision bot- tleneck that additional inference alone does not remove.

</details>


### [25] [LAVE: Latent Visual Evidence-Enhanced Planning for Video Tool-use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07585)
*Zijian Wang,Junnan Zhu,Rongzhen Li,Xiao Liu,Guohui Xiang,Quan Lu,Lijia Liu,Yining Wang,Jiang Zhong,Kaiwen Wei*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LAVE is a training-free framework that reuses latent visual evidence from completed tool calls to overcome the observation bottleneck in video tool-use agents, improving performance on Video-MME, LongVideoBench, and CG-Bench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Video tool-use agents rely on text-only interfaces between Planners and visual Tools, which provide lossy summaries and discard non-verbalized visual evidence, causing important computed information to be unavailable for subsequent planning steps.

Method: LAVE introduces a dual-channel observation interface: a visible channel preserving the original textual trajectory, and a latent channel storing pre-verbal visual updates with Tool roles, source-frame timestamps, and visual locations. During planning, it retrieves evidence relevant to the current Planner state but not covered by text, and integrates it via bounded timestamp-aligned latent updates with entropy-constrained frame-time routing.

Result: Experiments on Video-MME, LongVideoBench, and CG-Bench show consistent improvements across backbones. Under a comparable frame budget, LAVE improves Video-MME overall score by 3.76 points over the strongest baseline.

Conclusion: Latent visual evidence reuse from completed tool calls effectively enhances multi-step video-agent planning without additional training, frame replay, or modifications to the original orchestration.

Abstract: Long-video understanding requires models to efficiently acquire and reuse sparse visual evidence from long and redundant video streams. Recent video tool-use agents address this challenge by iteratively invoking visual Tools at different temporal scales, but their Tool-Planner communication typically relies on textual observations. Such text-only interfaces provide lossy summaries of Tool computations, causing previously computed visual evidence not verbalized to be discarded and unavailable for subsequent planning. We identify this limitation as the Tool observation bottleneck and propose Latent Visual Evidence-Enhanced Planning (LAVE), a training-free framework for reusing latent visual evidence from completed Tool calls. LAVE introduces a dual-channel observation interface: the visible channel preserves the original textual trajectory, while the latent channel stores pre-verbal visual updates with their Tool roles, source-frame timestamps, and visual locations. During planning, LAVE retrieves evidence relevant to the current Planner state but not covered by textual observations, and integrates it through bounded timestamp-aligned latent updates with entropy-constrained frame-time routing. This enables video agents to reuse existing visual computation without additional training, frame replay, or modifications to the original orchestration. Extensive experiments on Video-MME, LongVideoBench, and CG-Bench show that LAVE consistently improves video tool-use agents across backbones. Under a comparable frame budget, LAVE improves the Video-MME overall score by 3.76 points over the strongest baseline, demonstrating the effectiveness of latent visual evidence reuse for multi-step video-agent planning.

</details>


### [26] [MAGIC-SSCIL: Manifold Anchoring and Geometric Incremental Calibration for Semi-Supervised Class Incremental Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07586)
*Yousef Abdi,Mohammad Asadpour,Yousef Seyfari*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MAGIC is a framework for exemplar-free Semi-supervised Class Incremental Learning that stabilizes feature representation without storing past data, using graph-based geometry calibration to generate phantom features and a structural alignment objective to prevent feature drift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SSCIL in the exemplar-free setting is extremely challenging because existing methods suffer from catastrophic forgetting due to feature drift, and pseudo-labels become increasingly unreliable as the label space expands. Current approaches fail most severely when no past data can be stored.

Method: MAGIC consists of two key components: (1) Soft-Weighted Geometry Calibration (SWGC) which uses graph-based label propagation on the plastic feature space to calibrate class means and variances computed on the frozen backbone, then samples phantom features to represent previous task data; (2) Geometric Structural Alignment (GSA) objective that preserves representation topology by matching relational structures between student and teacher heads and aligning feature anchors with fixed classifier prototypes to lock feature space orientation.

Result: Using a frozen ResNet-18 backbone with a learnable plastic adapter, MAGIC improves average incremental accuracy over most supervised CIL methods with FixMatch and native SSCIL baselines across CIFAR-100, CUB-200, and ImageNet-R at label ratios of 1%, 5%, and 10%. The largest gains are observed in the fine-grained, low-label setting where confidence thresholding fails most clearly.

Conclusion: The geometric constraints of MAGIC prevent the adapter from drifting, keeping inter-class geometric relations stable as new classes arrive, thereby addressing catastrophic forgetting in exemplar-free SSCIL settings.

Abstract: Semi-supervised Class Incremental Learning (SSCIL) is a severe challenge for neural networks, and it is hardest in the exemplar-free setting where no past data may be stored. Existing methods forget catastrophically due to feature drift, and their pseudo-labels become increasingly unreliable as the label space grows. In this paper, we propose MAGIC (Manifold Anchoring and Geometric Incremental Calibration), a framework that stabilizes plasticity without storing exemplars. MAGIC's design centers on two components. The first is Soft-Weighted Geometry Calibration (SWGC), which uses graph-based label propagation on the learner's plastic feature space to weight and calibrate class means and variances computed on the frozen backbone; from these calibrated Gaussians, we sample phantom features that stand in for data from previous tasks. The second is a Geometric Structural Alignment (GSA) objective that preserves representation topology by matching the relational structure of student and teacher heads and aligning feature anchors with the fixed classifier prototypes, locking the orientation of the feature space. Together, these constraints keep the adapter from drifting, so geometric relations between classes remain stable as new classes arrive. We implement MAGIC with a frozen ResNet-18 backbone and a learnable plastic adapter. Across CIFAR-100, CUB-200, and ImageNet-R, at label ratios of 1%, 5%, and 10%, MAGIC improves average incremental accuracy over most of the supervised CIL methods equipped with FixMatch and native SSCIL baselines; the largest gains occur in the fine-grained, low-label setting, where confidence thresholding fails most clearly.

</details>


### [27] [NewtonGS: Physics-Structured Object-Level Neural Newtonian Dynamics for Gaussian Scene Animation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07598)
*Lianlei Shan,Feiyang Ye,Yan Chen,Yong Wu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: NewtonGS is a physics-structured framework that provides object-level state rollout and Gaussian scene animation by representing each object with a 22-dimensional state and using a Gaussian Neural Newtonian Dynamics model to predict object motion and transform associated Gaussians.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing dynamic Gaussian methods primarily reconstruct time-varying scenes or simulate deformation rather than providing compact object states for direct control, creating a gap in controllable object-level animation of static 3D Gaussian scenes.

Method: NewtonGS represents each object with a 22-dimensional state (pose, linear/angular velocity, anisotropic scale, mass, contact properties). A Gaussian Neural Newtonian Dynamics (Gaussian-NND) model combines analytic physics equations (translation, quaternion kinematics, gravity, damping, scale-restoration) with learned continuous and contact residuals. A discrete event map handles floor contact. Predicted poses and scales define a shared affine transformation to update means and covariances of all Gaussians per object.

Result: On State-32 datasets (in-distribution and velocity-range-shift splits), NewtonGS achieves lower trajectory RMSE, final displacement error, and velocity RMSE than five analytic baselines. Experiments on Gaussian-32 demonstrate effective conversion from predicted states to animated Gaussian objects.

Conclusion: NewtonGS provides accurate and controllable object-level state rollout and Gaussian scene animation, filling the gap in compact object-state representation for dynamic Gaussian scenes.

Abstract: Animating objects in a static 3D Gaussian scene requires an explicit object-level dynamic state and a controllable model of object motion. Existing dynamic Gaussian methods primarily reconstruct time-varying scenes or simulate deformation, rather than provide compact object states for direct control. To address this gap, we present NewtonGS, a physics-structured framework for object-level state rollout and Gaussian scene animation. NewtonGS represents each object with a 22-dimensional state covering pose, linear and angular velocity, anisotropic scale and its rate, mass, and contact properties. Its Gaussian Neural Newtonian Dynamics (Gaussian-NND) model combines analytic translation, quaternion kinematics, gravity, damping, and scale-restoration dynamics with learned continuous and contact residuals. A discrete event map handles floor contact. Predicted poses and scales define a shared affine transformation that updates the means and covariances of all Gaussians associated with each object. We construct two procedurally generated datasets: State-32 for state-rollout evaluation and Gaussian-32 for state-to-Gaussian transformation. On both the in-distribution and velocity-range-shift splits of State-32, NewtonGS achieves lower trajectory RMSE, final displacement error, and velocity RMSE than five analytic baselines. Experiments on Gaussian-32 further demonstrate effective conversion from predicted states to animated Gaussian objects.

</details>


### [28] [HSMLA: Hierarchical Softmax Multi-scale Linear Attention for Efficient Vision Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07616)
*Dong Liu,Yanxuan Yu,Renata Borovica-Gajic,Ying Nian Wu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: HSMLA is a hierarchical softmax multi-scale linear attention mechanism that combines ReLU-based linear attention for global context, selective softmax refinement for local features, and multi-scale token representations, achieving up to 4.2x inference speedup with competitive accuracy across dense prediction tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision transformers suffer from quadratic computational complexity in self-attention, making them expensive for high-resolution dense prediction. While linear attention is efficient, it sacrifices local context modeling capability.

Method: HSMLA combines three components: (1) ReLU-based linear attention for efficient global context capture, (2) selective softmax refinement to preserve critical local features, and (3) multi-scale token representations via depthwise convolutions.

Result: Achieves up to 4.2x inference-time speedup across dense prediction tasks, 87.3% Dice with 3.2x speedup on CT organ segmentation, and 94.2% AUC with 4.1x speedup on pathology whole-slide images.

Conclusion: HSMLA demonstrates that combining linear attention with selective softmax refinement and multi-scale representations offers a superior accuracy-efficiency trade-off for high-resolution dense prediction in vision transformers.

Abstract: Vision transformers face significant computational overheads in high-resolution dense prediction due to the quadratic complexity of self-attention. Linear attention offers efficiency but sacrifices local context modeling. We propose \textbf{HSMLA (Hierarchical Softmax Multi-scale Linear Attention)}, which combines ReLU-based linear attention for global context, selective softmax refinement for critical local features, and multi-scale token representations via depthwise convolutions. HSMLA achieves superior accuracy-efficiency trade-offs: up to $4.2\times$ inference-time speedup across dense prediction tasks, $87.3%$ Dice with $3.2\times$ speedup on CT organ segmentation, and $94.2%$ AUC with $4.1\times$ speedup on pathology WSI.

</details>


### [29] [FlowErase-OPD: Multi-Concept Erasure via Anchored On-Policy Distillation in Flow Matching Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07620)
*Yi Sun,Yimin Zhou,Xinhao Zhong,Zhiqi Zhang,Junhao Li,Bin Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: FlowErase-OPD is a framework for multi-concept erasure in flow matching text-to-image models, using on-policy distillation with Anchored Multi-Teacher Distillation and Adaptive Retention Control to achieve state-of-the-art performance while preserving generative quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Flow matching models have improved text-to-image quality but raise safety concerns. Existing concept erasure methods only handle individual concepts, and effectively erasing multiple concepts simultaneously remains a challenge due to trade-offs between erasure effectiveness and generative capability preservation.

Method: The framework distills multiple single-concept erased models into a unified LoRA module. It introduces Anchored Multi-Teacher Distillation (AMTD) with a retention teacher to balance erasure and preservation, and Adaptive Retention Control (ARC) to dynamically adjust sampling frequency, loss weights of each erasure teacher, and the relative contribution of erasure vs. retention teachers during training.

Result: Experiments on nudity, object, and artistic-style erasure show consistent improvements in the trade-off between erasure effectiveness, image quality, and semantic alignment, achieving state-of-the-art performance across diverse multi-concept erasure settings. Models also exhibit strong robustness against adversarial attacks.

Conclusion: On-policy distillation serves as a principled framework for safe and controllable generation in flow matching models, effectively addressing multi-concept erasure while preserving image quality and robustness.

Abstract: Recent advances in flow matching models have substantially improved the quality of text-to-image generation, but have also raised increasing safety concerns due to their potential to generate harmful or undesirable content. Existing concept erasure methods for flow matching models predominantly focus on removing individual concepts, while effectively erasing multiple concepts simultaneously remains challenging. We propose FlowErase-OPD, a framework for multi-concept erasure based on on-policy distillation (OPD). Our approach first distills multiple single-concept erased models into a unified LoRA module and introduces Anchored Multi-Teacher Distillation (AMTD), which incorporates a retention teacher to mitigate the trade-off between concept erasure and preservation of generative capabilities. To further improve the coordination of multiple erasure objectives, we develop Adaptive Retention Control (ARC), which dynamically adjusts the sampling frequency and loss weight of each erasure teacher, together with the relative contribution of erasure and retention teachers throughout training. Extensive experiments on nudity, object, and artistic-style erasure demonstrate that FlowErase-OPD consistently improves the trade-off between erasure effectiveness, image quality, and semantic alignment, achieving state-of-the-art performance across diverse multi-concept erasure settings. Furthermore, the resulting models exhibit strong robustness against adversarial attacks. These results highlight the potential of on-policy distillation as a principled framework for safe and controllable generation in flow matching models.

</details>


### [30] [HeatCast: A Benchmark for Neighborhood-Scale LST Forecasting across 124 U.S. Cities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07640)
*Jesus Guerrero,Isaac Corley,Leon Najafirad,Maryam Tabar,Paul Rad*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: HeatCast is a new benchmark for monthly Land Surface Temperature (LST) forecasting at 30 m resolution across 124 U.S. cities (2013–June 2025), containing 30 m monthly tiles with LST, elevation, surface reflectance RGB, spectral indices, albedo, quality masks, and Local Climate Zone labels. A CNN+LSTM and Earthformer are evaluated for next-month forecasting, with Earthformer achieving 7.74 K RMSE vs. 10.42 K for CNN+LSTM.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is no shared benchmark for LST forecasting at 30 m resolution; prior studies cover only one to three cities, use kilometer-scale products, or fail to release data and code.

Method: The authors introduce HeatCast, a Landsat-based benchmark with 30 m monthly LST tiles across 124 U.S. cities, including auxiliary channels (elevation, RGB, spectral indices, albedo, quality masks, LCZ labels), a fixed temporal split, LCZ-stratified metrics, and a reference evaluation harness. They evaluate a CNN+LSTM model and Earthformer for next-month forecasting.

Result: Earthformer reaches 7.74 K RMSE vs. 10.42 K for CNN+LSTM. Forecasting from the eight non-LST channels alone reaches 7.72 K, outperforming LST history (8.15 K) and RGB (8.68 K).

Conclusion: The authors conclude that HeatCast provides an open, standardized benchmark for 30 m LST forecasting, and that using non-LST auxiliary channels leads to competitive or better predictive performance than LST history alone. All data, code, and weights are released under MIT license.

Abstract: Land Surface Temperature (LST) is a widely used satellite-derived measure of urban surface heat, but there is no shared benchmark for forecasting it at 30 m. Prior studies usually cover one to three cities, use kilometer-scale products, or do not release data and code. We introduce HeatCast, a Landsat-based benchmark for monthly LST forecasting across 124 U.S. cities from 2013 through June 2025. HeatCast contains 30 m monthly tiles with LST, elevation, surfacereflectance RGB, three spectral indices, broadband albedo, quality masks, and Local Climate Zone (LCZ) labels, together with a fixed temporal split, LCZ-stratified metrics, and a reference evaluation harness. We evaluate a CNN+LSTM and Earthformer on next-month forecasting, where Earthformer reaches 7.74 K RMSE against 10.42 K for the CNN+LSTM. Forecasting from the eight nonLST channels alone reaches 7.72 K, against 8.15 K from LST history and 8.68 K from RGB. The data, code, and weights are released under MIT at https://doi.org/10.57967/hf/9889.

</details>


### [31] [Data collection from highways: a geometric, class-agnostic approach to embedded vehicle counting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07643)
*Lucas Gouveia Omena Lopes,William W. M. Lira,Alexandre M. Lima,Thales M. A. Vieira*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A purely geometric, class-agnostic traffic counting pipeline for Single Board Computers that uses background subtraction and an imaginary inductive loop line, achieving 83-100% accuracy without any object model, training data, or per-object trajectories, and running faster than real-time on Raspberry Pi hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning object detectors followed by tracking-by-detection dominate traffic data collection, but presuppose a detector trained on the classes one wants to count—often missing in practice. The authors revisit a class-agnostic geometric approach as a simpler, cheaper alternative usable on resource-constrained edge hardware.

Method: A class-agnostic detection pipeline: background subtraction and thresholding for moving objects, followed by a geometric counting rule based on an imaginary line across the road (software inductive loop detector). Two counting rules are described: a constant average speed rule (analytic accuracy ~86% under Gaussian speed distribution) and a self-calibrating pre-calibration rule that recovers lane geometry from blob statistics and counts lane occupancy edges, yielding per-vehicle average speed as a byproduct.

Result: Over four videos the self-calibrating rule achieves 83.3%-100% accuracy; field deployment reaches 91% vs 37.5% for a blob-tracking baseline at the same compute budget. Findings include a resolution floor below which accuracy collapses, a frame rate floor where vehicles alias past the counting line, performance gaps between curated clips and long uncontrolled footage, and CPU trade-offs: Python (easier to tune, 100% CPU) vs C++ (40% CPU, thermally viable).

Conclusion: Motion-based, class-agnostic detection remains the right tool for open-set classes with no annotated data, tight power budgets, privacy-constrained installations, and bootstrapping training crops to cold-start a learned detector. These limitations and trade-offs are properties of sampling geometry, not of older hardware, and still constrain edge deployments today.

Abstract: Traffic data collection is dominated today by deep object detectors followed by tracking-by-detection, a pipeline that presupposes what is often missing in practice: a detector already trained on the class one wants to count. We revisit a purely geometric traffic-sensing pipeline for Single Board Computers in which detection is class-agnostic: moving objects come from background subtraction and thresholding, and counting is decided by a geometric rule on an imaginary line across the road, a software inductive loop detector. With no object model, training set or per-object trajectory, it runs faster than real time on Raspberry Pi class hardware. Two counting rules are described: a constant average speed rule, whose expected accuracy is derived analytically as about 86% under a Gaussian speed distribution, and a self-calibrating pre-calibration rule that recovers the lane geometry from blob statistics and counts edges of lane occupancy, additionally yielding per-vehicle average speed at no extra cost. Over four videos the latter counts with 83.3%-100% accuracy; in a field deployment it reaches 91% against 37.5% for a blob-tracking baseline under the same compute budget. We report the observations of that period in detail: the resolution floor below which accuracy collapses, the frame rate floor at which vehicles alias past the counting line, the gap between short curated clips and long uncontrolled footage, and the trade-off between Python (easier to tune, 100% CPU) and C++ (40% CPU, thermally viable). These are properties of the sampling geometry, not of the hardware of the time, and still constrain edge deployments. We close by arguing where motion-based, class-agnostic detection remains the right tool: open-set classes with no annotated data, tight power budgets, privacy-constrained installations, and the cold start of mining training crops to bootstrap a learned detector.

</details>


### [32] [Keep It Simple: Multi-Key Episodic Memory Retrieval for Ultra-Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07663)
*Yeeun Choi,Youngbeom Yoo,Joon-Young Lee,Hyolim Kang,Seon Joo Kim*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MERIT is a simple agentic framework for ultra-long video understanding that prioritizes high-recall retrievability during memory construction and defers query-specific relational composition to inference time, using episodic multi-key representations and inference-time temporal expansion to achieve SOTA results on three long-video benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Processing ultra-long videos (hours to days) end-to-end is impractical for current MLLMs, requiring a two-stage query-agnostic memory construction + retrieval-based inference paradigm. Prior work over-invests in complex memory construction to pre-model high-level relations without knowing downstream queries, which is inefficient. The key insight is to prioritize high-recall retrievability during memory building and defer query-specific relation composition to inference time.

Method: MERIT introduces two key components: (1) an episodic multi-key representation that enables precise retrieval of fine-grained memories via simple key-matching, and (2) a neighbor filtering mechanism that expands temporal scope only around retrieved segments at inference time, capturing broader semantic context without the massive computational cost of global memory construction.

Result: MERIT achieves state-of-the-art performance across three long-video benchmarks: EgoLifeQA, LVBench, and Video-MME (Long).

Conclusion: Simple key-matching with on-demand temporal expansion is sufficient and superior to complex global memory construction for ultra-long video understanding, demonstrating that deferring high-level relation composition to inference time is a more effective strategy.

Abstract: When videos extend from hours to days, directly processing them end-to-end becomes impractical for current Multi-modal Large Language Models (MLLMs). This ultra-long setting necessitates a two-stage paradigm: query-agnostic memory construction followed by retrieval-based inference. Prior work invests in complex memory construction to pre-model high-level relations in videos, despite not knowing the downstream query at build time. We instead prioritize high-recall retrievability during memory building, and defer query-specific, high-level relation composition to inference time. To this end, we propose MERIT(Multi-key Episodic Retrieval with Inference-time Temporal expansion), a simple yet effective agentic framework for ultra-long video understanding. First, we formulate an episodic multi-key representation that enables precise retrieval of fine-grained memories through a simple key-matching mechanism. Second, we introduce a neighbor filtering mechanism to capture broader semantic context without the massive computational overhead of global memory construction. This is achieved by expanding the temporal scope exclusively around the retrieved segments at inference time. By leveraging simple key-matching with this on-demand temporal expansion, MERIT achieves state-of-the-art performance across three long-video benchmarks: EgoLifeQA, LVBench, and Video-MME (Long).

</details>


### [33] [CosmosAlign: Adapting a World Foundation Model for Generative Traffic Video Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07693)
*Quang Minh Dinh,Tuan Kiet Doan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CosmosAlign is a generative traffic video forecasting framework built on the pretrained Cosmos3-Nano world foundation model. It uses a two-stage LoRA adaptation strategy for distribution alignment rather than increasing model capacity, plus training-free inference improvements (medoid sample selection and motion-adaptive blending). It achieves first place with a score of 76.49 on the AI City Challenge 2026 Track 5 benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The key insight is that adapting large pretrained world models to downstream forecasting tasks depends primarily on distribution alignment rather than increased model capacity. This motivates a focused alignment strategy rather than scaling up the model.

Method: A two-stage LoRA adaptation strategy: (1) aligns the conditioning-mode distribution with the target forecasting task, and (2) aligns training captions with the model's native structured prompting interface via an LLM-based re-captioning pipeline. During inference, a fully training-free procedure using consensus-based medoid sample selection and motion-adaptive blending of static scene regions improves prediction quality.

Result: CosmosAlign achieves a final score of 76.49 on the AI City Challenge 2026 Track 5 benchmark, ranking first on the final leaderboard.

Conclusion: Distribution alignment, through targeted LoRA adaptation and caption re-captioning, is more effective than scaling model capacity for adapting pretrained world models to traffic video forecasting. The training-free inference refinements further boost performance.

Abstract: Generative traffic video forecasting aims to synthesize long-horizon, temporally coherent future videos of traffic scenes from a short observation history and textual descriptions. In this paper, we present CosmosAlign, a generative traffic video forecasting framework built upon the pretrained Cosmos3-Nano world foundation model. Our approach is motivated by the observation that successfully adapting large pretrained world models to downstream forecasting tasks depends primarily on distribution alignment rather than increased model capacity. To this end, we propose a two-stage LoRA adaptation strategy that first aligns the conditioning-mode distribution with the target forecasting task, and then aligns the training captions with the model's native structured prompting interface through an LLM-based re-captioning pipeline. During inference, we further improve prediction quality using a fully training-free procedure consisting of consensus-based medoid sample selection and motion-adaptive blending of static scene regions. CosmosAlign achieves a final score of 76.49 on the AI City Challenge 2026 Track 5 benchmark, ranking first on the final leaderboard. Our code is publicly available at https://quangminhdinh.github.io/CosmosAlign/.

</details>


### [34] [SpikeWorld: Fast-State Adaptation for Frozen Spiking World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07712)
*Ziqiao Yu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SpikeWorld is a 1.45M-parameter sparse spiking model that jointly handles sensory prediction, semantics, image-text binding, and action-conditioned dynamics. It uses a novel post-deployment adaptation scheme where all parameters remain frozen, but delayed next-state residuals update two external paths: cumulative fixed-bank losses for action correction and route-specific residual matrices for next-state prediction. This approach requires no labels, rewards, or true shift values. The method improves action next-state MSE by 17.10%, multimodal prediction, semantic accuracy, and image-text retrieval. On held-out shear/attenuation streams it improves aggregate prediction by 5.48%/30.01% and tracking by 24.20%/3.94%. In a six-arm Meta-World study (450 trajectories), SpikeWorld boosts frozen-policy reward by 7.90 (95% CI [2.48, 14.06]).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-supervised signals from observing action consequences are valuable after deployment, but are difficult to exploit when dynamics and semantics share parameters. Freezing prevents adaptation while weight updates require optimizer state and risk altering learned representations. The paper addresses this tension by seeking a way to adapt at deployment without modifying the trained model or requiring external supervision.

Method: SpikeWorld is a 1.45M-parameter sparse spiking model jointly trained for heterogeneous sensory prediction, semantics, image-text binding, and action-conditioned dynamics. At deployment, all trained parameters are frozen. Adaptation is achieved via two external paths: (1) cumulative fixed-bank losses select bounded action corrections, and (2) route-specific residual matrices refine next-state prediction. Notably, a 16-byte RLS (recursive least squares) estimator is used. No labels, teacher outputs, rewards, success signals, or true shift values are required.

Result: Joint optimization improves action next-state MSE by 17.10% while also improving multimodal prediction, semantic accuracy, and image-text retrieval. On held-out shear and attenuation streams, the combined external state improves aggregate prediction by 5.48% and 30.01%, and its fixed-bank action path improves tracking by 24.20% and 3.94% respectively. In a six-arm study with 450 new Meta-World trajectories (75 per arm), SpikeWorld raises frozen-policy reward by 7.90 (95% CI [2.48, 14.06]), with a 13.33-point descriptive success difference (CI [0, 40]). For identical sensory inputs, model parameters and inherited semantic outputs remain bitwise unchanged.

Conclusion: The contribution is not superior linear identification (as evidenced by the 16-byte RLS estimator achieving the highest non-oracle reward on linear attenuation), but rather the integration of external adaptive paths with a frozen multimodal spiking checkpoint. The approach enables post-deployment adaptation without compromising the integrity of the trained model, setting it apart from linear identification baselines and demonstrating strong empirical gains across prediction and control benchmarks.

Abstract: A predictive model receives a self-supervised signal whenever the consequence of an action is observed. Using that signal after deployment is difficult when dynamics and semantics share parameters: freezing prevents adaptation, whereas weight updates require optimizer state and may alter the learned representation. Here we introduce SpikeWorld, a 1.45M-parameter sparse spiking model jointly trained for heterogeneous sensory prediction, semantics, image-text binding and action-conditioned dynamics. At deployment, all trained parameters are frozen. Delayed next-state residuals update two external paths: cumulative fixed-bank losses select the bounded action correction, while route-specific residual matrices refine next-state prediction. Neither path uses labels, teacher outputs, rewards, success signals or the true shift value. Joint optimization improves action next-state MSE by 17.10\% while also improving multimodal prediction, semantic accuracy and image-text retrieval. On held-out shear and attenuation streams, the combined external state improves aggregate prediction by 5.48\% and 30.01\%; its fixed-bank action path improves tracking by 24.20\% and 3.94\%, respectively. In a six-arm study comprising 450 new Meta-World trajectories (75 per arm), SpikeWorld raises frozen-policy reward by 7.90 (95\% CI [2.48, 14.06]); the 13.33-point success difference is descriptive (CI [0, 40]). For identical sensory inputs, model parameters and inherited semantic outputs remain bitwise unchanged. A 16-byte RLS estimator obtains the highest non-oracle reward on linear attenuation, showing that the contribution is not superior linear identification, but its integration with a frozen multimodal spiking checkpoint. Reference code is publicly available at https://github.com/Oooorca/SpikeWorld.

</details>


### [35] [Tokenizer Generator Coupling in Medical Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07713)
*Liam Chalcroft*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether treating the latent tokenizer as fixed preprocessing in medical image generation is valid. Through controlled ChestMNIST experiments at 64x64 resolution, the authors show that rankings of tokenizers, generators, and samplers are interdependent—the best quantizer depends on the generator, and sampler selection changes apparent generator rankings. They introduce a generator-free statistic (neighbour-conditional predictive gain) that separates quantizer families by downstream generation quality, and demonstrate that retuning diffusion samplers (D3PM, SE-D3PM) on LFQ-1024 dramatically improves FID scores. All claims are scoped to low-resolution medical-style images with non-clinical FID-based metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether the common practice of treating latent medical image tokenizers as fixed preprocessing is valid, and to understand how tokenizer, generator, and sampler choices interact in latent medical image generation.

Method: Controlled ChestMNIST study at 64x64 with a shared latent grid, crossing discrete tokenizers (including LFQ-1024), generator families (D3PM, SE-D3PM and others), and sampler settings. Includes retraining interaction blocks at three seeds, introduces a generator-free statistic (neighbour-conditional predictive gain), and uses rate-distortion-modelability framing for interpretation. Metrics include FID-192, standard FID-2048, and label-free classifier two-sample tests.

Result: Tokenizers, generators, and samplers jointly determine rankings: best quantizer changes with generator, sampler selection changes apparent generator ranking. The interaction survives across seeds (6 of 9 pairwise quantizer comparisons exceed three seed standard deviations). Reconstruction PSNR is unreliable for selection; neighbour-conditional predictive gain achieves rank-AUC 1.00 in separating quantizer families. Retuning D3PM and SE-D3PM on LFQ-1024 improves FID-192 from 0.44/0.41 to 0.09/0.10 at lower NFE. FID-192 ranks consistently with FID-2048 (Spearman 0.80) and label-free classifier two-sample tests (0.78).

Conclusion: The separation of tokenizer and generator is not valid in latent medical image generation; their interactions matter. Reconstruction metrics alone are insufficient for quantizer selection, and a generator-free predictive-gain statistic is proposed as a better selection criterion. Results are interpreted through a rate-distortion-modelability framing where modelability is conditional on generator, sampler, and inference budget. All claims are scoped to 64x64 unconditional ChestMNIST with non-clinical FID-based metrics.

Abstract: Latent medical image generators usually treat the tokenizer as fixed preprocessing. We test whether this separation is valid in a controlled ChestMNIST study at 64x64, crossing discrete tokenizers, generator families, and sampler settings under a shared latent grid, with continuous-latent reference cells. In this controlled setting, rankings depend jointly on the tokenizer, generator, and sampler: the best quantizer changes with the generator, and validation-based sampler selection changes the apparent generator ranking. We retrain the vocabulary-1024 interaction block at three seeds and the interaction survives (6 of 9 pairwise quantizer comparisons exceed three seed standard deviations), and we scope the wider single-seed grid accordingly. Reconstruction PSNR alone is not a reliable selection criterion; we instead introduce a generator-free statistic, neighbour-conditional predictive gain, that separates the quantizer families by downstream generation quality (rank-AUC 1.00) where reconstruction PSNR and marginal token entropy do not. On LFQ-1024, retuning D3PM and SE-D3PM (selected on a held-out validation split) moves them from default FID-192 0.44/0.41 to 0.09/0.10 at lower NFE, replicated across seeds; the continuous references were not given an equivalent sampler sweep. We report FID-192 as an internal ranking metric; it ranks consistently with standard FID-2048 (Spearman 0.80) and with a label-free classifier two-sample test (0.78). We interpret these results through a rate-distortion-modelability framing, where modelability is conditional on the generator, sampler, and inference budget. All experiments are at 64x64 on low-resolution medical-style images, unconditional, and evaluated with non-clinical FID-based metrics, and we scope every claim to that setting. Code: https://github.com/liamchalcroft/medtokenizers and https://github.com/liamchalcroft/medlatents.

</details>


### [36] [Vision Meets WiFi: Physics-Grounded Estimation of Volumetric Mechanical Properties](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07726)
*Ali Bahri,Hongliang Li,Soufiane Lamghari,Jie Chuai,Zhitang Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ViWi is an object-centric framework that estimates volumetric mechanical properties (Young's modulus, Poisson's ratio, density) by representing objects as compact material slots that aggregate voxel evidence and incorporate WiFi-band RF descriptors from electromagnetic simulation to resolve visual ambiguity, improving per-voxel and mass-estimation metrics over prior state of the art.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Estimating volumetric mechanical properties from vision alone is intrinsically ambiguous since visually similar objects can have different material compositions and physical behavior. Existing voxel-independent prediction methods overlook the piecewise-constant material structure of real objects, producing noisy/inconsistent estimates for voxels sharing the same material and lacking an explicit mechanism to resolve visual ambiguity.

Method: ViWi represents each object using a compact set of material slots that aggregate evidence from voxels sharing material identity and produce coherent slot-level property predictions. It incorporates a compact RF descriptor generated through WiFi-band electromagnetic simulation using permittivity and conductivity. The RF descriptor conditions material slots with global composition cues unavailable from images, while visual features preserve voxel-level spatial localization.

Result: Across volumetric mechanical-property and mass-estimation benchmarks, ViWi improves over the prior state of the art on four of six per-voxel metrics, and its vision-only variant improves all mass-estimation metrics.

Conclusion: Combining object-centric material structure with complementary RF evidence enables more accurate and physically coherent volumetric property estimation beyond what is possible from visual appearance alone.

Abstract: Estimating volumetric mechanical properties, including Young's modulus, Poisson's ratio, and density at each voxel, is intrinsically ambiguous from vision alone, as visually similar objects may have substantially different material compositions and physical behavior. Existing approaches predict these properties independently across voxels, overlooking the piecewise-constant material structure of real objects and producing noisy or inconsistent estimates for voxels that share the same material, while lacking an explicit mechanism to resolve visual ambiguity. We introduce ViWi (Vision Meets WiFi), an object-centric framework for volumetric mechanical-property estimation. ViWi represents each object using a compact set of material slots that aggregate evidence from voxels with a shared material identity and produce coherent slot-level property predictions. To complement visual appearance, ViWi incorporates a compact RF descriptor generated through WiFi-band electromagnetic simulation using permittivity and conductivity. The RF descriptor conditions the material slots with global composition cues that may be unavailable from images, while visual features preserve voxel-level spatial localization. Across volumetric mechanical-property and mass-estimation benchmarks, ViWi improves over the prior state of the art on four of six per-voxel metrics, while its vision-only variant improves all mass-estimation metrics. These results demonstrate that combining object-centric material structure with complementary RF evidence enables more accurate and physically coherent volumetric property estimation beyond what is possible from visual appearance alone.

</details>


### [37] [Ghost Features and Spooky Transfer Learning for Hypercomplex-Valued Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07735)
*Guilherme Vieira Neto,Marcos Eduardo Valle*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper introduces hypercomplex-valued neural network layers where the real part corresponds to a traditional real-valued layer's output, while additional imaginary parts produce "ghost features" containing enhanced information. These ghost features can be integrated into trained networks via "spooky transfer learning," leading to more efficient neural networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hypercomplex numbers offer algebraic and geometrical properties beyond real-valued representations that can benefit machine learning, but their potential for enhancing standard neural network layers has not been fully explored.

Method: The authors construct hypercomplex-valued neural network layers in which the real component matches the output of a standard real-valued layer, while the imaginary components generate supplementary "ghost features." They introduce "spooky transfer learning" to integrate these ghost features into already-trained networks.

Result: The ghost features provide enhanced information not present in real-valued outputs, and their integration results in more efficient neural networks.

Conclusion: Hypercomplex-valued layers with ghost features offer a promising approach to augment neural networks with additional representational richness while maintaining compatibility with traditional real-valued architectures.

Abstract: Hypercomplex numbers extend the concept of complex numbers by introducing additional imaginary components. Besides increasing dimensionality, operations on the imaginary parts provide algebraic and geometrical properties that can be beneficial for solving machine learning problems. In this paper, we show how to create hypercomplex-valued neural network layers where the real part corresponds to the output of a traditional real-valued layer. The additional imaginary parts of these hypercomplex-valued layers produce what we call ``ghost features,'' which contain enhanced information that is not present in the output of the real-valued layer. Moreover, ghost features can be effectively integrated into a trained neural network through a process we refer to as ``spooky transfer learning.'' This approach allows us to harness the richness of ghost features, leading to more efficient neural networks. The source code and Jupyter Notebook are available at https://github.com/mevalle/v-nets/.

</details>


### [38] [BRUCE: Benchmarking Robustness Under Corruption Escalation for Scientific Vision-Language Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07742)
*Saim Rehman,Muhammad Shafique*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: BRUCE is a benchmarking framework that evaluates the robustness of vision-language models under escalating image corruptions (e.g., blur, low contrast) for scientific reasoning tasks, introducing two novel metrics (RCI and T-RCI) to quantify reasoning degradation, and providing interpretable failure analysis across four reasoning domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLMs struggle with robustness in real-world scenarios due to low- or varying-quality input images, yet existing evaluation frameworks primarily focus on clean-task accuracy and rarely analyze how reasoning stability degrades across robustness dimensions.

Method: BRUCE applies a wide range of input perturbations/distortions to images and employs two novel metrics—Robustness Corruption Index (RCI) and Traversal-RCI (T-RCI)—to quantify how rapidly multimodal reasoning performance deteriorates as visual corruption severity increases under progressive perturbation scaling. Additionally, it analyzes corruption-induced prediction failures across four high-level reasoning domains: OCR-dependent reasoning, spatial reasoning, symbolic reasoning, and semantic failures, with fine-grained failure subtypes.

Result: The framework is evaluated across chemistry and mathematical reasoning tasks using multiple datasets, providing interpretable failure analysis by categorizing prediction failures into detailed reasoning-domain-specific subtypes.

Conclusion: BRUCE offers a comprehensive and interpretable approach to benchmarking VLM robustness under progressive image corruption, enabling detailed understanding of how and why multimodal reasoning degrades under real-world visual challenges.

Abstract: Visual-language models (VLMs) frequently struggle with robustness issues in real-world situations due to low- or varying-quality input images. In this paper, we aim at analyzing VLMs' robustness by applying perturbations and distortions to the input images, such as blur or low contrast. Toward this goal, we propose BRUCE (Benchmarking Robustness Under Corruption Escalation, a multimodal reasoning fragility framework for scientific vision-language reasoning.
  State-of-the-art evaluation frameworks/studies primarily focus on clean-task accuracy and rarely analyze how reasoning stability degrades across robustness dimensions. Besides varying over a wide-range of input perturbations, BRUCE employs two novel metrics -- Robustness Corruption Index (RCI) and Traversal-RCI (T-RCI) -- to quantify how rapidly multimodal reasoning performance deteriorates in VLMs as visual corruption severity increases under progressive perturbation scaling. We evaluate BRUCE across chemistry and mathematical reasoning tasks for multiple datasets, while analyzing corruption-induced prediction failures in terms of four high-level reasoning domains: OCR-dependent reasoning, spatial reasoning, symbolic reasoning, and semantic failures, with each containing fine-grained corruption specific failure subtypes, thereby enabling an interpretable failure analysis.

</details>


### [39] [LoRSA: Toward Generalizable Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning for Biomedical Downstream Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07749)
*Saed Moradi,Benyamin Ghojogh,M. Hadi Sepanj,Yimin Yang,Ashirbani Saha*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LoRSA is a global-residual adaptation framework for parameter-efficient fine-tuning of vision foundation models in biomedical tasks. It jointly learns a dense low-rank component (capturing global task structure) and a dynamically structured-sparse low-rank component (providing residual corrections), enabling better generalization to unseen imaging domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A single low-rank update in parameter-efficient fine-tuning constrains all task-specific changes to one narrow parameter subspace, which may prevent the model from simultaneously representing globally shared task structure and localized residual directions needed for generalization to unseen imaging domains.

Method: LoRSA jointly learns two complementary components: (1) a dense low-rank component capturing globally coordinated task adaptation, and (2) a dynamically structured-sparse low-rank component providing residual corrections whose support evolves during training. The framework is characterized through representational capacity, approximation properties, rank structure, and singular-subspace complementarity.

Result: Evaluated on four-class breast-density classification with DINOv3-Base using VinDr-Mammo as source domain and MammosighTR and RSNA as unseen domains. LoRSA remains competitive internally and achieves best external macro-F1, improving by 2.15 and 3.09 percentage points over the strongest competing method on the two target datasets. Weight-matrix analysis shows ~92% energy of each component lies outside the bilateral singular subspace of the other.

Conclusion: Organizing adaptation capacity into distinct global and residual paths can improve external-domain generalization of parameter-efficiently adapted biomedical vision models, with the two learned components being largely complementary in their update directions.

Abstract: Parameter-efficient fine-tuning enables the adaptation of vision foundation models to biomedical tasks under limited computational resources, but a single low-rank update can constrain all task-specific changes to one narrow parameter subspace. This restriction may prevent the model from simultaneously representing globally shared task structure and localized residual directions required for generalization to unseen imaging domains. We introduce LoRSA, a global--residual adaptation framework that jointly learns a dense low-rank component and a dynamically structured-sparse low-rank component. The dense component captures globally coordinated task adaptation, while the structured component provides complementary residual corrections whose support evolves during training. We characterize the representational capacity, approximation properties, rank structure, and singular-subspace complementarity of this decomposition. We evaluate LoRSA for four-class breast-density classification using DINOv3-Base, with VinDr-Mammo as the source domain and MammosighTR and RSNA as unseen external domains. LoRSA remains competitive on the internal validation set and achieves the best external macro-F1 on both target datasets, improving upon the strongest competing method by 2.15 percentage points on MammosighTR and 3.09 percentage points on RSNA. Weight-matrix analysis further shows that approximately $92\%$ of the energy of each adaptation component lies outside the bilateral singular subspace of the other, indicating that the two components learn largely complementary update directions. These results suggest that organizing adaptation capacity into distinct global and residual paths can improve the external-domain generalization of parameter-efficiently adapted biomedical vision models.

</details>


### [40] [Multi-Task Consistency-based Detection of Adversarial Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07750)
*Cong Chen,Jean-Philippe Monteuuis,Jonathan Petit*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes an efficient adversarial attack detection scheme that leverages multi-task perception in vision systems, detecting attacks by measuring inconsistencies between outputs of multiple vision tasks (e.g., object detection and instance segmentation). The approach achieves 99.9% ROC-AUC detection against PGD attacks on the BDD100k dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep Neural Networks are vulnerable to adversarial attacks, which raises concerns for safety-critical applications like autonomous driving. Existing defense methods are often cost-inefficient and impractical for resource-constrained deployments.

Method: The method detects adversarial perturbations by exploiting inconsistencies between inference outputs of multiple vision tasks within a complex vision system. A consistency score metric is developed to measure inter-task inconsistency, and an approach is designed to select the best model pairs for effective inconsistency detection.

Result: The defense was evaluated against PGD attacks across multiple vision models on the BDD100k validation dataset, achieving a ROC-AUC performance of 99.9% detection within the considered attacker model.

Conclusion: A cost-efficient adversarial detection scheme based on multi-task perception inconsistency is effective and practical for resource-constrained applications such as autonomous driving, demonstrating near-perfect detection performance.

Abstract: Deep Neural Networks (DNNs) have found successful deployment in numerous vision perception systems. However, their susceptibility to adversarial attacks has prompted concerns regarding their practical applications, specifically in the context of autonomous driving. Existing defenses often suffer from cost inefficiency, rendering their deployment impractical for resource-constrained applications. In this work, we propose an efficient and effective adversarial attack detection scheme leveraging the multi-task perception within a complex vision system. Adversarial perturbations are detected by the inconsistencies between the inference outputs of multiple vision tasks, e.g., object detection and instance segmentation. To this end, we developed a consistency score metric to measure the inconsistency between vision tasks. Next, we designed an approach to select the best model pairs for detecting inconsistencies effectively. Finally, we evaluated our defense against PGD attacks across multiple vision models on the BDD100k validation dataset. The experimental results demonstrated that our defense achieved a ROC-AUC performance of 99.9% detection within the considered attacker model.

</details>


### [41] [Rethinking 3D Segmentation from Individual LiDAR Scans: Incidence-Aware Sampling on the SIP Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07757)
*Seongyong Kim,Jingdao Chen,Yong Kwon Cho*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses 3D scene understanding in construction by evaluating how sampling resolution and point allocation affect segmentation under a fixed per-fragment point budget. It introduces an incidence-aware sampling strategy for individual LiDAR scans that maps points to a geometry-normalized manifold space for voxel-based selection while preserving Euclidean coordinates. Experiments on the Site in Pieces benchmark with Point Transformer and PointNeXt show improved segmentation performance, especially for non-planar elements and ladders, and reduced sensitivity to sampling resolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most 3D scene understanding methods for construction are developed on curated datasets that don't reflect real site sensing conditions. Individual LiDAR scans have limited surface coverage, acquisition-driven density variation, and severe class imbalance. Since large point clouds must be downsampled, sampling choices directly affect performance but are often treated as generic preprocessing rather than an active component.

Method: The proposed method introduces an incidence-aware sampling strategy that maps points into a geometry-normalized manifold space for voxel-based selection, while preserving original Euclidean coordinates for downstream learning. It requires only point coordinates and normals, and requires no backbone modification to existing architectures.

Result: Using the Site in Pieces (SIP) benchmark, experiments with Point Transformer and PointNeXt show improved resolution-averaged segmentation performance, especially for non-planar elements and ladders, while reducing sensitivity to sampling resolution.

Conclusion: Acquisition-aware sampling can provide a more stable geometric representation and should be treated as an active component of individual-scan 3D segmentation rather than generic preprocessing.

Abstract: 3D scene understanding is increasingly important in construction, yet most methods are developed on curated datasets that do not fully reflect real site sensing conditions. In many workflows, individual LiDAR scans provide rapid local updates rather than complete scene representations, producing limited surface coverage, acquisition-driven density variation, and severe imbalance between dominant planar surfaces and sparse construction elements. Because large point clouds must be downsampled, sampling resolution and point allocation directly affect the balance between geometric detail and spatial context. This study evaluates these effects under a fixed per-fragment point budget and introduces an incidence-aware sampling strategy for individual LiDAR scans. The method maps points to a geometry-normalized manifold space for voxel-based selection while preserving original Euclidean coordinates for downstream learning. It requires only point coordinates and normals and no backbone modification. Using the Site in Pieces (SIP) benchmark, experiments with Point Transformer and PointNeXt show improved resolution-averaged segmentation performance, especially for non-planar elements and ladders, while reducing sensitivity to sampling resolution. The results show that acquisition-aware sampling can provide a more stable geometric representation and should be treated as an active component of individual-scan 3D segmentation rather than generic preprocessing.

</details>


### [42] [XClipGS: Exact Half-Space Clipping for Medical Volume Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07760)
*Zhongpai Gao,Benjamin Planche,Meng Zheng,Anwesa Choudhuri,Chaoyi Zhou,Terrence Chen,Ziyan Wu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: XClipGS introduces a mathematically exact clipping operator for Gaussian splatting proxies of medical volumes. It factorizes the ray integral of a half-space-restricted Gaussian into an ordinary 2D footprint and a conditional Gaussian CDF, yielding a closed-form, differentiable per-pixel clipping operator with no learned parameters. The interior is supervised via multi-distance reference views with varied clipping planes, and a paired clipped/unclipped cut-face protocol with CDE and Leak metrics is introduced. XClipGS outperforms ClipGS on all eight CT/MRI volumes in PSNR (33.56 vs 32.34 dB), renders at over 650 FPS, reduces leakage by ~40x, and matches RaRa's face fidelity with ~16x less leakage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gaussian-splatting proxies enable interactive rendering of medical volumes, but clipping reveals hidden interior anatomy not constrained by external-view training, and conventional splatting can only keep or drop whole primitives when intersecting a clipping plane, causing artifacts.

Method: The paper splits the problem into two parts: (1) a render-time clip operator and (2) supervision of the hidden interior. Under the local affine model of EWA splatting, the ray integral of a half-space-restricted Gaussian factorizes exactly into its ordinary 2D footprint times a conditional Gaussian CDF whose argument is affine in pixel coordinates, giving a closed-form per-pixel operator that is differentiable w.r.t. primitives and the plane. Interior supervision uses multi-distance reference views with varied clipping-plane axes and offsets. A paired clipped/unclipped cut-face protocol is introduced with difference-referenced cut error (CDE) and culled-side leakage (Leak) metrics.

Result: On eight CT and MRI volumes with unseen plane offsets, XClipGS achieves the highest PSNR on every volume (33.56 dB vs 32.34 dB for ClipGS) and renders at over 650 FPS (versus 278 FPS). On voxel-axis cut-face views, average band SSIM rises from 0.809 to 0.860 and leakage is reduced roughly 40x. Without retraining, it achieves the best average across all four metrics on arbitrary-normal planes, and on a fixed interior matches RaRa's face fidelity with about 16x less leakage.

Conclusion: XClipGS provides an exact, differentiable, and fast clipping solution for Gaussian-splatting medical volume rendering, substantially improving cut-face fidelity and reducing leakage while maintaining real-time interactive frame rates.

Abstract: Gaussian-splatting proxies enable interactive rendering of volumetric medical scans, but a clipping plane exposes anatomy not constrained by external-view training and intersects primitives that conventional splatting can only keep or drop whole. We present XClipGS (eXact Clipping), which treats these as two separate problems: the render-time clip operator and supervision of the hidden interior. Under the local affine model used by EWA splatting, the ray integral of a half-space-restricted Gaussian factorizes exactly into its ordinary 2D footprint and a conditional Gaussian CDF whose argument is affine in pixel coordinates. The resulting closed-form per-pixel operator introduces no learned clipping parameters or auxiliary network and remains differentiable with respect to the primitive and plane. We use multi-distance reference views with varied clipping-plane axes and offsets to supervise the interior through the same operator. We also introduce a paired clipped/unclipped cut-face protocol with difference-referenced cut error (CDE) and culled-side leakage (Leak), because global image metrics dilute errors near the plane. On eight CT and MRI volumes with plane offsets not used for training, XClipGS attains the highest PSNR on every volume (33.56 versus 32.34 dB for ClipGS) while rendering at over 650 FPS, far above real time, versus 278 FPS. On voxel-axis cut-face views, it raises average band SSIM from 0.809 to 0.860 and leaks roughly 40 times less. Without retraining, it also achieves the best average across all four metrics on arbitrary-normal planes; on a fixed interior, it matches RaRa's face fidelity with about 16 times less leakage. Project page: https://gaozhongpai.github.io/XClipGS/

</details>


### [43] [DINO-3DRA: Leveraging 2D Foundation Model Semantics for 3D Cerebral Aneurysm Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07767)
*Jiayang Lu,Fengming Lin,Alejandro F. Frangi,Ali Sarrami-Foroushani*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DINO-3DRA is a dual-path framework that transfers frozen DINOv3 2D vision foundation features into a 3D U-Net via Room-Lite spatial mixing and calibrated residual fusion, achieving state-of-the-art aneurysm segmentation in 3DRA (Dice 0.758, HD95 2.75mm, +13% over nnU-Net) with only 5.72M trainable parameters and eliminating catastrophic failure cases without fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate aneurysm segmentation in 3DRA is challenged by extreme class imbalance, morphological similarity between aneurysms and vessels, and the absence of large-scale 3D pretraining. While 2D vision foundation models encode strong structural priors from 1.7 billion images, naive slice-wise transfer fragments anatomical continuity and destabilizes optimization, motivating a principled cross-dimensional transfer approach.

Method: DINO-3DRA employs a dual-path architecture that injects frozen DINOv3 (2D foundation model) features into a 3D U-Net backbone. This is achieved through two key components: Room-Lite spatial mixing to fuse cross-dimensional features and calibrated residual fusion to stabilize the integration, enabling effective semantic transfer from 2D to 3D without fine-tuning the foundation model.

Result: On multi-centre 3DRA data, DINO-3DRA achieves state-of-the-art aneurysm segmentation (Dice: 0.758; HD95: 2.75 mm; +13% over nnU-Net) with only 5.72M trainable parameters. Ablation studies confirm gains arise from structured cross-dimensional transfer rather than loss design alone. Without fine-tuning on CADA and SHINY-ICARUS, DINO-3DRA eliminates all catastrophic failure cases seen in baseline architectures.

Conclusion: DINO-3DRA demonstrates that effective cross-dimensional transfer from 2D foundation models to 3D medical imaging is achievable through structured feature bridging, yielding robust generalization across heterogeneous imaging protocols and eliminating catastrophic failures while maintaining parameter efficiency.

Abstract: Accurate aneurysm segmentation in 3D rotational angiography (3DRA) is hindered by extreme class imbalance, morphological similarity to vessels, and absent large-scale 3D pretraining. 2D vision foundation models encode dense structural priors from 1.7 billion images, yet naïve slice-wise transfer fragments anatomical continuity and destabilises optimisation. We propose DINO-3DRA, a dual-path framework achieving effective cross-dimensional semantic transfer by injecting frozen DINOv3 features into a 3D U-Net backbone via Room-Lite spatial mixing and calibrated residual fusion. On multi-centre 3DRA data, DINO-3DRA achieves state-of-the-art aneurysm segmentation (Dice: 0.758; HD95: 2.75 mm; +13% over nnU-Net) with only 5.72M trainable parameters. Ablation studies confirm that gains arise from structured cross-dimensional transfer rather than loss design alone, with bridged foundation features improving anatomical continuity between aneurysms and parent vessels. Without fine-tuning on CADA and SHINY-ICARUS, DINO-3DRA eliminates all catastrophic failure cases observed in baseline architectures, demonstrating robust generalisation across heterogeneous imaging protocols.

</details>


### [44] [SeqLoc: Beyond the Single Frame for Cross-View Geo-Localization in Feature-Sparse Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07835)
*Junwei Zheng,Yun Huang,Ruize Dai,Ruiping Liu,Yufan Chen,Kunyu Peng,Kailun Yang,Jiaming Zhang,Guangming Wang,Olaf Wysocki,Rainer Stiefelhagen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces CV-FSS, a benchmark for Cross-View Geo-Localization in feature-sparse rural scenes using OSM maps, and proposes SeqLoc, a test-time sequence aggregation mechanism with Entropy-Tempered Uncertainty, Map-Guided Relocalization, and Peak-Anchored Smoothing that improves position and orientation recall by over 50% compared to single-frame methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cross-View Geo-Localization with OSM works well in structure-rich urban environments but fails in feature-sparse rural scenes. Existing benchmarks do not adequately capture this failure mode, motivating the need for a dedicated rural benchmark and methods that can handle such challenging conditions.

Method: SeqLoc is an online test-time sequence aggregation mechanism that recursively maintains a log-belief volume with three components: (1) Entropy-Tempered Uncertainty (ETU) weights incoming pose likelihood volumes by normalized entropy; (2) Map-Guided Relocalization (MGR) mixes a map-shaped recovery distribution into the belief for recovering suppressed true poses; (3) Peak-Anchored Smoothing (PAS) extracts the final pose at sub-grid precision. The paper also introduces CV-FSS, a benchmark pairing sequential panoramas from five rural regions with aligned OSM maps.

Result: Experiments on CV-FSS and CV-RHO show SeqLoc outperforms single-frame localization methods by a large margin, improving both position and orientation recall by over 50%. Single-frame methods degrade drastically on the new CV-FSS benchmark, confirming the failure mode in feature-sparse scenes.

Conclusion: Sequence aggregation at test time effectively mitigates the collapse of CVGL in feature-sparse rural environments, and the CV-FSS benchmark provides a valuable resource for future research in this under-explored setting.

Abstract: Cross-View Geo-Localization (CVGL) with OpenStreetMap (OSM) performs well in structure-rich urban environments but collapses in feature-sparse scenes such as rural roads. To study this failure mode, in this work, we introduce CV-FSS, a benchmark that pairs sequential panoramas from five rural regions with aligned OSM maps, on which single-frame methods degrade drastically. We then propose SeqLoc, an online test-time sequence aggregation mechanism that recursively maintains a log-belief volume with three key components: (1) Entropy-Tempered Uncertainty (ETU) tempers each incoming pose likelihood volume by its normalized entropy; (2) Map-Guided Relocalization (MGR) mixes a map-shaped recovery distribution into the belief so that a suppressed true pose can recover; (3) Peak-Anchored Smoothing (PAS) derives the final pose at sub-grid precision. Extensive experiments on CV-FSS and CV-RHO demonstrate that SeqLoc outperforms single-frame localization by a large margin, improving both position and orientation recall by over 50%. The benchmark and source code will be made publicly available.

</details>


### [45] [IRPol-Fuse: Energy-structure coordination for infrared polarization fusion under low visibility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07848)
*Zhuangfan Huang,Chusheng Fang,Xiaosong Li,Yang Liua,Xiaoqi Cheng,Haishu Tan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: IRPol-Fuse is a novel infrared-polarization image fusion framework with three modules (Polarization Attention Fusion, Infrared Highlight Injector, Polarization Texture Injector) that preserves both thermal saliency and polarization structural details under low-visibility conditions, validated on a new LI-PI dataset and public LDDRS benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing infrared-polarization image fusion (IPIF) methods overemphasize dominant infrared responses, causing weak yet informative polarization textures in dark regions to be suppressed, leading to degraded fused imagery under low-visibility conditions.

Method: Proposes IRPol-Fuse with an energy-structure coordinated framework comprising three modules: Polarization Attention Fusion for adaptive allocation, Infrared Highlight Injector for highlight-guided preservation, and Polarization Texture Injector for texture restoration. Also introduces LI-PI, a dedicated evaluation dataset for low-visibility scenarios.

Result: Experiments on LI-PI and LDDRS datasets show favorable performance in thermal target preservation, structural detail recovery, and visual naturalness. Region-aware evaluation and downstream object detection confirm effective preservation of both infrared saliency and polarization structure.

Conclusion: The energy-structure coordination strategy effectively balances infrared target saliency and polarization-derived structural information, demonstrating robust performance for challenging low-visibility perception tasks.

Abstract: Robust perception under low-visibility conditions requires fused imagery that jointly preserves infrared thermal saliency and polarization-derived structural details. However, existing infrared-polarization image fusion (IPIF) methods often overemphasize dominant infrared responses, causing weak yet informative polarization textures in dark regions to be suppressed. To address this issue, we propose IRPol-Fuse, an energy-structure coordinated IPIF framework for challenging low-visibility scenarios. The proposed framework contains three key modules: Polarization Attention Fusion for adaptive infrared-polarization allocation, Infrared Highlight Injector for highlight-guided infrared preservation, and Polarization Texture Injector for polarization texture restoration and fine-detail recovery. We further construct LI-PI, a dedicated infrared-polarization evaluation dataset for low-visibility and visually concealed scenes. Experiments on LI-PI and the public LDDRS dataset demonstrate that IRPol-Fuse achieves favorable performance in thermal target preservation, structural detail recovery, and visual naturalness. Region-aware evaluation and downstream object detection further verify that the proposed energy-structure coordination strategy effectively preserves both infrared target saliency and polarization-derived structural information. Code is available at https://github.com/1hzf/IRPolar-Fuse .

</details>


### [46] [Distilling CT Foundation Models into Editable Concept Bottlenecks for Lung Nodule Malignancy Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07857)
*Fakrul Islam Tushar,Stephen Adamo,Geoffrey D. Rubin*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper develops concept bottleneck models that map frozen CT foundation-model embeddings to eight radiologist-defined pulmonary-nodule attributes, predicting malignancy from these estimated concepts plus nodule size. The approach achieves AUROCs of 0.86 internally and 0.72-0.73 externally, comparable to nodule size alone, while providing transparent, decomposable predictions through concept interventions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Foundation models provide transferable CT representations, but direct predictions from these embeddings are difficult to interpret. The authors aim to create interpretable malignancy predictions by bridging foundation-model embeddings with clinically meaningful radiologist-defined nodule attributes.

Method: Two frozen CT foundation-model representations (CT-FM whole-CT encoder and FMCIB nodule-focused contrastive encoder) were mapped via eight ridge-regression concept heads to radiologist-defined pulmonary-nodule attributes. Malignancy was predicted from estimated concepts and nodule size using additive models trained on LUNA25, with concept fidelity assessed via five-fold cross-validated R2 and malignancy discrimination via AUROC with bootstrap confidence intervals.
