Result: FMCIB showed higher concept fidelity than CT-FM (e.g., subtlety R2 0.24 vs 0.11; spiculation 0.17 vs 0.08). Both concept+size models achieved internal AUROC of 0.86 and external AUROC of 0.72-0.73, comparable to nodule size alone (0.73) and superior to embedding-only probes (0.60-0.67). Predictions could be decomposed and modified via controlled concept interventions.

Conclusion: Concept bottlenecks provide transparent malignancy predictions with discrimination similar to nodule size alone. Differences in concept fidelity between foundation models suggest that concept recovery depends on the underlying representation, highlighting the importance of representation choice for interpretable clinical models.

Abstract: Foundation models provide transferable CT representations, but predictions based directly on these embeddings are difficult to interpret. We developed concept bottleneck models that map two frozen CT foundation-model representations to eight radiologist-defined pulmonary-nodule attributes and predict malignancy from the estimated concepts and nodule size. The models included CT-FM, a whole-CT self-supervised encoder using a 96^3-voxel nodule-centered patch, and FMCIB, a nodule-focused contrastive encoder using a 50-mm crop. Eight ridge-regression concept heads were trained on 2,610 LIDC-IDRI nodules. Malignancy models were trained on LUNA25 and evaluated on a held-out internal test set and the external DLCS cohort. Concept fidelity was assessed using five-fold cross-validated R^2, and malignancy discrimination was assessed using AUROC with 95% confidence intervals estimated by patient-grouped bootstrap resampling. Concept fidelity was modest but higher for FMCIB than CT-FM for subtlety (R2, 0.24 vs. 0.11), spiculation (0.17 vs. 0.08), texture (0.17 vs. 0.07), and lobulation (0.15 vs. 0.05). Internally, the CT-FM and FMCIB concept+size models achieved AUROCs of 0.86 (95% CI, 0.80-0.92) and 0.86 (0.79-0.92), respectively. Externally, AUROCs were 0.72 (0.68-0.75) and 0.73 (0.70-0.76), compared with 0.73 for nodule size alone and 0.60 and 0.67 for the corresponding embedding only probes. Additive predictions could be decomposed into feature-level contributions and modified through controlled concept interventions. Concept bottlenecks provided transparent malignancy predictions with discrimination similar to nodule size alone, while differences in concept fidelity suggest that concept recovery depends on the underlying foundation-model representation.

</details>


### [47] [How Much Does It Cost to Answer My Question? Benchmarking Cloud VLM-based VQA Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07861)
*Henri Vanhuynegem,Weitao Xu,Yiran Shen,Guohao Lan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VQABench is the first systematic benchmark evaluating how client-side image preprocessing techniques affect the cost, quality, and latency of mobile VQA systems that offload inference to commercial cloud VLMs. Through 95,168 API calls across 12 preprocessing techniques, 3 datasets, and 4 cloud VLMs, the authors show that preprocessing is not universally beneficial—its impact depends on the target model, API paradigm, token-accounting rules, and task formulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mobile VQA systems increasingly offload inference to cloud-based VLMs, making client-side image preprocessing a key optimization variable. However, despite many preprocessing techniques being proposed, their cost-quality impact on commercial cloud VLMs has never been systematically studied.

Method: The authors present VQABench, a benchmark that treats client-side input preprocessing as a controlled variable for cloud-VLM-based VQA. They evaluate 12 preprocessing techniques across three VQA datasets and four commercial VLMs from three providers, totaling 95,168 API calls.

Result: Preprocessing is not universally beneficial: its effectiveness depends on the target model, API paradigm, provider token-accounting rules, and task formulation. Poorly selected preprocessing can increase deployment cost or latency while degrading answer accuracy.

Conclusion: The benchmark clarifies when preprocessing helps, when it fails, and why, providing insights to guide future research and real-world deployment of VQA systems.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) are becoming a practical backend for mobile visual question answering (VQA) systems, enabling smartphones and smart glasses to answer users' questions about the physical world. Since modern VLMs remain difficult to run on mobile and edge devices, VQA systems increasingly offload inference to cloud-based VLMs. This gives mobile devices access to stronger computation, but it also makes visual input preparation a key system variable: how the image is prepared before offloading affects not only answer quality but also payload size, token cost, and system latency. Proprietary APIs expose little control over model internals or serving behavior, leaving client-side preprocessing as the main practical optimization space for downstream developers. Many such techniques have been proposed for visual offloading, yet their cost-quality impact on commercial cloud VLMs has never been studied. To fill this gap, we present VQABench, the first systematic benchmark that treats client-side input preprocessing as a controlled variable for cloud-VLM-based VQA. We evaluate 12 preprocessing techniques across three VQA datasets and four commercial VLMs from three providers, totaling 95,168 API calls. Our results show that preprocessing is not universally beneficial: its effectiveness depends on the target model, API paradigm, provider token-accounting rule, and task formulation. A poorly selected preprocessing strategy can increase deployment cost or latency while degrading answer accuracy. Overall, our benchmark clarifies when preprocessing helps, when it fails, and why, providing insights to guide future research and real-world deployment of VQA systems.

</details>


### [48] [LHSDet: High-Resolution AI-Generated Image Detection via Visual Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07863)
*Qian Yao,Jun-Jie Huang,Yongjun Wang,Luming Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LHSDet is a novel high-resolution AI-generated image detector that formulates detection as a Visual Question Answering task using a vision-language framework with a triple-branch architecture (low-level visual, high-level visual, and semantic textual branches) to capture both fine-grained textures and global semantics, achieving robust detection across diverse diffusion and autoregressive generative models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI-generated image detection methods often downsample images, missing critical low-level texture details in high-resolution images, and struggle with the continual emergence of unknown generative models that make large-scale pre-training datasets inaccessible.

Method: LHSDet formulates image detection as a Visual Question Answering problem using a fine-tuned vision-language framework. It uses a triple-branch architecture: (1) a low-level visual branch aggregating non-overlapping patches for local texture cues, (2) a high-level visual branch based on SigLIP2 for global perception features, and (3) a semantic-level textual branch generating captions via BLIP-2, enabling multi-modal feature fusion.

Result: Extensive experiments show LHSDet achieves high detection accuracy and robust performance across diverse generative models, including both diffusion and autoregressive models.

Conclusion: LHSDet effectively addresses the limitations of prior detectors by preserving high-resolution details through multi-modal complementary information extraction, demonstrating strong generalization across varied generative model types.

Abstract: Driven by advances in diffusion models and autoregressive models, the fidelity and resolution of AI-generated images now rival those of real images. However, existing AI-generated image detection methods often downsample the images, inevitably overlooking critical low-level texture details in high-resolution AI-generated images, therefore limiting their detection performance. In addition, the ceaseless emergence of unknown generative models makes large-scale pre-training datasets inaccessible. To address these challenges, we propose a novel high-resolution AI-generated image detector, termed LHSDet. Specifically, we formulate the AI-generated image detection task as a Visual Question Answering problem, leveraging a fine-tuned vision-language framework to fully exploit the complementary information between visual and textual modalities. Recognizing that the default visual encoder of existing vision-language models is not tailored for AI-generated image detection, we redesign a visual encoder to better capture both the low-level and high-level artifacts inherent in AI-generated images. Furthermore, we incorporate a semantic-level textual branch to enable multi-modal feature fusion and detection. Consequently, LHSDet employs a triple-branch architecture to extract complementary multi-modal features: a low-level visual branch that aggregates non-overlapping patches for local texture cues, a high-level visual branch based on SigLIP2 for global perception feature extraction, and a semantic-level textual branch that generates captions using BLIP-2. Extensive experimental results demonstrate that LHSDet achieves high detection accuracy and robust performance across diverse generative models, including both diffusion and autoregressive models.

</details>


### [49] [UniScale: Arbitrary-Scale Industrial Anomaly Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07864)
*Shilei Zeng,Linxin Guan,Xurui Li,Yaohan Tang,Yu Zhou*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: UniScale is a unified training and inference framework for generating high-fidelity industrial anomalies at arbitrary scales, addressing the problem of small-scale anomaly information loss in latent space caused by downsampling in diffusion models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrial anomaly inspection lacks real-world anomaly samples. While generative models can create synthetic anomalies, existing methods fail on small-scale anomalies because extreme downsampling in diffusion models causes small anomaly information to be lost in latent space.

Method: UniScale has two key components: (1) Error-Suppressed Multi-Scale Training (EMT) strategy that enables learning rich location-aware textures while suppressing upsampling-induced interpolation errors, allowing the model to learn both small and regular scale anomalies; (2) Generation-then-Fusion Denoising for inference, which decouples anomaly generation from background integration to prevent small anomalies from being overwhelmed.

Result: UniScale outperforms state-of-the-art competitors in anomaly generation quality and downstream detection. It achieves relative IS(a) improvements of 45.86% (1.81 to 2.64) on VisA and 37.70% (1.22 to 1.68) on MVTec AD 2, plus downstream pixel-level IoU improvement of 4.22% on VisA and AUROC improvement of 6.55% on MVTec AD 2.

Conclusion: UniScale effectively addresses the small-scale anomaly generation problem in industrial anomaly inspection through its multi-scale training strategy and decoupled generation-fusion inference, achieving state-of-the-art performance in both generation quality and downstream detection tasks.

Abstract: Industrial anomaly inspection faces a major challenge due to the lack of real-world anomaly samples. While generative models are used to create anomaly data, existing methods still struggle when handling small-scale anomalies.This failure occurs because extreme downsampling in diffusion models causes the information of small anomalies to be lost in the latent space.To address this, we introduce UniScale, a unified training and inference framework for high-fidelity industrial anomaly generation across arbitrary scales.During training, we introduce an Error-Suppressed Multi-Scale Training (EMT) strategy, which enables the model to learn the rich location-aware textures of anomalies, while suppressing upsampling-induced interpolation errors in texture acquisition, ensuring the model is capable of learning small-scale anomalies, while remaining effective for regular scale anomalies.For inference, we propose Generation-then-Fusion Denoising. It decouples anomaly generation from background integration, preventing small anomalies from being overwhelmed.Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method outperforms state-of-the-art competitors in both anomaly generation quality and downstream detection performance.It achieves a relative IS(a) improvement of 45.86\% (from 1.81 to 2.64) on VisA and 37.70\% (from 1.22 to 1.68) on MVTec AD 2, while also improving the downstream pixel-level IoU by 4.22\% on VisA and AUROC by 6.55\% on MVTec AD 2. Code is available at https://github.com/HUST-SLOW/UniScale.

</details>


### [50] [Vision-Language Grounding as Bidirectional Concept Correspondence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07886)
*Jieyu Zhang,Ziqi Gao,Luke Zettlemoyer,Ranjay Krishna*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces ConCor-1, a vision-language grounding model that reformulates grounding as bidirectional concept correspondence—recovering all correspondences between visually referential text spans and instance-level image segments without requiring prespecified text phrases. Using learnable bridge tokens on a pretrained VLM, it jointly predicts text masks, image masks, and correspondence scores, unifying phrase grounding, referring expression grounding, and open-vocabulary detection. It improves correspondence F1 by 48% on long-caption data and 29% on zero-shot LVIS.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing vision-language grounding is typically formulated as unidirectional localization, assuming the relevant text phrase or category is already provided. This overlooks the more fundamental challenge of determining which parts of the text are visually referential and how they align with image entities—a prerequisite for grounded communication in real-world settings.

Method: The paper formulates grounding as bidirectional concept correspondence over image-text pairs, recovering all correspondences between visually referential text spans and instance-level image segments without given text spans. It introduces ConCor-1, built on a pretrained vision-language model, which uses learnable bridge tokens to represent candidate correspondences and predicts a text mask, an image mask, and a correspondence presence score per token. Diverse grounding and segmentation datasets are converted into a unified correspondence format for training and evaluation.

Result: ConCor-1 consistently outperforms baselines across tasks, achieving a 48% improvement in correspondence F1 on the long-caption dataset and a 29% improvement on zero-shot LVIS, where the large category list serves as the text input.

Conclusion: Bidirectional concept correspondence is a more complete and unified formulation of grounding that removes the assumption of prespecified text phrases. ConCor-1 demonstrates that modeling text segmentation, image segmentation, and cross-modal alignment jointly in a single correspondence prediction framework yields strong performance across diverse grounding tasks.

Abstract: Vision-language grounding connects language to visual content, yet most existing formulations reduce grounding to a unidirectional localization problem: given a prespecified text phrase or category name, identify the corresponding image region. This setup assumes that the relevant linguistic unit is already known, overlooking a more basic challenge in grounded communication: determining which parts of the text are visually referential and how they correspond to entities in the image. We formulate grounding as $\textit{bidirectional concept correspondence}$ over an image-text pair. Given an image and its paired text, the goal is to recover all correspondences between visually referential text spans and instance-level image segments, without assuming that the relevant text spans are provided. This formulation unifies common grounding tasks, including phrase grounding, referring expression grounding, and open-vocabulary detection, by treating text segmentation, image segmentation, and cross-modal alignment as a single correspondence prediction problem. To address this task, we introduce $\textbf{ConCor-1}$, a grounding model built on top of a pretrained vision-language model. It uses learnable $\textit{bridge tokens}$ to represent candidate image-text correspondences and predicts, for each token, a text mask, an image mask, and a correspondence presence score. To train and evaluate this task, we convert diverse grounding and segmentation datasets into a unified correspondence format. Experiments show that $\textbf{ConCor-1}$ consistently outperforms baselines, improving correspondence F1 by 48% on the long-caption dataset and by 29% on zero-shot LVIS, where the large category list serves as the text input.

</details>


### [51] [DeCo: Zero-Shot Industrial Anomaly Generation through Decoupling and Recoupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07904)
*Shilei Zeng,Xurui Li,Yaohan Tang,Yu Zhou*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DeCo is a zero-shot industrial anomaly generation method that decouples anomaly structure from its source product and recouples it with normal textures of the target product, using Dual-Routing Flow (DR-Flow) and Product-Invariant Flow (PI-Flow) for anomaly acquisition, plus hybrid injection and Product Compatibility Correction (PCC) for anomaly-product fusion, achieving SOTA with 5.1% and 8.2% pixel AP improvements on MVTec AD and VisA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrial anomaly inspection is limited by the scarcity of real anomalous data. Zero-shot anomaly generation methods exist but suffer from two critical limitations: inaccurate anomaly information acquisition and uncontrolled anomaly-product fusion.

Method: DeCo decouples anomaly structure from its source product and recouples it with target product normal textures. It uses Dual-Routing Flow (DR-Flow) to bind texture-invariant anomaly structure to an abnormal token, with Product-Invariant Flow (PI-Flow) as a parallel constraint to prevent binding the source product. For fusion, hybrid injection recouples the anomaly structure with the target product, and Product Compatibility Correction (PCC) compensates for incompatibility between the anomaly and the product.

Result: DeCo establishes a new state-of-the-art. Training downstream detection models on generated data yields massive improvements of 5.1% pixel AP on MVTec AD and 8.2% pixel AP on VisA.

Conclusion: DeCo effectively addresses the limitations of existing zero-shot anomaly generation methods through explicit decoupling and recoupling of anomaly structures, demonstrating significant improvements in downstream anomaly detection performance.

Abstract: Industrial anomaly inspection is severely hindered by the scarcity of real anomalous data.Zero-shot industrial anomaly generation addresses this by generating anomalies on specific products without requiring any of their real anomalous images. However, existing methods suffer from two critical limitations, i.e., inaccurate anomaly information acquisition and uncontrolled anomaly-product fusion.To overcome these challenges, we propose DeCo, which decouples the anomaly structure from its source product, and explicitly recouples it with the normal textures of the target product. During anomaly information acquisition,Dual-Routing Flow (DR-Flow) binds the texture-invariant anomaly structure to an abnormal token, while a parallel constraint, Product-Invariant Flow (PI-Flow), prevents the abnormal token from binding the source product. During anomaly-product fusion, we propose a hybrid injection to recouple the acquired anomaly structure with the target product, and Product Compatibility Correction (PCC) to compensate for the incompatibility between the acquired anomaly structure and the product. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DeCo establishes a new state-of-the-art. Training downstream detection models on our generated data yields massive pixel AP improvements of 5.1\% on MVTec AD and 8.2\% on VisA. Code is available at https://github.com/HUST-SLOW/DeCo.

</details>


### [52] [SegDem: Segmentation helps Demosaicing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07916)
*Ping Chen,Xiangming Wang,Yongyong Chen,Jiezhang Cao,Kai Zhang,Jingyong Su,Jie Liu,Haijin Zeng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SegDem is a cross-task decoder representation transfer framework for image demosaicing that leverages instance segmentation to learn region- and boundary-aware representations, anchored to a shared frozen DINOv2 space, improving reconstruction across convolutional, Transformer, and state-space backbones for Single- and Quad-Bayer CFAs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing demosaicing methods treat it purely as pixel-level reconstruction relying on local textures, cross-channel correlations, and low-level statistics, overlooking that reconstruction and visual understanding are complementary views of the same underlying scene structure and should be consistent.

Method: SegDem learns region- and boundary-aware representations via instance-aware structural pretraining with instance segmentation, transfers the decoder to RAW-conditioned reconstruction, and anchors segmentation- and demosaicing-conditioned features to a shared frozen DINOv2 representation space. It is instantiated with convolutional, Transformer-based, and state-space backbones for unified Single- and Quad-Bayer demosaicing.

Result: Extensive experiments on synthetic, external, and challenging datasets demonstrate consistent improvements across different architectures and CFA layouts.

Conclusion: Integrating high-level structural understanding (instance segmentation) with low-level reconstruction tasks improves demosaicing performance consistently across diverse architectures and CFA configurations.

Abstract: Image demosaicing reconstructs a full-color image from incomplete color measurements produced by a sensor covered with a color filter array (CFA). Most existing methods formulate demosaicing as pixel-level reconstruction and mainly rely on local textures, cross-channel correlations, and low-level image statistics. Our core insight is that reconstruction and visual understanding can be viewed as complementary views of shared scene structure: both are grounded in the same underlying physical world, and therefore the structural and physical information inferred from an image should remain consistent across the two tasks. We instantiate this idea with instance segmentation and propose \emph{SegDem}, a cross-task decoder representation transfer framework for demosaicing. SegDem first learns region- and boundary-aware representations through instance-aware structural pretraining and then transfers the decoder to RAW-conditioned reconstruction. Segmentation- and demosaicing-conditioned features are further anchored to a shared frozen DINOv2 representation space to preserve structural organization across tasks. We instantiate SegDem with convolutional, Transformer-based, and state-space backbones for unified Single- and Quad-Bayer demosaicing. Extensive experiments on synthetic, external, and challenging datasets demonstrate consistent improvements across different architectures and CFA layouts.

</details>


### [53] [Forged Peer Judgments Mislead Multimodal LLM Judge Panels: Source-Blind Anchoring and Panel-Consensus Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07920)
*Yang Shu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper identifies source-blind anchoring as a text-level attack surface in multimodal LLM judge panels, where quoted judgments (even when fabricated) can overturn correct verdicts. The authors propose a defense called panel-consensus verification that cross-checks quotes against independent blind votes, blocking 84.9% of attacks and reducing net harm by 97.5%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal LLM judge panels rely on cross-referencing peer judgments, but quoted peer judgments are themselves untrusted. The paper exposes a vulnerability where source-blind anchoring—where the content of a quote, not its provenance—can manipulate verdicts in VLM panels, creating a low-cost attack surface that needs both characterization and defense.

Method: The authors conduct controlled experiments measuring anchoring gaps under self and peer framing (19–26 percentage points), use a matched-content label-only control to isolate the label effect, and compare deliberately generated wrong quotes versus naturally occurring wrong peer statements. They then introduce panel-consensus verification, which cross-checks quotes against independently collected blind votes, and evaluate it under leave-one-out re-verification.

Result: Self/peer framing creates large anchoring gaps (19–26pp) that cannot be explained by the label itself (control difference only -0.17pp). Fabricated wrong quotes overturned correct verdicts 1.5–2.7x more often than natural wrong peer statements. Panel-consensus verification blocked 84.9% of fabricated attacks, cut net harm by 97.5%, and preserved positive (though statistically inconclusive) estimates for genuine peer information.

Conclusion: Source-blind anchoring is a low-cost, text-level attack surface in multimodal judge panels, and panel-consensus verification provides a concrete and effective defense for safer multimodal collaborative evaluation, though the benefit from genuine peer information remains statistically inconclusive.

Abstract: Multimodal LLM judge panels can cross-reference peers, but a quoted peer judgment may itself be untrusted. We expose source-blind anchoring as a text-level attack surface in vision-language model (VLM) panels. Quoting independent visual judgments creates large anchoring gaps (19--26 percentage points) under both self and peer framing. A matched-content, label-only control changes the broken rate by only $-0.17$pp (95\% CI $[-0.68,0.35]$), showing that the self/peer label itself does not explain the effect. Under our tested construction, deliberately generated, concise wrong quotes overturn originally-correct verdicts 1.5--2.7$\times$ more often than naturally occurring wrong peer statements, with bootstrap 95\% CIs excluding parity across two datasets and seven VLM judges. Because the two statement populations differ in selection and form, this ratio measures differential damage under the tested attack rather than a provenance-only causal effect. We then introduce panel-consensus verification, which cross-checks a quote against independently collected blind votes. It blocks 84.9\% of fabricated attacks, cuts their net harm by 97.5\%, and preserves the positive but statistically inconclusive point estimate for genuine peer information under leave-one-out re-verification. These results identify a low-cost attack surface and a concrete defense for safer multimodal collaborative evaluation.

</details>


### [54] [SCoPE: Training-Free Audio-Visual Event Perception via Sparse Cross-Modal Prior Exchange](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07923)
*Jaemo Jeong,Junho Yoon,Hyunju Kim,Dongman Lee*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SCoPE is a training-free framework for audio-visual event perception that resolves false co-activations (FCAs) by making all queried labels compete for shared evidence and letting each modality guide event selection in the other, improving performance on multiple benchmarks without fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing training-free methods for audio-visual event perception (AVEP) fail because related labels share evidence, causing incorrect labels to score as high as correct ones (false co-activation, FCA). No scalar cutoff can separate these, and class-specific thresholds only hide FCAs in final predictions without fixing the underlying score corruption.

Method: SCoPE is a training-free framework where all queried labels compete for shared evidence, with each modality (audio and visual) guiding event selection in the other. The authors derive an exact condition for when this competition removes a false co-activation in a two-label fit.

Result: Using identical frozen CLIP+CLAP backbones on LLP, SCoPE improves Type@seg by 7.45 points and Event@seg by 5.04 points compared with reported AV²A values. The same fixed configuration transfers unchanged to OV-AVEBench and VGGSound-AVEL100k.

Conclusion: SCoPE effectively removes false co-activations in audio-visual event perception through a training-free competition mechanism, achieving significant improvements and demonstrating strong generalization across benchmarks with a single fixed configuration.

Abstract: Audio-visual event perception (AVEP) determines which events occur in a video, when they occur, and whether they are audible, visible, or both. Training-free methods query new event vocabularies by matching frozen audio and visual features with text-encoded event names. However, related labels share evidence. An incorrect label can then score at least as high as a correct one. We call this a false co-activation (FCA). No scalar cutoff can reject the incorrect label while keeping every correct one. Class-specific thresholds may prevent that label from becoming a final prediction, but the FCA remains in the underlying score vector. We introduce SCoPE, a training-free framework in which all queried labels compete for shared evidence and each modality guides event selection in the other. We derive an exact condition for when this competition removes an FCA in a two-label fit. With identical frozen CLIP+CLAP backbones on LLP, SCoPE improves Type@seg by 7.45 points and Event@seg by 5.04 points compared with the reported AV$^2$A values. The same fixed configuration transfers unchanged to OV-AVEBench and VGGSound-AVEL100k.

</details>


### [55] [SportsGrounder: Proposal-Aided Interleaved Grounding for Dense Sports Video Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07932)
*Yizhi Li,Jiawei Jiang,Guanhong Wang,Yingcai Wu,Gaoang Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SportsGrounder is a framework that enhances dense sports video reasoning in Large Multimodal Models by leveraging an open-vocabulary visual expert with interleaved grounding, including an Interleaved Grounding Fusion (IGF) mechanism for fine-grained spatial localization, an Action-Aware Supervision (AAS) module to reduce language bias, and Mixed Preference Optimization (MPO) to distinguish deceptive distractors, achieving state-of-the-art accuracy on dense sports VQA datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dense sports video reasoning is challenging because it requires fine-grained understanding of small-scale, highly interactive, and visually homogeneous entities (players with identical uniforms, ball) across long temporal contexts. Current LMMs struggle with such dense visual complexities and often over-rely on textual priors to guess answers, especially when distinguishing visually similar actions and players.

Method: SportsGrounder leverages an open-vocabulary visual expert for interleaved grounding. It uses (1) domain-guided object proposals for precise spatial localization, (2) an Interleaved Grounding Fusion (IGF) mechanism that frame-by-frame integrates bounding box coordinates and implicit visual semantics with global grid features while preserving temporal alignment and preventing sequence length explosion, (3) an Action-Aware Supervision (AAS) module that regularizes the model's hidden states to learn accurate motion representations, and (4) Mixed Preference Optimization (MPO) to better distinguish deceptive distractors.

Result: Experiments on newly curated dense sports VQA datasets (derived from SoccerNet and FineSports) demonstrate that SportsGrounder significantly improves fine-grained reasoning and achieves state-of-the-art accuracy.

Conclusion: SportsGrounder effectively addresses the dense visual complexities in sports video reasoning by combining explicit grounding signals with implicit visual semantics, reducing textual prior reliance, and improving fine-grained action and player discrimination, ultimately achieving superior performance over existing methods.

Abstract: Sports video analysis is crucial for athletic analytics and broadcasting enhancement. Dense sports video reasoning, however, demands a fine-grained understanding of numerous small-scale, highly interactive, and visually homogeneous entities (e.g., players sharing identical uniforms, the ball) across long temporal contexts. Current Large Multimodal Models (LMMs) inherently struggle with such dense visual complexities. Due to the lack of fine-grained visual details, these models often over-rely on textual priors to guess answers, especially when distinguishing visually similar actions and players. To address this, we propose \textbf{SportsGrounder}, a framework that leverages an open-vocabulary visual expert to aid interleaved grounding specifically for dense sports video reasoning. To achieve precise spatial localization, we extract domain-guided object proposals and introduce an Interleaved Grounding Fusion (IGF) mechanism. The IGF frame-by-frame integrates explicit bounding box coordinates and implicit visual semantics with global grid features. This design preserves strict temporal alignment and prevents sequence length explosion. Furthermore, we design an Action-Aware Supervision (AAS) module that directly regularizes the model's hidden states, forcing the network to learn accurate motion representations rather than relying on language bias. Optimized with Mixed Preference Optimization (MPO) to better distinguish deceptive distractors, our extensive experiments on newly curated dense sports VQA datasets (derived from SoccerNet and FineSports) demonstrate that SportsGrounder significantly improves fine-grained reasoning and achieves state-of-the-art accuracy.

</details>


### [56] [FlexSplat: Flexible Feed-Forward 3D Gaussian Splatting without Point Cloud Correspondence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07937)
*Amir Sabbaghziarani,Hanting Ye,Maria Gorlatova,Yi Ding*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: FlexSplat is a feed-forward framework for novel view synthesis from uncalibrated, object-centric multi-view images. It removes the need for camera poses by jointly training a geometry transformer with a Gaussian decoder to predict camera parameters and depth, which ground a depth-guided Gaussian parameterization and multi-view deformable cross-attention. It matches or approaches posed state-of-the-art methods on ShapeNet-SRN and GSO without requiring camera poses or ground-truth depth, staying within 0.7 dB PSNR of posed methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing query-based methods for 3D Gaussian reconstruction from multi-view images assume camera poses are given, which limits their applicability in real-world scenarios where calibration information is often unavailable. The authors aim to eliminate this assumption to enable calibration-free operation.

Method: FlexSplat jointly trains a geometry transformer with a Gaussian decoder to predict per-image camera parameters and depth. These predictions ground a depth-guided Gaussian parameterization and a multi-view deformable cross-attention mechanism that aggregates evidence across all views into a single view-consistent set of primitives. An uncertainty-weighted depth-consistency objective adapts the geometry to the reconstruction task, while cross-view consensus during decoding absorbs residual errors from estimated cameras and depth. The representation uses a compact Gaussian budget decoupled from input resolution and view count.

Result: On ShapeNet-SRN and Google Scanned Objects (GSO), FlexSplat matches or approaches posed state-of-the-art reconstructors without requiring camera poses or ground-truth depth. It matches the best perceptual (LPIPS) quality among compared methods on GSO and stays within 0.7 dB PSNR of posed methods.

Conclusion: A jointly trained geometry front-end is sufficient to bring calibration-free operation to query-based Gaussian reconstruction while maintaining competitive quality with posed methods, demonstrating that camera pose estimation can be effectively integrated into the reconstruction pipeline rather than treated as a separate prerequisite.

Abstract: We present FlexSplat, a feed-forward framework for novel view synthesis (NVS) from uncalibrated, object-centric multi-view image collections. A recent line of query-based methods reconstructs a compact set of 3D Gaussians by treating them as transformer queries that are refined with multi-view deformable attention; these methods, however, assume that camera poses are given. FlexSplat removes this assumption: a geometry transformer is trained jointly with the Gaussian decoder to predict per-image camera parameters and depth, which in turn ground a depth-guided Gaussian parameterization and a multi-view deformable cross-attention that aggregates evidence across all input views into a single, view-consistent set of primitives. An uncertainty-weighted depth-consistency objective lets the jointly trained geometry adapt to the reconstruction task, while the cross-view consensus formed during decoding absorbs the residual error of the estimated cameras and depth. The representation uses a compact Gaussian budget that is decoupled from the input resolution - unlike pixel-aligned methods, the primitive count does not grow with the image grid - and is not dictated by the number of views. On ShapeNet-SRN and Google Scanned Objects (GSO), FlexSplat matches or approaches posed state-of-the-art reconstructors while requiring neither camera poses nor ground-truth depth, and matches the best perceptual (LPIPS) quality among the compared methods on GSO. Our results indicate that a jointly trained geometry front-end is sufficient to bring calibration-free operation to query-based Gaussian reconstruction while staying within 0.7 dB PSNR of posed methods and matching their perceptual quality.

</details>


### [57] [LAD-COD: Language-Aligned Dense Perception for Camouflaged Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07941)
*Shangye Song,Tianzhi Zhu,Syed Ariff Syed Hesham,Xin He,Yun Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LAD-COD is a framework for camouflaged object detection that aligns top-down instruction-conditioned semantic guidance from large multimodal models with bottom-up hierarchical visual features, using a trainable hierarchical visual branch and Language-Aligned Dual Visual Fusion to preserve fine structural details while leveraging semantic target guidance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Camouflaged objects have high visual similarity to their surroundings, reducing foreground-background discriminability and weakening boundary evidence. Existing LMM-based segmentation systems only use language embeddings to condition the mask decoder, leaving dense visual features that must preserve low-contrast boundaries without explicit guidance.

Method: LAD-COD learns a trainable hierarchical visual branch capturing camouflage-sensitive texture, boundary, and contextual information, and applies Language-Aligned Dual Visual Fusion (LADVF) to extend language target embeddings beyond sparse prompting, querying patch-level language-aligned features and gating their residual integration with hierarchical features.

Result: Experiments on CAMO, COD10K, and NC4K show LAD-COD achieves the best reported value in all 12 dataset-metric comparisons.

Conclusion: Aligning top-down semantic guidance with bottom-up hierarchical visual features enables effective camouflaged object detection by preserving fine structural details while leveraging language-aligned target semantics.

Abstract: Camouflaged object detection (COD) aims to segment objects that exhibit high visual similarity to their surroundings, which reduces foreground-background discriminability and weakens boundary evidence across appearance, texture, and structure. Such limitations motivate the use of instruction-conditioned semantics as top-down guidance for identifying which weak visual cues are relevant to the target. Recent segmentation systems built on large multimodal models (LMMs) demonstrate this possibility through instruction-conditioned target embeddings that guide mask decoding. However, in this language-to-mask paradigm, the generated target embedding conditions mainly the mask decoder, leaving the dense visual features that must preserve low-contrast boundaries and fine local structure without explicit guidance. We propose Language-Aligned Dense perception for COD (LAD-COD), a framework that aligns top-down semantic target guidance with bottom-up hierarchical visual features. Instead of fully adapting a large generic image encoder, LAD-COD learns a trainable hierarchical visual branch that captures camouflage-sensitive texture, boundary, and contextual information. To align these features with the target embedding, LAD-COD applies Language-Aligned Dual Visual Fusion (LADVF), which extends the embedding beyond sparse prompting to query patch-level language-aligned features and to gate their residual integration with the hierarchical features. This design allows semantic information to guide localization while preserving the fine structural details needed for camouflage segmentation. Experiments on CAMO, COD10K, and NC4K show that LAD-COD obtains the best reported value in all 12 dataset-metric comparisons.

</details>


### [58] [SynVAR: Synergizing Spatial and Semantic Alignment in Visual Autoregressive Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07948)
*Zhennan Chen,Tianxing Shi,Pengcheng Xu,Kepan Nan,Qian Wang,Zili Yi,Jian Yang,Ying Tai*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SynVAR is a training-free enhancement framework for VAR (next-scale prediction) models that uses a spatial-semantic collaborative control strategy to suppress cross-scale error propagation and improve generation quality in complex scenes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VAR's next-scale prediction paradigm suffers from performance bottlenecks in complex scenes with multiple objects and attributes, and existing diffusion-based enhancement methods fail to address the unique challenge of cross-scale error propagation and accumulation in VAR.

Method: SynVAR introduces a spatial-semantic collaborative control strategy with three key components: (1) Global guidance for reasonable spatial structure in early stages, (2) Receptive field constraints to mitigate early-stage semantic confusion, and (3) High-frequency compensation to recover fine-grained details.

Result: Extensive quantitative and qualitative experiments demonstrate significant improvements in SynVAR's ability to enhance VAR's capability for complex scene modeling.

Conclusion: SynVAR is the first training-free enhancement framework specifically tailored for the VAR paradigm, effectively suppressing cross-scale propagation errors and improving generation quality in complex scenes.

Abstract: VAR has gained widespread popularity due to its next-scale prediction paradigm. However, it faces substantial performance bottlenecks when handling complex scenes with multiple objects and attributes. Existing diffusion-based enhancement methods fail to adequately address the unique challenge of cross-scale error propagation and accumulation in VAR. To this end, we propose SynVAR, the first training-free enhancement framework specifically tailored for the VAR paradigm, which introduces a spatial-semantic collaborative control strategy to effectively suppress propagation error and improve generation quality. SynVAR comprises three key components: (1) Global guidance to ensure reasonable spatial structure in the early stages, (2) Receptive field constraints to mitigate early-stage semantic confusion, (3) High-frequency compensation to recover fine-grained details. Extensive quantitative and qualitative experiments demonstrate the significant improvements in the ability of SynVAR to enhance the VAR's capability for complex scene modeling.

</details>


### [59] [LIBAD: A Multimodal Anomaly Detection Benchmark for Li-Ion Battery Electrode Manufacturing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07958)
*Wenbo Sui,Daniel Lichau,Harold Phelippeau,Zhao Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces LIBAD, the first multimodal anomaly detection benchmark for Li-ion battery electrode manufacturing using real roll-to-roll production data with aligned visible-light and X-ray imaging. The authors also propose DA-Core, a memory-based method that improves coreset selection by jointly considering feature-space coverage and local density, reducing FPR95 from 60.4% to 54.3% and inference time by 43.9% compared to standard approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal industrial anomaly detection research focuses on discrete products with strongly correlated RGB and 3D observations, leaving continuous process manufacturing (like battery electrode production) and weakly correlated sensing modalities underexplored. There is a lack of benchmarks and methods suitable for these settings where defect evidence may be strong in one modality but weak or absent in another.

Method: The paper first introduces LIBAD, a benchmark collected from real roll-to-roll production lines featuring double-sided visible-light imaging, high-resolution X-ray radiography, and inline-compatible low-resolution X-ray radiography. The authors then propose DA-Core, a memory-based anomaly detection method that enhances coreset selection by jointly considering feature-space coverage and local density of normal features, preserving fine-grained normal variations in compact memory banks.

Result: With a coreset ratio of 0.05, DA-Core reduces FPR95 from 60.4% to 54.3% compared to standard farthest point sampling. At this ratio, DA-Core outperforms the best standard coreset result (obtained at 0.20) while reducing inference time by 43.9%. Representative baseline methods exhibit limited transferability and consistently high false-positive rates under the inline-compatible setting.

Conclusion: The results indicate that both the data distribution of normal features and the modality relationship require explicit consideration when designing anomaly detection methods for process manufacturing, highlighting the gap between existing approaches and the unique challenges of continuous production environments.

Abstract: Multimodal industrial anomaly detection has largely focused on discrete products using strongly correlated RGB and 3D observations, leaving continuous process manufacturing and weakly correlated sensing modalities underexplored. We introduce LIBAD, the first multimodal anomaly detection benchmark for Li-ion battery electrode manufacturing. Collected from real roll-to-roll production lines, LIBAD provides aligned double-sided visible-light imaging, high-resolution X-ray radiography, and inline-compatible low-resolution X-ray radiography. Electrode patches in LIBAD exhibit highly homogeneous material appearance, while defect evidence can be strong in one modality but weak or absent in another, resulting in pronounced cross-modal anomaly inconsistency. Benchmarks of representative methods under the inline-compatible visible-light and low-resolution X-ray setting exhibit limited transferability and consistently high false-positive rates. We therefore propose DA-Core, a memory-based method that jointly considers feature-space coverage and local density of normal features during coreset selection, allowing compact memory banks to better preserve fine-grained normal variations. With a coreset ratio of 0.05, DA-Core reduces FPR95 from 60.4% to 54.3% compared with standard farthest point sampling. At this ratio, DA-Core also outperforms the best standard coreset result (obtained at 0.20) while reducing inference time by 43.9%. These results suggest that both the data distribution of normal features and the modality relationship itself require explicit consideration when designing anomaly detection methods for process manufacturing.

</details>


### [60] [Distilling Physical Priors into Streaming World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07981)
*Liangliang Zhao,Junying Wang,Danni Yang,Yifan Chang,Bin Fu,Yu Qiao,Bowen Zhou,Yihao Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PhyS is a three-stage framework for distilling physical priors into streaming world models. It introduces PhyS-120K, a dataset of 120K real-world physical-interaction videos with structured annotations, and uses physics-aware supervised fine-tuning on a 14B bidirectional DiT teacher, followed by distillation into a 1.3B causal DiT, and online RL with Temporal Credit Routing to enforce physical plausibility. PhyS significantly outperforms prior methods across multiple physics-aware benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Streaming world models often produce rollouts that violate basic physical constraints. Existing approaches distill pretrained bidirectional DiTs into causal generators, but these suffer from (1) limited physical priors from visually oriented pretraining and (2) further loss of those limited priors during bidirectional-to-causal distillation.

Method: Three-stage framework: (1) Constructs PhyS-120K, a 120K real-world physical-interaction video dataset covering rigid-body dynamics, soft-body deformation, fluid phenomena, and phase transitions, each annotated with structured descriptions of object properties and causal state transitions; (2) Physics-aware supervised fine-tuning injects physical priors into a bidirectional 14B DiT teacher, then distills it into a lightweight 1.3B causal DiT for few-step autoregressive streaming generation; (3) Online reinforcement learning with Temporal Credit Routing (TCR) evaluates physical consistency over overlapping temporal windows and routes group-relative advantages to temporally aligned denoising actions.

Result: On PhysicsIQ, PhyS improves the Wan2.1-14B teacher by 18.2% and outperforms Self Forcing, Rolling Forcing, and Causal Forcing by 23.7%, 14.8%, and 31.4% respectively. Improvements are also observed on physics-aware video benchmarks VideoPhy, VideoPhy2, and PhyGenBench.

Conclusion: PhyS demonstrates that explicitly injecting real-world physical priors through curated data, supervised fine-tuning, and RL-based temporal credit assignment can substantially improve physical consistency in streaming world models.

Abstract: Streaming world models predict future visual states online while maintaining physically coherent dynamics over long horizons. However, their rollouts often violate basic physical constraints. A common approach distills pretrained bidirectional DiTs into few-step causal generators. However, this paradigm suffers from two fundamental limitations: generic bidirectional teachers acquire limited physical priors from visually oriented pretraining, and the limited priors suffer further loss during bidirectional-to-causal distillation. We present PhyS, a three-stage framework for distilling physical priors into streaming world models. To acquire physical priors from real-world interactions, we construct PhyS-120K, a dataset of 120K real-world physical-interaction videos spanning rigid-body dynamics, soft-body deformation, fluid phenomena, and phase transitions. Each video is annotated with structured descriptions of object properties and causal state transitions. Physics-aware supervised fine-tuning injects the physical priors into a bidirectional 14B DiT teacher, which we then distill into a lightweight 1.3B causal DiT for few-step autoregressive streaming generation. Finally, we use online reinforcement learning to incentivize the distilled model to generate physically plausible rollouts and further propose Temporal Credit Routing (TCR) to address temporal credit assignment. TCR evaluates physical consistency over overlapping temporal windows and routes the resulting group-relative advantages to temporally aligned denoising actions. On PhysicsIQ, PhyS improves the Wan2.1-14B teacher by 18.2\% and the Self Forcing, Rolling Forcing, and Causal Forcing by 23.7\%, 14.8\%, and 31.4\%, respectively. Results also improve the physics-aware video benchmarks VideoPhy, VideoPhy2, and PhyGenBench. The dataset, code, and more sample videos are available on our Project Page.

</details>


### [61] [AdaDINO: Pair-Aware In-Backbone Adaptation of Frozen DINO for Efficient Remote Sensing Change Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07982)
*Xu Zhang,Xinqing Li,Jianpeng Xie,Zeshuai Zhu,Xin He,Yun Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: AdaDINO is a pair-aware adaptation framework that equips a frozen DINO encoder with bi-temporal interaction for efficient remote sensing change detection, using Change-aware Gated Local Adaptation (CGLA), Batch-Shared Chunk Selection (BSCS) for computation reduction, and a CGLA-Prior-Guided Refinement (CPGR) decoder, achieving competitive performance with significant speedup.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision foundation models like DINO are pretrained for single-image representation, but remote sensing change detection requires reasoning over bi-temporal image pairs. Existing VFM-based methods encode the two images independently and compare them only afterward, leaving the backbone unaware of cross-temporal relations—this mismatch motivates the need for pair-aware adaptation within the backbone.

Method: AdaDINO introduces: (1) Change-aware Gated Local Adaptation (CGLA) that couples the two streams after selected frozen DINO blocks and injects a shared temporal residual with opposite signs to enhance genuine change responses while preserving pair midpoint; (2) Batch-Shared Chunk Selection (BSCS) that reduces FFN computation by retaining a batch-shared subset of channel chunks executed as a compact dense FFN; (3) A CGLA-Prior-Guided Refinement (CPGR) decoder that reuses encoder-side change responses for coarse-to-fine prediction.

Result: Experiments on four remote sensing change detection benchmarks show AdaDINO achieves competitive or superior performance against VFM-based baselines, with the largest gain on SYSU-CD. With 62.5% of FFN hidden width removed, it reaches 85.29% F1 on SYSU-CD while delivering a 1.41x throughput speedup.

Conclusion: By enabling in-backbone bi-temporal interaction for frozen VFMs, AdaDINO effectively bridges the single-image/single-pair mismatch, demonstrating that pair-aware adaptation of frozen foundation models can deliver both strong change detection accuracy and notable computational efficiency gains.

Abstract: Vision foundation models (VFMs) such as DINO are pretrained for single-image representation, whereas remote sensing change detection requires reasoning over a bi-temporal pair. Existing VFM-based methods usually encode the two images independently and compare them only afterward, leaving the VFM backbone unaware of cross-temporal relations. To bridge this mismatch, we present AdaDINO, a pair-aware in-backbone adaptation framework that equips a frozen DINO encoder with bi-temporal interaction for efficient change detection. Its core component, Change-aware Gated Local Adaptation (CGLA), couples the two streams after selected frozen blocks and injects a shared temporal residual into them with opposite signs, enhancing genuine change responses while preserving the pair midpoint. Batch-Shared Chunk Selection (BSCS) further reduces feed-forward network (FFN) computation by retaining a batch-shared subset of channel chunks that can be executed as a compact dense FFN. A CGLA-Prior-Guided Refinement (CPGR) decoder reuses encoder-side change responses for coarse-to-fine prediction. Experiments on four remote sensing change detection benchmarks show that AdaDINO achieves competitive or superior performance against VFM-based baselines, with the largest gain on the category-agnostic SYSU-CD dataset. With 62.5% of the FFN hidden width removed, AdaDINO still achieves an F1 score of 85.29% on SYSU-CD while delivering a 1.41$\times$ throughput speedup. The code will be released.

</details>


### [62] [AgriField-40K: Adapting Vision Models to Agriculture With Efficient Continual Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07984)
*Vasileios Tzouras,Paraskevas Pegios,Lazaros Nalpantidis*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper introduces AgriField-40K, a field-centric agricultural dataset curated from 17 public sources, and AgriMAE, a parameter-efficient continual pretraining baseline that adapts a masked autoencoder (pretrained on natural images) to agriculture by training only lightweight adapters. AgriMAE achieves comparable or better performance than full fine-tuning while using up to 9× fewer trainable parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Field-based agricultural computer vision is crucial for precision agriculture but suffers from expensive annotation costs and costly adaptation of large pretrained models, motivating the need for a field-centric dataset and parameter-efficient adaptation methods.

Method: The authors curate AgriField-40K from 17 public resources covering diverse crops, weeds, and field conditions. They propose AgriMAE, a parameter-efficient continual pretraining baseline that adapts a masked autoencoder pretrained on natural images using only lightweight adapters, and explore semantic feature reconstruction as an alternative pretraining objective.

Result: AgriMAE consistently improves downstream performance and can match or outperform full fine-tuning while using up to 9× fewer trainable parameters, demonstrating the practical value of AgriField-40K for continual pretraining in agricultural vision.

Conclusion: AgriField-40K serves as a practical resource for continual pretraining in agricultural vision, and AgriMAE shows that parameter-efficient adaptation can achieve strong performance at a fraction of the training cost.

Abstract: Field-based agricultural computer vision is important for precision agriculture, yet it largely depends on expensive annotations and costly adaptation of large pretrained models. We introduce AgriField-40K, a field-centric dataset curated from 17 public resources and covering diverse crops, weeds, and field conditions. Building on this, we present AgriMAE, a parameter-efficient continual pretraining baseline that adapts a masked autoencoder pretrained on natural images by training only lightweight adapters. We further explore semantic feature reconstruction as an alternative pretraining objective and evaluate transfer across multiple tasks. AgriMAE consistently improves downstream performance and can match or even outperform full fine-tuning while using up to $9\times$ fewer trainable parameters, showing that AgriField-40K is a practical resource for continual pretraining in agricultural vision. Project page: https://dtu-pas.github.io/agrifield40k/

</details>


### [63] [Advantage-Guided Gate: Reshaping Open-Ended Reasoning for Vision-Based Spatial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07987)
*Ling Lin,Yang Bai,Congcong Zhu,Jiangming Shi,Meng Wang,Yang Long,Jingrun Chen,Ling Shao,Huazhu Fu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes an advantage-guided gating framework for multimodal large language models (MLLMs) that dynamically intervenes in step-by-step reasoning to correct deviations, using Monte Carlo value evaluation on reasoning trees to provide intermediate supervision. The framework includes Step-Advantage Gate and Trajectory-Advantage Gate, trained on a new Reasoning-Tree-160k dataset via two-stage learning, significantly improving spatial understanding and reasoning performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MLLMs excel at spatial reasoning but suffer from error accumulation and decision errors in open-ended reasoning, causing unstable answer quality. The paper aims to address these issues by providing intermediate supervision and dynamic correction mechanisms.

Method: Models step-by-step reasoning as a finite-horizon decision process; applies Monte Carlo value evaluation on reasoning trees for intermediate supervision. Introduces Step-Advantage Gate and Trajectory-Advantage Gate for selecting high-value reasoning steps and high-quality trajectories respectively. Uses multi-branch sampling to generate reasoning trees for gate training, combines shared-parameter initialization with task-specific heads, and constructs the Reasoning-Tree-160k dataset with two-stage learning. During inference, greedily selects high-value prefixes and chooses optimal reasoning heads based on problem type.

Result: Extensive experiments demonstrate that the advantage-guided gating framework effectively enhances the performance of benchmark MLLMs in visual-based spatial understanding and reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: The advantage-guided gating framework provides an effective mechanism for improving reasoning stability and accuracy in MLLMs for spatial tasks, with publicly released code for research.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have demonstrated significant potential in complex spatial scene understanding and reasoning tasks. However, their open-ended reasoning process is prone to decision errors and error accumulation, leading to instability in answer quality. To address this, we propose an advantage-guided gating framework that dynamically intervenes in and corrects deviations during the reasoning process. Specifically, we model step-by-step reasoning as a finite-horizon decision process and introduce Monte Carlo value evaluation on the reasoning tree to provide intermediate supervision signals. The framework includes Step-Advantage Gate and Trajectory-Advantage Gate, which dynamically select high-value reasoning steps and high-quality complete reasoning trajectories, respectively. During training, we perform supervised learning for the gates using reasoning trees generated via multi-branch sampling, and combine shared-parameter initialization with task-specific heads to achieve cross-task robustness and diversity. During inference, the model greedily selects high-value prefix reasoning steps while choosing the optimal reasoning head based on the problem type, thereby significantly improving the accuracy of the final answer. Furthermore, we constructed the Reasoning-Tree-160k dataset and performed two-stage learning on it. Extensive experiments demonstrate that this advantage-guided gating framework effectively enhances the performance of benchmark MLLMs in visual-based spatial understanding and reasoning tasks. The code is open to the public for research: https://github.com/LingLin-ll/Advantage-Guided-Gate.

</details>


### [64] [MRBench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Human Motion-Text Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07993)
*Fulong Liu,Liang Xu,Chengqun Yang,Yuhao Zhang,Yichao Yan,Xiaokang Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Introduces MRBench, a comprehensive motion-text retrieval benchmark with heterogeneous motions, balanced categories, and multi-granular descriptions, plus a lightweight granularity-aware retrieval model that improves mixed-granularity performance without sacrificing standard-caption results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing motion-text retrieval benchmarks suffer from homogeneous indoor motions, imbalanced distributions, and oversimplified repetitive texts, hindering reliable measurement of cross-domain and cross-granularity alignment.

Method: Constructs MRBench via a multi-stage curation pipeline (filtering, balancing, semantic verification, multi-granularity caption generation) yielding 3,390 motions across 4 sources and 118 categories with 10,170 captions. Proposes a granularity-aware model built on a frozen standard-caption-aligned backbone, using LLM-generated captions for pseudo-supervision of granularity-specific extractors/adapters and granularity-aware score fusion for inference.

Result: Evaluations reveal significant cross-dataset generalization gaps and sensitivity to query granularity in existing baselines. The proposed granularity-aware model improves mixed-granularity retrieval without degrading standard-caption performance.

Conclusion: MRBench serves as a comprehensive testbed for motion-language alignment evaluation, and the granularity-aware approach demonstrates an effective solution for handling varied description granularities in retrieval.

Abstract: Human motion-text retrieval provides a rigorous means of assessing cross-modal alignment. Prevailing benchmarks are dominated by homogeneous indoor motions, imbalanced motion distributions, and oversimplified, repetitive texts, which hinder the reliable measurement of cross-domain and cross-granularity alignment. We thus introduce MRBench, a comprehensive motion-text retrieval benchmark featuring heterogeneous motions, broad and balanced category coverage, and reliable, discriminative, multi-granular descriptions. MRBench is constructed through a meticulously designed multi-stage data curation pipeline, which filters and balances candidates, verifies unambiguous semantic alignment, and generates motion-grounded descriptions at multiple granularities. The resulting benchmark contains 3,390 motions drawn from motion capture, in-the-wild videos, synthetic videos, and motion generative models, covering 118 fine-grained categories. Each motion is paired with concise, standard, and fine-grained descriptions, yielding 10,170 captions. Extensive evaluations of representative retrieval baselines on MRBench reveal a substantial cross-dataset generalization gap and pronounced sensitivity to query granularity. We propose a lightweight granularity-aware model anchored at a frozen standard-caption-aligned retrieval model. LLM-based concise and fine-grained captions provide pseudo-supervision for extra-branch granularity-specific motion extractors and text adapters. For inference, granularity-aware score fusion integrates global and adapted similarities while strictly maintaining score comparability across all description levels. The resulting model improves mixed-granularity retrieval without compromising standard-caption performance. We believe that our MRBench provides a comprehensive testbed for advancing motion-language alignment evaluation.

</details>


### [65] [PE-Mamba: Bidirectional Selective Layer Aggregation for AI-Generated Image Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07999)
*Kutub Uddin,Nusrat Tasnim,Khalid Malik*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PE-Mamba is a novel AI-generated image detection framework built on a pre-trained PE-Core vision transformer with lightweight LoRA adaptation, featuring three complementary cross-layer feature aggregation components (BSA, SWA, SGA) that achieve state-of-the-art performance on UniversalFakeDetect (96.6% mACC) and AIGCDetect (95.3% mACC) while training only 1.3% of total parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI-generated image detection is increasingly difficult due to rapid advances in generative models and the shrinking gap between synthetic and authentic content. Existing vision transformer-based detectors using weighted-sum aggregation overlook the ordered semantic progression of hierarchical features across transformer layers, from shallow texture cues to deep semantic representations.

Method: The proposed PE-Mamba framework uses a pre-trained PE-Core vision transformer with LoRA adaptation and introduces three components: (1) a bidirectional selective aggregator (BSA) that performs forward (shallow-to-deep evidence accumulation) and backward (deep-to-shallow contextual refinement) selective scans on classification tokens; (2) a softmax-weighted aggregator (SWA) computing a learned global summary of all layer tokens; and (3) a sigmoid-gated blend (SGA) that adaptively fuses BSA and SWA outputs via a learnable scalar gate to balance directional sequential evidence and global aggregation.

Result: PE-Mamba achieves 96.6% mACC and 99.5% mAP on UniversalFakeDetect, and 95.3% mACC and 98.1% mAP on AIGCDetect, outperforming 18 detectors with superior generalization across diverse generative models while training only 1.3% of total parameters (0.13% for LoRA alone).

Conclusion: The experiments demonstrate that PE-Mamba achieves state-of-the-art AI-generated image detection performance with superior generalization across diverse generative models and significantly reduced training cost, suggesting that structured bidirectional cross-layer aggregation is more effective than simple weighted-sum strategies for forensic detection.

Abstract: AI-generated image (AIGI) detection has become increasingly challenging due to the rapid advancement of generative models and the diminishing gap between synthetic and authentic content. Existing vision transformer-based detectors commonly rely on weighted-sum strategies to aggregate intermediate representations across transformer layers, often overlooking the inherently ordered semantic progression of hierarchical features from shallow texture cues to deep semantic representations. In this work, we propose \textbf{PE-Mamba}, a novel framework built upon a pre-trained PE-Core vision transformer with lightweight LoRA adaptation that introduces three complementary components for cross-layer feature aggregation and fusion. First, a bidirectional selective aggregator (BSA) processes layer-wise classification tokens through forward and backward selective scans, where the forward scan progressively accumulates shallow-to-deep forensic evidence, and the backward scan performs deep-to-shallow contextual refinement to reinterpret low-level cues in light of high-level semantic context. Second, a softmax-weighted aggregator (SWA) computes a learned global summary of all layer tokens as a complementary aggregation path. Third, a sigmoid-gated blend (SGA) adaptively fuses the BSA and SWA outputs via a learnable scalar gate, allowing the model to dynamically balance directional sequential evidence and global layer-wise aggregation. Extensive experiments on UniversalFakeDetect (96.6\% mACC, 99.5\% mAP) and AIGCDetect (95.3\% mACC, 98.1\% mAP) demonstrate that \methodname{} outperforms 18 detectors with superior generalization across diverse generative models, while training only 1.3\% of total parameters (0.13\% for LoRA alone).

</details>


### [66] [Evidence-Grounded Forensic Reasoning for Detecting and Grounding Multi-Modal Media Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08009)
*Yichun Yeh,Yiheng Li,Xiaobo Hu,Zhen Lei,Yang Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes EFR, an Evidence-Grounded Forensic Reasoning framework for Detecting and Grounding Multi-Modal Media Manipulation (DGM4). It introduces an Anchor-and-Verify reasoning chain with modality-isolated perception, a verifiable reward system for evidence-conclusion consistency, and a Modality-Decoupled Advantage (MDA) routing mechanism to improve both detection accuracy and explainability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing DGM4 detection methods are black-box and lack decision rationale, which limits their reliability in forensic practice. While MLLMs offer explainability potential, they face two challenges: explanations disconnected from predicted evidence locations (unverified attribution), and unreliable joint training due to uniform signals that fail to distinguish localization tokens from classification tokens.

Method: EFR framework includes: (1) an Anchor-and-Verify reasoning chain enforcing modality-isolated perception before cross-modal comparison, using conclusion coordinates as explicit anchors for spatial evidence correspondence; (2) a verifiable reward system enforcing evidence-conclusion consistency during training; and (3) a Modality-Decoupled Advantage (MDA) routing mechanism to mitigate credit misassignment across prediction tasks.

Result: EFR achieves state-of-the-art performance on DGM4 and produces structured forensic reasoning records that explicitly bind explanations to evidence.

Conclusion: The proposed framework demonstrates that enforcing evidence-grounding in MLLM-based multi-modal manipulation detection yields both superior detection performance and verifiable, explainable forensic reasoning.

Abstract: Fake news increasingly relies on cross-modal image-text forgeries, making transparent and verifiable reasoning chains an urgent need for Detecting and Grounding Multi-Modal Media Manipulation (DGM4). Existing methods produce black-box detection results without any decision rationale, limiting their reliability in forensic practice. Multi-modal Large Language Models (MLLMs) offer a natural path toward explainability, but applying them to DGM4 raises two difficulties. First, models tend to generate explanations disconnected from predicted evidence locations, producing unverified attribution. Second, enforcing evidence-conclusion consistency requires active optimization, yet uniform training signals fail to distinguish localization tokens from classification tokens, making multi-head joint training unreliable. We propose a multi-modal manipulation detector based on an Evidence-Grounded Forensic Reasoning (EFR) framework. EFR introduces an Anchor-and-Verify reasoning chain that enforces modality-isolated perception before cross-modal comparison, with conclusion coordinates as explicit anchors to which downstream evidence must spatially correspond. A verifiable reward system then enforces evidence-conclusion consistency during training, while a Modality-Decoupled Advantage (MDA) routing mechanism mitigats credit misassignment across prediction tasks. Experiments show that EFR achieves state-of-the-art performance while producing structured forensic reasoning records that explicitly bind explanations to evidence.

</details>


### [67] [EgoTrack3D: A Modular Framework for Egocentric 3D Object Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08016)
*Jan Kulik,Bjarni Dagur Thor Karason,Yung-Hsu Yang,Boyang Sun,Marc Pollefeys,Xi Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: EgoTrack3D is a modular framework that reconstructs and maintains dynamic 3D scene representations from egocentric RGB video, using point-based motion scoring and voxel-based merging heuristics to track both static and dynamic objects, achieving 11% improvement in PCL on the ADT dataset while remaining robust under degraded conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding 3D scenes from egocentric video is critical for robotics and autonomous navigation, but rapid viewpoint changes and partial occlusions make structured representation building challenging. Existing methods either handle explicit interactions or assume static scenes, limiting their ability to capture complex dynamics.

Method: EgoTrack3D lifts 2D segmentation masks into a global 3D coordinate frame, employing a point-based motion scoring mechanism alongside a voxel-based merging heuristic for object track association. It also demonstrates robustness under degraded conditions by replacing dense depth maps with sparse 3D bounding box estimation and integrating interaction-guided dynamic association.

Result: EgoTrack3D achieves an 11% improvement in percentage of correct locations (PCL) relative to the strongest baseline on the Aria Digital Twin (ADT) dataset, and maintains accurate representations under degraded, noisy observation conditions.

Conclusion: EgoTrack3D addresses the more general setting of persistent 3D tracking for both static and dynamic objects in egocentric video, demonstrating robustness under real-world deployment constraints while outperforming existing baselines.

Abstract: Understanding 3D scenes from egocentric video is fundamental for robotics and autonomous navigation, yet rapid viewpoint changes and partial occlusions make building structured representations challenging. Existing 3D tracking and scene graph construction methods primarily address explicit interactions or assume static scenes, limiting their ability to capture complex dynamics. We introduce EgoTrack3D, a modular framework that reconstructs and maintains a dynamic 3D scene representation directly from egocentric RGB video. The framework lifts 2D segmentation masks into a global 3D coordinate frame, using a point-based motion scoring mechanism alongside a voxel-based merging heuristic to associate object tracks. EgoTrack3D maintains accurate representations over time, achieving an 11% improvement in percentage of correct locations (PCL) relative to the strongest baseline on the Aria Digital Twin (ADT) dataset, while addressing the more general setting of persistent 3D tracking for both static and dynamic objects. Furthermore, to demonstrate the system's robustness under degraded conditions that simulate real-world deployment constraints, we replace dense depth maps with sparse 3D bounding box estimation and integrate interaction-guided dynamic association, enabling EgoTrack3D to maintain accurate spatial representations despite noisy observations.

</details>


### [68] [Evidence-RL: Towards Evidence-intensive Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08021)
*Haojie Huang,Xinlei Yu,Chengming Xu,Zhangquan Chen,Cheng Yang,Qingdong He,Yu Yang,Jiangning Zhang,Xiaobin Hu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CED is a training-time audit that neutralizes object-centric evidence regions and compares support drops against non-evidence regions, combined with answer correctness in GRPO to reward correct answers that rely on genuine evidence rather than shortcuts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLMs often answer from language priors, dataset shortcuts, or irrelevant visual context rather than concrete image evidence. Existing perception-aware post-training methods use perturbations or attention proxies but fail to test whether a sampled answer causally depends on the specific local evidence supporting it.

Method: For each response, CED neutralizes an object-centric Evidence Region and compares the resulting support drop against matched non-evidence Regions. This signal is combined with answer correctness inside GRPO, rewarding correct answers that rely on the evidence path rather than shortcut or nuisance paths. CED uses weak object-level proposals, requires no question-specific evidence annotations, and adds no inference-time overhead.

Result: Across nine public benchmarks and four backbones, CED outperforms prior RL-based post-training methods, with targeted analyses verifying its object-centric signal.

Conclusion: CED provides an effective training-time counterfactual evidence audit that improves VLM grounding by ensuring answers causally depend on local image evidence, outperforming prior RL-based post-training approaches across diverse benchmarks and backbones.

Abstract: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) should answer from concrete image evidence rather than language priors, dataset shortcuts, or irrelevant visual context. Existing perception-aware post-training methods encourage image use through global perturbations or attention proxies, but they do not test whether a sampled answer causally depends on the local evidence that supports it. We propose Counterfactual Evidence Disentanglement (CED), a training-time evidence audit for VLM grounding. For each response, CED neutralizes an object-centric Evidence Region and compares the resulting support drop against matched non-evidence Regions.
