We combine this signal with answer correctness inside GRPO, rewarding correct answers that rely on the evidence path rather than shortcut or nuisance paths. CED uses weak object-level proposals, requires no question-specific evidence annotations, and adds no inference-time overhead. Across nine public benchmarks and four backbones, CED outperforms prior RL-based post-training methods, with targeted analyses verifying its object-centric signal.

</details>


### [69] [ZOMP: Zeroth-Order Multi-Modal Prompt Tuning for Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08060)
*Sajjad Ghiasvand,Yifan Yang,Mahnoosh Alizadeh,Ramtin Pedarsani*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ZOMP is a query-efficient, fully forward-only (backpropagation-free) method for prompt tuning of frozen CLIP models. It tunes deep prompts in both vision and text branches using simultaneous perturbation stochastic approximation, combining cross-modal low-rank reparameterization, gradient-correction momentum, and a budget-indexed rank schedule. It outperforms prior BP-free methods across 13 benchmarks under a matched 5,000-query budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-tuning vision-language models like CLIP requires backpropagation through the full model, which is infeasible in forward-pass-only settings common for edge devices and proprietary models. Prior BP-free zeroth-order prompt-tuning methods either tune prompts in a single modality or require thousands of forward passes for convergence, which is impractical under realistic query budgets.

Method: ZOMP tunes deep prompts in both vision and text branches of a frozen CLIP model using simultaneous perturbation stochastic approximation (SPSA). It combines three key components: (1) cross-modal low-rank reparameterization that shares a factor between the two branches to keep search dimensionality small, (2) a gradient-correction momentum term to stabilize noisy zeroth-order estimates, and (3) a budget-indexed rank schedule that progressively unlocks model capacity as the query budget is consumed.

Result: Across 13 vision-language benchmarks under a matched 5,000-query budget, ZOMP consistently outperforms prior BP-free prompt-tuning methods in both few-shot accuracy and query efficiency. It also generalizes better across base-to-new class generalization, cross-dataset transfer, and out-of-distribution settings.

Conclusion: Jointly exploiting multimodality and low-rank structure is an effective route to practical, query-efficient backpropagation-free prompt tuning, enabling competitive performance even in forward-pass-only deployment scenarios.

Abstract: Fine-tuning vision-language models such as CLIP typically requires backpropagation (BP) through the full model, which is infeasible when only forward-pass access is available, as is common for memory-constrained edge devices and proprietary model deployments. Prior BP-free, zeroth-order prompt-tuning methods avoid this requirement but often tune prompts in a single modality or optimize over a search space large enough that convergence requires thousands of forward passes, which is impractical under realistic query budgets. We propose ZOMP (Zeroth-Order Multimodal Prompt tuning), a query-efficient, fully forward-only method that tunes deep prompts in both the vision and text branches of a frozen CLIP model using simultaneous perturbation stochastic approximation. ZOMP combines three ingredients: a cross-modal low-rank reparameterization that ties the two branches through a shared factor and keeps the effective search dimensionality small, a gradient-correction momentum term that stabilizes the noisy zeroth-order estimate, and a budget-indexed rank schedule that unlocks capacity as the query budget is spent. Across 13 vision-language benchmarks under a matched 5,000-query budget, ZOMP consistently outperforms prior BP-free prompt-tuning methods in both few-shot accuracy and query efficiency, and it generalizes better across base-to-new, cross-dataset transfer, and out-of-distribution settings. Our results show that jointly exploiting multimodality and low-rank structure is an effective route to practical, query-efficient BP-free prompt tuning.

</details>


### [70] [EvBS: Event-guided Blur Synthesis for Domain-adaptive Motion Deblurring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08066)
*Junsik Jung,Seokryun Choi,Yoonki Cho,Woo Jae Kim,Andrew Jeong,Sung-Eui Yoon*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: EvBS is an event-guided blur synthesis framework that decouples motion from visual content using event cameras' high temporal resolution, generating diverse training pairs via intrinsic and extrinsic blur synthesis to adapt pre-trained deblurring models to target domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pre-trained deblurring models suffer performance degradation in real-world scenarios due to domain shift between training and testing distributions, and existing synthesis methods are constrained by the inherent entanglement between motion and visual content.

Method: EvBS leverages event cameras to decouple motion from content, using two complementary strategies: Intrinsic-Blur Synthesis (blurring sharp content with its own motion patterns) and Extrinsic-Blur Synthesis (transferring motion from blurry patches to distinct sharp content), generating diverse training pairs for fine-tuning pre-trained models.

Result: Extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate that EvBS effectively enhances the robustness of existing deblurring models on unseen testing datasets, achieving enhanced domain-adaptive deblurring performance.

Conclusion: By breaking the inherent constraints of naturally coupled motion and content through event-guided synthesis, EvBS provides an effective framework for domain adaptation in motion deblurring.

Abstract: Motion deblurring has achieved remarkable progress with deep learning, yet pre-trained deblurring models often suffer from performance degradation in real-world scenarios due to the domain shift between training and testing distributions. To remedy this, we propose EvBS, an event-guided blur synthesis framework that generates diverse training pairs for calibrating pre-trained models to the target domain. While existing methods are constrained by the inherent entanglement between motion and visual content, our method leverages the high temporal resolution of event cameras to effectively decouple them. This enables us to utilize not only the intrinsic motion that is inherent to the given content but also extrinsic motion transferred from different sources within the target domain, thereby facilitating effective adaptation via fine-tuning. Specifically, EvBS comprises two complementary strategies: Intrinsic-Blur Synthesis, which blurs sharp contents with their own motion patterns, and Extrinsic-Blur Synthesis, which transfers motion from blurry patches to distinct sharp content. This approach generates a diverse set of training pairs that break the inherent constraints of naturally coupled motion and content, resulting in enhanced domain-adaptive deblurring performance. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate that EvBS effectively enhances the robustness of existing deblurring models on unseen testing datasets.

</details>


### [71] [NeuroGuard: Neural Gradient Update Aware of Representation Damage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08068)
*Taigo Sakai,Kazuhito Hotta*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: NeuroGuard is a parameter-free update-control method for long-tailed class-incremental learning that adaptively controls gradient scaling at task boundaries via three components (AGS, CRK, FBE), improving over the DGR baseline across all five LT-CIL settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LT-CIL methods focus on replay, classifiers, or losses, but overlook how strongly the feature representation should be updated at each task boundary—a factor that can significantly impact learning from imbalanced streams while retaining old classes.

Method: NeuroGuard adds three components to DGR without learnable parameters: (1) Adaptive Gradient Scaling (AGS) converts teacher uncertainty into a task-wise gradient scale; (2) Confidence-Ranked Knowledge Distillation Reweighting (CRK) assigns larger KD weights to replay samples where the teacher is less decisive; (3) Fragility-Blended Entropy Gate (FBE) incorporates old-memory leakage into the scaling decision.

Result: NeuroGuard improves over DGR in all five LT-CIL settings, achieves the best task-agnostic accuracy in four main benchmarks, improves both old- and new-class accuracy, and consistently improves medium-frequency accuracy. AGS outperforms a fixed-scale control in all five settings, showing boundary-specific scaling beats uniform scaling.

Conclusion: Adaptive, context-aware gradient scaling at task boundaries is more effective than generic gradient suppression, demonstrating that controlling feature update strength is a valuable and orthogonal direction for improving LT-CIL performance.

Abstract: Long-tailed class-incremental learning (LT-CIL) must learn new classes from imbalanced streams while retaining old classes. Existing methods mainly change replay, classifiers, or losses. We study a different factor, namely how strongly the feature representation should be updated at each task boundary. We propose NeuroGuard, an update-control method added to DGR, a replay-based LT-CIL baseline, without adding learnable parameters. NeuroGuard preserves DGR's replay memory, classifier, and set of loss terms. Adaptive Gradient Scaling (AGS) converts teacher uncertainty into one task-wise gradient scale. Confidence-Ranked Knowledge Distillation Reweighting (CRK) gives larger knowledge-distillation weights to replay samples that the teacher predicts less decisively. Fragility-Blended Entropy Gate (FBE) adds old-memory leakage to the scale decision. Across five LT-CIL settings, NeuroGuard improves over DGR in every setting. In the four main benchmark comparisons, it achieves the best task-agnostic accuracy among the compared methods. The gains extend to both old- and new-class accuracy, while medium-frequency accuracy improves consistently across all five settings. Controlled comparisons show that the gain does not come from generic gradient suppression: AGS outperforms a matched fixed-scale control in all five settings, demonstrating that boundary-specific scaling is more effective than applying the same average scale throughout learning.

</details>


### [72] [SCTD 3.0: Sonar Common Target Detection in the Wild - A Large-Scale, Multi-Scene Dataset from Real Marine Surveys](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08106)
*Peng Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SCTD 3.0 is a large-scale real-measured Synthetic Aperture Sonar (SAS) dataset containing over 10,000 image snippets from multi-frequency systems covering ten target categories across diverse seabed environments, with a hierarchical annotation protocol for fine-grained attributes and a multi-task benchmark for detection, classification, and attribute prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large-scale, high-quality SAS datasets are scarce, and existing benchmarks are small and limited to single scenarios, failing to capture the complex acoustic scattering, diverse seabeds, and multi-pose imaging conditions found in real underwater detection.

Method: The paper collects over 10,000 real SAS image snippets from multi-frequency systems (240 kHz, 450 kHz, and others), covering ten target categories across varied seabed geomorphologies with multiple observation angles and ranges. They establish a rigorous hierarchical annotation protocol that decouples intrinsic physical properties, deployment characteristics, and scattering phenomena. They also construct a multi-task benchmark evaluating mainstream deep learning models under cross-domain, cross-scene, cross-frequency, and cross-view generalization.

Result: SCTD 3.0 provides a large-scale real-measured dataset and multi-task benchmark for object detection, fine-grained classification, and attribute prediction, enabling evaluation of deep learning models under challenging cross-domain generalization settings.

Conclusion: SCTD 3.0 fills the gap in large-scale, diverse SAS datasets and is expected to serve as a critical data cornerstone for robust underwater target perception in open-water environments.

Abstract: Synthetic Aperture Sonar (SAS) is core for wide-area detection of small underwater targets. However, large-scale, high-quality SAS datasets are scarce, hindering data-driven recognition. Existing benchmarks are small and limited to single scenarios, failing to reproduce complex acoustic scattering, diverse seabeds, and multi-pose imaging in real detection. To fill this gap, we introduce SCTD 3.0 - a large-scale real-measured dataset for Sonar Common Target Detection in the Wild in natural waters. It contains over 10,000 high-quality real SAS image snippets from multi-frequency systems (240 kHz, 450 kHz, and others), covering ten typical target categories across varied seabed geomorphologies, with multiple observation angles, detection ranges, and frequency bands. We establish a rigorous hierarchical annotation protocol that decouples labeling of intrinsic physical properties, deployment characteristics, and scattering phenomena - covering material, geometry, internal structure, burial state, shadow integrity, specular highlights, edge diffraction, and resonance effects. This enables fine-grained target characterization. We also construct a multi-task benchmark for object detection, fine-grained classification, and attribute prediction, evaluating mainstream deep learning models under cross-domain, cross-scene, cross-frequency, and cross-view generalization. SCTD 3.0 is expected to provide a critical data cornerstone for robust underwater target perception in open-water environments. SCTD 3.0 is available at https://github.com/automlresearch/SCTD-3.0.

</details>


### [73] [SUMI: Scalable Unified Model for 3D Point Cloud Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08115)
*Yanlong LI,Kanchana Thilakarathna*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SUMI is a diffusion-enhanced refinement module for coarse-to-fine point cloud completion that injects noisy geometric features into cross-attention with coarse structural features, enabling reverse denoising to refine local geometry while preserving global consistency. It improves over strong baselines on PCN, ShapeNet-55/34, and MVP benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing coarse-to-fine point cloud completion methods improve global structure recovery but the fine stage is limited by simple upsampling and insufficient interaction with coarse structural features, making local detail reconstruction challenging.

Method: SUMI injects noisy geometric features into cross-attention with coarse structural features, using reverse denoising to refine local geometry while preserving global consistency. Unlike prior diffusion-based methods that treat diffusion as a standalone point generator, SUMI serves as a flexible refinement module that can be integrated into existing coarse-to-fine models.

Result: SUMI achieves the best overall CD and F1-score on PCN, reduces CD by up to 16.1% on ShapeNet-55, and obtains the best CD across all output densities on MVP.

Conclusion: Diffusion-based refinement integrated with coarse structural features via cross-attention provides a more effective paradigm for local detail reconstruction in point cloud completion compared to standalone diffusion generation approaches.

Abstract: Point cloud completion commonly follows a coarse-to-fine paradigm, where a low-density coarse shape is first predicted and then upsampled to the target resolution. Although recent methods have improved global structure recovery, the fine stage often remains limited by simple upsampling and insufficient interaction with coarse structural features, making local detail reconstruction challenging. We propose SUMI, a diffusion-enhanced refinement module for coarse-to-fine point cloud completion. Unlike prior diffusion-based completion methods that use diffusion as a standalone point generator, SUMI injects noisy geometric features into cross-attention with coarse structural features, enabling reverse denoising to refine local geometry while preserving global consistency. SUMI can also be integrated into existing coarse-to-fine models as a flexible refinement module. Experiments on PCN, ShapeNet-55/34, and MVP demonstrate consistent improvements over strong baselines. SUMI achieves the best overall CD and F1-score on PCN, reduces CD by up to 16.1% on ShapeNet-55, and obtains the best CD across all output densities on MVP.

</details>


### [74] [Staying True to the Origin: Continuous Image Stylization with Smooth Transitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08125)
*Rui Xu,Hanmo Zhang,Songhua Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a two-stage training strategy with style-strength-aware spline interpolation to enable continuous control over stylization strength in Diffusion Transformer (DiT)-based multi-reference editing models, allowing smooth transitions between content preservation and strong stylization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing generative models struggle with image stylization because preserving the semantic content of the input image while referencing style patterns from another image is challenging and often leads to uncontrollable results. The authors aim to enable precise, continuous control over stylization strength—moving beyond binary or discrete stylization levels.

Method: The method uses a two-stage training strategy. In the first stage, the model is trained to produce strongly stylized outputs while preserving content semantics. In the second stage, with the base model frozen, a set of anchor projectors is learned that maps various stylization strengths into the model parameter space. During inference, style-strength-aware spline interpolation is performed in a low-rank space to enable continuous control over stylization strength, even though training uses only a few discrete strength levels.

Result: Extensive experiments demonstrate that the method supports precise and continuous manipulation of stylization strength while generating high-fidelity results with modern DiT-based models.

Conclusion: The proposed approach successfully enables continuous control over stylization strength in DiT-based editing models, achieving both faithful content preservation and strong stylization effects with smooth transitions between the two.

Abstract: Recent advances in generative models have achieved remarkable performance in text- and image-conditioned editing. However, preserving the content of a given image while referencing style patterns from another remains challenging, often leading to uncontrollable stylization results. In this paper, we approach image stylization from the perspective of continuous control, aiming to enable modern Diffusion Transformer (DiT)-based multi-reference editing models to (1) faithfully preserve the semantic structure of the content image, (2) render strong stylization effects, and (3) smoothly transition between the two. To this end, we propose a simple yet effective two-stage training strategy along with a style-strength-aware spline formulation. Specifically, in the first stage, the model is trained to produce strongly stylized outputs while preserving the content semantics as much as possible. In the second stage, with the base model frozen, we learn a set of anchor projectors that map various stylization strengths into the model parameter space. During inference, by performing style-strength-aware spline interpolation in a low-rank space, our method enables continuous control over stylization strength, even though the model is trained with only a few discrete strength levels. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method supports precise and continuous manipulation of stylization strength while generating high-fidelity results with modern DiT models. Project page: https://reychiaro.github.io/StyleController.

</details>


### [75] [When Does An Extra View Help? Adapting Single-View 3D Reconstruction with Extra Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08132)
*Y Huynh,Duc Thanh Nguyen,Thao Minh Le,Mohamed Abdelrazek*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ASV3D is a framework that adapts single-view 3D object reconstruction to test-time data using one additional image as support, offering both zero-shot and optimized (contrastive learning-based) adaptation strategies to improve reconstruction quality and cross-view consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single-image 3D reconstruction suffers from missing viewpoint information that is critical for completing 3D structures. While an additional view could help resolve this, there is no existing mechanism to integrate extra views into the single-view reconstruction principle.

Method: ASV3D proposes two adaptation strategies: (i) a zero-shot adaptation scheme that uses an auxiliary image to improve reconstruction quality without retraining, and (ii) an optimized adaptation scheme that employs contrastive learning to enhance visual fidelity and cross-view consistency.

Result: Applied to two state-of-the-art single-view 3D reconstruction pipelines, ASV3D consistently improves reconstruction accuracy and robustness under unconstrained multi-view inputs, outperforming baselines in both quantitative metrics and human preference on benchmark and real-world datasets.

Conclusion: ASV3D effectively integrates an additional view into single-view 3D reconstruction, demonstrating improved accuracy and robustness across multiple datasets and pipelines, with code and a real-world dataset released publicly.

Abstract: Reconstruction of 3D objects from a single image is a challenging research problem in computer vision. The key challenge is the lack of critical information from viewpoints to complete 3D structures. Using an additional view may help to resolve the issue. However, there is no mechanism that can integrate the extra view into the single-view 3D reconstruction principle. We address this challenge by proposing ASV3D, a framework for adapting single-view 3D object reconstruction to test-time data with support from one additional image. We introduce two adaptation strategies: (i) a zero-shot adaptation scheme that leverages the auxiliary image to improve the reconstruction quality of an object without retraining, and (ii) an optimised adaptation scheme that further enhances visual fidelity and cross-view consistency via contrastive learning. We apply our ASV3D to improve two state-of-the-art single-view 3D reconstruction pipelines on both benchmark and real-world datasets. Results demonstrate that our approach consistently improves reconstruction accuracy and robustness under unconstrained multi-view inputs, outperforming the baselines in both quantitative metrics and human preference. We publish our code and the real-world object dataset in our project page at https://github.com/YNhuHuynh/ASV3D/tree/main.

</details>


### [76] [Compositional Cross-Modality Translation via Whole-Volume Multitask Latent Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08135)
*Daniele Molino,Alessio Zoboli,Camillo Maria Caruso,Valerio Guarrasi,Paolo Soda*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces a whole-volume, multi-task cross-modality medical image translation framework that uses a large-scale pretrained 3D variational autoencoder to provide a strong volumetric prior, reducing translation to conditional flow-matching in latent space. This enables joint training across inter- and intra-modality tasks, outperforming patch-based methods and enabling zero-shot generalization and compositional cross-dataset translation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cross-modality medical image translation is limited by two coupled problems: methods operate on 2D slices or 3D patches rather than whole volumes, and each translation task requires a separate model. Both issues stem from the absence of a strong volumetric prior, which makes learning anatomical appearance and cross-modality mapping simultaneously an ill-posed problem given the scale of available paired datasets.

Method: The authors decouple anatomical appearance learning from cross-modality mapping. A large-scale pretrained 3D variational autoencoder captures a compact latent representation of volumetric appearance. Translation is reduced to a conditional flow-matching problem in this latent space, making whole-volume processing tractable. A resolution-aware sampling strategy preserves native anatomical scale. A single model is trained jointly across inter-modality tasks (MRI→CT, CBCT→CT) and intra-modality tasks (MRI→MRI) over three multi-center datasets.

Result: Whole-volume processing outperforms patch-based counterparts across all tasks. The multi-task model matches task-specific baselines while replacing N networks with one. Joint training enables zero-shot generalization to unseen anatomical regions within 0.15 SSIM of the fully supervised model, and compositional cross-dataset translation along never-supervised paths.

Conclusion: Combining a strong volumetric prior with multitask training is a scalable route toward medical image synthesis systems that generalize beyond their training distribution.

Abstract: Cross-modality medical image translation can reduce the burden of multi-modal acquisitions, yet the field remains constrained by two coupled limitations: methods operate on 2D slices or 3D patches rather than whole volumes, and train a separate model for each translation task. Both stem from a single cause, the absence of a sufficiently strong volumetric prior, which forces generative models to learn anatomical appearance and cross-modality mapping simultaneously, an ill-posed problem at the scale of available paired datasets. We propose to decouple these objectives. A large-scale pretrained 3D variational autoencoder provides a compact latent representation of volumetric appearance, reducing translation to a conditional flow-matching problem. This compression makes whole-volume processing tractable, while a resolution-aware sampling strategy preserves native anatomical scale. We train a single model jointly across inter-modality (MRI$\to$CT, CBCT$\to$CT) and intra-modality (MRI$\to$MRI) tasks over three multi-center datasets. Across all tasks, whole-volume processing outperforms its patch-based counterpart, and the multi-task model matches task-specific baselines while replacing $N$ networks with one. Crucially, joint training unlocks capabilities inaccessible to task-specific approaches: zero-shot generalization to anatomical regions unseen during training, within 0.15 SSIM of the fully supervised model, and compositional cross-dataset translation along paths never directly supervised. These results suggest that combining a strong volumetric prior with multitask training is a scalable route toward synthesis systems that generalize beyond their training distribution. Code is available at https://github.com/arco-group/Whole-Volume-Latent-FM.

</details>


### [77] [EFFEKT: Efficient Federated Knowledge Transfer to Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08138)
*Matteo Caligiuri,Francesco Barbato,Pietro Zanuttigh,Francesco Restuccia*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: EFFEKT is a multi-domain federated learning framework where lightweight client-side proxy models collaborate with a server-side Foundation Model (FM) to learn new concepts without sharing private data. It uses domain-specific LoRA adapters trained server-side with feature-space alignment preserved via novel bi-directional cross-distillation strategies. Experiments on real-world datasets and low-power edge devices show improvements over state-of-the-art baselines while keeping client-side computation lightweight.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated Learning faces challenges in resource-constrained settings because increasing model sizes impose substantial computational demands on client devices, limiting FL applicability in such environments.

Method: The framework pairs lightweight client-side proxy models with a server-side Foundation Model. It enables efficient server-side training of domain-specific LoRA adapters while preserving feature-space alignment between the FM and proxy extractors via novel bi-directional cross-distillation strategies.

Result: Experiments on multiple real-world datasets and deployments on low-power edge devices demonstrate improvements over state-of-the-art baselines in most considered domains while maintaining lightweight computation at the client side.

Conclusion: EFFEKT effectively addresses the computational bottleneck of FL in resource-constrained settings by distributing learning across lightweight client proxies and a powerful server-side foundation model, achieving better performance without sacrificing client efficiency or privacy.

Abstract: Recent data protection laws have accelerated the adoption of Federated Learning (FL) for privacy-preserving decentralized training. Nevertheless, increasing model sizes impose substantial computational demands on client devices, limiting FL applicability in resource-constrained settings. We introduce a novel multi-domain federated learning framework in which lightweight client-side proxy models collaborate with a server-side Foundation Model (FM) to learn new concepts without sharing private data. Our approach, EFFEKT, enables efficient server-side training of domain-specific LoRA adapters while preserving feature-space alignment between the FM and proxy extractors via novel bi-directional cross-distillation strategies. Experiments on multiple real-world datasets and deployments on low-power edge devices demonstrate improvements over state-of-the-art baselines in most considered domains while maintaining lightweight computation at the client side.

</details>


### [78] [Learning Structural Illumination for Unsupervised Low-light Enhancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08153)
*Tianle Du,Peiyuan He,Hainuo Wang,Tianxiu Yu,Xiaojie Guo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RISE is an unsupervised low-light image enhancement framework that decouples relative illumination structure from absolute exposure, infers it from reliable bright regions, and uses a Dual-Metering Exposure Reference for scene-adaptive enhancement, achieving state-of-the-art performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing unsupervised LLIE methods fail to separate spatially varying illumination patterns from absolute exposure levels, are biased by unreliable low SNR regions, and rely on fixed exposure targets that limit adaptation across diverse lighting conditions.

Method: RISE decouples relative illumination structure from absolute exposure, infers the illumination structure from reliable bright regions only, and introduces a Dual-Metering Exposure Reference derived from each input to adapt enhancement strength per scene.

Result: RISE achieves state-of-the-art performance among unsupervised LLIE methods on benchmarks and real-world generalization experiments, producing visually natural results.

Conclusion: By separating illumination structure from exposure and adapting to per-scene lighting conditions via dual-metering, RISE demonstrates that robust and interpretable unsupervised enhancement is achievable across diverse lighting scenarios.

Abstract: Existing unsupervised low-light image enhancement (LLIE) methods often estimate illumination directly from the entire low-light input, without separating its spatially varying illumination pattern, termed relative illumination structure, from the absolute exposure level or preventing unreliable low signal-to-noise ratio regions from biasing the estimate. Moreover, fixed exposure targets impose a scene-agnostic enhancement criterion, limiting adaptation across diverse lighting conditions. Inspired by the spatial propagation of light, we propose a Relative Illumination Structure Estimation (RISE) framework that decouples relative illumination structure from absolute exposure and infers it from reliable bright regions, enabling interpretable and robust enhancement. For scene-adaptive exposure adjustment, we further propose a Dual-Metering Exposure Reference derived from each input, allowing RISE to adapt the enhancement strength to individual scenes and generalize across diverse lighting conditions. Extensive benchmark and real-world generalization experiments show that RISE achieves state-of-the-art performance among unsupervised LLIE methods while producing visually natural results.

</details>


### [79] [Wiener Representation Filtering for VLM Hallucination Suppression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08167)
*Ameen Ali,Tamim Zoabi,Lidor Brami,Lior Wolf*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A training-free, post-hoc representation editing technique that reduces object hallucination in VLMs by estimating covariance structures from paired data (truthful vs. hallucinated) on the language backbone's representation space, deriving a Wiener-type filter applied once to feed-forward output projections of deeper layers, without changing inference speed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLMs frequently hallucinate objects, attributes, or relations not present in the image, degrading reliability of open-ended captioning and visual QA tasks. The authors seek a correction that avoids expensive fine-tuning or gradient updates while remaining effective.

Method: An offline, one-time calibration on a modest paired dataset using only forward passes and empirical second-order statistics to estimate covariance structures. Hidden states are modeled as superposition of truthful and hallucination-associated components; a Wiener-type estimator with closed-form optimal gains is derived from covariances. Eigendecomposition applies mode-wise attenuation with a stability criterion for continuous response to noise. The correction is absorbed into the model's existing weights (feed-forward output projections of selected deeper layers) so inference runs unchanged at the same speed.

Result: Experiments on LLaVA-1.5, MiniGPT-4, Gemma3, and mPLUG-Owl2 show consistent reductions in object hallucination on CHAIR, POPE, and MME benchmarks while maintaining caption fluency and response quality. The approach also generalizes to the TempCompass video benchmark and discrete diffusion language models for grounded dialogue, reducing hallucinations in temporal video reasoning and multi-step sequence-wide denoising.

Conclusion: Representation filtering in the language backbone is a simple, effective, and generalizable post-hoc correction for hallucinations across diverse VLM architectures, modalities (image/video), and generation paradigms (autoregressive and diffusion).

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) excel at open-ended captioning and visual QA but often describe objects, attributes, or relations absent from the image, a phenomenon known as object hallucination. We propose a {training-free, post-hoc representation editing technique} that operates in the representation space of the language backbone. The method performs a lightweight, one-time offline calibration on a modest paired dataset to estimate the required covariance structures, using only forward passes and empirical second-order statistics with no gradient updates or fine-tuning, after which the correction is absorbed directly into the model's existing weights. By modeling hidden states as a superposition of truthful and hallucination-associated components, we derive a Wiener-type estimator whose optimal gains are given in closed form from the covariances of paired truthful and hallucinated representations. An eigendecomposition yields mode-wise attenuation that respects a stability criterion, i.e., the filter responds continuously to estimation noise. The correction is applied once to the feed-forward output projections of selected deeper layers, at inference time, the model runs unchanged and at the same speed. Experiments on LLaVA-1.5, MiniGPT-4, Gemma3, and mPLUG-Owl2 demonstrate consistent reductions in object hallucination on CHAIR, POPE, and MME while maintaining caption fluency and overall response quality. We further demonstrate the generality of our approach on the TempCompass video understanding benchmark and on discrete diffusion language models for grounded dialogue, showing that representation filtering reduces hallucinations even in temporal video reasoning and multi-step, sequence-wide denoising settings.

</details>


### [80] [BAP-MOS: Bandit-Based Adaptive Prompting for Boundary-Sensitive Multi-Organ Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08191)
*Satvik Praveen,Shengji Jin,Ahmed Lamidi,Xin Qian,Yi Sheng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: BAP-MOS is a closed-loop adaptive prompting framework for multi-organ ultrasound segmentation that formulates prompt selection as an organ-specific multi-armed bandit problem, using an outer TPE loop and inner UCB-Tuned loop, along with an organ-scaled negative prompt ring. It achieves state-of-the-art results on TRUS and external PFUS1 benchmarks while keeping the foundation-model backbone frozen.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-organ ultrasound segmentation is challenging because anatomically adjacent structures must be delineated jointly, and localized boundary errors persist even when Dice scores are high. Existing methods fail to adequately address boundary-sensitive errors in multi-organ segmentation.

Method: BAP-MOS formulates prompt selection as an organ-specific multi-armed bandit problem over box, point, and combined prompts. An outer Tree-structured Parzen Estimator (TPE) loop selects the prompt-selection parameter vector, while an inner UCB-Tuned loop adapts per-organ prompt preferences using a bounded Dice-MSD-HD95 validation-probe reward. It introduces an organ-scaled negative prompt ring to adapt sparse prompt geometry across anatomical scales, keeping image and prompt encoders frozen and updating only the mask decoder.

Result: On pooled prostate-region TRUS cohorts, BAP-MOS achieves Dice 0.982, HD95 0.482, and MSD 0.204, reducing HD95 by ~48% and MSD by ~45% relative to the strongest conventional baseline. External validation on the PFUS1 pelvic-floor ultrasound corpus also produced good results.

Conclusion: Adaptive prompt allocation is an effective mechanism for improving boundary-sensitive multi-organ ultrasound segmentation without modifying the foundation-model backbone. The framework generalizes to external datasets and the source code is publicly available.

Abstract: Multi-organ ultrasound segmentation remains challenging when anatomically adjacent structures must be delineated jointly, as localized boundary errors can persist even when Dice scores are high. To address these challenges, we propose Boundary-Adaptive Prompting for Multi-Organ Segmentation (BAP-MOS), a closed-loop adaptive prompting framework. BAP-MOS formulates prompt selection as an organ-specific multi-armed bandit problem over box, point, and combined prompts. An outer Tree-structured Parzen Estimator (TPE) loop selects the prompt-selection parameter vector, while an inner UCB-Tuned loop adapts per-organ prompt preferences during fine-tuning using a bounded Dice--MSD--HD95 validation-probe reward. The framework further introduces an organ-scaled negative prompt ring to adapt sparse prompt geometry across anatomical scales, while keeping the image and prompt encoders frozen and updating only the mask decoder. We evaluate BAP-MOS on pooled prostate-region TRUS cohorts against U-Net, nnU-Net, MedSAM, fixed-prompt SAM/MedSAM, and adaptive policy variants. On this benchmark, BAP-MOS achieves Dice 0.982, HD95 0.482, and MSD 0.204, reducing HD95 by approximately 48% and MSD by 45% relative to the strongest conventional baseline. To verify the generalization ability of the framework, we tested it on the external PFUS1 pelvic-floor ultrasound corpus using MedSAM and its adaptive strategy variants, and the results were good. These results support adaptive prompt allocation as an effective mechanism for improving boundary-sensitive multi-organ ultrasound segmentation without modifying the foundation-model backbone. Source Code is available at: https://github.com/SatvikPraveen/BAP-MOS

</details>


### [81] [VTO: Visual Tool Orchestration for Video Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08219)
*Rui Wang,Yeteng Wu,Xianling Zhang,Mengshi Qi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VTO is a process-supervised reinforcement learning framework for video anomaly detection that uses a foundation model-driven cognitive evaluator to provide step-wise semantic feedback, enabling agents to dynamically orchestrate multiple visual tools with significantly improved accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional deep learning methods for video anomaly detection suffer from poor generalization across diverse scenarios. Multimodal agents using supervised fine-tuning struggle with complex tool orchestration, and standard reinforcement learning fails due to coarse-grained outcome rewards that cause premature termination.

Method: VTO introduces a process-supervised reinforcement learning framework with (1) a foundation model-driven cognitive evaluator providing context-aware semantic feedback, (2) Process-Supervised Cognitive Alignment for fine-grained step-wise supervision, and (3) explicit penalization of logical truncation while rewarding complete causal chains. The framework is supported by VAD-Tool, a hierarchical set of 12 specialized vision tools.

Result: Extensive experiments on VAD-Tool show VTO significantly outperforms baselines, achieving up to 10.2% absolute accuracy improvement in tool scheduling.

Conclusion: Process-supervised reinforcement learning with semantic feedback effectively addresses the limitations of existing approaches in video anomaly detection, enabling agents to dynamically explore and interact with the environment through improved multi-step reasoning and tool orchestration.

Abstract: Video anomaly detection (VAD) is a critical yet challenging task due to the complex and diverse nature of real-world scenarios. Traditional deep learning approaches are fundamentally limited by poor generalization across diverse scenarios. While multimodal agents offer a promising tool-learning paradigm for VAD, current systems relying on supervised fine-tuning struggle with complex orchestration, and standard reinforcement learning often causes premature termination due to coarse-grained outcome rewards. To address these challenges, we propose VTO, a process-supervised reinforcement learning framework. Moving beyond static tool usage, VTO enables the agent to dynamically explore and interact with the environment. Specifically, we introduce a foundation model-driven cognitive evaluator to provide context-aware semantic feedback, which is seamlessly integrated into a Process-Supervised Cognitive Alignment that delivers fine-grained, step-wise supervision. By explicitly penalizing logical truncation and rewarding complete causal chains, the agent optimizes its multi-step reasoning policy for interrelated tool orchestration. To support our proposed framework, we meticulously crafted VAD-Tool, a hierarchical visual tool set comprising 12 specialized vision tools spanning from entity tracking to high-stakes hazard detection, and established the corresponding benchmark for rigorous multi-step reasoning evaluation. Extensive experiments on VAD-Tool demonstrate that VTO significantly outperforms baselines, achieving up to a 10.2\% absolute accuracy improvement in tool scheduling. Code and data are available at https://github.com/MICLAB-BUPT/VTO.

</details>


### [82] [Ego-OSCAR: Egocentric Open source Stereo CAptuRe System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08285)
*Gunjan Paul,Senthil Palanisamy,Satpal Singh Rathore,Pratyush Kumar Patnaik,Shubhanshu Khatana,Abhishek Anand*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Ego-OSCAR is an open-hardware, low-cost (<$200) head-mounted stereo-inertial capture device for egocentric data collection, paired with a complete software stack and ~550 hours of annotated stereo video with IMU data collected by distributed contributors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To lower the activation energy and cost barrier for crowdsourced egocentric data collection at scale, providing a cheap, defensible alternative to research-grade systems like Project Aria.

Method: Pairs a hardware-synchronized global-shutter stereo camera with a 6-axis IMU, embedded Linux SBC for on-device encoding, and a realtime microcontroller for user feedback/watchdog, built from commercially available parts and 3D-printed components under USD 200, along with an open-source software stack and dataset release.

Result: Approximately 550 hours of egocentric stereo video per camera with synchronized IMU from a distributed contributor network, annotated with open-vocabulary action captions and per-frame 3D hand reconstructions plus per-session stereo calibration.

Conclusion: Ego-OSCAR does not match per-unit fidelity of research-grade systems but provides the cheapest defensible substrate for crowdsourced egocentric capture, with all hardware designs, software, and dataset open-sourced.

Abstract: We present Ego-OSCAR, an open-hardware, low-cost, head-mounted stereo-inertial capture device for egocentric data collection in the wild. EgoOSCAR pairs a hardware-synchronized global-shutter stereo camera with a 6- axis IMU, an embedded Linux SBC for on-device video encoding, and a realtime microcontroller for user feedback and watchdog functions. The complete bill of materials is under USD 200 per unit, using only commercially available components and 3D-printed parts. Alongside the device, we release a complete software stack (hardware-accelerated recording pipeline, IMU sampling daemon, time-synchronization tooling, and watchdog firmware) and roughly 550 hours of egocentric stereo video per camera with synchronized IMU, collected by a distributed contributor network across everyday indoor environments. The release is annotated rather than raw: free-form action captions cover essentially the entire recorded timeline with an open vocabulary, and per-frame 3D hand reconstructions ship alongside per-session stereo calibration. Ego-OSCAR does not aim to match the per-unit fidelity of research-grade systems such as Project Aria; it aims to be the cheapest defensible substrate for crowdsourced egocentric capture, and to lower the activation energy for any team that wants to collect egocentric data at scale. All hardware designs, software, and the dataset are open-sourced

</details>


### [83] [What Irregularity Costs: CUDA C++, Rust, and Triton on a Hash-Blocked GPU Workload](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08287)
*Petr Korolev*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper benchmarks three GPU languages (CUDA C++, Rust via cuda-oxide, and Triton) on an irregular workload—a hash-blocked TSDF fusion kernel with open-addressed hashing, compare-exchange insertion, and data-dependent probing—and finds that while all languages perform comparably on regular, tiled workloads, Triton is more than an order of magnitude slower on the irregular stage due to expressiveness limitations (compile-time bounded probe loops, unmasked atomic_cas), while Rust suffers from L1 cache-bypassing due to the type-correct scoped atomic semantics. The paper also reports a correctness bug in Triton's bounded probing that silently drops surface data, and a defect found and fixed in cuda-oxide.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard GPU language benchmarks focus on tiled dense linear algebra where all toolchains perform well and differences are negligible, masking real-world performance gaps that emerge on irregular workloads like hash tables with data-dependent control flow and contended atomic operations.

Method: The authors implement the same hash-blocked TSDF fusion kernel in three languages (CUDA C++, Rust via cuda-oxide, Triton) and measure performance on a workload featuring an open-addressed hash table with compare-exchange insertion, per-lane variable probe depth, and contended scatter. They use hardware counters to diagnose performance differences and analyze specific language expressiveness limitations.

Result: On the regular stage all three languages perform within a small factor; on the irregular stage, Rust stays close to CUDA C++ while Triton is over 10x slower. Triton's slowdown stems from compile-time-bounded probe loops and tl.atomic_cas lacking a mask, forcing suboptimal scratch structures. Rust's slowdown was traced via hardware counters to L1 cache misses caused by scoped atomic loads that must be SM-coherent, bypassing L1 on every access. Triton additionally has a correctness defect: bounded probing silently discards hash blocks at realistic load factors.

Conclusion: Language choice matters primarily for irregular workloads, not the regular tiled computations typically benchmarked. The observed gaps are attributable to specific expressiveness limitations in each language framework, and the paper documents fixes (a cuda-oxide defect) and flags correctness issues (Triton's silent data loss) that need attention.

Abstract: GPU language comparisons are almost always run on tiled dense linear algebra, where every toolchain is good and the differences are small. We implement the same hash-blocked TSDF fusion kernel in CUDA C++, in Rust through NVIDIA's cuda-oxide, and in Triton, and measure it on a workload with the opposite character: an open-addressed hash table with compare-exchange insertion, data-dependent per-lane probe depth, and contended scatter.
  The result is a split. On the regular stage, which walks a truncation band and accumulates, all three languages land within a small factor of each other. On the irregular stage, which probes and inserts, Rust stays close to hand-written CUDA C++ while Triton is more than an order of magnitude slower. Language choice is nearly free on the work that is usually benchmarked and expensive on the work that is not.
  We attribute both gaps to specific things the languages cannot express, not to ratios. Triton's cost follows from a probe loop that must run to a compile-time bound and from tl.atomic_cas taking no mask, which forces a scratch structure with no counterpart in CUDA. Rust's cost was invisible in every instruction count: its kernel issues fewer instructions, fewer compare-exchanges and fewer registers at identical occupancy, yet was slower. Hardware counters located it in L1 residency. A GPU-scope atomic load must be coherent across SMs, no NVIDIA L1 is, so the type-correct way to read a shared location bypasses the cache on every access.
  Triton's bounded probe is also a correctness problem for fusion: at load factors an ordinary depth trajectory reaches, it silently discards blocks and the reconstruction loses patches of surface with nothing reported. We also report a defect found and fixed in cuda-oxide itself, now merged upstream: its scoped atomic load and store could not be called at all in the build mode that produces real kernels.

</details>


### [84] [Test-Time Prototype Adaptation for Open-Vocabulary Semantic Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08290)
*Haozhe Wang,Jintao Cheng,Weibin Li,Xiaoyu Tang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: TPA is a training-free, plug-in module for open-vocabulary semantic segmentation that operates at the output level, using a transductive adaptation phase to build per-class prototypes from DINO features of confident anchor patches, then fusing a cosine similarity score with the host model's logits at inference—improving five OVSS hosts across eight benchmarks without modifying the host's weights or forward pass.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods that improve CLIP's spatial behavior for OVSS either redesign internal attention or inject features from auxiliary vision foundation models, both of which require access to the host's internal computation and are tailored to its specific forward pass—limiting generality and practicality.

Method: TPA is a training-free, output-level plug-in that: (1) runs a lightweight transductive adaptation on a small pool of unlabeled deployment-domain images, (2) identifies confident anchor patches from the host's own predictions, (3) aggregates their frozen DINO features into per-class prototypes, and (4) fuses a cosine similarity lookup score linearly with the host's logits at inference.

Result: TPA consistently improves segmentation accuracy across five representative OVSS hosts (spanning attention-redesign and VFM-injection designs), three CLIP backbones, eight benchmarks, and multiple internal VFM choices—using a single set of hyper-parameters with no per-host tuning or parameter updates, and requiring as few as ~10% of unlabeled deployment-domain images for effective bank construction.

Conclusion: Output-level, training-free adaptation can effectively enhance OVSS performance without altering the host model, offering a general and lightweight complementary approach for OVSS systems.

Abstract: Open-vocabulary semantic segmentation (OVSS) repurposes a pretrained CLIP encoder for dense prediction without additional labeled supervision. Existing methods improve CLIP's spatial behavior either by redesigning its internal attention or by injecting features from auxiliary vision foundation models; both require access to the host's internal computation and are tailored to its specific forward pass. In this work, we propose Test-time Prototype Adaptation (TPA), a training-free plug-in that operates at the output level, leaving the host's forward pass and weights unmodified. By leveraging a lightweight transductive adaptation phase, TPA identifies confident anchor patches from the host's own output predictions on a small pool of unlabeled deployment-domain images, and aggregates their frozen DINO features into per-class prototypes; at inference, a single cosine similarity lookup against this frozen bank provides an auxiliary score fused linearly with the host's logits. TPA composes with five representative OVSS hosts spanning attention-redesign and VFM-injection designs, across three CLIP backbones, eight benchmarks, and multiple internal VFM choices. Under a single set of hyper-parameters and without per-host tuning or parameter updates, TPA consistently improves segmentation accuracy, with as few as approximately 10% of unlabeled deployment-domain images sufficing for effective bank construction on most benchmarks.

</details>


### [85] [Frequency-Domain Dual-Branch Fusion for Medical Visual Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08307)
*Yusra Tariq,Rakesh Chandra Joshi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces a dual-branch frequency-domain fusion module for medical Visual Question Answering (VQA) that conditions spectral filtering on the input question, enabling adaptive selection of low-frequency structure and high-frequency detail. It uses complementary features from a frozen BiomedCLIP encoder aligned with question representations via symmetric InfoNCE, and a BioBART decoder, achieving improved performance on VQA-RAD and SLAKE benchmarks with a lightweight architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal fusion approaches for medical VQA operate in the spatial domain and may not fully exploit complementary frequency information present in visual and textual representations, limiting alignment of subtle visual evidence (lesion texture, boundary sharpness, diffuse density changes) with clinical language.

Method: The method consists of: (1) a dual-branch frequency-domain fusion module that conditions spectral filtering on the input question for adaptive selection of low-frequency structure and high-frequency detail; (2) extraction of complementary features from early texture-sensitive and final semantic layers of a frozen BiomedCLIP encoder; (3) alignment of both with question representation using symmetric InfoNCE objective; (4) staged joint training with a BioBART decoder; (5) pretraining on PMC-VQA and fine-tuning on VQA-RAD and SLAKE benchmarks.

Result: Frequency-aware multimodal fusion improves medical VQA performance on VQA-RAD and SLAKE benchmarks while maintaining a lightweight and efficient architecture.

Conclusion: The proposed frequency-domain fusion approach effectively exploits complementary spectral information in medical images and text, improving multimodal understanding for medical VQA in an efficient manner.

Abstract: Medical Visual Question Answering (VQA) requires aligning subtle visual evidence, including lesion texture, boundary sharpness, and diffuse density changes, with clinical language. Existing multimodal fusion approaches operating in the spatial domain may not fully exploit complementary frequency information present in visual and textual representations. We introduce a dual-branch frequency-domain fusion module that conditions spectral filtering on the input question, enabling adaptive selection of global low-frequency structure and fine-grained high-frequency detail before reconstructing the spatial representation for answer generation. To provide a richer spectrum for filtering, we extract complementary features from early texture-sensitive and final semantic layers of a frozen BiomedCLIP encoder and align both with the question representation using a symmetric InfoNCE objective prior to staged joint training with a BioBART decoder. We pretrain the proposed model on PMC-VQA and fine-tune it on the VQA-RAD and SLAKE benchmarks, demonstrating that frequency-aware multimodal fusion improves medical VQA performance while maintaining a lightweight and efficient architecture.

</details>


### [86] [Open-World Semantic Segmentation with Sensitivity Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08308)
*Anastasios Romanos Varvarigos,Nikos Giakoumoglou,Tania Stathaki*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses open-world semantic segmentation by extending a dual-decoder baseline with a third sensitivity decoder that captures fine-grained texture irregularities and activation instabilities, providing three complementary signals (logit-space OOD distance, embedding-space energy, and local activation instability). Experiments on Cityscapes and BDD-Anomaly show improved anomaly segmentation and novel-class discovery with +2.4% AUROC and 2.5 pp. FPR@95TPR reduction over baseline, while maintaining competitive closed-set accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern vision systems must operate in open-world settings where models need to recognize known categories while detecting unseen or anomalous content. Conventional semantic segmentation models assume a closed world and often produce overconfident misclassifications on novel content.

Method: The authors extend a dual-decoder baseline with a third complementary decoder within a unified encoder-decoder design: (1) a closed-set segmentation decoder using Gaussian prototypes for known categories, (2) a contrastive feature learning decoder to isolate unknown regions in embedding space, and (3) a key sensitivity decoder capturing fine-grained texture irregularities and activation instabilities indicative of semantic uncertainty. These three decoders provide complementary signals across logit space, embedding space, and activation scales.

Result: Experiments on Cityscapes and BDD-Anomaly show the method improves anomaly segmentation and novel-class discovery while maintaining competitive closed-set accuracy, with gains of +2.4% AUROC and a 2.5 pp. reduction in FPR@95TPR on BDD-Anomaly over the baseline.

Conclusion: The sensitivity decoder provides complementary uncertainty signals that neither semantic prototypes nor contrastive norms can reliably detect, and the unified three-decoder design effectively addresses open-world semantic segmentation.

Abstract: Modern vision systems must operate in "open-world" settings, where models must recognize known categories and detect unseen or anomalous content. Conventional semantic segmentation models operate under a "closed-world" assumption, often producing overconfident misclassifications on novel content. We address open-world semantic segmentation, the joint task of segmenting known classes while detecting and grouping novel or anomalous content without additional supervision, by extending a dual-decoder baseline with a third, complementary decoder within a unified encoder-decoder design. The first decoder performs closed-set segmentation using Gaussian prototypes for known categories. The second uses contrastive feature learning to isolate unknown regions in embedding space. The third, our key contribution, is a sensitivity decoder that captures fine-grained texture irregularities and activation instabilities indicative of semantic uncertainty, which neither semantic prototypes nor contrastive norms can reliably detect. The three decoders provide genuinely complementary signals: class-level OOD distance in logit space, global feature energy in embedding space, and local activation instability across encoder scales. Experiments on Cityscapes and BDD-Anomaly show that our method improves anomaly segmentation and novel-class discovery while maintaining competitive closed-set accuracy, with gains of +2.4% AUROC and a 2.5 pp. reduction in FPR@95TPR on BDD-Anomaly over the baseline.

</details>


### [87] [Three Necessary Principles for Self-Supervised Visual Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08309)
*Nikos Giakoumoglou,Paschalis Giakoumoglou,Tania Stathaki*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes that label-free visual representation learning requires three joint, non-overlapping objectives—semantic invariance (observation), patch-level spatial prediction, and representational non-degeneracy (regularization)—and provides theoretical proofs plus controlled experiments showing that each is necessary and that no pair can substitute for the third.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand what training signals are fundamentally necessary and jointly sufficient for unsupervised visual representation learning, and to explain why existing self-supervised methods succeed or fail in terms of collapse and missing spatial/semantic signals.

Method: Formalizes the three objectives as observation, prediction, and regularization principles; proves theoretical properties (e.g., constant encoder as global minimizer without regularization, gradient-complementarity, momentum encoder fixed-point equivalence); unifies major self-supervised methods under a single energy decomposition; pairs every claim with controlled experiments including patch-retrieval evaluation.

Result: Shows that (i) observation+prediction without regularization collapses to a constant encoder; (ii) observation and prediction are gradient-complementary and non-conflicting at the encoder output; (iii) momentum encoder converges to the same fixed point as the online encoder with no collapse guarantee; contrastive alignment offers only self-limiting collapse resistance; and no pair of objectives substitutes for the third at the studied scale.

Conclusion: A complete self-supervised visual representation learning objective must jointly include semantic invariance, spatial prediction, and non-degeneracy; dropping any one forfeits an essential training signal that the others cannot compensate for.

Abstract: We argue that learning visual representations without labels requires a training signal jointly complete across three non-overlapping objectives: semantic invariance across augmented views, patch-level spatial prediction, and representational non-degeneracy. We formalize these as the observation, prediction, and regularization principles and prove (i) that combining observation and prediction without regularization admits the constant encoder as a global minimizer under negative-free alignment; (ii) that the two objectives are gradient-complementary and structurally non-conflicting at the encoder output; and (iii) that the momentum encoder converges to the same fixed point as the online encoder and provides no collapse guarantee at convergence. Contrastive alignment provides only self-limiting collapse resistance, formalized via an explicit gradient-decay argument. Dropping prediction withholds the spatial training signal by construction; dropping observation forfeits cross-view semantic invariance by construction; at the scale we study, no pair substitutes for the third. Every major self-supervised method is a special case of a single unified energy decomposition. We pair every theoretical claim with a controlled experiment, including a patch-retrieval evaluation for the spatial consequence of prediction.

</details>


### [88] [Your VLM Already Knows When: Training-Free Temporal Grounding by Asking Yes or No](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08315)
*Ji Huang,Barry Devereux,Hui Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Multimodal LLMs can recognize events but fail at temporal localization; the paper shows this is a task-interface problem, not a perception problem. By replacing timestamp regression with a training-free coarse-to-fine scan of binary questions, they achieve FV-Action, reaching 56.8% R@0.5 on Charades-STA and surpassing strong baselines without any temporal supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Strong VLMs fail catastrophically at temporal localization (as low as 3.8% R@0.5 on Charades-STA), and error detection is impossible because models are confidently wrong (77-80% of errors have low output entropy). The authors aim to determine whether this failure stems from perception or from the task interface design.

Method: The authors keep model weights frozen and replace timestamp regression with a coarse-to-fine scan of binary questions, using only first-token probabilities as a ranking signal. They decompose residual failures into two axes: a perception axis (backbone-dependent) and a geometry axis (analytically predictable from output-window/event-width ratio). This yields FV-Action, a training-free method.

Result: FV-Action improves R@0.5 by 28 to 50 points across four frozen backbones, reaches 56.8% R@0.5 on Charades-STA (above native grounding pipelines and strongest training-free results), surpasses all TVG-trained models evaluated zero-shot on TACoS, and improves over direct prediction on ActivityNet Captions and QVHighlights.

Conclusion: The failure of multimodal LLMs at temporal localization is an interface artifact rather than a perceptual limitation. A task interface that consumes LLM uncertainty as rankings rather than forcing regression enables training-free, strong temporal video grounding across multiple benchmarks and backbones.

Abstract: Multimodal LLMs that recognise events reliably still fail to say when they happen. Prompted for timestamps, strong VLMs reach as little as $3.8\%$ R@0.5 on Charades-STA, and $77$ to $80\%$ of their wrong predictions carry low output entropy: the models are confidently wrong, and entropy-based error detection stays below a random classifier. We show that this failure lives in the task interface, not in perception. Holding the weights fixed, replacing timestamp regression with a coarse-to-fine scan of binary questions, whose first-token probabilities are consumed only as a ranking, raises R@0.5 by $28$ to $50$ points across four frozen backbones. The residual failures decompose into two measurable axes: a perception axis that moves with the backbone, and a geometry axis that is analytically predictable from the ratio of the output-window and event widths. FV-Action, the training-free method built on this analysis, reaches $56.8\%$ R@0.5 on Charades-STA, above the same backbone's native grounding pipeline and the strongest training-free result on this benchmark; it surpasses every TVG-trained model evaluated zero-shot on TACoS, and improves over direct prediction on ActivityNet Captions and QVHighlights, with no temporal supervision at any stage.

</details>


### [89] [A continually expandable foundation model for brain MRI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08319)
*Michail Mamalakis,Carmen Jimenez-Mesa,Yonghao Li,Hao Chen,Chao Li,Antonios Mamalakis,John Suckling,Richard Bethlehem,Stephen J. Price,Richard J. Gilbertson,Pietro Lio*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Alcmaeon is a 3D brain MRI foundation model pretrained on 425,000+ unlabeled volumes that can be sequentially expanded across clinical domains using Graph-Blueprint Pruning (GBP), which protects task-relevant network modules to prevent catastrophic forgetting while enabling continued learning. It demonstrates reduced forgetting compared to standard sequential adaptation and elastic weight consolidation, with applications spanning image synthesis, disease classification, survival modeling, and postoperative prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Brain MRI models are typically developed for individual diseases, populations, or imaging protocols, and foundation models tend to lose earlier capabilities when updated with new data (catastrophic forgetting). The authors aim to create a foundation model that can grow with emerging data while retaining previously learned capabilities.

Method: Alcmaeon combines volumetric encoding and latent diffusion generation with Graph-Blueprint Pruning (GBP), which identifies and protects network modules important to earlier domains while leaving remaining capacity trainable during sequential expansion across clinical domains (from healthy ageing and neurodegeneration to developmental, psychiatric, and tumour imaging).

Result: GBP showed less forgetting than sequential adaptation and elastic weight consolidation across voxel-level reconstruction measures, with the largest advantage after adaptation to tumour imaging. The blueprints provided inspectable records of model capacity protection and reuse. Representations from different model levels supported image synthesis, disease classification, survival modelling, and postoperative prediction, though no single representation was optimal for every task.

Conclusion: This work provides a viable route toward brain MRI foundation models that can be incrementally expanded with new clinical data while preserving earlier capabilities, addressing the challenge of catastrophic forgetting in sequential model updates.

Abstract: Brain magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) is central to neuroscience and clinical assessment, but models are commonly developed for individual diseases, populations or imaging protocols. Foundation models promise more general representations, yet they are usually pretrained once and can lose earlier capabilities when updated with new data. Here we show that Alcmaeon, a three-dimensional brain MRI foundation model pretrained without manual labels on more than 425,000 volumes and derived imaging maps, can be expanded sequentially across clinical domains. Alcmaeon combines volumetric encoding and latent diffusion generation with Graph-Blueprint Pruning (GBP), which protects network modules important to earlier domains while leaving the remaining capacity trainable. Across expansion from healthy ageing and neurodegeneration to developmental, psychiatric and tumour imaging, GBP showed less forgetting than sequential adaptation and elastic weight consolidation across voxel-level reconstruction measures, with its largest advantage after adaptation to tumour imaging. The blueprints provided an inspectable record of how model capacity was protected and reused. Representations from different model levels supported image synthesis, disease classification, survival modelling and postoperative prediction, although no single representation was optimal for every task. These findings provide a route towards brain MRI foundation models that can grow with emerging data while retaining earlier capabilities.

</details>


### [90] [Circuit Fine-Tuning for Compute-Efficient Transformer Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08336)
*Uri Z. Kialy,Gil Ben-Artzi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Circuit Fine-Tuning (CFT) is a compute-efficient PEFT framework that uses circuit discovery to select a subgraph of ViT modules for fine-tuning before training, achieving peak accuracy in ~20 epochs with 2.3-6.6x fewer FLOPs and up to 16x less wall-clock time while adding zero parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Parameter count in PEFT does not imply compute efficiency — parameter-sparse methods can still incur full-model training cost per step and need long schedules to reach peak accuracy.

Method: CFT uses circuit discovery to select modules for fine-tuning before training. It formulates attribution against a near-zero-initialized probe head (rather than a trained task head) to isolate backbone response to the target distribution, then fine-tunes only the recovered subgraph without learning-rate warmup.

Result: CFT reaches peak accuracy in ~20 epochs on average vs 44-96 for strong PEFT baselines, yielding 2.3-6.6x fewer training FLOPs and up to 16x less wall-clock time, with zero added parameters and no inference operations. Validated on VTAB-1k, Swin, CBIS-DDSM, and Gemma-3 on CUB-200.

Conclusion: Circuit discovery, conventionally used for model explanation, can be repurposed for compute-efficient fine-tuning, demonstrating that compute efficiency should be a first-class optimization target in PEFT alongside parameter efficiency.

Abstract: Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) has become the de facto standard for adapting Vision Transformers (ViTs) to downstream tasks. While parameter count has been the dominant efficiency metric in PEFT, it does not imply \textit{compute efficiency}: parameter-sparse methods can still incur full-model training cost per step, and typically need long schedules to reach peak accuracy. We introduce Circuit Fine-Tuning (CFT), a compute-efficient framework that uses circuit discovery---conventionally used to explain trained models---to select modules for fine-tuning before training. Whereas attribution is conventionally formulated against a trained task head, we formulate it against a near-zero-initialized probe head, which isolates the response of the backbone to the target distribution rather than the preferences of a particular classifier. CFT then fine-tunes only the recovered subgraph. CFT needs no learning-rate warmup and reaches peak accuracy in ${\sim}20$ epochs on average---versus $44$--$96$ for strong PEFT baselines---yielding $2.3$--$6.6\times$ fewer training FLOPs and up to $16\times$ less wall-clock time, while adding zero parameters and no inference operations. Experiments across a standard visual transfer benchmark (VTAB-1k), hierarchical backbones (Swin), domain-shifted medical imaging (CBIS-DDSM), and a vision-language model (Gemma-3 on CUB-200) demonstrate the effectiveness of CFT. Code is available at https://github.com/UriKialy/CFT

</details>


### [91] [Tropical Cyclone Forecasting via Latent Rectified Flow using Satellite Imagery and Atmospheric Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08354)
*Meheru Zannat,Sk. Md. Masudul Ahsan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper presents a single-pass deep generative model that jointly forecasts tropical cyclone satellite imagery and atmospheric fields up to nine hours ahead, using a variational autoencoder with conditional rectified-flow UNet, and improves track accuracy via reward fine-tuning against a differentiable steering-flow track error.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tropical cyclones are becoming more destructive, and current deep generative forecast models are limited: they cannot jointly predict satellite imagery and atmospheric fields, require many sampling steps unsuitable for modest hardware, and their storm tracks come from regression heads disconnected from the physical atmosphere.

Method: A five-channel variational autoencoder compresses 5x256x256 frames into 4x64x64 latents. A conditional rectified-flow UNet with factorized temporal-attention predicts the next three frames from three past frames, track coordinates, and timestamps. The model is reward-fine-tuned (DRaFT) against a differentiable track error derived from predicted winds via a steering-flow calculation.

Result: On held-out 2022 storms, the model achieves 16.35 dB PSNR and 0.759 SSIM, outperforming a cascaded-diffusion baseline at all lead times (+0.84 dB at +9 h) while sampling ~30x faster (56 ms vs 1673 ms). Track error at +9 h is 62.4 km (15% below baseline); reward fine-tuning yields a further 8-11% track-error reduction across sampler budgets.

Conclusion: A single-pass joint forecasting model can deliver high-quality imagery and atmospheric field predictions with fast sampling on modest hardware, and physically grounded reward fine-tuning improves track accuracy beyond what pure generative quality metrics capture.

Abstract: Tropical cyclones are growing more destructive in a changing climate, and efficient forecasting of their structure and track has become a necessity. Deep generative models promise an alternative to computationally expensive numerical weather prediction (NWP), yet current systems produce either satellite imagery or atmospheric fields, never both; they need many sampling steps, putting them out of reach of modest hardware; and their storm tracks come from regression heads with no physical link to the generated atmosphere. This work presents a single-pass model that jointly forecasts GRIDSAT-B1 infrared imagery and four ERA5 atmospheric fields (U-wind, V-wind, air temperature, and surface pressure) out to nine hours. A five-channel variational autoencoder compresses each 5 x 256 x 256 frame to a 4 x 64 x 64 latent, and a conditional rectified-flow UNet with a factorized temporal-attention module predicts the next three frames from three past frames, their best-track coordinates, and timestamps. The model is then reward-fine-tuned (DRaFT) against a differentiable track error derived from the predicted winds through a steering-flow calculation. On held-out 2022 storms the model reaches 16.35 dB PSNR and 0.759 SSIM, ahead of a reproduced cascaded-diffusion baseline at every lead time (+0.84 dB at +9 h) while sampling ~30x faster (56 ms vs. 1673 ms). Track error at +9 h is 62.4 km, 15% below the baseline, and a reward fine-tuning study demonstrates a further 8-11% track-error reduction across sampler budgets.

</details>


### [92] [VOICE: A Vision-Omics Foundation Model Integrating Direct and Retrieval-Based Prediction of In-situ Single-Cell Gene Expression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08366)
*Xin Luo,Yicheng Tao,Haoxuan Zeng,Suyuan Wang,Chenzi Ouyang,Meiqi Zhu,Kai Liu,Shuibing Chen,Jie Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VOICE is a multimodal foundation model that predicts single-cell gene expression from H&E images using paired Xenium spatial transcriptomics data, combining a regression branch and a retrieval branch fused with per-gene weights, outperforming prior methods on seven metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spatial transcriptomics is costly, limited to targeted gene panels, and applicable to few samples, whereas H&E imaging is cheap and collected routinely at scale. Predicting single-cell expression from morphology would enable molecular analysis on large tissue archives.
