Our method enables reliable joint pose-scene optimization initialized from coarse pose priors, eliminating the need for computationally expensive SLAM-style pipelines. EvTrajGS parameterizes camera motion as a continuous-time trajectory initialized from discrete camera poses, providing a unified representation for pose refinement. We then aggregate adjacent trajectory states into a temporally coupled pose, promoting temporally consistent pose updates during joint optimization. Additionally, we introduce a loss-reweighted event sampling strategy to adaptively emphasize temporally under-reconstructed intervals. Extensive experiments on both synthetic and real-world datasets demonstrate that EvTrajGS outperforms state-of-the-art methods in terms of both geometric reconstruction quality and pose estimation accuracy, achieving 3.8 dB higher PSNR, 0.1 higher SSIM, and over 40\% lower ATE RMSE while retaining high computational efficiency.

</details>


### [115] [RobustDefect-LLM: Explainable and Robustness-Aware Industrial Surface Defect Classification with Decision Support and AI-Assisted Reporting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08589)
*Nazlıcan Düşünmez,Halûk Gümüşkaya*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RobustDefect-LLM is an integrated industrial surface-defect inspection framework combining deep-learning classification, confidence-aware review routing, AI-assisted reporting, and mobile interaction. MobileNetV3-Large achieved 99.26% test accuracy on NEU-DET, though performance degraded under synthetic image perturbations. A conservative confidence-based routing policy provided selective accuracy improvements by flagging low-confidence predictions for human review.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrial surface-defect inspection requires not only accurate classification but also trustworthy, explainable, and practically deployable systems. The authors aim to integrate multiple quality-control components—classification, visual evidence, confidence-based decision support, reporting, and traceability—into a unified workflow while explicitly evaluating robustness under controlled image degradation rather than assuming intrinsic robustness.

Method: Four transfer-learning CNNs (ResNet50, EfficientNet-B0, DenseNet121, MobileNetV3-Large) were evaluated on 1,799 six-class NEU-DET images with fixed partitions. The best model was stress-tested under combined synthetic degradation. Grad-CAM provided visual evidence, and a confidence-based routing policy (confidence <0.90 or top-2 margin <0.10) flagged predictions for HUMAN REVIEW. AI-assisted report generation was validated via deterministic consistency checks.

Result: MobileNetV3-Large achieved the highest test accuracy (99.26%) and macro F1-score (0.9926) with a bootstrap 95% CI of 0.9815-1.0000, though no significant difference from DenseNet121 (McNemar p=1.000). CPU inference was 0.060s per forward pass. Accuracy dropped to 87.78% under mild degradation and below 40% under strong degradation. The review-routing policy provided 12.22% automatic coverage and 100% selective accuracy among 33 eligible cases, routing both observed errors to review. All 100 generated reports passed consistency checks with 1.66s mean latency.

Conclusion: The integrated workflow is feasible, but the authors emphasize the need for confidence calibration, repeated evaluation, and real-world industrial validation. The substantial accuracy drop under image degradation and low automatic coverage highlight that the system requires careful robustness engineering and human-in-the-loop support before practical deployment.

Abstract: This paper presents RobustDefect-LLM, an industrial surface-defect inspection framework integrating deep-learning classification, operator-facing visual evidence, confidence-aware decision support, controlled AI-assisted reporting, traceable storage, and mobile interaction in a unified quality-control workflow. Here, robustness-aware denotes explicit evaluation under controlled image degradation and confidence-aware review routing, not an intrinsic robustness guarantee. Four transfer-learning-based convolutional neural networks, ResNet50, EfficientNet-B0, DenseNet121, and MobileNetV3-Large, were evaluated on 1,799 images from the six-class NEU-DET dataset using fixed training, validation, and held-out in-domain test partitions. MobileNetV3-Large achieved the highest numerical test accuracy (99.26%) and macro F1-score (0.9926), with a bootstrap 95% accuracy CI of 0.9815-1.0000. An exact paired McNemar test found no significant difference from DenseNet121 (p = 1.000). The selected model averaged 0.060 s per CPU forward pass (16.66 FPS). Under combined synthetic degradation, accuracy fell to 87.78% at mild intensity and below 40% at stronger intensities, revealing sensitivity to severe image-quality deterioration. Grad-CAM supplied visual evidence, while predictions with confidence below 0.90 or a top-2 margin below 0.10 were routed to HUMAN REVIEW. This conservative policy provided 12.22% automatic coverage and 100% observed selective accuracy among 33 eligible cases (95% CI: 89.43%-100.00%), while routing both observed classification errors to review. Under nominal controlled conditions, all 100 generated reports passed deterministic consistency checks, with a mean latency of 1.66 s. Results support the feasibility of the integrated workflow while emphasizing the need for calibration, repeated evaluation, and real-world industrial validation.

</details>


### [116] [Goal-oriented Navigation Instruction Generation with Tour Video Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08596)
*Fangdi Li,Juncheng Liao,Changxu Cheng,Jiazhi Wang,Senda Chen,Tao Wang,Wuyue Zhao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces VideoNIG, a goal-oriented video-grounded navigation instruction generation task that produces step-by-step navigation instructions from ego-centric tour videos without relying on intermediate graph or map representations. It proposes a two-stage Curriculum Learning framework and validates it on a simulator benchmark with 60K tour videos and 37K multimodal prompts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing NIG studies treat instruction generation as an auxiliary task for VLN (data augmentation or multi-task learning). Generating instructions from compact environmental priors requires meticulous spatial reasoning, which remains challenging for current multimodal models, especially when the target route deviates from demonstrated tours.

Method: Two-stage Curriculum Learning framework: (1) Action Warmup for spatial action-view alignment, then (2) Complexity Progression using trajectories with increasing exploratory difficulty. Instantiated in a controlled simulator benchmark with 60K tour videos and 37K multimodal prompts at progressive difficulty levels.

Result: Existing MLLMs struggle with VideoNIG; the proposed approach significantly improves instruction quality across complementary diagnostic metrics (text similarity, choice-based spatial consistency tests, and downstream navigation execution). VideoNIG-generated instructions are shown to be executable by a VLN agent for end-to-end navigation.

Conclusion: VideoNIG is a challenging and meaningful task formulation that decouples NIG from graph/map priors, and the Curriculum Learning approach effectively improves navigation instruction generation quality and executability.

Abstract: Navigation Instruction Generation (NIG) aims to produce step-by-step natural language instructions for navigation guidance. Existing studies primarily treat NIG as an auxiliary task for vision-andlanguage navigation (VLN), focusing on data augmentation or multi-task learning. However, generating navigation instructions from compact environmental priors requires meticulous spatial reasoning, especially when the target route does not simply follow the demonstrated tour, and remains challenging for current multimodal models. In this work, we introduce VideoNIG, a goal-oriented video-grounded NIG task that generates navigation instructions from ego-centric tour videos, an initial observation, and a textual or visual goal, without relying on intermediate representations such as graphs and maps. We instantiate VideoNIG in a controlled simulator benchmark with 60K tour videos across continuous indoor environments and 37K multimodal prompts with progressive difficulty levels. We further introduce a diagnostic evaluation protocol that combines text similarity, choice-based spatial consistency tests, and downstream navigation execution. To address this task, we propose a two-stage Curriculum Learning framework that decomposes the learning into foundational motion perception and long-horizon navigation reasoning. Specifically, we first employ Action Warmup for spatial action-view alignment, followed by Complexity Progression using trajectories with increasing exploratory difficulty. Extensive experiments show that existing MLLMs struggle with VideoNIG, while our approach significantly improves instruction quality across complementary diagnostic metrics. Finally, integrating VideoNIG-generated instructions with a VLN agent demonstrates the executability of this task formulation for end-to-end navigation.

</details>


### [117] [Population-Scalable Multi-Agent World Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08600)
*Renjie Zhao,Yuxiang Wu,Mingyu Zhang,Jiaxin Li,Sisi Li,Yimin Sheng,Tianxi Tan,Zhenkai Zhang,Jianyi Zhu,Yong-Lu Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Khora is a scalable multi-agent world model that decouples world-state evolution from visual rendering, enabling inference-time expansion to arbitrary numbers of agents without retraining while maintaining cross-view consistency through a shared population-agnostic state.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent world models assume a fixed number of agents during training and inference, which limits scalability. There is a need for models that can generalize to unseen numbers of agents without retraining while maintaining cross-view consistency.

Method: Khora decouples world-state evolution from visual rendering and introduces a population-agnostic rendering mechanism. Cross-view consistency is achieved through a shared world state that doesn't assume a predefined number of agents, and agent-specific observations are generated by querying this state via a unified rendering interface. This avoids dense inter-observation-stream interactions inside the video generator, enabling approximately linear scaling with the number of queried views.

Result: Qualitative experiments show generalization to unseen numbers of agents while maintaining visual quality and multi-agent consistency. A real-time interactive system demonstrates scalable open-world simulation.

Conclusion: Khora addresses the scalability challenge in multi-agent world models by leveraging a population-agnostic shared world state, enabling inference-time expansion to arbitrary agent counts and approximately linear practical scaling.

Abstract: World models have recently achieved impressive progress in visual prediction and interactive generation, but extending them to multi-agent environments introduces a fundamental scalability challenge. Existing methods generally assume a fixed number of agents during training and inference, which ties the model to a pre-determined agent population and limits inference-time scalability. Our key insight is that cross-view consistency should arise from a shared world state whose evolution does not assume a predefined number of agents, while agent-specific observations should be generated by querying this state through a unified rendering interface. Based on this insight, we propose Khora, a scalable multi-agent world model that supports inference-time expansion to arbitrary numbers of agents without retraining. Our framework decouples world-state evolution from visual rendering and introduces a population-agnostic rendering mechanism for incorporating other agent information. This design maintains cross-view consistency through the shared world state rather than through dense interactions among observation streams inside the expensive video generator, enabling approximately linear practical scaling with the number of queried views. Qualitative experiments demonstrate that our approach generalizes to unseen numbers of agents while maintaining visual quality and multi-agent consistency. We further implement a real-time interactive system to demonstrate scalable open-world simulation.

</details>


### [118] [REVEAL: A Rubric-Guided Agent for Explicit Evidence Sufficiency Verificationin Long-Video Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08612)
*Caijun Yan,Yang Zhou,Meixing Shi,Haoran Sun,Yichen Li,Yuxiang Cai,Yankai Jiang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: REVEAL is a rubric-guided agent framework for long-video question answering that uses an adaptive visual-similarity-based preprocessing pipeline to construct offline-online memory, and an automatically constructed rubric library to explicitly verify evidence sufficiency and trigger targeted re-retrieval when evidence is missing, outperforming state-of-the-art methods without extra training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing retrieval-augmented and memory-augmented methods for long-video QA rely on rigid fixed-length temporal chunking and static offline memory banks that fragment coherent events and fail to adapt during real-time reasoning. Moreover, current approaches prioritize retrieval relevance over evidence sufficiency, stopping once semantically relevant clues are found even when key temporal, causal, or fine-grained action evidence is still missing.

Method: REVEAL consists of (1) an adaptive visual-similarity-based preprocessing pipeline that groups visually coherent adjacent frames into natural event units, constructing an offline-online video memory that captures global video context offline while dynamically maintaining question-conditioned memory online; and (2) an automatically constructed rubric library that explicitly verifies whether retrieved evidence satisfies sufficiency criteria, pinpoints missing clues upon verification failure, and directs targeted re-retrieval for complementary information.

Result: Without any extra training, REVEAL consistently outperforms both closed-source and open-source state-of-the-art methods across extensive experiments, demonstrating that explicitly verifying evidence sufficiency retrieves decisive clues that prior methods miss and yields more reliable long-video reasoning.

Conclusion: Explicitly verifying evidence sufficiency, rather than stopping at semantic relevance, is key to retrieving the decisive clues needed for reliable long-video reasoning. REVEAL's training-free rubric-guided approach proves effective across comprehensive benchmarks.

Abstract: Recently, retrieval-augmented and memory-augmented methods have emerged as two promising paradigms for long-video question answering. However, existing methods typically rely on rigid, fixed-length temporal chunking (e.g., 10s) and static offline memory banks, which not only fragment coherent continuous events but also fail to adapt during real-time reasoning. Moreover, whether using multi-scale summaries or multimodal knowledge graphs, current approaches prioritize retrieval relevance while overlooking evidence sufficiency, often stopping to answer once only semantically relevant clues are retrieved, even when key temporal, causal, or fine-grained action evidence is still missing. To tackle these challenges, we propose REVEAL, a rubric-guided agent framework. As a foundation, we introduce an adaptive visual-similarity-based preprocessing pipeline that groups visually coherent adjacent frames into natural event units to construct an offline-online video memory---capturing global video context offline while dynamically maintaining question-conditioned memory online. Built upon this structured memory, REVEAL uses an automatically constructed rubric library to explicitly verify whether retrieved evidence satisfies sufficiency criteria, pinpoints missing clues upon verification failure, and directs targeted re-retrieval for complementary information. Without any extra training, REVEAL consistently outperforms both closed-source and open-source state-of-the-art methods across extensive experiments. These results show that explicitly verifying evidence sufficiency, rather than stopping at semantic relevance, retrieves the decisive clues that prior methods miss and yields more reliable long-video reasoning.

</details>


### [119] [VADER: Adaptive Debiasing for Hallucination Mitigation in Video Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08622)
*Dong Xing,Jiaxin Chen,Hang Yang,Peixun Liu,Qiushi Yang,Yuqing Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VADER is a training-free framework for reducing hallucinated responses in large vision-language models during video understanding, using two complementary modules—Visual Focus Reallocation (VFR) for video-specific intervention policies and Selective Evidence Erasure (SEE) for constructing a hard-to-compensate contrastive branch—that together down-weight evidence-unsupported predictions and achieve state-of-the-art results on event-level grounding benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LVLMs can produce fluent but video-unsupported responses. Existing training-free debiasing methods either apply a globally fixed visual intervention that cannot adapt to video-dependent fusion paths, or rely on input perturbation whose contrastive branch can be compensated by cross-frame redundancy, both limiting their effectiveness.

Method: VADER consists of two modules: (1) Visual Focus Reallocation (VFR) diagnoses layer-wise visual-to-text evidence flow to automatically determine where and how strongly to reallocate pre-softmax attention from system-token to video-token blocks per video-question input; (2) Selective Evidence Erasure (SEE) independently masks high-importance visual tokens in every frame, creating a prior-biased branch that is difficult to compensate via neighboring frames. Contrastive decoding then down-weights predictions that remain confident after erasure.

Result: VADER yields substantial improvements across multiple VideoLLMs on event-level grounding and temporal consistency, achieving 72.60% accuracy on EventHallusion with LLaVA-Video-7B.

Conclusion: VADER demonstrates that video-adaptive, evidence-driven intervention combined with selective erasure-based contrastive decoding effectively mitigates hallucinations in LVLMs, offering a training-free solution adaptable to diverse video understanding architectures.

Abstract: Large vision-language models (LVLMs) have demonstrated strong performance in open-ended video understanding, yet they remain prone to fluent responses unsupported by video evidence. Existing training-free methods typically apply a globally fixed visual intervention or construct a contrastive branch through input perturbation. The former cannot accommodate video-dependent fusion paths, while the latter can be compensated by cross-frame redundancy. We therefore propose Video-Adaptive Debiasing via Evidence Reweighting (VADER), a training-free framework with two complementary modules. Visual Focus Reallocation (VFR) automatically instantiates an intervention policy for each video-question input: it diagnoses layer-wise visual-to-text evidence flow, determines where to intervene, and derives how strongly to reallocate pre-softmax attention from system-token to video-token blocks. Selective Evidence Erasure (SEE) independently masks high-importance visual tokens in every frame, constructing a prior-biased branch that is difficult to compensate through neighboring frames. Contrastive decoding then down-weights predictions that remain confident after selective evidence erasure. Across multiple VideoLLMs, VADER yields substantial improvements on event-level grounding and temporal consistency; on LLaVA-Video-7B, it reaches 72.60% accuracy on EventHallusion.

</details>


### [120] [VLZip: Unified Visual and Textual Compression for Interleaved Long-Context Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08630)
*Yuqi Zhang,Cheng Chen,Yuyu Guo,Wenjie Yang,Lingchen Meng,Peng Di,Hang Yu,Zuxuan Wu,Yu-Gang Jiang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VLZip is a framework that unifies visual and textual compression for VLMs by hierarchically distilling segments into layer-specific "soft prefixes" injected into decoder hidden states, drastically shortening attention sequences while preserving global context. It introduces LongVLBench benchmark and achieves training up to 120K tokens (6x baseline increase), inference beyond 280K tokens, and memory scalability to 2M tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLMs struggle with ultra-long interleaved image-text sequences due to quadratic self-attention complexity. Existing solutions either use aggressive token pruning (risking irreversible information loss) or efficient but less precise architectures, and largely ignore the textual component—creating a need for a unified approach that compresses both modalities without compromising fidelity.

Method: VLZip hierarchically distills visual and textual segments into compact, layer-specific "soft prefixes" and injects them into each decoder layer's hidden states, shortening the attention sequence while preserving fine-grained global context within a pure Transformer architecture.

Result: VLZip achieves leading performance on long-context multimodal reasoning, enables training up to 120K tokens (6x increase over baseline), supports inference beyond 280K tokens with significantly reduced memory, and demonstrates memory scalability to handle up to 2M tokens—excelling at extreme context lengths where existing methods collapse.

Conclusion: VLZip establishes an efficient and powerful new standard for long-context multimodal AI by unifying visual and textual compression, and introduces LongVLBench to address deficient evaluations in the field.

Abstract: Vision Language Models (VLMs) face significant challenges with ultra-long, interleaved image-text sequences due to the quadratic complexity of self-attention. Current solutions either resort to aggressive token pruning, risking irreversible information loss, or adopt efficient but less precise architectures, while largely ignoring the equally vital textual component. We introduce VLZip, a framework that unifies visual and textual compression for high-fidelity reasoning within a pure Transformer. At its core, VLZip hierarchically distills visual and textual segments into compact, layer-specific "soft prefixes" and injects them into each decoder layer's hidden states, drastically shortening the attention sequence while preserving fine-grained global context. To address deficient evaluations in the field, we also introduce LongVLBench, a new benchmark derived from video narratives that demands holistic, narrative-level reasoning. Extensive experiments show VLZip achieves leading performance on long-context multimodal reasoning, enabling training up to 120K tokens, a 6x increase over the baseline, and inference beyond 280K tokens with significantly reduced memory, while demonstrating the memory scalability to handle up to 2M tokens. By excelling at extreme context lengths where existing methods collapse, VLZip establishes an efficient and powerful new standard for long-context multimodal AI. Code is available at https://github.com/ShareLab-SII/VLZip.

</details>


### [121] [Agentic Visual Reasoning in Whole-Slide Pathology Images via Active Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08648)
*Jingyun Chen,Fengchun Liu,Linghan Cai,Songhan Jiang,Shenjin Huang,Hongpeng Wang,Lequan Yu,Yongbing Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: AdaptivePath is an active-perception framework for whole-slide pathology image reasoning that formulates evidence acquisition as sequential decision-making. It uses a Navigator policy trained with pathologist-reviewed labels via alternating representation learning and PPO to hierarchically select sparse observation regions across magnifications, coupled with a Morphology Interpreter, Deliberator, and Arbiter to produce question-conditioned answers. It achieves SOTA zero-shot performance on WSI and region pathology VQA benchmarks, 80.14% accuracy on six TCGA cancer subtype classification cohorts, and 82.9% accuracy in a blinded diagnostic-utility study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing WSI methods either compress densely sampled patches into global representations (weakening links between predictions and morphology) or use pretrained VLMs with heuristic region selection (lacking pathology-trained observation policies). There is a need for a learnable, active-perception approach that can identify sparse diagnostic evidence and integrate observations across spatial scales in gigapixel pathology slides.

Method: AdaptivePath treats WSI evidence acquisition as sequential decision-making. The Navigator learns question-agnostic abnormality-driven navigation from pathologist-reviewed labels (avoiding costly question-specific trajectory annotations), trained via alternating representation learning and proximal policy optimization, then fine-tuned with geometric and appearance consistency objectives. During inference, the Navigator hierarchically acquires sparse observations from low to high magnification under a limited ROI budget. A Morphology Interpreter converts observations into question-conditioned evidence; a Deliberator evaluates evidence and revises intermediate answers across magnifications; an Arbiter integrates deliberation history to produce final answers.

Result: AdaptivePath achieves state-of-the-art zero-shot performance on WSI and region pathology VQA benchmarks, reaches 80.14% accuracy for cancer subtype classification across six TCGA cohorts, and attains 82.9% accuracy in a blinded diagnostic-utility study where pathologists used AdaptivePath-selected observation sequences.

Conclusion: Learned active perception—combining navigation policies with hierarchical observation and multi-stage deliberation—enables effective and traceable visual reasoning over gigapixel pathology slides, offering a promising alternative to static compression or heuristic selection approaches.

Abstract: Whole-slide visual reasoning requires identifying sparse diagnostic evidence in gigapixel pathology slides and integrating observations across spatial scales. Existing WSI methods either compress densely sampled patches into global representations or use pretrained vision-language models with heuristic region selection, weakening links between predictions and morphology or lacking pathology-trained observation policies. We present AdaptivePath, an active-perception framework that formulates WSI evidence acquisition as sequential decision making. The Navigator learns question-agnostic abnormality-driven navigation from pathologist-reviewed labels to select observation locations and spatial extents, avoiding costly question-specific trajectory annotations. We train this policy through alternating representation learning and proximal policy optimization, followed by fine-tuning with geometric and appearance consistency objectives to stabilize focus trajectories. During inference, the Navigator hierarchically acquires sparse observations from low to high magnification under a limited ROI budget. A Morphology Interpreter converts observations into question-conditioned evidence, while the Deliberator evaluates evidence and revises intermediate answers across magnifications. The Arbiter integrates deliberation history to produce final answers. AdaptivePath achieves state-of-the-art zero-shot performance on WSI and region pathology VQA benchmarks and reaches 80.14% accuracy for cancer subtype classification across six TCGA cohorts. In a blinded diagnostic-utility study, pathologists using AdaptivePath-selected observation sequences achieve 82.9% accuracy. These results demonstrate that learned active perception enables effective and traceable visual reasoning over gigapixel pathology slides.

</details>


### [122] [JSGS: JPEG State-Guided Supervision for 3D Gaussian Splatting from Mixed-Quality Views](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08659)
*Jinhua Cui,Anhong Wang,Kai Hu,Donghan Bu,Peihao Li,Tammam Tillo,Hao Jing,Shiao Xu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: JSGS is a method for 3D Gaussian Splatting that handles mixed-quality JPEG images by using per-view JPEG quantization tables to construct a domain-matched observation operator, enabling weighted frequency-band supervision and guided regularization of small high-opacity Gaussians, achieving state-of-the-art perceptual and structural quality metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard 3DGS assumes all input images faithfully sample scene radiance, but mixed-quality JPEG images violate this assumption due to compression-induced blocking and ringing artifacts that corrupt Gaussian updates shared across views.

Method: JSGS uses luminance and chrominance quantization tables from each JPEG file to construct a view-specific JPEG observation operator that encodes/decodes rendered views for domain-matched comparison. It applies a low-frequency-band loss for coarse structure anchoring and a weighted middle-frequency-band loss using continuous weights from the luminance quantization table. Block disagreement additionally guides the Gaussian Controller to regularize small, high-opacity primitives in disagreement regions.

Result: Across seven scenes and three mixed-quality schedules, JSGS achieves the lowest mean LPIPS and highest mean SSIM under every schedule while rendering at approximately 150 FPS.

Conclusion: JSGS effectively addresses JPEG compression artifacts in 3DGS reconstruction from mixed-quality views by exploiting JPEG metadata for per-view domain-matched supervision and guided regularization, achieving superior quality without sacrificing rendering speed.

Abstract: Standard 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) assumes that every input image faithfully samples scene radiance. However, mixed-quality JPEG images violate this assumption because compression-induced blocking and ringing artifacts can corrupt updates to Gaussians shared across views. To address this problem, we propose JPEG State-Guided Supervision for 3D Gaussian Splatting from Mixed-Quality Views (JSGS). JSGS uses luminance and chrominance quantization tables stored in each JPEG file to construct a view-specific JPEG observation operator. This operator encodes and decodes each rendered view for domain-matched comparison with the corresponding decoded input image. The luminance quantization table supplies continuous weights within a fixed middle frequency band. A loss in the low frequency band anchors coarse structure, while the weighted middle frequency loss redistributes supervision among the selected DCT coordinates. The resulting block disagreement also guides the Gaussian Controller to regularize small primitives with high opacity in disagreement regions. Across seven scenes and three mixed-quality schedules, JSGS achieves the lowest mean LPIPS and the highest mean SSIM under every schedule while rendering at approximately 150 FPS. Code: https://github.com/Jayden-Cui/JSGS.

</details>


### [123] [Degradation-Guided Underwater Image Restoration with Task-Oriented Latent Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08661)
*Xu Zhang,Xuhui Cao,Kangzhe Yuan,Laibin Chang,Yichu Xu,Shi Chen,Huan Zhang,Yong Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PROTEUS is an underwater image restoration method that couples degradation-guided feature adaptation with task-oriented latent control, addressing the dual role of degradation information (spatial/spectral cues for restoration vs. entangled features in decoding) via two complementary modules: a Guided Dynamic Feature Modulation Block for spatially varying degradation adaptation and a task-oriented latent controller for channel-wise modulation of skip features.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing underwater image restoration methods overlook the dual role of degradation information—they either underexploit degradation cues or directly forward encoder features through skip connections, allowing degradation-entangled features to be propagated without explicit regulation during decoding.

Method: PROTEUS consists of two complementary perspectives: (1) at the feature level, a Guided Dynamic Feature Modulation Block exploits spatially varying degradation cues to adapt feature processing across network stages; (2) at the representation level, a task-oriented latent controller learns a structured control code under discriminative regularisation and uses it for channel-wise modulation of skip features, without requiring metrically cleaner embedding.

Result: Extensive experiments on five paired and four non-reference underwater benchmarks demonstrate that PROTEUS achieves highly competitive restoration performance with a favourable balance between restoration quality and computational cost.

Conclusion: PROTEUS effectively leverages degradation information in a dual manner—both adapting features dynamically using degradation cues and regulating skip connections via a task-oriented latent controller—achieving strong restoration performance while maintaining computational efficiency.

Abstract: Degradation information in underwater images plays a dual role: its spatial and spectral cues can guide adaptive restoration, while degradation-entangled features may be propagated without explicit regulation during decoding. Existing methods largely overlook this dual role, either underexploiting degradation cues or directly forwarding encoder features through skip connections. To address this issue, we propose PROTEUS, which couples degradation-guided feature adaptation with task?oriented latent control. PROTEUS tackles this problem from two complementary perspectives. At the feature level, the Guided Dynamic Feature Modulation Block exploits spatially varying degradation cues to adapt feature processing across network stages. At the representation level, the task-oriented latent controller learns a structured control code under discriminative regularisation and uses it for channel-wise modulation of skip features, without requiring the code to form a metrically cleaner embedding. Extensive experiments on five paired and four non-reference underwater benchmarks demonstrate that PROTEUS achieves highly competitive restoration performance, with a favourable balance between restoration quality and computational cost.

</details>


### [124] [FiRe: Fixed-Noise Refinement for Visual Counterfactual Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08664)
*Yan Zeng,Changlu Guo,Oskar Kristoffersen,Anders Nymark Christensen,Morten Rieger Hannemose,Anders Bjorholm Dahl*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: FiRe is a fixed-noise refinement framework for visual counterfactual explanations that maps inputs to a fixed noise level and iteratively refines the noisy state, using Pixel Mean Flow for clean-image prediction and three specific controls (dual-mask, adaptive guidance, early stopping) for minimal and localized edits. It achieves ~3x faster inference and 8x fewer FLOPs than the strongest baseline while maintaining comparable or better quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing DDPM-based counterfactual explanation methods struggle because they follow long reverse denoising trajectories where changing noise levels make semantic editability and spatial control difficult to balance. Additionally, the editable noisy states don't match the clean images that target classifiers are trained on, leading to costly recursive denoising or low-quality one-step estimates.

Method: FiRe works by: (1) mapping input to a fixed noise level instead of following a reverse denoising trajectory, (2) iteratively refining the noisy state at that fixed level, (3) adapting Pixel Mean Flow to predict clean images directly from noisy states for classifier guidance, and (4) introducing three controls—dynamic dual-mask strategy, adaptive guidance, and early stopping—to ensure minimal and localized edits.

Result: On five tasks across three datasets, FiRe achieves approximately 3x faster online inference and 8x fewer FLOPs compared to the strongest recent baseline, while obtaining comparable or state-of-the-art counterfactual quality.

Conclusion: The fixed-noise refinement paradigm with clean-image prediction via Pixel Mean Flow and three FiRe-specific controls provides an efficient and effective alternative to trajectory-based counterfactual editing, resolving the noise-level mismatch and balancing editability with spatial control.

Abstract: Visual counterfactual explanations aim to change classifier decisions through realistic and localized edits while preserving decision-irrelevant content. Existing DDPM-based methods typically perform classifier-guided editing along a long reverse denoising trajectory. The changing noise levels make semantic editability and spatial control difficult to balance, and the editable state is noisy, whereas the target classifier is trained on clean images. As a result, these methods require either costly recursive denoising or low-quality one-step estimates to obtain classifier-facing clean images. We propose FiRe, a Fixed-noise Refinement framework for visual counterfactual explanations. Rather than following a reverse denoising trajectory, FiRe maps the input to a fixed noise level and iteratively refines the noisy state at that level. To provide clean images for classifier guidance, FiRe first adapts Pixel Mean Flow to visual counterfactual explanation, enabling direct clean-image prediction from noisy states. To make fixed-noise refinement produce minimal and localized counterfactual edits, FiRe introduces three FiRe-specific controls: a dynamic dual-mask strategy, adaptive guidance, and early stopping, which determine where edits accumulate, which changes become visible, and when refinement stops. Experiments on five tasks across three datasets show that, compared with the strongest recent baseline, FiRe achieves about 3$\times$ faster online inference and 8$\times$ fewer FLOPs while obtaining comparable or state-of-the-art counterfactual quality.

</details>


### [125] [UniSpace: Unified Visual Representation and Scalable Multimodal Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08676)
*Jinbo Yan,Limeng Qiao,Jie Qin,Junyan He,Feize Wu,Guanglu Wan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes Patch Reparameterization, which adds a reconstruction-aware patch embedding to a frozen semantic ViT, creating a unified visual representation (UniSpace) that supports understanding, generation, and editing in a single space without a separate VAE pathway, scaled to an 8B Mixture-of-Transformer-Experts model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Semantic vision encoders excel at multimodal understanding but discard fine-grained visual details in their final tokens, limiting their use in reconstruction-sensitive tasks like image generation and editing. The authors ask whether understanding, generation, and editing can be unified in a single pretrained semantic ViT representation space.

Method: The authors show that frozen ViT blocks are not inherently incapable of preserving visual details; rather, the original patch parameterization drives semantic abstraction. They introduce Patch Reparameterization, which keeps the original semantic pathway while adding a reconstruction-aware patch embedding that feeds fine-grained visual information to the same frozen ViT blocks. This is scaled into UniSpace, an 8B Mixture-of-Transformer-Experts model.

Result: The unified representation preserves multimodal understanding while enabling high-fidelity image reconstruction and a favorable reconstruction-generation trade-off. System-level evaluations demonstrate practical text-to-image generation and instruction-based image editing.

Conclusion: A reparameterized pretrained ViT can serve as a unified visual interface for scalable multimodal modeling, eliminating the need for a separate VAE pathway and unifying understanding, generation, and editing in one visual space.

Abstract: Semantic vision encoders have become a central visual interface for multimodal understanding and semantic conditioning in image generation. However, their final tokens discard fine-grained visual details, leading to poor pixel reconstruction and limiting their use in reconstruction-sensitive tasks such as image generation and editing. In this work, we ask whether understanding, generation, and editing can be modeled in a single visual representation space built from a pretrained semantic ViT. We show that the frozen Transformer blocks of a semantic ViT are not intrinsically unable to preserve visual details. Instead, the original patch parameterization drives the representation toward semantic abstraction, making fine-grained information difficult to recover from the final tokens. Based on this observation, we introduce \emph{Patch Reparameterization}, which preserves the original semantic pathway while adding a reconstruction-aware patch embedding that provides fine-grained visual information to the same frozen ViT blocks. The resulting unified representation preserves multimodal understanding while enabling high-fidelity image reconstruction and a favorable reconstruction--generation trade-off. We further scale this representation into \emph{UniSpace}, an 8B Mixture-of-Transformer-Experts model that performs understanding, generation, and editing in the same visual space without a separate VAE pathway. System-level evaluations demonstrate practical text-to-image generation and instruction-based image editing, showing that a reparameterized pretrained ViT can serve as a unified visual interface for scalable multimodal modeling.

</details>


### [126] [Semi-Dense Matching Uncertainty Is Not Just Local Confidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08685)
*Khoa Hoang,Hoang-Tuan Nguyen,Huong Ninh,Hai Tran,Long Q. Tran*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes a lightweight, post-hoc uncertainty estimation framework for semi-dense matching that uses a two-component calibrated Laplace mixture model with only 9 learnable parameters. It captures both sharp local refinement noise and broader tail failures from coarse-assignment errors, and introduces the CoRe (Coarse-success posterior Refit) module for geometric refitting. The method improves downstream geometric accuracy across various pretrained matchers and robust estimators with minimal computational overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing semi-dense matching methods under the coarse-to-fine paradigm lack well-quantified uncertainties. Coarse-assignment failures are often ignored, leading to truncated error distributions and misjudged geometric estimations, which can severely degrade downstream tasks.

Method: The paper proposes a lightweight post-hoc uncertainty estimation framework with a two-component calibrated Laplace mixture model (only 9 learnable parameters) to explicitly model both sharp local refinement noise and the broader tail of coarse-assignment failures. It also introduces the Coarse-success posterior Refit (CoRe) method, a geometric refitting module that uses posterior probabilities of coarse-assignment success as soft correspondence weights.

Result: Extensive experiments demonstrate consistent improvement in downstream geometric accuracy across various pretrained-only matchers and robust estimators, achieved with minimal computational overhead.

Conclusion: The proposed probabilistic framework effectively captures and quantifies uncertainty in semi-dense matching, improving geometric estimation robustness by explicitly modeling coarse-assignment failures.

Abstract: Reliable semi-dense matching is essential for modern geometric vision systems. Designed under a coarse-to-fine paradigm, it achieves an optimal balance between performance and computational cost. However, existing methods often struggle to provide well-quantified uncertainties, where catastrophic coarse-assignment failures are ignored, leading to truncated error distributions and severely misjudged geometric estimations. In this paper, we propose a lightweight, post-hoc overall uncertainty estimation framework that introduces a two-component calibrated Laplace mixture model with only 9 learnable parameters. The objective is to explicitly capture both the sharp local refinement noise and the broader tail of coarse-assignment failures. We introduce the Coarse-success posterior Refit (CoRe) method, a geometric refitting module that utilizes the posterior probability of coarse-assignment success as soft correspondence weights. Extensive experiments show that our method consistently improves downstream geometric accuracy across various pretrained-only matchers and robust estimators with minimal computational overhead. Our code is available at https://github.com/khoavpt/Probabilistic-matching.

</details>


### [127] [CUPA-T2*: Covariance-Aware Uncertainty Propagation and Alignment for T2* Mapping in Accelerated MRI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08693)
*Gideon N. L. Rouwendaal,Natascha Niessen,Hannah Eichhorn,Dirk H. J. Poot,Christine Preibisch,Julia A. Schnabel*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CUPA-T2* is a framework that propagates voxel-wise inter-echo uncertainty from stochastic Monte Carlo dropout reconstructions into downstream T2* fitting via covariance-aware sampling, using a heteroscedastic MLP with a correlation-based regularizer. It achieves competitive T2* fitting performance (improving white-matter results at higher accelerations) and substantially improves alignment between reconstruction uncertainty and predicted T2* variance, while trading off with calibration and selective prediction performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantitative T2* mapping has high biomarker potential but is limited by long acquisition times. While k-space undersampling with learning-based reconstruction accelerates scans, reconstruction artifacts and noise propagate into downstream T2* fitting, degrading accuracy. Existing approaches do not explicitly account for reconstruction uncertainty during T2* fitting.

Method: CUPA-T2* explicitly propagates voxel-wise inter-echo uncertainty from stochastic Monte Carlo dropout reconstructions to T2* fitting via covariance-aware sampling. T2* fitting is performed with a heteroscedastic MLP and a correlation-based regularizer that enforces alignment between predicted variance and reconstruction uncertainty.

Result: On accelerated brain MRI data, CUPA-T2* achieves competitive overall T2* fitting performance and improves white-matter performance at higher accelerations. It substantially increases alignment between reconstruction uncertainty and predicted T2* variance compared to a heteroscedastic baseline, though with a trade-off against calibration (ECE) and selective prediction performance (AURC).

Conclusion: CUPA-T2* enables reconstruction uncertainty-aware T2* fitting and provides voxel-wise uncertainty maps to support interpretation of quantitative T2* estimates, representing a step toward practical accelerated quantitative MRI with reliable uncertainty quantification.

Abstract: Quantitative T2* maps have strong potential for biomarker discovery but are limited by long scan times, rendering them impractical in clinical settings. Significant acceleration can be achieved through undersampling in k-space combined with learning-based reconstruction. However, reconstruction artifacts and noise can propagate into downstream T2* fitting, degrading its accuracy. We introduce CUPA-T2*, a framework that explicitly propagates voxel-wise inter-echo uncertainty from stochastic Monte Carlo dropout reconstructions to downstream T2* fitting via covariance-aware sampling. T2* fitting is performed with a heteroscedastic MLP and a correlation-based regularizer that encourages alignment between predicted variance and reconstruction uncertainty. Experiments on accelerated brain MRI data show tissue-dependent behavior: CUPA-T2* achieves competitive overall T2* fitting performance and improves white-matter performance at higher accelerations. Compared with a heteroscedastic baseline, the proposed framework substantially increases alignment between reconstruction uncertainty and predicted T2* variance, while also revealing a trade-off with calibration (ECE) and selective prediction performance (AURC). CUPA-T2* enables reconstruction uncertainty-aware T2* fitting and delivers voxel-wise uncertainty maps to support the interpretation of quantitative T2* estimates.

</details>


### [128] [OccAnyScene: Towards Unified Indoor-Outdoor 3D Occupancy Predictio](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08696)
*Junjie Liu,Wanshui Gan,Zitong Dai,Guiping Cao,Yan Li,Ke Chen,Dongmei Jiang,Xiangyuan Lan,Jianguo Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: OccAnyScene introduces Cross-Scene 3D Semantic Occupancy Prediction, a unified framework that handles heterogeneous indoor and outdoor scenes with varying cameras, spatial ranges, voxel specifications, and semantic taxonomies using a pixel-frustum-centered Gaussian approach built on a pretrained depth foundation model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing 3D semantic occupancy methods are specialized to fixed scene types and occupancy protocols, lacking the ability to handle diverse indoor and outdoor scenes with varying camera configurations, spatial scales, voxel specifications, and semantic taxonomies in a single model.

Method: The framework uses two key components: (1) Pixel-Aligned Frustum Feature Aggregation to construct camera-aware frustum queries for each feature pixel, and (2) Frustum-Parameterized Gaussian Construction to decode each query into multiple Gaussians whose positions and sizes are constrained by predicted pixel depth and corresponding frustum geometry. It is built upon a pretrained depth foundation model.

Result: OccAnyScene achieves 59.92% mIoU on the indoor Occ-ScanNet and 23.06% mIoU on the outdoor SurroundOcc-nuScenes, setting new state-of-the-art results on both benchmarks.

Conclusion: The pixel-frustum-centered Gaussian framework provides metric-consistent yet scene-adaptive image-to-3D lifting across varying camera configurations and scene scales, enabling a single model to effectively handle cross-scene 3D semantic occupancy prediction.

Abstract: 3D occupancy prediction is fundamental to scene understanding, yet existing 3D semantic occupancy methods are typically specialized to fixed scene types and occupancy protocols. We introduce Cross-Scene 3D Semantic Occupancy Prediction, a new task setting which requires a single model to handle heterogeneous indoor and outdoor scenes with varying cameras, spatial ranges, voxel specifications, and semantic taxonomies. This setting poses a fundamental challenge: achieving metric-consistent yet scene-adaptive image-to-3D lifting across varying camera configurations and scene scales. To address this challenge, we propose OccAnyScene, a pixel-frustum-centered Gaussian framework built upon a pretrained depth foundation model. Specifically, the framework employs Pixel-Aligned Frustum Feature Aggregation to construct a camera-aware frustum query for each feature pixel, and Frustum-Parameterized Gaussian Construction to decode each query into multiple Gaussians whose positions and sizes are constrained by the predicted pixel depth and corresponding frustum geometry. OccAnyScene sets new state-of-the-art results, achieving 59.92% mIoU on the indoor Occ-ScanNet and 23.06% mIoU on the outdoor SurroundOcc-nuScenes.

</details>


### [129] [SRE-FER: Regional residual evidence learning for mitigating local evidence dilution in fine-grained facial expression recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08702)
*Jiaye Song,Ruochen Zhang,Yuliang Wang,Jiaqi Wu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SRE-FER is a readout-level regional residual evidence learning framework for fine-grained facial expression recognition that adds zero-initialized residual logits (RERA) to recover sparse muscular cues diluted by global aggregation, using Action Unit guidance for anatomical priors during training and achieving competitive performance on three benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-grained FER requires capturing subtle muscular cues distinguishing adjacent emotions, but existing methods face a dilemma: detector-based methods rely on fragile landmark pipelines, while directly transferring foundation models with global readouts causes local evidence dilution—early global aggregation washes out sparse muscular signals, leading to persistent confusion between categories like fear/surprise and sad/neutral.

Method: The proposed SRE-FER framework features a core module called RERA that adds zero-initialized residual logits to refine class boundaries while preserving the backbone's global prediction. Training uses Action Unit (AU) guidance based on FACS anatomical priors to steer regional features toward expression-relevant areas, requiring no external facial pipeline at inference. An optional Full setting routes sample-specific non-redundant tokens.

Result: SRE-FER achieves 92.76% on RAF-DB, 91.32% on FERPlus, and 67.78% on AffectNet-7, demonstrating highly competitive performance compared to existing FER methods.

Conclusion: The readout-level regional residual evidence learning framework effectively recovers diluted local muscular evidence without requiring external facial pipelines at inference, achieving state-of-the-art competitive results across multiple facial expression recognition benchmarks.

Abstract: Fine-grained facial expression recognition (FER) hinges on capturing subtle muscular cues that distinguish adjacent emotions. Yet capturing these cues presents a dilemma. Detector-based methods depend on fragile landmark pipelines, whereas we find that directly transferring foundation models such as DINOv3 under conventional global readouts can cause local evidence dilution: early global aggregation washes out sparse muscular signals and leaves persistent confusion between categories such as fear/surprise and sad/neutral. To recover this evidence, we propose SRE-FER, a readout-level regional residual evidence learning framework. Its core module, RERA, adds zero-initialized residual logits that refine class boundaries while preserving the backbone's global prediction. Training-time action unit (AU) guidance steers regional features toward expression-relevant areas using Facial Action Coding System (FACS)-based anatomical priors, without requiring an external facial pipeline at inference. An optional Full setting further routes sample-specific non-redundant tokens. On three benchmarks, SRE-FER attains 92.76% on RAF-DB, 91.32% on FERPlus, and 67.78% on AffectNet-7, demonstrating highly competitive performance compared to existing FER methods.

</details>


### [130] [Resolution Meets Reduction: Efficient Visual Context for 3D Radiology Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08713)
*Jonathan Suprijadi,Raphael Stock,Moritz Langenberg,David Zimmerer,Kim-Celine Kahl,Stefan Denner,Yannick Kirchhoff,Karol Gotkowski,Maximilian Rokuss,Jeremias Traub,Tassilo Wald,Constantin Ulrich,Klaus Maier-Hein*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper systematically investigates how to efficiently apply vision-language models to 3D CT volume-based radiology report generation, evaluating the allocation of vision token budgets across field of view, spatial resolution, and vision-to-language projection. Through extensive experiments with four vision encoders, five token-reducing projectors, and five instruction-tuned LLMs on two CT report datasets, they find that anatomy-guided region cropping is the most consistent strategy (improving macro F1 by +3.7 on average for 3D ViT), while the PerceiverResampler with higher-resolution features yields the strongest configurations, achieving state-of-the-art macro F1 scores of 49.5 on CT-RATE and 49.0 on Merlin.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Applying vision-language models to full 3D CT volumes is computationally challenging because modern vision encoders produce tens of thousands of vision tokens per scan, making the visual sequence a primary bottleneck for the LLM. While vision-to-language projectors can compress sequences, compression may discard clinically relevant detail. The optimal allocation of the vision-token budget across field of view, spatial resolution, and projection remains an open design question that this paper addresses.

Method: The authors systematically evaluate four heterogeneous vision encoders (CNN- and ViT-based), five token-reducing projectors (up to 64x compression) plus a non-reducing MLP projector baseline, and five instruction-tuned LLMs (1.7B-4B) on two large-scale CT report datasets (CT-RATE and Merlin). They investigate matched LLM token budgets and compare strategies such as anatomy-guided region of interest cropping and increasing input resolution.

Result: Anatomy-guided region of interest cropping is the most consistent strategy, improving clinical macro F1 in 19 of 20 settings by +3.7 points on average for the 3D ViT Primus encoder and +1.1 for the slice-based 2D ViT Curia encoder. Increasing resolution is strongly projector-dependent, with PerceiverResampler paired with higher-resolution Curia features giving the strongest configuration. Best configurations achieve state-of-the-art macro F1 of 49.5 on CT-RATE and 49.0 on Merlin.

Conclusion: Vision-token budget allocation matters significantly for 3D CT radiology report generation. Anatomy-guided cropping is the most reliable strategy across settings, while optimal resolution scaling depends heavily on the choice of projector. The paper establishes new state-of-the-art results on both benchmark datasets and will release code and models to facilitate further research.

Abstract: Vision-language models offer a promising path toward automating radiology report generation, but applying them to full 3D CT volumes poses substantial computational challenges. Modern foundation vision encoders (VEs) can produce tens of thousands of vision tokens per scan, making the visual sequence passed to the large language model (LLM) a primary computational bottleneck. Vision-to-language projectors can compress this sequence to reduce computation, but may discard clinically relevant detail; conversely, effective compression can accommodate higher-resolution inputs while keeping the downstream token count fixed. How this vision-token budget should be allocated across input field of view, spatial resolution, and vision-to-language projection therefore remains an open design question. We systematically evaluate four heterogeneous VEs (CNN- and ViT-based), five token-reducing projectors at up to 64x compression alongside a non-reducing MLP projector baseline, and five instruction-tuned LLMs (1.7B--4B) on two large-scale CT report datasets (CT-RATE and Merlin). At matched LLM token budgets, anatomy-guided region of interest cropping is the most consistent strategy, improving clinical macro F1 in 19 of 20 settings by +3.7 points on average for the 3D ViT Primus encoder and +1.1 for the slice-based 2D ViT Curia encoder. Increasing input resolution further is strongly projector-dependent: the PerceiverResampler, paired with higher-resolution Curia features, yields the strongest configuration in the resolution study on both datasets. Our best configurations achieve state-of-the-art clinical macro F1 on the test sets, reaching 49.5 on CT-RATE and 49.0 on Merlin. Code and models will be published upon publication.

</details>


### [131] [High-Quality Exposure Correction with Diffusion-Based Image Generation Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08720)
*Ziwen Li,Meng Cao,Jinpu Zhang,Chunyang Li,Long Bao,Heng Sun,Yuehuan Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DPEC is a novel framework for image exposure correction that leverages pre-trained large-scale diffusion models as priors. It uses an efficient fine-tuning strategy to derive an exposure corrector with single-step denoising, and a joint cross-attention module to integrate multi-scale diffusion prior features with regression models, enabling better handling of extreme exposure regions while preserving high-frequency details.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing exposure correction methods focus excessively on pixel-wise accuracy, which leads to suboptimal perceptual quality, especially for extreme exposure regions. While diffusion models have shown remarkable performance in image generation, their application to exposure correction remains challenging because stochastic diffusion processes make it difficult to generate accurate image structures while maintaining high fidelity.

Method: DPEC (Diffusion Prior-based Exposure Correction) introduces: (1) an efficient fine-tuning strategy to derive an exposure corrector from pre-trained large-scale diffusion models, enabling single-step denoising for enhanced image generation; (2) a joint cross-attention module that combines diffusion models with regression models to integrate multi-scale diffusion prior features; (3) a design where the diffusion model focuses on low-frequency content while regression handles intricate texture details, jointly preserving high-frequency details and minimizing random artifacts.

Result: Experimental results show that DPEC consistently outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods on multiple exposure correction datasets in terms of fidelity, perceptual quality, and visual effects.

Conclusion: By effectively leveraging diffusion-based image generation priors and combining them with regression models through cross-attention, DPEC successfully addresses the challenge of extreme exposure correction while maintaining high perceptual quality and image fidelity.

Abstract: Although most existing exposure correction methods achieve high fidelity, they often place excessive focus on overall pixel-wise accuracy, making it challenging to effectively model extreme exposure regions, which results in suboptimal perceptual quality. Recently, diffusion models have received significant attention due to their remarkable performance in the realm of image generation. However, their successful application to exposure correction remains a challenging and open question. The key challenge lies in generating accurate image structures and maintaining high image fidelity during stochastic diffusion processes. In this paper, we propose DPEC (Diffusion Prior-based Exposure Correction), a novel framework for image exposure correction that utilizes diffusion-based image generation priors encapsulated in pre-trained large-scale diffusion models. Specifically, we first propose an efficient fine-tuning strategy to derive an exposure corrector from pre-trained models, enabling the generation of enhanced images in a single-step denoising process. Moreover, we seamlessly combine the strengths of diffusion models and regression models, and design a joint cross-attention module to integrate multi-scale diffusion prior features, thereby effectively preserving high-frequency details and minimizing random artifacts. The diffusion model focuses on dealing with low-frequency content rather than all the intricate texture details. The experimental results demonstrate that the proposed DPEC method consistently outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods on multiple exposure correction datasets, whether in terms of fidelity, perceptual quality, or visual effects.

</details>


### [132] [TomaMMU: A Comprehensive Multimodal Understanding Benchmark for Tomato Leaf Diseases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08727)
*Gia-Han Truong,Khang Nguyen Quoc,Luyl-Da Quach*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: TomaMMU is a large-scale tomato leaf disease multimodal understanding dataset with 28,808 images, 15 categories, and 213,119 human-annotated VQA pairs, along with TomaBench, a hierarchical three-level benchmark (Basic Perception, Pathology Understanding, Expert Diagnosis) for evaluating VLMs. Experiments with 14 state-of-the-art VLMs reveal significant gaps in fine-grained recognition and diagnostic reasoning, but fine-tuning on TomaMMU dramatically improves accuracy to 96.09% on challenging MCQs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current vision-language models (VLMs) struggle to translate visual perception into reliable diagnostic knowledge in agricultural domains, particularly for plant pathology, and there is a lack of large-scale, high-quality multimodal datasets and benchmarks specifically designed for tomato leaf disease understanding to systematically evaluate and improve these models.

Method: The paper introduces TomaMMU dataset built through a three-stage pipeline (Data Collection, Human Annotation, Question-Answer Generation), and TomaBench which organizes seven agricultural tasks into a hierarchical three-level taxonomy (Basic Perception, Pathology Understanding, Expert Diagnosis) to evaluate VLMs from low-level visual recognition to high-level diagnostic reasoning. They evaluate 14 state-of-the-art VLMs and also perform simple fine-tuning on TomaMMU to assess improvement.

Result: All 14 state-of-the-art VLMs consistently underperform on both challenging MCQs and open-ended questions, revealing pronounced gaps in fine-grained recognition and factually grounded reasoning. However, simple fine-tuning on TomaMMU substantially narrows this gap, boosting accuracy on challenging MCQs to 96.09%, outperforming recent VLMs.

Conclusion: Current VLMs cannot reliably translate visual perception into diagnostic knowledge for plant pathology, highlighting the need for targeted domain adaptation. Fine-tuning on TomaMMU demonstrates promising directions for improving agricultural VLM performance, and the dataset/benchmark are released publicly to support future work.

Abstract: To address this gap, we introduce TomaMMU, a large-scale Tomato leaf disease MultiModal Understanding dataset, alongside TomaBench, a benchmark for evaluating VLMs on tomato disease understanding. TomaMMU comprises 28,808 high-quality images spanning 15 categories and 213,119 human-annotated visual question-answer pairs, generated through a three-stage pipeline comprising Data Collection, Human Annotation, and Question-Answer Generation. Building on this foundation, TomaBench organizes seven agricultural tasks into a hierarchical three-level taxonomy spanning Basic Perception, Pathology Understanding, and Expert Diagnosis, which together enable systematic evaluation from low-level visual recognition to high-level diagnostic reasoning. The tasks assess visual symptom recognition, taxonomic relationships, and diagnostic reasoning, offering a comprehensive view of how well models grasp plant pathology. Our results pronounced gaps in fine-grained recognition and factually grounded reasoning with 14 state-of-the-art VLMs, consistently underperforming on both challenging MCQs and open-ended questions. These results suggest that current VLMs struggle to translate visual perception into reliable diagnostic knowledge, motivating the need for targeted domain adaptation. Simple fine-tuning on TomaMMU substantially narrows this gap, boosting accuracy on challenging MCQs to 96.09%, outperforming recent VLMs, and pointing toward promising directions for future work. All data and code is available in https://huggingface.co/datasets/enalis/TomaMMU.

</details>


### [133] [AnchorFold: A Focus-Then-Fold Framework via Recursive Attention Propagation for Efficient Multi-Vector Visual Document Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08732)
*Haoyu Zuo,Yibo Yan,Xin Zou,Shuliang Liu,Yi Cao,Mingdong Ou,Xuming Hu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: AnchorFold is a training-free focus-then-fold framework for compressing document-side indexes in multi-vector vision-language retrievers, using Recursive Attention Propagation to identify important anchor tokens and centrality-weighted aggregation to fold remaining tokens, achieving near-lossless compression (98.3% NDCG@5 at 5x) and outperforming all training-free baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-vector vision-language retrievers enable fine-grained Visual Document Retrieval through late interaction but incur substantial storage and computational overhead from storing and scoring hundreds of visual patch embeddings per page. Existing training-free compression methods (pruning and merging) have limitations: pruning degrades sharply under aggressive compression, and merging does not explicitly prioritize important regions when forming representatives.

Method: AnchorFold is a two-stage training-free framework. (1) Focus stage: applies Recursive Attention Propagation over visual self-attention graphs with multi-step propagation within each attention head and score integration across heads and layers to select highest-centrality tokens as anchors. (2) Fold stage: assigns remaining tokens to their most similar anchors in the normalized retrieval space and summarizes each anchor-centered group through centrality-weighted aggregation, preserving non-anchor contributions while concentrating capacity on structurally important tokens.

Result: Across ViDoRe v1/v2 and REAL-MM-RAG with three diverse retrieval backbones, AnchorFold consistently outperforms all evaluated training-free baselines at compression ratios ≤ 0.20. On ViDoRe v1/v2, it retains 98.3% of full-index NDCG@5 on average at 5x compression (near-lossless) and 92.4% at 20x compression.

Conclusion: AnchorFold achieves near-lossless document-side index compression for multi-vector vision-language retrievers by combining attention-graph centrality-based anchor selection with centrality-weighted aggregation, demonstrating that training-free compression can preserve retrieval quality even at aggressive compression ratios.

Abstract: Multi-vector vision-language retrievers enable fine-grained Visual Document Retrieval (VDR) through late interaction, but storing and scoring hundreds of visual patch embeddings per page incurs substantial overhead. Existing training-free methods rely on pruning or merging: pruning degrades sharply under aggressive compression, whereas merging does not explicitly prioritize important regions when forming representatives. We introduce AnchorFold, a training-free focus-then-fold framework for document-side index compression. AnchorFold applies Recursive Attention Propagation over visual self-attention graphs, performing multi-step propagation within each attention head and integrating scores across heads and layers. The focus stage selects the highest-centrality tokens as anchors. The fold stage assigns remaining tokens to their most similar anchors in the normalized retrieval space and summarizes each anchor-centered group through centrality-weighted aggregation. This preserves non-anchor contributions while concentrating capacity on structurally important tokens. Across ViDoRe v1/v2 and REAL-MM-RAG with three diverse retrieval backbones, AnchorFold consistently outperforms all evaluated training-free baselines at $γ\leq 0.20$. On ViDoRe v1/v2, it retains 98.3% of full-index NDCG@5 on average at $5\times$ compression, achieving near-lossless compression, and 92.4% at $20\times$ compression.

</details>


### [134] [IDATA: Scalable Invertible Diffusion for Unrestricted Adversarial Transfer Attack](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08734)
*Yi Pan,Jun-Jie Huang,Tianrui Liu,Zihan Chen,Lin Liu,Zhao Wentao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: IDATA is a memory-efficient diffusion framework for unrestricted adversarial transfer attacks, featuring an Invertible Diffusion Module (IDM) for constant-memory backpropagation and a Low-Frequency Constraint Module (LFCM) using DWT to restrict perturbations to semantically stable low-frequency subspaces, improving attack success rate, memory efficiency, and visual imperceptibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing diffusion-based adversarial attack methods suffer from memory-intensive multistep backpropagation through the denoising chain and frequency-agnostic perturbation over intermediate latents, limiting their practical applicability for black-box robustness evaluation.

Method: IDATA introduces two key components: (1) an Invertible Diffusion Module (IDM) that reformulates adversarial optimization as an invertible process, enabling constant-memory backpropagation via on-demand reconstruction of intermediate states; and (2) a Low-Frequency Constraint Module (LFCM) that uses Discrete Wavelet Transform (DWT) to decompose latents into low/high-frequency components, restricting perturbations to low-frequency subspaces.

Result: Extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks and diverse model architectures show IDATA consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in attack success rate, memory efficiency, and visual imperceptibility.

Conclusion: IDATA is a promising tool for black-box robustness evaluation of deep visual models, with code publicly available.

Abstract: Unrestricted adversarial transfer attacks are important for evaluating the black-box robustness of deep visual models. Diffusion-based attacks have shown promising transferability and visual imperceptibility by optimizing adversarial perturbations along denoising trajectories in latent space. However, existing methods are limited by two challenges: memory-intensive multistep backpropagation and frequency-agnostic perturbation over intermediate latents. To address these issues, we propose IDATA, a memory-efficient diffusion framework for unrestricted adversarial transfer attack. IDATA consists of two key components: an Invertible Diffusion Module (IDM) and a Low-Frequency Constraint Module (LFCM). Specifically, IDM reformulates adversarial optimization over diffusion trajectories as an invertible process, enabling constant-memory backpropagation through on-demand reconstruction of intermediate states instead of storing the full denoising chain. Moreover, LFCM leverages Discrete Wavelet Transform (DWT) to decompose latent variables into low- and high-frequency components, restricting perturbations to semantically stable low-frequency subspaces, thereby improving transferability while preserving visual imperceptibility. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks and diverse model architectures demonstrate that IDATA consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in attack success rate, memory efficiency, and visual imperceptibility. These results suggest that IDATA is a promising tool for black-box robustness evaluation of deep visual models. Code is available at https://github.com/colourful-pan/IDATA.

</details>


### [135] [Parcel2Progression: An Anatomy-aware Longitudinal Framework for Alzheimer's Disease Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08753)
*Madhumitha Venkatesh,Shanawaj S Madarkar,Konda Reddy Mopuri*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Parcel2Progression (P2P) is a Longitudinal Transformer Framework for Alzheimer's disease progression analysis using high-resolution, variable-length 4D structural MRI scans. It uses an Atlas-guided Parcel Encoder to tokenize 3D scans into anatomically grounded representations and a Longitudinal Transformer to integrate irregular longitudinal visits with patient age, achieving linear computational scaling and superior performance on AD classification and MCI-to-AD conversion prediction across multiple datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Alzheimer's disease progression is a longitudinal process with early pathological cues, yet computational constraints have limited existing neuroimaging models to either compromising spatial information or restricting the number of longitudinal scans. The authors aim to overcome this bottleneck by fully leveraging high-resolution, variable-length 4D sMRI scan sequences.

Method: The framework uses (1) an Atlas-guided Parcel Encoder that tokenizes 3D scans into anatomically grounded representations, and (2) a Longitudinal Transformer that integrates irregular, arbitrary-length longitudinal visits with patient age. This design provides parcel-specific interpretability and computational tractability scaling linearly with scan count (vs quadratic cost of naive 4D ViT).

Result: P2P outperforms prior works and baselines in MCI-to-AD conversion prediction and AD vs. CN classification across ADNI, AIBL, and MIRIAD datasets. Longitudinal scans improve performance over single-scan baselines by up to 5% balanced accuracy for AD classification and 7% for MCI conversion prediction. Interpretability reveals clinically consistent atrophy patterns, and the framework shows reliability in anomaly detection and generalizability to Frontotemporal Dementia.

Conclusion: The proposed P2P framework successfully addresses the computational bottleneck in longitudinal neuroimaging analysis, delivering both performance gains and clinical interpretability while maintaining computational efficiency and cross-disease generalizability.

Abstract: Alzheimer's disease (AD) progression is a longitudinal process with subtle pathological cues in the early stages. Yet, computational constraints have limited most neuroimaging models to either compromise spatial information or limit the number of longitudinal scans. We aim to overcome this bottleneck and fully leverage high-resolution, variable-length T1w structural MRI (4D sMRI) scan sequences. We introduce Parcel2Progression (P2P), a Longitudinal Transformer Framework which tackles this challenge using an Atlas-guided Parcel Encoder that tokenizes 3D scans into a set of richer anatomically grounded representations. A Longitudinal Transformer then integrates irregular, arbitrary-length longitudinal visits with patient age. This synergy delivers two key advantages: (1) parcel-specific interpretability, and (2) computational tractability for long-term analysis, which scales linearly with the number of scans compared to a naive quadratic 4D ViT cost. P2P outperforms prior works and baselines in both MCI (Mild Cognitive Impairment) to AD conversion prediction and AD vs. CN (Cognitively Normal) classification tasks across ADNI, AIBL, and MIRIAD datasets. Leveraging longitudinal scans boosts performance over single-scan baselines by up to 5% and 7% in balanced accuracy for AD classification and MCI conversion prediction tasks, respectively. Interpretability analysis using parcel saliencies and attention rollouts reveals clinically consistent atrophy patterns in AD and MCI subjects. We also demonstrate the frameworks' reliability in anomaly detection using a synthetic dataset, and test the model's generalizability for other neurodegenerative diseases like Frontotemporal Dementia.

</details>


### [136] [UPolarSQ: Polar Representation Learning for Optic Disc and Peripapillary Atrophy Segmentation and Quantification in Fundus Photographs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08771)
*Mengxian He,Yunyun sun,Ziyue Gao,Wengkei Lam,Shunyi Zhang,Wu Yuan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: UPolarSQ is a unified polar-domain framework for segmenting the Optic Disc (OD) and Peripapillary Atrophy (PPA) in myopic fundus images, and for quantifying clinical biomarkers. It maps OD-centered regions into polar coordinates, uses a U-Net-based network with a Radial-Angular-Decoupled Module for segmentation, and deterministically extracts biomarkers from polar masks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
