Motivation: PPA appears as irregular and partially visible crescents adjacent to the OD in Cartesian fundus images, causing fragmented segmentation and post-processing-dependent quantification, which undermines the reliability of structural biomarkers for myopic analysis.

Method: UPolarSQ maps an OD-centered ROI into polar coordinates where OD/PPA boundaries are represented as radial profiles. It uses UPolarSeg, a U-Net enhanced with a Radial-Angular-Decoupled Module and boundary-aware auxiliary supervision, to model anisotropic polar features and radial boundary transitions. Clinical biomarkers are deterministically extracted from predicted polar masks.

Result: Experiments on internal and external cohorts show UPolarSQ improves OD/PPA segmentation and supports reliable polar-native biomarker estimation for myopic analysis.

Conclusion: The polar-domain representation unifies segmentation and quantification in a shared geometric framework, improving reliability of OD/PPA structural biomarkers in myopic fundus imaging.

Abstract: Myopia-induced posterior-pole remodeling is frequently accompanied by Optic Disc (OD) deformation and Peripapillary Atrophy (PPA), both of which provide clinically relevant structural biomarkers. In Cartesian fundus images, however, PPA often appears as an irregular and partially visible crescent adjacent to the OD, leading to fragmented segmentation and post-processing-dependent quantification. We propose UPolarSQ, a unified polar-domain framework for OD/PPA segmentation and biomarker quantification in myopic fundus images. UPolarSQ first maps an OD-centered region of interest into polar coordinates, where OD and PPA boundaries can be represented as radial profiles. It then employs UPolarSeg, a U-Net-based segmentation network enhanced with a Radial-Angular-Decoupled Module and boundary-aware auxiliary supervision to model anisotropic polar features and radial boundary transitions. Clinical biomarkers, including disc shape and PPA-width-related measurements, are deterministically extracted from the predicted polar masks, aligning segmentation and quantification within a shared geometric representation. Experiments on internal and external cohorts demonstrate that UPolarSQ improves OD/PPA segmentation and supports reliable polar-native biomarker estimation for myopic analysis.

</details>


### [137] [LASA: Language-and-Source-Anchored Alignment for Domain Generalized Semantic Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08805)
*Jinhong Zhu,Weiqi Yan,Shengchuan Zhang,Liujuan Cao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes the Language-and-Source-Anchored Alignment (LASA) framework for Domain Generalization Semantic Segmentation (DGSS), addressing limitations of conventional methods (style randomization and feature normalization) that impair feature integrity. LASA comprises three components: TSGST (which uses source features and VLM priors to prevent manifold distortion), DAQA (which recalibrates object queries to restore suppressed details), and DADO (which aligns query distributions for consistent cross-domain responses). The method significantly outperforms state-of-the-art approaches on challenging benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional DGSS methods (style randomization and feature normalization) mitigate domain shifts but impair feature integrity: style randomization distorts the feature manifold due to coarse granularity, while feature normalization suppresses discriminative, domain-sensitive semantic details due to rigid design.

Method: The LASA framework consists of three synergistic components: (1) Text-and-Source-Guided Style Transfer (TSGST) uses source features as structural anchors and VLM priors as fine-grained guidance to address manifold distortion; (2) Domain-Aware Query Adapter (DAQA) recalibrates object queries via categorical guidance and domain-aware signatures to restore suppressed details; (3) Domain-Aware Decoder Optimizer (DADO) aligns resulting query distributions with a shared classifier for consistent categorical responses across domains.

Result: Extensive experiments on challenging benchmarks demonstrate that the proposed LASA method significantly outperforms state-of-the-art approaches in DGSS.

Conclusion: By jointly addressing manifold distortion and suppression of discriminative details through source-anchored alignment and VLM-guided adaptation, the LASA framework provides a robust solution for DGSS that achieves superior generalization performance across unseen domains.

Abstract: Domain Generalization Semantic Segmentation (DGSS) focuses on generalizing knowledge from labeled source domains to unseen target domains where data is unavailable during the training phase. While conventional methods utilize style randomization or feature normalization to mitigate domain shifts, they often impair feature integrity. Specifically, style randomization distorts the underlying feature manifold due to its coarse-grained nature, while feature normalization suppresses discriminative, domain-sensitive semantic details owing to its rigid design. To address these limitations, we propose the Language-and-Source-Anchored Alignment (LASA) framework, which comprises three synergistic components: Text-and-Source-Guided Style Transfer (TSGST), Domain-Aware Query Adapter (DAQA), and Domain-Aware Decoder Optimizer (DADO). Concretely, the TSGST module addresses manifold distortion by utilizing source features as structural anchors and vision-language model (VLM) priors as fine-grained guidance. To restore suppressed discriminative and domain-sensitive details, the DAQA module recalibrates object queries via categorical guidance and domain-aware signatures, while the DADO module aligns the resulting query distributions with a shared classifier to ensure consistent categorical responses across domains. Extensive experiments on challenging benchmarks demonstrate that our method significantly outperforms state-of-the-art approaches.

</details>


### [138] [AdapterMoE: A Two-Stage Hard-Routing Mixture-of-Experts Architecture for Multi-Crop Disease Recognition with Calibrated Rejection and Incremental Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08808)
*Pin-Hsun Huang,Shaou-Gang Miaou*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: AdapterMoE proposes a deterministic hard-routing Mixture-of-Experts architecture for multi-crop disease identification using a RouterHead crop classifier, per-crop adapters on a frozen backbone, and an energy+KNN out-of-distribution module, achieving comparable accuracy to baselines while cutting training cost to ~9% and enabling incremental crop expansion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional soft-routing MoE for multi-crop disease recognition suffers from expert collapse (few experts dominate), lack of semantic correspondence to crops, high retraining costs, unstable rejection of non-target inputs, and a saturated accuracy ceiling. The paper shifts the objective from pure accuracy toward balancing deployment cost, scaling flexibility, and rejection stability.

Method: AdapterMoE uses deterministic hard routing: a RouterHead classifies the crop and rejects non-target crops via Maximum Softmax Probability thresholding; a dual-gate Energy+KNN out-of-distribution module catches distribution-shifted inputs; five per-crop Adapters on a frozen EfficientNet-B0 backbone discriminate diseases, each calibrated with Temperature Scaling. Hard isolation at the data level prevents expert collapse and enables an add_crop interface for local per-crop updates instead of full retraining.

Result: On PlantVillage (5 crops, 26 classes), across a five-system comparison, AdapterMoE achieves accuracy statistically indistinguishable from the best baselines (Macro-F1 within a 0.24-point band) while cutting training cost to about 9% of full-network baselines, with the ability to expand to new crops incrementally.

Conclusion: AdapterMoE demonstrates that shifting from accuracy-centric soft routing to cost-, flexibility-, and stability-oriented hard routing yields a practical MoE design for crop-disease identification with comparable accuracy, dramatically lower training costs, and modular scalability.

Abstract: Timely crop-disease identification is critical to food security. Multi-crop recognition suits Mixture-of-Experts (MoE), but conventional soft-routing MoE learns crop assignment freely end-to-end, letting a few experts dominate (expert collapse) with no semantic correspondence to crops, and facing high retraining costs, unstable rejection of non-target inputs, and a saturated accuracy ceiling. We shift the objective from accuracy toward a trade-off among deployment cost, scaling flexibility, and rejection stability, using deterministic hard routing. We propose AdapterMoE: a RouterHead classifies the crop and rejects non-target crops via a Maximum Softmax Probability threshold, with a dual-gate Energy+KNN out-of-distribution module catching distribution-shifted inputs; five per-crop Adapters atop a frozen EfficientNet-B0 backbone discriminate diseases, each calibrated via Temperature Scaling. Because experts are hard-isolated at the data level, the design avoids expert collapse and exposes an add_crop interface for local, per-crop updates instead of full retraining. On PlantVillage (5 crops, 26 classes), across a fair five-system comparison, AdapterMoE attains accuracy statistically indistinguishable from the best baselines (Macro-F1 within a 0.24-point band) while cutting training cost to about 9% of full-network baselines, expanding to a new crop in

</details>


### [139] [360CityArena: A Realistic Virtual Urban Navigation Benchmark for Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08814)
*Kenta Watanabe,Atsuyuki Miyai,Mizuki Takenawa,Kiyoharu Aizawa,Toshihiko Yamasaki*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: 360CityArena is a benchmark for evaluating embodied agents' urban exploration in a photorealistic environment reconstructed from 602 real 360-degree video segments of Tokyo's Akihabara district, featuring 175 human-crafted tasks across Environment Understanding, Path Reasoning, and Spatial Reasoning. State-of-the-art LMM-based agents significantly underperform humans (17.1% vs 77.3%), revealing substantial gaps in city-scale navigation and reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing outdoor benchmarks for embodied agents lack either sufficient photorealism or complexity, creating a considerable gap from real-world urban environments. There is a need for a benchmark that provides realistic and challenging testbeds for evaluating urban exploration capabilities.

Method: The benchmark is built on a photorealistic reconstruction of Tokyo's Akihabara district using 602 360-degree video segments covering 85 streets. It contains 175 human-crafted tasks organized into three categories: Environment Understanding, Path Reasoning, and Spatial Reasoning, covering abilities like localization, landmark search, path planning, and relational spatial reasoning.

Result: Evaluation of state-of-the-art LMM-based agents shows the strongest model (Gemini 2.5 Flash) achieves only 17.1% accuracy compared to 77.3% for humans, demonstrating a substantial performance gap in city-scale embodied navigation and reasoning.

Conclusion: 360CityArena provides a necessary and challenging testbed for photorealistic urban-district navigation and spatial reasoning, highlighting significant remaining challenges for embodied agents in realistic urban environments.

Abstract: We present 360CityArena, a benchmark for evaluating the urban exploration capabilities of embodied agents within a photorealistic environment constructed from 360-degree videos. Existing outdoor benchmarks either lack sufficient photorealism or complexity, resulting in a considerable gap from real-world urban environments. 360CityArena is built on a realistic reconstruction of the Akihabara district in Tokyo, Japan, using 602 360-degree video segments covering 85 streets, and consists of 175 meticulously human-crafted tasks. It encompasses three task categories: Environment Understanding, Path Reasoning, and Spatial Reasoning, covering fundamental abilities required for urban exploration, such as localization, landmark search, path planning, and relational spatial reasoning, thereby enabling comprehensive evaluation in realistic urban scenes. Our evaluation using state-of-the-art LMM-based agents shows that even the strongest model, Gemini 2.5 Flash, performs far below human level (human: 77.3% vs. Gemini 2.5 Flash: 17.1%), revealing substantial challenges that remain in city-scale embodied navigation and reasoning. 360CityArena provides a necessary and challenging testbed for photorealistic urban-district navigation and spatial reasoning.

</details>


### [140] [MRI super-resolution in ten sampling steps using a diffusion bridge model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08819)
*Mojtaba Safari,Hang Yu,Zach Eidex,Mingzhe Hu,Ryan J. Sanford,Alexandru Florea,Shansong Wang,Chih-Wei Chang,Erik H Middlebrooks,Aditya Juloori,Stanley L. Liauw,Ralph Weichselbaum,Xiaofeng Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper presents SR-DBM, a super-resolution diffusion bridge model for MRI that casts image super-resolution as stochastic transport between low-resolution and high-resolution image distributions, using a Doob's h-transform to pin the process to the paired images at endpoints, enabling reconstruction from measured anatomy rather than Gaussian noise. It recovers HR images with only ten deterministic sampling steps and achieves state-of-the-art performance on 7T brain and prostate MRI datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MRI suffers from long acquisition times causing patient discomfort and motion artifacts, forcing a trade-off between spatial resolution and scan time. Existing diffusion-based super-resolution methods require many sampling steps and initialize from a Gaussian prior that is ill-suited for image restoration tasks.

Method: SR-DBM is a super-resolution diffusion bridge model that formulates SR as stochastic transport between LR and HR image distributions. It applies a Doob's h-transform of a mean-reverting stochastic differential equation to pin the process to paired HR and LR images at endpoints. A network predicts the clean image at each of only ten sampling steps along a deterministic reverse trajectory.

Result: SR-DBM achieved the highest PSNR and SSIM and the lowest GMSD on both 7T brain T1 MP2RAGE maps and pelvic T2-weighted prostate datasets (brain: 27.66±1.52 dB, 0.96±0.02, 7.96±1.86; prostate: 27.87±2.29 dB, 0.80±0.05, 8.38±1.44), with statistically significant gains over all nine comparison methods. The strongest baseline, SR-EMamba, ranked second. Qualitatively, SR-DBM produced the smallest residual errors and best preserved fine structures and lesions.

Conclusion: The diffusion bridge approach that initializes from measured anatomy rather than Gaussian noise is an effective and efficient strategy for MRI super-resolution, achieving superior quantitative and qualitative performance with only ten sampling steps.

Abstract: Objective. MRI provides excellent soft-tissue contrast, but long acquisition times can cause patient discomfort and lead to motion artifacts, forcing a trade-off between spatial resolution and scan time. Diffusion-based super-resolution (SR) reconstructs high-resolution (HR) images from low-resolution (LR) inputs, but typically needs many sampling steps and initializes from a Gaussian prior ill-suited to image restoration. We developed an efficient diffusion framework that reconstructs HR MRI directly from LR data. Approach. We propose super-resolution diffusion bridge model (SR-DBM), a super-resolution diffusion bridge model that casts SR as a stochastic transport between the LR and HR image distributions. Through a Doob's h-transform of a mean-reverting stochastic differential equation, SR-DBM pins the process to the paired HR and LR images at its endpoints, initializing reconstruction from the measured anatomy rather than from Gaussian noise. The HR image is recovered by a deterministic reverse trajectory in which a network predicts the clean image at each of only ten sampling steps. We evaluated SR-DBM on ultra-high-field 7T brain T1 MP2RAGE maps and pelvic T2-weighted prostate images against nine comparison methods using PSNR, SSIM, GMSD, and LPIPS. Main results. SR-DBM attained the highest PSNR and SSIM and the lowest GMSD on both datasets (brain: 27.66+-1.52 dB, 0.96+-0.02, 7.96+-1.86$; prostate: 27.87+-2.29 dB, 0.80+-0.05, 8.38+- 1.44), with statistically significant gains over every comparison method (two-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank test with Holm correction, p<0.05). The strongest baseline, SR-EMamba, ranked second. Qualitatively, SR-DBM produced the smallest residual errors and best preserved fine structures and lesions.

</details>


### [141] [LogiShot: Logically Coherent Cross-Shot Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08820)
*Shuai Guo,Yuhang Yang,Zeyu Zhang,Pengfei Yu,Wei Zhai,Yang Cao,Zheng-Jun Zha*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LogiShot is a cross-shot video generation framework that ensures logical coherence and visual consistency across shots by jointly encoding context video with conditioning signals and maintaining visual memory throughout generation, outperforming existing baselines on a new 110K-sample dataset and benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current cross-shot video generation relies on isolated scripts or reference images, causing generated clips to appear plausible individually but fail to align with the overall narrative when instructions are ambiguous. Logical coherence across shots requires both logical connections and visual consistency, which existing methods lack.

Method: LogiShot uses two complementary paths: (1) joint encoding of context video and conditioning signals to produce dense multimodal cues providing visual-semantic evidence, and (2) a visual memory mechanism that preserves the context video's visual characteristics throughout generation. A 110K-sample dataset and dedicated benchmark are also constructed.

Result: LogiShot consistently outperforms existing baselines in logical coherence across multiple shots on the proposed benchmark.

Conclusion: Establishing logical connections and maintaining visual consistency across shots is key to coherent cross-shot video generation, and LogiShot demonstrates this effectively. Model and data will be released publicly.

Abstract: Generating cross-shot videos that are logically connected is essential for content creation. Currently, most cross-shot video-generation workflows, such as short-drama production, still rely on isolated textual scripts or explicit reference images to specify the generated content. Consequently, when user instructions are underspecified or ambiguous, a generated clip may appear visually plausible on its own but fail to align with the overall narrative, leading to disjointed content. We argue that achieving cross-shot logical coherence in video generation requires establishing logical connections across shots and maintaining visual consistency. To this end, we propose LogiShot, which incorporates information through two complementary paths: 1) LogiShot jointly encodes the context video and other conditioning signals, yielding dense multimodal cues that provide visual-semantic evidence for cross-shot generation; 2) the model maintains a visual memory of the context video throughout generation to preserve visual consistency across shots. Additionally, we construct a dataset with 110K samples and a dedicated benchmark for evaluating cross-shot logical coherence. Experiments demonstrate that LogiShot consistently outperforms existing baselines in terms of logical coherence across multiple shots. Model and data will be made publicly available.

</details>


### [142] [Visual Token Codec: Unleashing Spatial Redundancy for ViT Feature Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08832)
*Donghui Feng,Fengxi Zhang,Changsheng Gao,Wenhan Yang,Qi Wang,Qunshan Gu,Hongwei Hu,Zhengxue Cheng,Li Song*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VTC is a dual-path learned codec for ViT features that separates global and patch tokens, exploiting the spatial structure of patch tokens on the original grid via a spatial-channel context entropy model, achieving 15.7x-37.4x bitrate reduction at 90% of uncompressed-feature performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Distributed deployment of vision foundation models requires compressing intermediate ViT features for efficient transmission, but existing codecs flatten tokens into pseudo-images and fail to exploit the native 2D patch-grid structure, overlooking strong local spatial correlations in patch tokens.

Method: VTC uses a dual-path architecture: global tokens are compressed with a lightweight factorized prior, while patch tokens are encoded on the patch-token grid using a spatial-channel context entropy model. It also incorporates feature-matching supervision after subsequent ViT blocks and variable-rate modules for practical rate adaptation and intermediate-layer compression.

Result: On DINOv2 and SAM3, VTC consistently outperforms representative ViT feature coding baselines on classification, segmentation, and detection. At 90% of uncompressed-feature performance, it reduces bitrate by 15.7x-37.4x across tasks, with intermediate-layer rate-utility analyses provided.

Conclusion: Exploiting the native 2D spatial structure of ViT patch tokens via a dual-path codec is highly effective for feature compression, delivering substantial bitrate savings while preserving downstream task performance.

Abstract: Distributed deployment of large vision foundation models often partitions a ViT backbone and exchanges intermediate token features between computing nodes, making efficient feature compression critical under bandwidth and computation constraints. Existing ViT feature codecs typically flatten heterogeneous global and patch tokens into an L x C pseudo image, causing entropy models to mainly capture sequence-axis dependencies while overlooking the native two-dimensional patch-grid structure. In this paper, we show that ViT patch tokens retain strong local spatial correlations on the original grid. To exploit this structural prior, we propose the Visual Token Codec (VTC), a dual-path learned codec that separates global and patch tokens into dedicated coding paths. Global tokens are compressed with a lightweight factorized prior, whereas patch tokens are encoded on the patch-token grid using a spatial-channel context entropy model. To support intermediate-layer compression and practical rate adaptation, VTC further incorporates feature-matching supervision after subsequent ViT blocks and variable-rate modules within a single codec. Experiments on DINOv2 and SAM3 show that VTC consistently outperforms representative ViT feature coding baselines on classification, segmentation, and detection tasks. At 90% of uncompressed-feature performance, VTC reduces bitrate by 15.7x-37.4x across these tasks. We further provide intermediate-layer rate-utility analyses for practical transmission- and storage-oriented deployment scenarios.

</details>


### [143] [SLAP: Selective Local Vision-Language Alignment for Fish Re-Identification via Partial Optimal Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08840)
*Cigdem Beyan,Tonje Knutsen Sordalen,Kim Tallaksen Halvorsen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a selective local vision-language alignment framework for individual fish re-identification (ReID) using Partial Optimal Transport (POT) to establish localized correspondences between visual patch embeddings and identity-aware prompt embeddings, outperforming recent CLIP-based ReID methods on longitudinal fish datasets under closed-set and open-set protocols.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing CLIP-based ReID methods rely on global image-text alignment, allowing background and weakly discriminative regions to dominate cross-modal supervision. Since identity-discriminative cues in fish are localized to specific body regions, global alignment is suboptimal for producing discriminative representations.

Method: The framework uses Partial Optimal Transport (POT) to selectively match visual patch embeddings with multiple identity-aware prompt embeddings, enabling the model to emphasize strongest cross-modal correspondences while avoiding forced alignment of weakly matching regions. Training is end-to-end, but only the adapted visual encoder is retained for inference.

Result: Experiments on the longitudinal Symphodus melops dataset show consistent improvements over recent CLIP-based ReID methods under both closed-set and open-set evaluation protocols. Additional experiments on other datasets demonstrate generalization across diverse marine ReID benchmarks.

Conclusion: Selective local alignment via POT yields more discriminative visual representations for fish ReID compared to global CLIP alignment, and the approach generalizes well across multiple marine datasets.

Abstract: Individual fish re-identification (ReID) is a fine-grained recognition problem in which identity-discriminative cues are often localized to specific body regions rather than distributed uniformly across the animal. Nevertheless, recent CLIP-based ReID methods rely predominantly on global image-text alignment, allowing background and weakly discriminative regions to contribute to cross-modal supervision. We propose a selective local vision-language alignment framework that establishes localized correspondences between visual patch embeddings and multiple identity-aware prompt embeddings through Partial Optimal Transport (POT). Rather than enforcing exhaustive correspondence, POT enables selective matching between visual patches and prompt embeddings, allowing the model to emphasize the strongest cross-modal correspondences while avoiding forced alignment of weakly matching regions, thereby yielding more discriminative visual representations for retrieval. The framework is trained end-to-end, while only the adapted visual encoder is retained during inference. Experiments on the longitudinal Symphodus melops dataset demonstrate consistent improvements over recent CLIP-based ReID methods under both closed-set and open-set evaluation protocols. Additional evaluations on other datasets further demonstrate the generalization capability of the proposed method across diverse marine ReID benchmarks.

</details>


### [144] [Toward Mask Annotation-Free Surgical Instrument Segmentation from Endoscopic Images Using Text-Prompted Segment Anything Model 3 (SAM3)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08844)
*Nakul Poudel,Richard Simon,Cristian A. Linte*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A two-stage framework for annotation-free surgical instrument segmentation using SAM3's zero-shot text-prompting capability (with a generic "tool" prompt) followed by a fine-tuned vision-language model (Qwen) for instance-level classification, evaluated on EndoVis 2017/2018 datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing surgical instrument segmentation methods rely on pixel-level annotations or manual spatial prompts, limiting scalability and automation. While SAM3 offers annotation-free text-based segmentation, direct use of instrument names as prompts fails due to a large domain gap.

Method: A two-stage framework: (1) SAM3's zero-shot capability with a natural-language-aligned generic prompt "tool" produces binary masks; (2) a fine-tuned vision-language model (Qwen) on SAM3-generated masked regions performs instrument classification to extend masks to instance level.

Result: On EndoVis 2017 and 2018, the two-stage approach does not match fully supervised methods but significantly outperforms direct SAM3 text-prompt-based instance-level segmentation.

Conclusion: The findings highlight both limitations and potential of SAM3, suggesting a promising direction toward fully annotation-free surgical instrument segmentation.

Abstract: Surgical instrument segmentation is a fundamental task for computer-assisted interventions, yet most existing methods rely on pixel-level annotations or manual spatial prompts, which limit scalability and automation. The recently introduced Segment Anything Model 3 (SAM3) offers a pathway to annotation-free, automatic segmentation via text-based prompting; however, the instrument name as a text prompt could not be directly used due to a large domain gap. To overcome these limitations, we propose a two-stage framework that achieves instance-level segmentation without requiring ground truth masks or manual interaction. In the first stage, we leverage a natural-language-aligned generic prompt - "tool" - to produce binary masks using SAM3's zero-shot capability. In the second stage, these masks are extended to instance-level by integrating a vision-language model (Qwen) that is fine-tuned on SAM3-generated masked regions for instrument classification. We evaluate our approach on the EndoVis 2017 and 2018 datasets. Results show that, while our two-stage approach does not reach the performance of current fully supervised methods, it significantly outperforms the direct use of SAM3 for instance-level instrument segmentation with text prompts. Overall, our findings highlight both the limitations and potential of SAM3, suggesting a promising direction toward annotation-free surgical instrument segmentation.

</details>


### [145] [Zero-Shot Traffic Accident Detection via a Coarse-to-Fine VLM-Tracking Pipeline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08867)
*Dipit Saha,Shah Mohammad Abdul Mannan,Mohammad Raihan Rashid,Ruwad Naswan,Ahnaf Tahmid*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A training-free, two-pass coarse-to-fine pipeline using a frozen Qwen3-VL vision-language model with YOLO11x detection and BoT-SORT tracking achieves a 0.504 harmonic mean score on the ACCIDENT@CVPR benchmark, surpassing the best organizer baseline by 22% relative margin without any labeled real-world training data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automatically converting raw CCTV footage into structured accident event records (when, where, and what type of collision) at scale remains unsolved, particularly under the strict constraint of having no labeled real-world training data available.

Method: A training-free, two-pass coarse-to-fine pipeline: (1) a first pass sparsely samples the full video clip using a frozen Qwen3-VL-32B-Instruct vision-language model to anchor the collision moment temporally; (2) a second pass re-examines a tight window around that estimate using frames annotated with stable vehicle identities (from BoT-SORT tracking) and normalized bounding-box coordinates (from YOLO11x detection), providing the model with both a visual overlay and explicit numeric scene descriptions.

Result: On the official 2,027-clip real-CCTV test set, the system achieves a three-way harmonic mean score of 0.504, surpassing all organizer-published baselines, including the best multi-model ensemble (0.412), by a 22% relative margin.

Conclusion: A training-free pipeline combining frozen vision-language models with object detection and tracking can outperform supervised baselines on joint accident event prediction, demonstrating that multimodal reasoning with explicit spatial-temporal annotations is highly effective for this benchmark.

Abstract: Traffic surveillance cameras capture accidents continuously, yet converting raw CCTV footage into structured event records that pinpoint when, where, and what type of collision occurred remains unsolved at scale. The ACCIDENT @ CVPR benchmark evaluates exactly this joint prediction under a strict constraint: no labeled real-world training data is available. We introduce a training-free, two-pass coarse-to-fine pipeline that pairs a frozen Qwen3-VL-32B-Instruct vision-language model with YOLO11x object detection and BoT-SORT tracking. A first pass sparsely samples the full clip to anchor the collision moment in time; a second pass re-examines a tight window around that estimate using frames annotated with stable vehicle identities and normalized bounding-box coordinates, which gives the model both a visual overlay and an explicit numeric description of the same scene. On the official 2,027-clip real-CCTV test set, our system achieves a three-way harmonic mean score of 0.504, surpassing all organizer-published baselines including the best multi-model ensemble (0.412) by a 22% relative margin.

</details>


### [146] [Sparse Attention to Emotion: Efficient Facial Emotion Recognition via Token Reduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08873)
*Aya Manel Zitouni,Aicha Zenakhri,Karim Haroun,Larbi Boubchir*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SAE is a lightweight Facial Emotion Recognition model that discards up to 90% of image tokens deemed non-informative for emotion detection, achieving state-of-the-art accuracy on RAF-DB while significantly reducing computational cost compared to Vision Transformer-based approaches with quadratic complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Vision Transformer-based FER approaches suffer from quadratic computational complexity O(N^2), making them impractical for edge deployment. The authors hypothesize that not all facial information is needed for emotion recognition—only discriminative regions like eyes, mouth, and parts of cheeks are essential.

Method: The proposed Sparse Attention to Emotion (SAE) model employs a sparse attention mechanism that discards image tokens with no added value to the emotional context, retaining only informative tokens while maintaining accuracy and reducing computational cost.

Result: SAE achieves competitive accuracy to state-of-the-art methods even after suppressing 90% of image tokens, and achieves new state-of-the-art results on the RAF-DB dataset while reducing computational complexity by up to 90%.

Conclusion: The authors demonstrate that FER can be performed effectively with sparse facial information, providing a lightweight and efficient approach suitable for edge deployment without sacrificing accuracy.

Abstract: Facial Emotion Recognition (FER) is an important task that has significant implications across various fields such as biometrics, health, and human-computer interaction. Current Vision Transformer-based approaches display quadratic complexity $\mathcal{O}(N^2)$, with N being the input sequence length, making them cumbersome to deploy at the edge. In this paper, we hypothesize that the FER task does not necessarily require all facial information to correctly interpret emotional states, as specific regions such as the eyes, the mouth, and parts of the cheeks carry discriminative information that can be sufficient to recognize emotions. Based on this, we propose Sparse Attention to Emotion (SAE), a model that discards image tokens that have no added value to the emotional context, while preserving good accuracy and achieving a significant gain in computational cost. Surprisingly, even after suppressing 90\% of the image tokens, our model achieves competitive accuracy to state of the art methods at much lower cost, providing a lightweight Facial Emotion Recognition approach. Experimental results demonstrate that SAE achieves new state of the art results on the RAF-DB dataset while reducing the computational complexity by up to 90\%.

</details>


### [147] [AeroReformer2: Spoken-Query Referring Segmentation for Aerial Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08874)
*Rui Li,Chenxi Duan,Haoyang Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces a spoken-query benchmark for referring remote-sensing image segmentation, derived from RISBench with accent- and voice-diverse speech and hard evaluation sets with rotor/wind/mixed interference. It also proposes an efficient bilateral network combining a boundary-preserving visual path with token-preserving speech encoding, kernel linear cross-modal attention, and a resolution refinement head, achieving 62.09% mIoU on the clean test split and outperforming audio-adapted baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing referring remote-sensing image segmentation benchmarks only accept written expressions, despite spoken language being a natural, hands-free interface for specifying targets in dense remote-sensing imagery. This gap motivates creating a spoken-query benchmark and model.

Method: The paper proposes an efficient bilateral network combining: (1) a boundary-preserving visual path, (2) token-preserving speech encoding, (3) kernel linear cross-modal attention that conditions visual features at two scales without materializing a dense speech-visual affinity matrix, and (4) a resolution refinement head that restores fine boundaries using high-resolution visual features.

Result: On the clean test split, the model with Swin-Base achieves 62.09% mIoU and 68.22% oIoU, outperforming the strongest audio-adapted remote-sensing baseline by 5.38 and 2.08 percentage points respectively. It retains the best hard-set mIoU at 54.09%.

Conclusion: This is the first benchmark and model study of full-sentence spoken-query referring segmentation for remote-sensing imagery. The code will be made publicly available.

Abstract: Spoken language offers a natural, hands-free interface for specifying an arbitrary target in dense remote-sensing imagery, yet existing referring remote-sensing image segmentation benchmarks accept only written expressions. To bridge this gap, we introduce \dataset, a spoken-query benchmark derived from RISBench that adds accent- and voice-diverse speech while preserving the original image, mask, and data splits. Its hard evaluation sets combine rotor, wind, and mixed interference with three signal-to-noise levels. We also propose \model, an efficient bilateral network that combines a boundary-preserving visual path with token-preserving speech encoding, kernel linear cross-modal attention, and a resolution refinement head. The design conditions visual features at two scales without materializing a dense speech--visual affinity matrix, then restores fine boundaries using high-resolution visual features. On the clean test split, \model with Swin-Base achieves 62.09\% mean intersection over union (mIoU) and 68.22\% overall intersection over union (oIoU), outperforming the strongest audio-adapted remote-sensing baseline by 5.38 and 2.08 percentage points, respectively. It retains the best hard-set mIoU at 54.09\%. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first benchmark and model study of full-sentence spoken-query referring segmentation for remote-sensing imagery. The code will be made publicly available.

</details>


### [148] [City Sentinel: A Unified AI-Based Smart Surveillance Framework for Real-Time Multi-Threat Detection Using Deep Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08887)
*Hanan Syed Shabir,Noor Fatima,Safia Baloch,Masroor Hussain*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: City Sentinel is a unified AI-based surveillance framework that integrates six detection capabilities—facial recognition, ANPR, fire/smoke detection, weapon detection, violence detection, and road accident detection—into a single scalable platform, achieving 743 ms median latency, four concurrent RTSP streams, and strong detection accuracy on consumer-grade hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rapid urbanization has increased the demand for multi-risk public safety surveillance, but traditional systems rely on fragmented, separate solutions (facial recognition, vehicle ID, fire detection, behavioral analysis), leading to inefficient infrastructure and multiple operator interfaces.

Method: The system integrates a Next.js operator dashboard, FastAPI backend, cloud-based PostgreSQL storage, InsightFace and YOLOv8 vision models, and EasyOCR for plate recognition, with camera streams processed through dedicated inference workers using RTSP on a workstation with an NVIDIA RTX 3060 GPU.

Result: Achieves 743 ms median end-to-end latency, supports four concurrent RTSP streams within a two-second limit, 91.2% face-match rate, 85.7% plate-reading accuracy, and mAP@0.5 scores of 0.846–0.889 across fire, knife, and weapon modules. Operators can enroll identities in under a minute and identify flagged persons in ~12 seconds on average.

Conclusion: A modular, open-source, multi-model architecture can provide broad surveillance coverage, cloud-based auditability, and extensibility for new detection capabilities while maintaining practical real-time performance on consumer-grade hardware.

Abstract: Rapid urbanization has increased the need for surveillance systems that can monitor multiple public safety risks at the same time. Traditional systems often use separate solutions for facial recognition, vehicle identification, fire detection, and behavioral analysis, resulting in fragmented infrastructure and multiple interfaces for operators to manage. This paper presents City Sentinel, a unified AI-based surveillance framework that integrates six detection capabilities into one scalable platform: facial recognition, automatic number plate recognition (ANPR), fire and smoke detection, weapon and knife detection, violence detection, and road accident detection. The system combines a Next.js operator dashboard, FastAPI backend, cloud-based PostgreSQL event storage, InsightFace and YOLOv8 vision models, and EasyOCR for plate recognition. Camera streams are processed through dedicated inference workers using RTSP. On a workstation equipped with an NVIDIA RTX 3060 GPU, the system achieves a median end-to-end latency of 743 ms and supports four concurrent RTSP streams within a two-second latency limit. It achieves a 91.2% face-match rate, 85.7% plate-reading accuracy, and mAP@0.5 scores of 0.846 to 0.889 across the fire, knife, and weapon detection modules. In user-acceptance testing, operators could enroll a new identity in under one minute and identify a flagged person from live footage in an average of 12 seconds. The results demonstrate that a modular, open-source, multi-model architecture can provide broad surveillance coverage, cloud-based auditability, and flexibility for adding new detection capabilities while maintaining practical real-time performance.

</details>


### [149] [From Recovery to Drop-off: How Action Post-training Reduces a VLM's Late-Layer Depth Decodability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08904)
*Alexander Hackett,Arnaud Denis-Remillard,Axel Cassou*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper shows that vision-language-action (VLA) models lose depth perception compared to their base vision-language model (VLM), identifying two degradation patterns: a persistent "floor" across all layers and a "cliff" in late layers. The cliff is causally localized to late-layer MLP interference during action post-training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how much spatial understanding (specifically depth perception) is preserved or lost when a base VLM undergoes action post-training to become a VLA, and to identify where and why this degradation occurs.

Method: Probe depth perception from every decoder layer of a weight-matched base VLM/VLA pair (Molmo2-ER and MolmoAct2-LIBERO). Perform causal ablations (MLP writes vs. attention) in late layers to localize the source of degradation, and use module-level decomposition to analyze where depth information is stored.

Result: The VLA decodes depth worse at every layer (the "floor"), with an additional severe collapse in late layers (the "cliff"). Ablating late-layer MLP writes recovers most of the terminal decodability cliff, while attention ablations and the same intervention in the base VLM show no comparable recovery. The base VLM carries depth in accumulated MLP writes, whereas the VLA's late accumulated writes lose this information.

Conclusion: Action post-training degrades spatial understanding in VLMs in two distinct patterns, and the late-layer cliff is specifically caused by MLP interference—pointing to a mechanistic target for preserving spatial abilities during VLA training.

Abstract: How much of a vision-language model's (VLM) spatial understanding remains after the action post-training process of building a vision-language-action model (VLA)? We probe depth perception, a primitive of spatiogeometric understanding, from every decoder layer of a weight-matched open-source base VLM/VLA pair: Molmo2-ER and MolmoAct2-LIBERO. First, the VLA decodes depth worse at every layer, a persistent gap we call the floor. Second, the degradation is not uniform: while the base VLM's depth decodability improves through its final layers, the VLA's collapses, an additional late-layer drop we call the cliff. We causally localize the cliff to late-layer MLP interference: ablating the late-layer MLP writes recovers the majority of the terminal decodability cliff, while matched attention ablations and the same intervention in the weight-matched base VLM produce no comparable recovery. A module-level decomposition explains this dissociation: the base VLM carries depth most accessibly in accumulated MLP writes, whereas action post-training collapses depth decodability in the late accumulated writes.

</details>


### [150] [ToolVision: Learning When and How to Use Visual Tools with Capability-Aligned Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08907)
*Delin Mao,Chenghao Sun,Jingwei Song,Chishui Chen,Linfeng Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ToolVision is a novel training pipeline for multimodal LLMs that addresses supervision misalignments in SFT and RL stages when teaching visual tool use. During SFT, a multi-agent pipeline with student-scale model committees ranks trajectories by stepwise evidence gain. Before RL, it compares learner performance with/without tools to selectively reward tool use only where beneficial. ToolVision-8B outperforms larger models on high-resolution benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current SFT-then-RL recipes for teaching visual tool use create supervision misalignments: SFT makes students imitate tool-call patterns from stronger teachers without learning usefulness (since teachers may succeed via perception the student lacks), while RL with outcome-only rewards either suppresses tool use due to fallible execution or encourages valid-but-ineffective tool calls with blanket bonuses.

Method: ToolVision addresses the two misalignments with two components: (1) During SFT, a multi-agent pipeline explores candidate trajectories, and a committee of student-scale models scores stepwise evidence gain to rank and prune branches—only successfully executed trajectories with correct final answers are kept. (2) Before RL, ToolVision compares the learner's performance with and without tools, then assigns rewards for successful tool use only on questions where tools provide a clear benefit. Both signals are automatically constructed from public task data without additional human annotations.

Result: ToolVision-8B improves over its base on all seven main benchmarks, surpasses Thyme-7B, CodeVision-8B, and CodeDance-7B on all three high-resolution benchmarks, and outperforms Qwen3-VL-32B-Thinking on V* and HRBench 8K.

Conclusion: ToolVision demonstrates that aligning SFT and RL supervision to the learner's actual capabilities—rather than copying stronger teachers or applying uniform rewards—significantly improves visual tool use in multimodal models. The authors will publicly release datasets and source code.

Abstract: Thinking with images allows a multimodal model to compensate for limited perception by invoking visual tools through code. Yet the prevailing SFT-then-RL recipe creates a different supervision misalignment at each stage. SFT is expected to teach how to use tools, but trajectories from stronger teachers may succeed through perceptual capabilities that a smaller student cannot reliably reproduce or exploit, causing the student to imitate tool-call patterns without learning how to make them useful. RL is expected to teach when to use tools, but outcome-only rewards make fallible tool execution a liability and suppress tool use, whereas a blanket bonus for every correct tool-using trajectory encourages valid but ineffective operations. To address these two misalignments, we introduce ToolVision. During SFT, a multi-agent pipeline explores candidate trajectories, and a committee including student-scale models scores stepwise evidence gain to rank and prune the search branches. Only successfully executed trajectories with correct final answers are retained for SFT. Before RL, ToolVision compares the learner's performance with and without tools, then rewards successful tool use only on questions where tools provide a clear benefit. Both signals are constructed automatically from public task data without additional human annotations of tool use or necessity. ToolVision-8B improves over its base on all seven main benchmarks, surpasses Thyme-7B, CodeVision-8B, and CodeDance-7B on all three high-resolution benchmarks, and outperforms Qwen3-VL-32B-Thinking on V* and HRBench 8K. We will publicly release the datasets and source code.

</details>


### [151] [From Noise to Meaning: Meaningful Secret Sharing with Tamper Detection for Facial Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08924)
*Ajnas Muhammed,Iurii Medvedev,Nuno Gonçalves*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes a method to enhance visual secret sharing for face recognition systems by embedding secret shares into visually appealing cover images using adaptive LSB steganography, combined with dual-layer authentication (digital watermarking and cryptographic hashing) to ensure both privacy and integrity of facial biometric data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional visual secret sharing splits facial images into noise-like secret shares that are conspicuous, vulnerable to harvest-now-decrypt-later attacks, and lack tamper detection, making them unsuitable for protecting sensitive biometric data.

Method: The technique combines visual secret sharing with adaptive least significant bit (LSB) steganography to embed secret shares within visually appealing cover images, and employs a two-layer authentication mechanism using strong digital watermarking and cryptographic hashing to protect share integrity.

Result: Experiments on multiple public face datasets demonstrate improved face recognition accuracy, elimination of share conspicuousness, high resilience against bit-flipping, cropping, and substitution attacks, while guaranteeing share integrity.

Conclusion: The proposed framework establishes a new standard for facial data protection by simultaneously ensuring privacy, security, and integrity of biometric data used in AI-based face recognition systems.

Abstract: Popularity of AI-based face recognition system directly demands protection of sensitive biometric data used for training. Visual secret sharing is an interesting idea, as it splits facial images into secret shares that look random and spread across many institutions. However, these shares look like noise and can easily spark suspicion and recognized as encrypted content. This makes them open to targeted collection and harvest-now-decrypt-later attacks. Additionally, visual secret sharing does not detect tampering, allowing attackers to modify shares and threaten the integrity of reconstruction. In this paper, we introduce a new method that turns distracting noise-like secret shares into visually appealing cover images with additional cryptographic tamper detection. The proposed technique works with visual secret sharing and introduces cover images to embed the shares using adaptive least significant bit steganography. Here, cover images with perceptual transparency are used to store secret shares while guaranteeing complete privacy. A two layer authentication using strong digital watermarking and cryptographic hashing is used to protect the integrity of shares. The proposed technique shows high resilience in stopping bit-flipping, cropping, and substitution attacks. Extensive experiments on multiple public face datasets show that the technique shows better FR accuracy, while eliminating share conspicuousness and guaranteeing integrity. The proposed framework sets a new standard for protecting facial data in such a way that privacy, security, and integrity are protected.

</details>


### [152] [Zero-shot 2D Grounding with Novel Affordance Types](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08929)
*Haomeng Zhang,Raymond A. Yeh*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces zero-shot 2D affordance grounding with novel affordance types (NAT), proposes the AGD20K-NAT benchmark, and presents AffordAnything (training-free) and AffordAnything+ (trainable) methods that leverage segmentation cues, achieving a 12.3% absolute IoU@0.4 improvement over the prior SOTA (OOAL).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing affordance grounding methods only handle affordance types seen during training and fail to generalize to novel affordances, which is essential for real-world applications. The authors identify this gap and motivate the need for zero-shot affordance grounding with novel affordance types.

Method: The authors propose two methods: (1) AffordAnything, a training-free approach that leverages the strong correlation between affordance regions and object subparts using segmentation cues; and (2) AffordAnything+, a trainable variant that learns to combine these segmentation cues for improved performance. They also introduce the AGD20K-NAT benchmark to evaluate novel affordance type zero-shot grounding.

Result: On the AGD20K-NAT benchmark, AffordAnything+ achieves a substantial 12.3% absolute improvement in IoU@0.4 over the prior state-of-the-art affordance grounding method, OOAL.

Conclusion: The proposed methods demonstrate that leveraging segmentation cues is effective for zero-shot affordance grounding with novel affordance types, significantly outperforming existing affordance grounding approaches and providing a new benchmark for this task.

Abstract: 2D affordance grounding aims to locate the region of an object that a human can interact with. Existing research focuses on recognizing affordance types seen during training and does not study models' ability to generalize to novel affordances, which is crucial for real-world applications. We propose the task of zero-shot 2D grounding with novel affordance types (NAT) and introduce the NAT benchmarks. We then propose AffordAnything, a training-free method that leverages segmentation cues, motivated by the strong correlation between affordance regions and object subparts. To further improve performance, we develop AffordAnything+, a trainable variant that learns to combine these cues. On the proposed AGD20K-NAT benchmark, our best model AffordAnything+ achieves a substantial improvement of 12.3% (absolute) in IoU@0.4 over the SOTA affordance grounding method, OOAL.

</details>


### [153] [Can Webcam Gaze Constrain Mesa-Objectives in Driving Models? An Instrument Precision Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08947)
*Lennox Anderson,Ahmed Boutar,Jonah Mulcrone,Tal Erez*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper tests whether webcam-based eye tracking (WebGazer.js) can serve as privileged information to constrain mesa-objective formation in autonomous driving hazard detection. Despite collecting 137,663 frame-level gaze samples across 388 dashcam clips, no statistically significant improvement was found across calibration protocols, model architectures, or random seeds. The root cause is geometric: WebGazer's reported error (~130-257 px) exceeds 93% of hazard object sizes (median 36 px), making object-level gaze attribution physically impossible.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hazard detection systems in autonomous driving may develop mesa objectives—internal goals that exploit spurious correlations for high training performance rather than genuine hazard recognition. The authors investigate whether human gaze can serve as privileged information to constrain such undesirable learned behaviors.

Method: Collected 137,663 frame-level gaze samples synchronized with hazard annotations across 388 real dashcam clips. Tested gaze as privileged information across two calibration protocols (9-point/45-click and 11-point/440-click), two model architectures (Random Forest and causal Transformer), and five random seeds per experiment using paired t-tests. Conducted a geometric analysis of gaze precision versus hazard object sizes.

Result: No experiment yielded statistically significant improvement from gaze (p = 0.919, 0.578, and 0.667 respectively). Geometric analysis revealed WebGazer's reported error (~130-257 px) exceeds 93% of detected hazard object sizes (median 36 px), making object-level gaze attribution physically impossible at this instrument precision.

Conclusion: Webcam-based eye tracking at the precision level of WebGazer.js is insufficient to provide useful gaze-based privileged information for constraining mesa-objective formation in hazard detection. The physical precision limits of the instrument fundamentally preclude object-level gaze attribution, suggesting that higher-fidelity eye tracking or alternative privileged signals are needed.

Abstract: Current hazard detection systems in autonomous driving may develop mesa objectives, learned internal goals that achieve high training performance through spurious correlations rather than genuine hazard recognition. We investigate whether human gaze patterns, captured via webcam-based eye tracking (WebGazer.js), can serve as privileged information to constrain mesa-objective formation. We collected 137,663 frame-level gaze samples synchronized with hazard annotations across 388 real dashcam clips, then test this hypothesis across two calibration protocols (9-point/45-click and 11-point/440-click), two model architectures (Random Forest and causal Transformer), and five random seeds per experiment with paired t-tests. No experiment yields a statistically significant improvement from gaze (p = 0.919, 0.578, and 0.667 respectively). A geometric analysis reveals the root cause: WebGazer's reported error (~130-257 px depending on configuration) exceeds 93% of detected hazard object sizes (median 36 px), rendering object-level gaze attribution physically impossible at this instrument precision.

</details>


### [154] [EndoMD-SLAM: Endoscopic Gaussian Splatting SLAM under Optical Degradation with Memory and Static-Transient Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08949)
*Nuo Chen,Kangqi Ni,Lulin Liu,Joga Ivatury,Ying Ding,Farshid Alambeigi,Tianlong Chen,Zhiwen Fan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: EndoMD-SLAM is a dense 3D reconstruction framework for endoscopy that maintains stability under intermittent optical degradations (e.g., moving debris, water flushing) by using a memory-driven gating mechanism for tracking and a self-supervised static-transient decomposition for mapping, reducing trajectory error by 91% and improving PSNR by 9.9 dB.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gaussian Splatting SLAM systems rely on strict multi-view photometric consistency, which is severely violated in routine endoscopic procedures by intermittent optical degradations like moving debris and water flushing. Standard systems mistakenly fuse these camera-attached artifacts into persistent 3D geometry, leading to tracking drift and irreversible map corruption.

Method: EndoMD-SLAM employs two specialized mechanisms: (1) a memory-driven gating mechanism on the tracking side that detects unreliable observations to suspend map updates and uses historical keyframes for drift-aware relocalization; (2) a self-supervised static-transient decomposition on the mapping side that isolates visual contaminants into a dedicated transient field, preventing artifacts from entangling with the persistent anatomical map. A degradation-focused benchmark from colonoscopy videos is curated for evaluation.

Result: Extensive experiments on a degradation-focused colonoscopy benchmark show that while standard baselines fail under severe optical degradation, EndoMD-SLAM preserves geometric integrity, reducing absolute trajectory error by 91% and improving rendering fidelity by 9.9 dB PSNR.

Conclusion: Explicitly separating transient visual contaminants from persistent anatomical structures, combined with unreliable observation gating and historical keyframe relocalization, enables robust dense 3D reconstruction for clinical endoscopy despite severe optical degradation.

Abstract: Dense 3D reconstruction is critical for clinical endoscopic navigation and documentation. While Gaussian Splatting SLAM systems show promise in this domain, they fundamentally rely on strict multi-view photometric consistency. In routine procedures, this assumption is severely violated by intermittent optical degradations like moving debris and water flushing. Standard systems erroneously fuse these cameraattached artifacts into the persistent 3D geometry, causing severe tracking drift and irreversible map corruption. To address this limitation, we propose EndoMD-SLAM, a framework designed to maintain stability under optical degradation through specialized tracking and mapping mechanisms. On the tracking side, a memory-driven gating mechanism detects unreliable observations to suspend map updates and utilizes historical keyframes for drift-aware relocalization. On the mapping side, a self-supervised static-transient decomposition isolates visual contaminants into a dedicated transient field. This explicit separation prevents artifacts from structurally entangling with the persistent anatomical map. We curate a degradationfocused benchmark from colonoscopy videos to systematically evaluate these failure modes. Extensive experiments show that while standard baselines fail under severe optical degradation, EndoMD-SLAM preserves geometric integrity, reducing absolute trajectory error by 91% and improving rendering fidelity by 9.9 dB PSNR.

</details>


### [155] [Topology-Aware Global-Local Mamba Networks for Palm Vein Biometrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08951)
*Zhengxi Wu,Felix Marattukalam,Waleed H. Abdulla*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes a topology-aware global-local backbone for palm-vein recognition that combines multi-scale local features, a structure-guided directional stream using a fixed Sobel-magnitude edge prior, and a four-direction state-space scan global pathway within six Topology-Aware Blocks, integrated via staged gated fusion. It achieves 99.13% top-1 accuracy with 0.08% EER on HKPUNIR and 92.42% accuracy with 0.61% EER on VERA Palm Vein, with the lowest EER among compared methods at the smallest parameter count.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Palm-vein recognition is a fine-grained biometric task where both local vascular texture and global vessel tree layout carry discriminative information, yet public datasets are limited, motivating the need for an efficient and effective architecture that captures both perspectives.

Method: The method uses a topology-aware global-local backbone with three components: (1) multi-scale local features, (2) a structure-guided directional stream based on a fixed Sobel-magnitude edge prior, and (3) a four-direction state-space scan global pathway, organized within six Topology-Aware Blocks. A staged gated fusion integrates local, structural, and global representations sequentially.

Result: On HKPUNIR, the method achieves 99.13% top-1 accuracy and 0.08% EER with 7.2M parameters. On VERA Palm Vein, it achieves 92.42% accuracy and 0.61% EER. It attains the lowest EER among ResNet50, Vim-S, ViT-S, and GLVM at the smallest parameter count, though GLVM remains strongest in top-1 accuracy and cheapest in FLOPs.

Conclusion: The proposed topology-aware global-local backbone effectively balances accuracy and efficiency for palm-vein recognition, achieving state-of-the-art EER performance with minimal parameters, though GLVM still holds advantages in top-1 accuracy and computational cost.

Abstract: Palm-vein recognition is a fine-grained biometric task in which both local vascular texture and the global layout of the vessel tree carry discriminative information, while public datasets remain limited. We propose a topology-aware global-local backbone that combines multi-scale local features, a structureguided directional stream built on a fixed Sobel-magnitude edge prior, and a four-direction state-space scan global pathway within six Topology-Aware Blocks. A staged gated fusion integrates local, structural, and global representations in that order. On HKPUNIR, our method achieves 99.13% top-1 accuracy and 0.08% EER with 7.2 M parameters; on VERA Palm Vein, it achieves 92.42% accuracy and 0.61% EER. Across both datasets it attains the lowest EER among ResNet50, Vim-S, ViT-S, and GLVM at the smallest parameter count, while GLVM remains the strongest in top-1 accuracy and the cheapest in FLOPs. Code is available upon request.

</details>


### [156] [Damage Classification for 3D Point Cloud Data via 3D Data Analysis and Vision Foundation Model-based 2D Projections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08955)
*Evan Perez,Kalelo Dukuray,Erika Ardiles-Cruz,Jie Wei*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper compares two approaches for fine-grained damage classification of 3D point cloud data: a 3D-based method (3PDA) using topological data analysis (TDA) with anomaly detection, and a 2D projection-based method (2PDA) leveraging large vision foundation models (VFMs). The 3PDA achieves higher accuracy but only on narrow object geometries with high computational cost, while 2PDA offers slightly lower accuracy but is an order of magnitude faster and generalizes across a much broader range of object categories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-grained damage classification of 3D point cloud data remains challenging due to high computational demands and limited labeled data, motivating the exploration of both 3D-native and 2D-projection-based approaches to balance accuracy, efficiency, and generalizability.

Method: Two pipelines are examined: (1) 3PDA, which segments 3D PCD using PointNet, derives compact representations via TDA, and integrates anomaly detection algorithms to quantify structural degradation; (2) 2PDA, which projects 3D PCD into 2D views and leverages large vision foundation models for granular damage detection at reduced computational cost.

Result: TDA effectively compresses geometric structure into discriminative feature vectors, and anomaly detection models reliably distinguish damage severity in 3PDA. The 2PDA approach achieves competitive classification performance with only a fraction of the computational cost. 3PDA attains higher accuracy but only for narrow object geometries and at substantially higher cost, while 2PDA offers slightly lower accuracy but an order of magnitude reduction in time complexity and broader generalization.

Conclusion: 2D VFM-based pipelines (2PDA) are viable lightweight, resource-efficient alternatives to traditional 3D point cloud architectures (3PDA) for fine-grained damage classification, offering a favorable trade-off between accuracy, computational efficiency, and generalization across object categories.

Abstract: Fine-grained damage classification of 3D point cloud data (PCD) remains a persistent challenge, constrained by high computational demands and limited labeled data. This study examines two methods: 3D PCD-based damage assessment (3PDA) algorithm and 2D projection damage assessment (2PDA) In our 3PDA analysis algorithm, TDA is used to derive compact representations of 3D PCD segmented by pointNet, which are then integrated with anomaly detection algorithms to quantify structural degradation. We show that TDA effectively compresses geometric structure from VFM-segmented components into discriminative feature vectors and that anomaly detection models can reliably distinguish components with varying damage severity using only 3D PCD inputs. In the 2D projection analysis algorithm, we leverage large VFMs for granular damage detection by projecting 3D PCD into 2D views. These projections allow VFM based models to achieve competitive classification performance while requiring only a fraction of the computational cost associated with full 3D data processing. Our results demonstrate that 2D VFM pipelines in 2PDA can perform strongly on fine-grained damage classification tasks, highlighting their viability as lightweight, resource-efficient alternatives to traditional 3PDA architectures. Comparative evaluation shows that the 3PDA attains higher accuracy but only for a narrow subset of object geometries and at substantially higher computational cost due to its reliance on TDA and the scarcity of high-fidelity 3D datasets. In contrast, the 2PDA algorithm yields slightly lower accuracy but offers an order of magnitude reduction in time complexity and generalizes across a far broader range of object categories.

</details>


### [157] [RMR-Net: Degradation-Evidence-Guided Road-Image Restoration for Defect Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08957)
*Amir Ghorbani,Amirali K. Gostar,WeiQin Chuah,Vahid Ghorbani,Aidan Blair,Alireza Bab-Hadiashar*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RMR-Net is a compact task-aware restoration front end for vehicle-mounted road cameras that estimates degradation evidence, fuses it with corruption parameters, conditions lightweight restoration blocks, and returns high-frequency pavement detail via a bounded residual path. It improves road defect detection accuracy across eight held-out degradation conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vehicle-mounted road cameras suffer from motion blur, defocus, poor illumination, and noise, which erase thin cracks and pothole boundaries that road defect detectors need. This degradation severely limits the reliability of automated road inspection systems.

Method: RMR-Net uses a task-aware restoration front end that: (1) estimates degradation evidence from the image, (2) optionally fuses it with existing corruption context/parameters, (3) conditions lightweight restoration blocks, and (4) returns high-frequency pavement detail through a bounded residual path. A clean-trained, frozen YOLO11s detector evaluates all image sources.

Result: Across eight held-out degradation conditions, RMR-Net achieves the highest mAP50 in seven cases, including 0.140-0.427 for IVCNZ motion blur and 0.060-0.233 for PCM defocus. The bounded detail path is the largest local contributor, while degradation conditioning and detector-aware stability terms provide complementary guidance.

Conclusion: The task-aware restoration approach with bounded residual detail paths and degradation conditioning effectively improves downstream defect detection under diverse degradation conditions, though the experimental scope was deliberately limited to saved synthetic-generator parameters rather than measured vehicle telemetry.

Abstract: Vehicle-mounted road cameras are vulnerable to motion blur, defocus, poor illumination, and noise, which can erase thin cracks and pothole boundaries needed by road defect detectors. This paper presents RMR-Net, a compact task-aware restoration front end that estimates degradation evidence from the image, optionally fuses it with existing corruption context/parameters, conditions lightweight restoration blocks, and returns high-frequency pavement detail through a bounded residual path. The experimental scope is deliberately controlled: the conditioning information used on the Image and Vision Computing New Zealand (IVCNZ) pothole dataset and the Road Damage Dataset: Potholes, Cracks and Manholes (PCM) consists of saved synthetic-generator parameters, not measured vehicle telemetry. A clean-trained, frozen YOLO11s detector evaluates every image source. Across eight held-out degradation conditions, RMR-Net obtains the highest mAP50 in seven, including 0.140-0.427 for IVCNZ motion blur and 0.060-0.233 for PCM defocus. A compact ablation identifies the bounded detail path as the largest local contributor, while degradation conditioning and detector-aware stability terms provide complementary guidance.

</details>


### [158] [Fourier Self-Supervision for Fine-Grained Generalized Category Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08963)
*Sarah Rastegar,Mina Ghadimi Atigh,Pascal Mettes,Yuki M. Asano,Cees G. M. Snoek*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper introduces Fourier Self-Supervision, a dual frequency filtering strategy (low-pass and high-pass) applied in separate latent spaces to enhance fine-grained discrimination in Generalized Category Discovery, outperforming state-of-the-art methods on multiple fine-grained datasets even with unknown class counts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GCD methods based on self-supervision and contrastive learning struggle to capture fine-grained distinctions, relying on superficial visual cues rather than intrinsic attributes humans use for categorization.

Method: The method applies a dual frequency filtering strategy: a low-pass filter captures broad, abstract attributes for high-level category information, while a high-pass filter emphasizes fine details like edges and textures. Each filter operates on a dedicated latent space, and their overlapping representations are combined to yield a richer, more complete feature space.

Result: Experiments on multiple fine-grained datasets show that Fourier Self-Supervision outperforms state-of-the-art methods, even when the number of classes is unknown.

Conclusion: Incorporating Fourier Self-Supervision strengthens discriminative power in fine-grained category discovery and demonstrates effectiveness for Generalized Category Discovery, with code publicly available.

Abstract: Generalized Category Discovery aims to recognize known categories while identifying novel ones within unlabeled data. Existing methods, typically based on self-supervision and contrastive learning, often struggle to capture fine-grained distinctions, relying on superficial visual cues rather than the intrinsic attributes humans use for categorization. We introduce Fourier Self-Supervision, that leverages the Fourier transform of images to enhance the discrimination of subtle differences and support the discovery of new categories. Our method employs a dual frequency filtering strategy: a low-pass filter first extracts broad, abstract attributes that capture high-level category information, while a high-pass filter emphasizes fine details such as edges and textures that are essential for fine-grained recognition. Each operates on a dedicated latent space, and their overlapping representations together yield a richer, more complete feature space. This dual-frequency approach not only refines feature extraction to identify novel categories, but also strengthens the model's discriminative power in fine-grained category discovery. Experiments on multiple fine-grained datasets show that incorporating Fourier Self-Supervision outperforms state-of-the-art methods, even when the number of classes is unknown, demonstrating its effectiveness for Generalized Category Discovery. Our code is available at: https://github.com/SarahRastegar/FourEx.

</details>


### [159] [Detecting Clear Contact Lenses for Iris Recognition: A Two-Stage Mask-Guided Attention Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08977)
*Parisa Farmanifard,Arun Ross*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper investigates the impact of clear contact lenses on iris recognition and proposes a two-stage detection framework. The first stage uses an existing PAD model for patterned lenses, while the second stage employs a ConvNeXt-Base model with Mask-Guided Spatial Attention (MGSA) to detect clear lenses using subtle limbal cues. A z-score calibration method is introduced to adjust VeriEye match scores when clear lenses are detected, reducing EER by 4.1%-28.3% across four datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While cosmetic/patterned contact lenses have been well-studied under PAD, clear prescription contact lenses are largely neglected despite their widespread use. They are difficult to detect due to the absence of salient texture artifacts, and are often incorrectly assumed to have no impact on iris recognition performance.

Method: A two-stage contact-lens detection pipeline: Stage 1 uses an existing PAD model to detect patterned lenses; Stage 2 distinguishes clear lenses from no lenses using a ConvNeXt-Base model with a Mask-Guided Spatial Attention (MGSA) module that combines a Hough-derived anatomical ROI mask, learned spatial attention, and Squeeze-and-Excitation channel recalibration to focus on limbal cues. A z-score calibration method is additionally proposed to adjust VeriEye match scores when clear lenses are detected.

Result: Clear lenses marginally degrade genuine match scores and increase verification error on four benchmark datasets using the VeriEye matcher. The full pipeline achieves 90.0%-98.8% accuracy across datasets for combined patterned and clear lens detection. The z-score calibration reduces EER by 4.1%-28.3% across datasets.

Conclusion: Clear contact lenses do impact iris recognition performance, contrary to common assumptions. Reliable clear lens detection can directly improve iris verification performance, demonstrating the practical value of the proposed detection and calibration framework.

Abstract: This work focuses on the impact and detection of clear contact lenses in the context of iris recognition. While the detection of cosmetic or patterned contact lenses has been extensively studied under the presentation attack detection (PAD) paradigm, clear prescription contact lenses, that are typically transparent, have received comparatively less attention despite their widespread use. Unlike patterned lenses, clear lenses introduce no salient texture artifact, making them difficult to detect and are often assumed to have no impact on iris recognition. We first examine this assumption using the commercial VeriEye matcher on four benchmark datasets and show that clear lenses marginally degrade genuine match scores and increase verification error. We then propose a two-stage contact-lens detection framework. Stage~1 uses an existing PAD model to identify patterned lenses, while Stage~2 focuses on the more challenging clear-lens versus no-lens distinction using a ConvNeXt-Base model equipped with Mask-Guided Spatial Attention (MGSA). The proposed MGSA module incorporates a Hough-derived anatomical ROI mask together with learned spatial attention and Squeeze-and-Excitation channel recalibration, allowing the network to focus on subtle limbal cues associated with clear lens wear. Across four datasets, the full pipeline consisting of both patterned and clear contact lens detection achieves between 90.0\%--98.8\% accuracy. Finally, we introduce a z-score calibration method that adjusts VeriEye match scores when a clear lens is detected in the input images. This calibration reduces EER by 4.1\%--28.3\% across datasets, demonstrating that reliable clear contact lens detection can directly improve iris verification performance.

</details>


### [160] [DeepFreqMark: End-To-End Learnable Frequency-Domain Watermarking with Spherical Attack Simulation for Latent Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08999)
*Chen-Hsiu Huang,Mario Köppen,Ja-Ling Wu*

Main category: cs.CV
