A single residual message-passing step shares evidence among related findings, proving to be a bounded perturbation of the identity where co-occurrence enters each logit through an explicit bilinear interaction. On CheXpert, C$^2$A achieves a superior $0.895$ macro-mean AUROC, outperforming advanced context-gating baselines. Crucially, gains concentrate on highly co-occurrent classes with ambiguous spatial evidence (rescuing Atelectasis by $+1.5$ over GCG), demonstrating the prior's regularizing effect with a negligible overhead of one linear projection and a $C\!\times\!C$ edge matrix.

</details>


### [252] [NTIRE 2026 Low-light Enhancement: Twilight Cowboy Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09782)
*Aleksei Khalin,Egor Ershov,Artyom Panshin,Sergey Korchagin,Georgiy Lobarev,Arseniy Terekhin,Sofiia Dorogova,Amir Shamsutdinov,Yasin Mamedov,Bakhtiyar Khalfin,Bogdan Sheludko,Emil Zilyaev,Nikola Banić,Georgy Perevozchikov,Radu Timofte,Shuai Liu,Yuqian Zhang,Lize Zhang,Yibin Huang,Chaoyu Feng,Luyang Wang,Xiaotao Wang,Dongqing Zou,Lei Lei,Tianli Liu,Dejun Hao,Chunxia Lei,Furkan Kınlı,Andrei Mironov,Alexander Dikov,Aleksei Sadokhin,Vladimir Zvorygin,Constantine Habarlak,Shuwei Yue,Egor Mirantsov,Daniil Okunev,Dmitry Arkhipov,Aleksandr Yugay,Anas M. Ali,Bilel Benjdira,Wadii Boulila,Wei Zhou,Linfeng Li,Lingdong Kong,Jiachen Tu,Guoyi Xu,Yaoxin Jiang,Jiajia Liu,Yaokun Shi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents a review of the NTIRE 2026 Twilight Cowboy Challenge, a competition focused on merging misaligned smartphone images in the raw domain under low-light conditions into a single clean image. The challenge introduces a dataset of 585 real-world scenes and a three-stage evaluation protocol. Ten teams surpassed the baseline with up to +6.49 dB PSNR and +0.0101 SSIM improvements, establishing new state-of-the-art performance for burst-based low-light image enhancement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Low-light photography faces two simultaneous challenges: visual degradations (high noise, mixed scene illuminants) and geometric inconsistencies caused by hand movement during multi-frame capture. The competition aims to advance research in low-light and nighttime computational photography by addressing both problems jointly in a realistic setting.

Method: The competition collected a challenging dataset of 585 real-world scenes spanning indoor low-light and outdoor nighttime conditions. A three-stage evaluation protocol was employed: automatic validation via the CodaBench platform in stages one and two, followed by blind assessment on a private test set for the final ranking.

Result: Ten teams surpassed the established baseline, achieving improvements of up to +6.49 dB in PSNR and +0.0101 in SSIM, thereby establishing new state-of-the-art performance for burst-based low-light image enhancement.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate significant progress in handling real-world noise, motion, and illumination variability in the low-light setting. Comprehensive results, leaderboards, and additional information are publicly available at https://nightimaging.org.

Abstract: This paper presents a review of the NTIRE 2026 Low-light Enhancement: Twilight Cowboy Challenge. The objective of the competition was to merge a set of misaligned smartphone images in the raw domain, captured in low-light conditions, into a single, clean image. Introduced setup simultaneously addresses two problems of low-light photography: visual degradations such as high noise and mixed scene illuminants, and the geometric inconsistencies caused by hand movement during multi-frame capture. To advance research in low-light and nighttime computational photography, a challenging dataset was collected comprising 585 real-world scenes, spanning indoor low-light and outdoor nighttime conditions, for training and benchmarking participant solutions. The competition employed a three-stage evaluation protocol: automatic validation via the CodaBench platform in stages one and two, followed by blind assessment on a private test set for the final ranking. Ten teams surpassed the established baseline, achieving improvements of up to +6.49 dB in PSNR and +0.0101 in SSIM, thereby establishing new state-of-the-art performance for burst-based low-light image enhancement. These results demonstrate significant progress in handling real-world noise, motion, and illumination variability in the low-light setting. Comprehensive results, leaderboards, and additional information are publicly available at https://nightimaging.org.

</details>


### [253] [ADOPD: Reference-Privileged On-Policy Distillation for MLLM-Based Industrial Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09789)
*Jingtai He,Shiyuan Meng,Wenchao Meng,Qinmin Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ADOPD is a reference-privileged on-policy distillation framework that internalizes reference-comparison capabilities into a query-only student MLLM for industrial anomaly detection, achieving 77.31% average accuracy on MMAD under zero-shot inference, improving the Qwen3-VL-4B backbone by 6.14 points and outperforming its one-shot setting by 2.64 points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal LLMs benefit from comparing query images against reference images during inference for industrial anomaly detection, but this requires additional retrieval and processing at test time. The paper investigates whether these reference-comparison benefits can instead be internalized into model parameters during training, enabling zero-shot inference without references.

Method: ADOPD uses a reference-aware teacher to supervise a query-only student via reference-privileged on-policy distillation. The teacher evaluates student-generated rollouts under both matched and mismatched references. The matched-reference teacher-to-student log-ratio defines token-level learning direction, while the likelihood gap between matched and mismatched reference views estimates reference-specific support and calibrates sequence-level weights.

Result: ADOPD achieves 77.31% average accuracy on the MMAD benchmark under zero-shot inference, improving the Qwen3-VL-4B backbone by 6.14 points and outperforming its own one-shot setting by 2.64 points.

Conclusion: Experiments demonstrate that ADOPD successfully learns a fine-grained anomaly inspection strategy from reference comparison, effectively distilling reference-based reasoning into model parameters for zero-shot industrial anomaly detection.

Abstract: Industrial anomaly detection (IAD) requires identifying fine-grained deviations from normal visual patterns. Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can improve recognition accuracy by comparing query images with references at inference time, but these benefits rely on additional retrieval and processing. We investigate whether the benefits of reference comparison can instead be internalized in the model parameters. Access to references during training allows a reference-aware teacher to supervise a query-only student. However, the teacher may favor plausible responses based on query cues or language priors rather than valid visual information. We propose ADOPD, a reference-privileged on-policy distillation framework. The teacher evaluates student-generated rollouts under matched and mismatched references. The matched-reference teacher-to-student log-ratio defines the token-level learning direction, specifying what the student should learn. The likelihood gap between the two reference views estimates reference-specific support and calibrates the sequence-level weight. ADOPD achieves 77.31% average accuracy on the MMAD benchmark under zero-shot inference, improving the Qwen3-VL-4B backbone by 6.14 points and outperforming its one-shot setting by 2.64 points. Experiments show that ADOPD learns a fine-grained anomaly inspection strategy from reference comparison. The project will be available at https://github.com/withTai/ADOPD.

</details>


### [254] [Modern Backbones Improve Multi-task DETR for Mammography Classification and Lesion Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09801)
*Dinh Tan Nguyen,Quang-Hien Kha,Le-Hoang Nguyen,Minh-Toan Dinh,Xuan-Huy Nguyen,Dac Phu Ho,Cao Truong Tran,Sai Ho Ling,Lan T Ho-Pham,Liem Pham,Nguyen Quoc Khanh Le*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper studies a multi-task DETR framework for joint exam-level malignancy prediction and lesion localization in mammography. It evaluates various modern backbones on OPTIMAM and SGM1k datasets, finding that ConvNeXtV2 and DINOv3 perform best, with ConvNeXtV2 emerging as a particularly strong CNN backbone for mammography.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Combining exam-level prediction with candidate-region localization may improve the practical usefulness of AI support systems in mammography, but this multi-task setting is underexplored.

Method: A multi-task DETR framework is used, where shared representations support both image-level malignancy prediction and lesion localization, evaluated across multiple backbone architectures on OPTIMAM and a biopsy-confirmed SGM1k cohort.

Result: On OPTIMAM, ConvNeXtV2 achieved 97.96% AUC, 99.89% sensitivity, 25.08% mAP@.5, and 74.38% recall@.25. On SGM1k, DINOv3 achieved 90.97% AUC, 86.28% sensitivity, 82.00% specificity, 27.04% mAP@.5, and 77.32% recall@.25. Modern backbones consistently outperformed older ResNet-style features, while MambaVision was less competitive.

Conclusion: Backbone quality is a critical factor in effective multi-task mammography, with ConvNeXtV2 emerging as a particularly well-matched CNN backbone for this framework.

Abstract: Joint exam-level prediction and candidate-region localization may improve the usefulness of AI support in mammography. We study this setting using a multi-task DETR framework, where shared representations support both image-level malignancy prediction and lesion localization, and evaluate its performance on OPTIMAM and a biopsy-confirmed SGM1k cohort. Across both datasets, modern backbones consistently outperformed older ResNet-style features, with ConvNeXtV2 and DINOv3 giving the strongest overall results, whereas MambaVision was less competitive. On OPTIMAM, ConvNeXtV2 achieved the best overall performance, reaching 97.96% AUC, 99.89% sensitivity, 25.08% mAP@.5, and 74.38% recall@.25. On SGM1k, DINOv3 gave the strongest overall results, with 90.97% AUC, 86.28% sensitivity, 82.00% specificity, 27.04% mAP@.5, and 77.32% recall@.25. These findings suggest that backbone quality is a critical factor in effective multi-task mammography, with ConvNeXtV2 emerging as a particularly strong and well-matched CNN backbone for mammography in this framework.

</details>


### [255] [MedPixel: A Unified Pixel-Language Model for Medical Reasoning and Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09818)
*Haoyu Yang,Meixing Shi,Zengjie Chen,Haoran Sun,Haitao Leng,Xiaoming Shi,Yuxiang Cai,Yankai Jiang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MedPixel is a unified medical pixel-language model that bridges the gap between medical vision-language models (which lack precise localization) and medical segmenters (which rely on explicit spatial prompts), using a shared language-mask interface. It is trained on MedPLG-440K, a ~440K sample pixel-language dataset built via clinically motivated synthesis without external LLM annotation, and uses joint multi-task supervised fine-tuning plus Pixel-Level Preference Optimization (using ground-truth masks as offline verifiers). It supports explicit grounding, implicit reasoning, spatial interaction, grounded explanation, and medical VQA, achieving strong performance in both pixel-level and language generation tasks with effective zero-shot transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medical vision-language models lack precise pixel-level localization, while medical segmenters require explicit categories or spatial prompts. A supervision mismatch exists: segmentation datasets lack language supervision, and medical VLM datasets rarely pair language with dense spatial annotations.

Method: A unified medical pixel-language model built on a shared language-mask interface, trained with joint multi-task supervised fine-tuning followed by Pixel-Level Preference Optimization that uses ground-truth masks as offline verifiers to derive response preferences from mask quality. A novel dataset MedPLG-440K (~440K pixel-language samples) is constructed via clinically motivated synthesis without external LLM annotation.

Result: Achieves strong performance across the task spectrum (explicit grounding, implicit reasoning, spatial interaction, grounded explanation, medical VQA) in both pixel-level prediction and response generation. Demonstrates effective zero-shot transfer to external grounding benchmarks and robustness to imperfect spatial prompts.

Conclusion: MedPixel effectively unifies vision-language understanding with pixel-level grounding in the medical domain, addressing the supervision mismatch and demonstrating strong generalization capabilities. Code and model checkpoints will be made publicly available.

Abstract: Reliable medical image understanding requires models to connect clinical language and visual reasoning with pixel-level grounding. Yet medical vision-language models often lack precise localization, whereas medical segmenters typically rely on explicit target categories or precise spatial prompts. This divide is reinforced by a supervision mismatch: segmentation datasets provide precise masks but little language supervision, whereas medical vision-language data rarely pair language with dense spatial annotations. To address this gap, we present MedPixel, a unified medical pixel-language model built around a shared language--mask interface. To provide scalable supervision, we introduce MedPLG-440K, comprising approximately 440K pixel-language task samples constructed through a clinically motivated synthesis process without external LLM annotation. MedPixel is trained with joint multi-task supervised fine-tuning followed by Pixel-Level Preference Optimization, which uses ground-truth masks as offline verifiers to derive response preferences from mask quality. MedPixel supports a broad spectrum of tasks spanning explicit grounding, implicit reasoning, spatial interaction, grounded explanation, and medical VQA. Across this task spectrum, MedPixel achieves strong performance in both pixel-level prediction and response generation, together with effective zero-shot transfer to external grounding benchmarks and robustness to imperfect spatial prompts. Code and model checkpoints will be released at https://github.com/yhy-whu/Medpixel.

</details>


### [256] [From Diagnosis to Correction: Benchmarking and Improving Real-World Table Parsing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09842)
*Jutao Xiao,Yuan Qu,Dongsheng Ma,Fan Wu,Tianyao He,Weihong Li,Jie Yang,Yu Qiao,Bin Wang,Conghui He*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The authors introduce TableParseMap, a diagnostic benchmark of 916 real-world tables with five challenging scenarios and nine failure types, exposing that top document parsers achieving >93 TEDS on standard benchmarks only reach 85.03 TEDS on real-world tables. They propose DEC (Decompose-Enhance-Correct), a visual-consistency-guided agentic framework that uses a general VLM as controller to improve frozen table parsers without retraining, achieving 1.57-1.89 TEDS improvements across parsers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard table parsing benchmarks report high TEDS scores (above 93) that conceal persistent failures on complex real-world tables, creating a significant gap between benchmark performance and practical utility.

Method: DEC is a visual-consistency-guided agentic framework using a general VLM as controller. It includes: (1) Decompose - partitions large tables along structure-aware boundaries; (2) Enhance - exposes weak visual evidence and reparses transformed views; (3) Correct - diagnoses and repairs residual errors. A Visual Consistency Gate (VC-Gate) selectively triggers intervention, and a Visual Consistency Ranker (VC-Ranker) verifies candidate updates and supports rollback without ground-truth HTML at inference time.

Result: Across three frozen parsers, DEC improves TEDS by 1.57 points on average; on TableParseMap, gains reach 1.89 points overall, 2.62 on structural errors, and 5.66 on large tables. The strongest evaluated parser achieves only 85.03 TEDS on TableParseMap despite scoring above 93 on standard benchmarks.

Conclusion: Aggregate benchmark scores conceal substantial weaknesses in real-world table parsing. The DEC framework effectively improves frozen parsers without retraining, addressing three root causes: large tables exceeding single-pass processing limits, weak visual cues, and visual inconsistency between reconstructed tables and source images.

Abstract: Recent document parsers achieve table TEDS scores above 93 on OmniDocBench v1.6, yet community feedback and our audit reveal persistent failures on complex real-world tables. To quantify this gap, we introduce TableParseMap, a diagnostic benchmark of 916 real-world tables organized into five challenging scenarios and nine failure types. The strongest evaluated parser achieves only 85.03 TEDS, showing that aggregate benchmark scores conceal substantial weaknesses. Our analysis attributes these failures to three complementary limitations: large tables exceed the reliable processing scale of a single pass, weak or ambiguous visual cues hinder structure perception, and the reconstructed table may remain visually inconsistent with the image. We therefore propose DEC (Decompose--Enhance--Correct), a visual-consistency-guided agentic framework that improves frozen table parsers without retraining. DEC uses a general VLM as the controller: Decompose partitions large tables along structure-aware boundaries, Enhance exposes weak visual evidence and reparses transformed views, and Correct diagnoses and repairs residual errors. A Visual Consistency Gate (VC-Gate) selectively triggers intervention, while a Visual Consistency Ranker (VC-Ranker) verifies candidate updates and supports rollback without ground-truth HTML at inference time. We further derive a 1,977-table Consensus-Hard Set from 4,556 candidates through offline metrics and cross-model consensus. Across three frozen parsers, DEC improves TEDS by 1.57 points on average; on TableParseMap, gains reach 1.89 points overall, 2.62 on structural errors, and 5.66 on large tables.

</details>


### [257] [Sci-VBench: Evaluating Knowledge- and Reasoning-Intensive Video Generation in Science Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09873)
*Diandian Zhang,Tingyu Song,Lin Fu,Zheyuan Yang,Yilun Zhao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Sci-VBench is a comprehensive benchmark with 1,253 expert-annotated examples across 60 subjects in four scientific disciplines, designed to evaluate knowledge- and reasoning-intensive video generation. It introduces a rubric-based evaluation protocol where non-expert humans and MLLM-as-Judge can align well with expert judgments. Benchmarking 16 models reveals that while perceptual quality scores cluster tightly, Prompt Grounding and Scientific/Causal Correctness vary significantly with a proprietary-open-source gap, showing advances in visual realism haven't translated to reliable scientific/causal modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing video generation benchmarks focus primarily on surface-level visual plausibility and fail to assess whether models can generate videos requiring deep scientific reasoning and knowledge-grounded synthesis. There is a need for a benchmark that evaluates knowledge- and reasoning-intensive video generation across scientific domains.

Method: The paper constructs Sci-VBench with 1,253 expert-annotated examples spanning 60 subjects across four core disciplines (Natural Science, Healthcare, Humanities & Social Sciences, Engineering). A rubric-based evaluation protocol is established, and both non-expert human evaluators and MLLM-as-Judge systems are tested for agreement with expert judgments. 16 frontier proprietary and open-source models are benchmarked.

Result: Non-expert human evaluators and MLLM-as-Judge systems achieve relatively high agreement with expert judgments under the rubric protocol. Across 16 benchmarked models, automatic perceptual-quality scores cluster tightly, but Prompt Grounding and Scientific and Causal Correctness show substantial variation with a pronounced proprietary-open-source gap.

Conclusion: Recent advances in visual realism in video generation have not yet translated into reliable modeling of scientific and causal dynamics, highlighting a critical gap between perceptual quality and scientific correctness that the field must address.

Abstract: We introduce Sci-VBench, a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating knowledge- and reasoning-intensive video generation across scientific domains. It contains 1,253 expert-annotated examples spanning 60 subjects across four core disciplines: Natural Science, Healthcare, Humanities & Social Sciences, and Engineering. Each example requires models to generate temporally rich videos that demand scientific reasoning and knowledge-grounded synthesis, going beyond surface-level visual plausibility. We further establish a rubric-based evaluation protocol. Our analysis shows that, under this protocol, both non-expert human evaluators and MLLM-as-Judge systems can achieve relatively high agreement with expert judgments, supporting reproducible evaluation at scale. We benchmark 16 frontier proprietary and open-source models and find that, while automatic perceptual-quality scores cluster tightly across systems, performance on Prompt Grounding and Scientific and Causal Correctness varies substantially, with a pronounced proprietary-open-source gap. These findings show that advances in visual realism have not yet translated into reliable modeling of scientific and causal dynamics.

</details>


### [258] [Financial Numerical Prediction and Allocation as Token Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09880)
*Xu Ouyang,Moontae Lee*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: FinATOM introduces a head-free causal language model interface for financial prediction, using constrained token generation for both stock-return forecasting and ETF allocation, achieving improved Sharpe ratios in 2023-2025 tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional financial prediction separates the language model from the numerical object being evaluated by using task-specific regression, ranking, or policy heads. The authors investigate whether a causal language model can directly represent forecasts and decisions through constrained token generation, eliminating the need for separate task-specific heads.

Method: FinATOM uses a unified, head-free interface for two tasks: (1) three-step stock-return forecasting via autoregressive emission of volatility-standardized return tokens, trained with ordinal and ranking supervision followed by a one-epoch token-level policy stage; (2) dynamic five-ETF allocation generating normalized long-only weights, using supervised fine-tuning to imitate a causal mean-variance anchor and DAPO-augmented GRPO to optimize realized 21-day Sharpe subject to anchor consistency.

Result: In 2023-2025 ETF tests, the allocation policy improved pooled gross Sharpe from 1.428 to 1.529 and net Sharpe (5-bp transaction cost) from 1.394 to 1.494. Multimodal allocation input achieved the highest three-period mean Sharpe of 1.540 with the clearest advantage in 2025. On FinTexTS, SFT and policy strategies achieved 73.52%/2.68 and 73.72%/2.69 cumulative-return/Sharpe respectively.

Conclusion: The results support the feasibility of direct language-model token generation for financial numerical prediction and decision-making, while motivating broader tests across different assets, market regimes, and random seeds.

Abstract: Financial prediction typically relies on task-specific regression, ranking, or policy heads, separating the language model from the numerical object ultimately evaluated. We investigate whether a causal language model can instead represent forecasts and decisions directly through constrained token generation. FinATOM introduces a unified, head-free interface for three-step stock-return forecasting and dynamic five-ETF allocation. The forecasting model autoregressively emits volatility-standardized return tokens and is trained with ordinal and ranking supervision followed by a one-epoch token-level policy stage. The allocation model generates normalized long-only weights; supervised fine-tuning imitates a causal mean--variance anchor, and DAPO-augmented GRPO optimizes realized 21-day Sharpe subject to anchor consistency. In 2023--2025 ETF tests, the allocation policy improves pooled gross Sharpe from 1.428 to 1.529 and net Sharpe under a 5-bp transaction-cost model from 1.394 to 1.494. The multimodal allocation input attains the highest three-period mean Sharpe of 1.540, with its clearest advantage in 2025. On FinTexTS, the SFT and policy strategies achieve 73.52\%/2.68 and 73.72\%/2.69 cumulative-return/Sharpe, respectively. These results support the feasibility of direct language-model token generation for financial numerical prediction and decision-making, while motivating broader tests across assets, regimes, and random seeds.

</details>


### [259] [Space-Creating versus Dead Possession: An Off-Ball Possession-Quality Index for Broadcast Football](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09887)
*Seongjin Choi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces a two-layer framework for evaluating football possession quality beyond simple ball-possession statistics. Layer one is an event-based "junk-possession index" that flags low-threat possession sequences in tied-or-losing states (negatively correlated with points and xG difference), and layer two resolves flagged windows via a video-derived Space-Creation Index (SCI) measuring whether possession created space or was spatially dead. Analysis of 2026 FIFA World Cup data shows most flagged windows are non-space-creating, and the junk flag captures information beyond on-ball value models like VAEP.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ball possession statistics are the most cited but most misleading metric in football — 60% possession recycled in one's own half doesn't mean pinning the opponent back. Existing possession-value frameworks (expected threat, VAEP, on-ball value) price only on-ball actions and ignore the off-ball question of whether holding the ball actually created space or was just dead circulation.

Method: Layer 1: An event-side junk-possession index prices possession sequences by peak threat gain under an expected-threat grid, reconstructs the live scoreline to exclude lead-protecting circulation, and flags low-threat sequences in tied-or-losing states. Layer 2: For flagged windows, broadcast video is projected to pitch coordinates to compute a Space-Creation Index (SCI) — a net pitch-control change measuring whether possession seized space or pushed the opponent's block back.

Result: On 2026 FIFA World Cup data (103 matches), the junk flag correlates negatively with points (r=-0.37) and xG difference (r=-0.51). With VAEP and field tilt held fixed, the junk flag stays strongly negatively associated with points (p<0.0001) while VAEP is not significant. Across 31 of 35 flagged windows from nine matches, 74% were spatially non-space-creating, 19% weak progression, and 6% space-creating — including a side with 73% possession that exited on penalties.

Conclusion: The two-layer framework separates space-creating-but-unconverted from sterile possession — a distinction that event-only on-ball value models cannot make. The junk-possession index adds information beyond existing on-ball action value models, and the video-based SCI provides a way to resolve whether flagged possessions were spatially meaningful or dead.

Abstract: Ball possession is the most-cited and most-misleading number in football: 60% recycled in one's own half is not 60% spent pinning the opponent back. Existing event-based possession-value frameworks (expected threat, VAEP, on-ball value) price on-ball actions but ignore the off-ball question a sterile possession poses: did holding the ball create space, or was the circulation dead? We answer this in two layers. First, an event-side junk-possession index prices each possession sequence by its peak threat gain under an expected-threat grid and -- after reconstructing the live scoreline to exclude lead-protecting circulation -- flags low-threat sequences in tied-or-losing states. On the 2026 FIFA World Cup (103 matches, 206 team-matches) the flag correlates negatively with points (r=-0.37) and xG difference (r=-0.51, partly index-coupled). It is not a repackaging of on-ball value: with team offensive VAEP and field tilt held fixed, the junk flag stays strongly negatively associated with points (p<0.0001, also match-clustered) while VAEP is not significant -- in this same-match (descriptive) regression it adds information beyond this on-ball action-value model. Second, for a flagged window we resolve whether it was spatially dead or space-creating by projecting broadcast video to pitch coordinates and measuring a Space-Creation Index (SCI): a net pitch-control change capturing whether the possession seized space or pushed the opponent's block back. Across 31 of 35 flagged windows from nine World Cup matches (a purposive sample), 74% are spatially non-space-creating, 19% weak progression, and 6% space-creating windows the event flag alone would score as failure -- including a side with 73% of the ball that exited on penalties (two non-creating windows). The two layers separate space-creating-but-unconverted from sterile possession, a distinction event-only on-ball value cannot make.

</details>


### [260] [DistMoE: Private-data Rehearsal-free Routing in Mixture-of-Experts for Distributed Instruction Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09907)
*Mainak Singha,Niccolò Biondi,Elisa Ricci,Subhankar Roy*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DistMoE is a mixture-of-experts approach for distributed visual instruction tuning that augments public feedforward networks with client-specific private FFN experts, addresses cross-client drift via public-anchored expert composition with isotropic regularization, and enables token-wise modular routing over experts without explicit domain labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adapting MLLMs to diverse visual-language domains typically assumes centralized data access and costly joint training, which is restrictive when data is distributed across private, domain-specific, or permission-limited clients. Existing approaches lack a practical solution for distributed fine-tuning of multi-modal models.

Method: DistMoE augments each layer of the language decoder with a client-specific private FFN expert next to the public FFN. It introduces a public-anchored expert composition stage that updates only routers and lightweight private projection adapters on mixed local and public data using an isotropic regularization loss, enabling rehearsal-free composition. During inference, modular routing performs token-wise domain composition without explicit domain labels.

Result: Experiments across diverse visual-language benchmarks show DistMoE enables flexible expert reuse, effective domain adaptation, and competitive performance while preserving modular control over client-specific knowledge.

Conclusion: DistMoE demonstrates a practical and effective solution for distributed visual instruction tuning that balances domain adaptation quality with modular control and privacy preservation, with code publicly available.

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have shown strong multimodal instruction-following ability, but adapting them to diverse visual-language domains typically assumes centralized data access and costly joint training. This is restrictive when data is distributed across private, domain-specific, or permission-limited clients. To this end, we propose DistMoE, a mixture-of-experts (MoE) approach for distributed visual instruction tuning. In each layer of the language decoder it augments the public feedforward network (FFN) with a client-specific private FFN expert, with the goal to acquire domain-specific knowledge. However, independent expert training causes the private FFNs to learn representation of different scale and magnitudes, making merging the experts difficult. To reduce client-specific drift, we introduce a public-anchored expert composition stage that updates only routers and lightweight private projection adapters on a mix of local client data and public data, via an isotropic regularization loss, therefore making it cross-client rehearsal-free composition. During inference, DistMoE performs modular routing over public and private experts, enabling token-wise domain composition without explicit domain labels. Experiments across diverse visual-language benchmarks show that DistMoE enables flexible expert reuse, effective domain adaptation, and competitive performance while preserving modular control over client-specific knowledge. Codes are available at https://github.com/mainaksingha01/DistMoE.

</details>


### [261] [Beyond Hazard Resemblance: Contrastive Event Adjudication for Training-Free Video Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09908)
*Wenti Yin,Xiang Wang,Huaxin Zhang,Hanqing Wang,Hongbo Shao,Changxin Gao,Nong Sang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CEAVAD is a training-free video anomaly detection method that shifts inference from isolated anomaly concepts to falsifiable event hypotheses, establishing an inference-time explanatory boundary by contrasting hazard and benign event explanations against video evidence, achieving state-of-the-art performance on three VAD benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing training-free VAD methods leverage pretrained models' semantic knowledge but lack a direct anomaly decision criterion—richer anomaly descriptions capture hazard resemblance without resolving abnormality. Supervised methods require substantial in-domain annotations.

Method: CEAVAD constructs hazard-benign event contrasts from public-safety knowledge, pairing each hazard mechanism with generic normal and mechanism-specific benign counterparts. It determines whether the target interval better supports hazard or benign explanations, producing a revisable contrastive boundary, then adjudicates competing explanations to determine if the hazard hypothesis survives video evidence.

Result: CEAVAD achieves state-of-the-art performance under the training-free paradigm on three widely used VAD benchmarks, supporting both temporally localized anomaly detection and evidence-grounded explanations.

Conclusion: Shifting the unit of inference to falsifiable event hypotheses with contrastive adjudication provides a principled training-free approach to anomaly detection that resolves the ambiguity of isolated anomaly concepts.

Abstract: Video anomaly detection (VAD) aims to identify and temporally localize abnormal events in videos. Supervised methods learn anomaly decision boundaries from target-domain annotations but require substantial in-domain data. Existing training-free methods leverage the rich semantic knowledge and reasoning capabilities of pretrained models to interpret visual content, yet these capabilities do not directly define an anomaly decision criterion: richer anomaly descriptions better capture hazard resemblance without resolving abnormality. To this end, we propose Contrastive Event Adjudication for training-free Video Anomaly Detection (CEAVAD), which shifts the unit of inference from isolated anomaly concepts to falsifiable event hypotheses and establishes an inference-time explanatory boundary through the interaction between competing explanations and video evidence. Specifically, CEAVAD first uses public-safety knowledge to construct hazard-benign event contrasts, pairing each hazard mechanism with a generic normal account and a mechanism-specific benign counterpart. It then determines whether the target interval better supports a hazard explanation or its benign competitor, yielding a revisable contrastive boundary proposal for the target. Finally, CEAVAD adjudicates between the competing explanations to determine whether the hazard hypothesis survives the video evidence, supporting both temporally localized anomaly detection and evidence-grounded explanations. Experiments on three widely used VAD benchmarks demonstrate that CEAVAD achieves state-of-the-art performance under the training-free paradigm.

</details>


### [262] [Learning How the World Evolves: Extrapolative Video World Models via Latent Dynamics Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09926)
*Haodong Li,Shaoteng Liu,Tianyu Wang,Chongjian Ge,Sihui Ji,Jiahan Zhang,Xin Lin,Haolin Lu,Zhe Lin,Manmohan Chandraker*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LDR (Latent Dynamics Reasoning) is a video world model that explicitly models latent transitions via kinematic integration, learning lower-order dynamics numerically and regressing only the high-order residual. It significantly outperforms video diffusion baselines on out-of-distribution physics extrapolation (over 20x smaller gap) while being 26x smaller and 143x faster.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Leading video diffusion models fit pixels without modeling how pixels transit over time, producing visually plausible but physically inaccurate frames. The authors aim to capture true underlying dynamics purely from pixels, enabling extrapolation beyond the training distribution.

Method: LDR casts latent transition as explicit kinematic integration: lower-order dynamics are integrated numerically, while the model only regresses third- and higher-order residual that drives the rollout. Integration runs on a structured latent rather than dense convolutional features to improve extrapolation.

Result: On PhyWorld benchmark (5 physics tasks), LDR's in- vs out-of-distribution error gap is over 20x smaller than video diffusion baselines under single- and joint-task training at 256^2 resolution, using 26x fewer parameters and running 143x faster. It generalizes under severe distribution shift (e.g., red left-moving balls to blue right-moving squares).

Conclusion: LDR is the first video world model that extrapolates learned dynamics beyond its training distribution, directly modeling the laws of motion rather than just pixel appearance.

Abstract: The world evolves following its dynamics, i.e., its laws of motion. However, leading video diffusion models largely fit the pixels without modeling how the pixels transit over time. Thus, they render visually plausible frames but may not accurately obey the laws. To capture the dynamics purely from pixels, we introduce Latent Dynamics Reasoning (LDR). LDR casts the latent transition as an explicit kinematic integration, where the lower-order dynamics are integrated numerically and the model regresses only the third- and higher-order residual that drives the rollout. For this integration to extrapolate better, LDR runs it on a structured latent rather than dense convolutional features. Following PhyWorld, we validate LDR on a controlled white-box physics benchmark spanning five tasks (uniform motion, parabola, collision, bouncing, looming), focusing on out-of-distribution scenarios that reveal whether a model has truly learned the underlying dynamics. LDR extrapolates the learned dynamics far better: the gap between its in- and out-of-distribution error is over 20$\times$ smaller than the video diffusion baseline's, under both single- and joint-task training at 256$^2$ resolution, while using 26$\times$ fewer parameters and running 143$\times$ faster. LDR can even generalize under severe shift: for example, trained only on red balls moving left-to-right, it correctly predicts the motion of a blue square moving right-to-left. To our knowledge, this is the first video world model that extrapolates learned dynamics beyond its training distribution. Project page: https://lat-dyn-reason.github.io/

</details>


### [263] [Multimodal Model Diffing for Feature Discovery and Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09928)
*Hunar Batra,Lachin Naghashyar,Ashkan Khakzar,Philip Torr,Christian Schroeder de Witt,Constantin Venhoff,Ronald Clark*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MMDiff is a multimodal model-diffing framework that trains multimodal sparse autoencoders (SAEs) to discover, isolate, and control features driving Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) behavior. It supports feature isolation (via diffing base-LM vs. multimodal-adapted SAEs), task-specific causal feature detection, and feature-level control (removal/steering). Evaluated on LLaVA-MORE, PaliGemma 2, and InternVL3.5, it selectively degrades target behaviors (12% spatial, 17% OCR, 24% attack success reduction) and improves task accuracy via steering (+3.6% spatial, +1.8% OCR).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MLLMs demonstrate strong visual understanding, but the internal features responsible for this behavior are difficult to identify, audit, or control. Existing sparse autoencoders used on hidden states provide interpretable features but do not isolate which features are changed by multimodal training, nor are they directly useful for targeted control of multimodal behavior.

Method: MMDiff trains multimodal SAEs and turns them into feature-level interfaces. It has three uses: (i) feature isolation by diffing base-LM SAE against a multimodal-adapted counterpart; (ii) task-specific feature detection via per-token contrastive firing analysis to isolate causal features; and (iii) feature-level control by causally removing or steering discovered feature directions. SAEs are trained for three MLLM families (LLaVA-MORE, PaliGemma 2, InternVL3.5).

Result: MMDiff discovers sparse, causally specific features. Removing them selectively degrades target behaviors by average 12% on spatial tasks and 17% on OCR, and reduces attack success rate by 24% on multimodal safety attacks, with no impact on VQA performance. Steering features improves spatial and OCR accuracy by +3.6% and +1.8% on average over a standard single-layer steering baseline.

Conclusion: Multimodal SAEs can serve not only as interpretability tools, but as mechanisms for auditing, steering, and controlling MLLM behavior, toward safer and more capable generations.

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) exhibit strong visual understanding, yet the internal features that cause these behaviors remain difficult to identify, audit, or control. While applicable to post-hoc inspection, hidden states that are decomposed into interpretable feature directions using sparse autoencoders (SAEs) neither readily isolate which features are changed by multimodal training, nor are they directly useful for targeted control. We introduce MMDiff, a multimodal model-diffing framework that trains multimodal SAEs and turns them into feature-level interfaces for discovering and controlling multimodal behavior. MMDiff supports three uses: (i) feature isolation, by diffing a base-LM SAE against its multimodal-adapted counterpart to identify features altered by multimodal training; (ii) task-specific feature detection, via per-token contrastive firing analysis that isolates causal features; and (iii) feature-level control, by causally removing or steering the discovered feature directions. We train multimodal SAEs for three MLLM families, LLaVA-MORE, PaliGemma 2, and InternVL3.5, and evaluate on visual-spatial understanding, multimodal safety, and OCR. MMDiff discovers sparse, causally specific features whose removal selectively degrades target behaviors by an average of 12% on spatial tasks and 17% on OCR, and reduces attack success rate by 24% on multimodal safety attacks, with no impact on VQA performance. Steering these features improves spatial and OCR accuracy by +3.6% and +1.8% on average over a standard single-layer steering baseline. These results show that multimodal SAEs can serve not only as interpretability tools, but as mechanisms for auditing, steering, and controlling MLLMs behavior toward safer and more capable generations.

</details>


### [264] [Perception Before Supervision: Self-Contained Visual Distillation from Counterfactual Blind Spots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09931)
*Shravan Venkatraman,Omkar Thawakar,Ritesh Thawkar,Abdelrahman Shaker,Rao Muhammad Anwer*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CVPD is the first fully self-contained framework for dense, on-policy, token-level visual self-distillation for multimodal large language models (MLLMs). It identifies visual blind spots — regions where zooming sharpens the model's answers but removing them leaves behavior unchanged — and converts these into dense contrastive supervision without external annotations or stronger models. On Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct, it outperforms six self-evolving baselines including GPT-4o-supervised methods across twelve benchmarks with no regressions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-improvement for MLLMs relies on reward-based methods with coarse scalar feedback, while distillation offers richer token-level supervision but typically requires privileged context from external annotations, tools, or stronger models — which is not fully self-contained.

Method: CVPD identifies visual blind spots by detecting regions where zooming in sharpens the model's answer distribution while removing the region leaves full-image behavior unchanged, indicating perceptually encodable but under-utilized information. A three-gate Counterfactual Criterion selects these regions directly from the model's own responses, converting them into dense contrastive supervision for self-distillation.

Result: On Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct, CVPD outperforms six self-evolving baselines across twelve benchmarks, including methods using external GPT-4o supervision, with no regressions. Gains include +3.60 on OCRBench, +3.38 on MMStar Fine-Grained Perception, and +3.08 on MMStar Logical Reasoning, while maintaining or improving broader multimodal performance.

Conclusion: CVPD demonstrates that fully self-contained, dense token-level self-distillation is feasible for MLLMs, and that leveraging the model's own perceptual blind spots through contrastive counterfactual supervision can yield consistent improvements without external supervision.

Abstract: Self-improvement for multimodal large language models (MLLMs) is typically driven by reward-based methods that provide only coarse scalar feedback. Distillation offers a richer alternative through dense token-level supervision, but in the visual domain it usually depends on privileged context constructed using external annotations and tools, or stronger models. We introduce \textbf{CVPD} (Contrastive Counterfactual Visual Process Distillation), which, to the best of our knowledge, is the first fully self-contained framework for dense, on-policy, token-level visual self-distillation for MLLMs. CVPD identifies visual blind spots where zooming into a region changes and sharpens the model's answer distribution, while removing the same region leaves the full-image behavior largely unchanged. Such regions reveal perceptual information that the model can encode but fails to consistently utilize under full-image conditioning. We propose a three-gate Counterfactual Criterion that identifies these regions directly from the model's own responses and converts them into dense contrastive supervision for self-distillation. On Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct, CVPD outperforms six self-evolving baselines across twelve benchmarks, including methods that rely on external GPT-4o supervision, without a single regression. It achieves gains of $+3.60$ on OCRBench, $+3.38$ on MMStar Fine-Grained Perception, and $+3.08$ on MMStar Logical Reasoning, while maintaining or improving performance on broader multimodal benchmarks.

</details>
